Approximation Schemes for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on Geometric Intersection Graphs
Sándor Kisfaludi-Bak, Dániel Marx

Abstract
We give approximation schemes for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on unit disk graphs, and more generally, on intersection graphs of similarly sized connected fat (not necessarily convex) polygons in the plane. As a first step towards this goal, we prove spanner-type results: finding an induced subgraph of bounded size that is ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -equivalent to the original instance in the sense that the optimum value increases only by a factor of at most ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) when the solution can use only the edges in this subgraph. 1. For Subset TSP, our algorithms find a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -equivalent induced subgraph of size poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ⋅ OPT \mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\textup{OPT} in polynomial time, and use it to find a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximate solution in time 2 poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ⋅ n O ⁡ ( 1 ) 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)} . 2. For Steiner Tree, our algorithms find a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -equivalent induced subgraph of size 2 poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ⋅ OPT 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot\textup{OPT} in time 2 poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ⋅ n O ⁡ ( 1 ) 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)} , and use it to find a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximate solution in time 2 2 poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ⋅ n O ⁡ ( 1 ) 2^{2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}}\cdot n^{O(1)} . 3. An improved algorithm finds a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximate solution for Steiner Tree in time 2 poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ⋅ n O ⁡ ( 1 ) 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)} . An easy reduction shows that approximation schemes for unit disks imply approximation schemes for planar graphs. Thus our results are far-reaching generalizations of analogous results of Klein [STOC’06] and Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [ACM TALG’09] for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree in planar graphs. We show that our results are best possible in the sense that dropping any of (i) similarly sized, (ii) connected, or (iii) fat makes both problems APX-hard.



1
Introduction

Most of the basic combinatorial optimization problems defined on graphs are APX-hard, which means that there is a constant
c>1
such that, assuming
\textup{P}\neq\textup{NP}
, there is no polynomial-time approximation algorithm for the problem with approximation ratio better than
c
. In particular, this rules out the existence of a polynomial-time approximation scheme (PTAS), that is, an algorithm that takes and instance of the problem and an
\varepsilon>0
in the input, and outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution in time
n^{f(1/\varepsilon)}
for some function
f
. However, these problems can still admit a PTAS when restricted to some structured class of graphs. For example, there is a long line of research showing that several APX-hard problems admit approximation schemes when restricted to planar graphs (or perhaps even to bounded-genus or minor-free graphs)
[
70
,
35
,
36
,
58
,
57
,
54
,
16
,
24
,
69
,
68
,
13
,
12
,
14
,
37
,
35
,
36
,
39
,
40
,
7
,
18
,
17
,
44
,
60
,
26
,
27
,
23
,
22
,
79
,
1
,
32
,
33
]
. One of the most basic tools in the design of such approximation schemes is a shifting technique attributed to Baker
[
9
]
, which is widely applicable for problems that are local in a certain sense, for example Independent Set or Dominating Set.
The shifting technique reduces a planar instance into several instances where the treewidth of the graph is bounded by a function of
1/\varepsilon
. Such instances can usually be handled by standard dynamic-programming techniques on tree decompositions. Often, the shifting strategy leads to an
efficient polynomial-time approximation scheme (EPTAS)
, which is a PTAS with running time of the form
f(1/\varepsilon)\cdot n^{O(1)}
for some function
f
.

Klein
[
69
]
introduced a contraction-decomposition version of the shifting strategy. This technique can be used for problems where the solution needs to provide some form of connectivity, for example, in TSP (find a shortest closed walk visiting every vertex). Typically, the contraction-decomposition technique allows us to reduce the problem to instances with treewidth
O(1/\varepsilon)
, at the cost of an additive error that is
\varepsilon
times the total size (weight) of the input graph. For problems where the value of the optimum is at least the size of the graph, such as in TSP on unweighted graphs, this additive error results in a multiplicative
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation. However, we cannot make this assumption for TSP on edge-weighted graphs, or for problems where the solution can be much smaller than the graph, for example, in Subset TSP (find a shortest closed walk visiting every vertex of the given terminal set
T
) or Steiner Tree (find a shortest tree containing every vertex of the given terminal set
T
). These problems were approached by first computing a “spanner”: removing edges in a way that changes the optimum value
OPT
at most by a factor of
1+\varepsilon
and ensures that the remaining subgraph has total weight at most
f(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\textup{OPT}
. This strategy was successful for weighted TSP, Subset TSP, Steiner Tree, Steiner Forest, and other problems
[
69
,
68
,
12
,
16
,
15
,
14
,
70
,
27
,
23
,
22
]
.

In the realm of geometric problems (in the plane or in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for fixed
d
), PTASs have been achieved by leveraging the underlying spatial structure to simplify complex instances. For instance, Euclidean TSP has a celebrated PTAS by Arora
[
6
]
that employs a hierarchical partitioning of the Euclidean plane into a grid-like structure, allowing the optimal tour to be approximated by a tour that interacts with grid boundaries only a bounded number of times. More broadly, a range of geometric optimization problems—including clustering, facility location, and network design—benefit either from similar spatial decomposition techniques or from other techniques exploiting the metric properties of the space
[
5
,
47
,
36
,
25
,
15
,
6
,
8
,
1
,
62
,
41
,
38
,
34
,
31
,
30
,
67
]
.

Besides planar graphs and point sets in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, another equally natural and well-studied 2-dimensional setting is given by intersection graphs of various planar objects.
In these graphs, each vertex corresponds to a geometric object—such as disks, rectangles, or segments—and an edge represents the intersection between two objects. This spatial representation often imposes additional structure that can be exploited algorithmically. For instance, many NP-hard problems like Independent Set, Dominating Set, and Vertex Cover, when restricted to intersection graphs of simple geometric shapes (such as unit disks or similarly sized fat objects), admit PTASs
[
63
]
. The key insight is that the geometry enables a partitioning of the problem into regions with limited complexity, often through techniques analogous to the shifting strategy or local search methods. Moreover, for more complex settings where the objects vary in size, shape, or weight, additional layers of techniques are required
[
56
,
66
,
3
,
61
,
75
,
2
,
21
]
.

While many geometric problems on intersection graphs benefit from these techniques, there is a notable lack of PTAS results for connectivity problems. The most natural open question in this direction is settling if Steiner Tree and Subset TSP admit a (E)PTAS on geoemtric intersection graphs. Intuitively, the inherent non-locality of connectivity does not seem to allow any simple use of spatial decomposition techniques. Indeed, as we show later, an (E)PTAS for Steiner Tree and Subset TSP on unit disk intersection graphs implies an (E)PTAS for the same problem on planar graphs (Theorem
1.8
). Thus these geometric problems are
at least as hard
as their planar graph counterparts and hence an EPTAS for them would likely need to be at least as complex as the highly sophisticated spanner constructions for the planar versions
[
24
,
68
]
. It is far from clear if such an EPTAS is possible:
there are cases where a problem on intersection graphs is strictly harder than on planar graphs. There is such a gap even for very basic problems: for example, Independent Set and (Connected) Dominating Set admit an EPTAS on planar graphs, but there is no EPTAS for these problems in unit disk graphs (under suitable complexity assumptions)
[
71
,
72
,
73
,
48
,
45
]
. The intuitive similarity of Connected Dominating Set and Steiner Tree can be interpreted as a warning sign, suggesting that the substantial nonplanarity present in intersection graphs could potentially make Steiner Tree and Subset TSP strictly harder in, say, unit disk graphs compared to planar graphs.

Our main contribuitions are developing a set of tools to handle intersection graphs in various algorithmic and combinatorial contexts, and then using these tools to resolve the open questions by developping EPTASs for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on geometric intersection graphs. Specifically, we consider three results on planar graph EPTASs and show that (unlike in the case of Independent Set etc.), analogous results for geometric intersection graphs do exist. First, for Subset TSP, Klein
[
68
]
presented a way of obtaining a spanner of size at most
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
times the optimum, which, together with the contraction decomposition technique, gives an EPTAS.

Theorem 1.1

(Planar Subset TSP EPTAS via spanner
[
68
]
)
.

Given a planar instance
(G,T)
of Subset TSP and an
\varepsilon>0
,

1.

(Subset spanner) We can find a subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
such that
\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G^{\prime},T)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
and
|V(G^{\prime})|\leq\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
in polynomial time.

2.

(EPTAS) We can find an
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution of the instance
(G,T)
in time
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
.

Building on the Subset TSP result, Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu
[
24
]
obtained a spanner for Steiner Tree, but with a size exponential in
1/\varepsilon
. Consequently, the resulting approximation scheme has double-exponential dependence on
1/\varepsilon
.

Theorem 1.2

(Planar Steiner Tree EPTAS via spanner
[
24
]
)
.

Given a planar instance
(G,T)
of Steiner Tree and an
\varepsilon>0
,

1.

(Steiner-tree spanner) We can find a subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
such that
\mathrm{StTree}(G^{\prime},T)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
and
|V(G^{\prime})|\leq 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
in time
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
.

2.

(EPTAS) We can find a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution of the instance
(G,T)
in time
2^{2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}}\cdot n^{O(1)}
.

To obtain an EPTAS with better dependence on
1/\varepsilon
, the structural insights obtained in the spanner construction can be used in a more efficient way.

Theorem 1.3

(Faster EPTAS for planar Steiner Tree
[
24
]
)
.

Given a planar instance
(G,T)
of Steiner Tree and an
\varepsilon>0
, a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution can be found in time
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
.

1.1
Our results

Our main algorithmic results are counterparts of the EPTASs of Theorems
1.1
–
1.3
to the intersection graphs of unit disks and other objects. Part of our goal was to understand to what extent such EPTASs are possible, therefore we state the results in a more general form, for intersection graphs of “similarly sized fat” (not necessarily convex) polygons. (As we show in Lemma
A.1
, unit disk intersection graphs can be easily expressed by such objects.) We argue that this is a very reasonable and natural class of objects for these problems: we present a set of lower bound results that show that going beyond this class in various ways lead to APX-hard problems.

Formally, an object
p
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is called
\beta
-fat for some
\beta\in(0,1]
if there are balls
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
of radius
r_{1},r_{2}
such that
B_{1}\subseteq p\subset B_{2}
and
r_{1}/r_{2}\geqslant\beta
. A finite collection
X
of objects in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is called
\delta
-similarly-sized for some
\delta\in(0,1]
if
\frac{\min_{x\in X}\mathrm{diam}(x)}{\max_{x\in X}\mathrm{diam}(x)}\geqslant\delta
. Throughout this article, we will assume that our objects are
\beta
-fat and the objects collections
\delta
-similarly-sized for some universal positive constants
\beta,\delta
.

Before presenting our generalizations, let us remark that in planar graphs the edge-weighted versions of Subset TSP and Steiner Tree can be easily reduced to the unweighted versions by subdividing each edge an appropriate number of times. In case of geometric intersection graphs, defining edge-weighted problems usually does not make much sense: large cliques can easily appear in intersection graphs and an edge-weighted clique can be used to represent arbitrary graphs. Vertex-weighted problems do make sense for intersection graphs, but such problems can be much more challenging compared to the unweighted versions. Already for planar graphs, much less is known about the vertex-weighted version
[
49
]
; in particular, Theorems
1.1
–
1.3
work only in case of uniform vertex weights. Therefore, all our results are for the unweighted versions of the problems. In all the algorithmic results, we assume that an intersection graph is given in the input with a representation. The input size
n
is the total size of this representation, i.e., the total number of vertices of the polygons.

Theorem 1.4

(Geometric Subset TSP EPTAS via spanner)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, an intersection graph
G
of
\delta
-similarly sized
\beta
-fat polygons with its representation, and a set

T\subseteq V(G)
of terminals,

1.

(Subset spanner) We can find an induced subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
such that
|V(G^{\prime})|\leq O_{\beta,\delta}(1/\varepsilon^{10})\cdot\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
and
\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G^{\prime},T)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
in polynomial time.

2.

(EPTAS) We can find a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution of the instance
(G,T)
in time
2^{O_{\beta,\delta}(1/\varepsilon^{5}\log(1/\varepsilon))}\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

Theorem 1.5

(Geometric Steiner Tree EPTAS via spanner)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, an intersection graph
G
of
\delta
-similarly sized
\beta
-fat polygons with its representation, and a set

T\subseteq V(G)
of terminals,

1.

(Steiner-tree spanner) We can find an induced subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
such that
|V(G^{\prime})|\leq 2^{O_{\beta,\delta}(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
and
\mathrm{StTree}(G^{\prime},T)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)

in time
{2^{O_{\beta,\delta}(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}}\mathrm{poly}(m)
.

2.

(EPTAS) We can find a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution of the instance
(G,T)
in time
2^{2^{O_{\beta,\delta}(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}}\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

Theorem 1.6

(Faster EPTAS for geometric Steiner Tree)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, an intersection graph
G
of
\delta
-similarly sized
\beta
-fat polygons with its representation, and a set
T\subseteq V(G)
of terminals, we can find a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution in time
2^{O_{\beta,\delta}(1/\varepsilon^{33})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

We show that the EPTASs for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on intersection graphs of connected similarly sized fat objects is optimal in the sense that dropping any of the three conditions makes both problems APX-hard problems. Additionally, moving to three dimensions also make the problem APX-hard, even for the simple case of unit balls.

Theorem 1.7

(APX-hardness results)
.

Steiner Tree
and
Subset TSP
are APX-hard in intersection graphs of:

(a)

similarly sized fat (but potentially disconnected) objects, even for unit-diameter 1/3-fat objects with two connected components,

(b)

similarly sized connected (but potentially non-fat) objects, even for unit-diameter objects,

(c)

connected fat (but potentially differently sized) objects, even if each object is
0.18
-fat, and

(d)

similarly sized connected fat objects in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, even if each object is a unit-diameter ball.

To formally show that approximation schemes for intersection graphs imply approximation schemes in planar graphs, we present reductions of an appropriate form that transfers the existence of (E)PTASs. This reduction shows that Theorems
1.4
–
1.6
can be seen as generalizations of Theorems
1.1
–
1.3
(but note that the exponents in the
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
terms are somewhat weaker in our results).

Theorem 1.8

(Reduction from planar graphs to unit disk graphs)
.

There is a PTAS reduction from
Planar Subset TSP
to
Unit Disk Subset TSP
and from
Planar Steiner Tree
to
Unit Disk Steiner Tree
.

New concepts and techniques of independent interest

As the setting of intersection graphs is siginficantly more general than planar graphs, we need first to develop a number of basic tools that were not available for intersection graphs. These techniques could be of independent interest for other problems involving interection graphs. Note that we state these results in a very general form, considering similarly sized connected fat polygons, making them potentially useful in a wider range of applications.

•

The faces of a planar graph partition the plane into disjoint regions. Such a decomposition is crucially used in the proofs of Theorems
1.1
–
1.3
, but it is not clear what the intersection graph analog of such a clean partition would be. We introduce the notion of
wireframes,
which are planar graph representations of intersection graphs. Wireframes will be the main structures guiding our algorithm by decomposing the problem into regions. We also introduce object frames, a planar intersection graph whose objects are parts of the original objects.

•

Contraction decomposition
is an important step in planar PTASs, allowing us to reduce the problem to bounded-treewidth graphs. We show a clean way of proving such results for intersection graphs: first, we show how a
Lipschitz embedding
into planar graphs can be obtained, and then we show that contraction decomposition of the target planar graph can be lifted to the original intersection graph.

•

One obvious difference compared to planar graphs is that intersection graphs of even very simple objects can contain large cliques. However, we can preprocess the instance with a
sparsification
procedure, which, at the cost of
(1+\epsilon)
-factor increase of the optimum, reduces the maximum clique size to polynomial in
1/\varepsilon
(for Subset TSP) or exponential in
1/\varepsilon
(Steiner Tree).

•

Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu
[
24
]
improved Theorem
1.2
(2) to Theorem
1.3
using an elaborate dynamic programming algorithm designed specifically for their setting. We argue that an alternative, more streamlined way of doing this is via a reduction to a
vertex-weighted
Steiner Tree problem on bounded-treewidth graphs. This change of viewpoint is the starting point in our proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Having these fairly self-contained components at hand seems to be a prerequisite for working towards Theorems
1.4
–
1.6
. However, we want to emphasize that obtaining these results is only the start of the journey. The differences between planar graphs and intersection graphs make it necessary to deviate from the proof of Theorems
1.1
–
1.3
at every step, introducing new proof ideas that handle the phenomena specific to intersection graphs. In Sections
2.1
–
2.3
, the paragraphs marked with “(NEW)” overview these additional proof ideas.

It turns out that perhaps the most significant initial difference between planar graphs and intersection graphs is that in planar graphs whenever two paths cross, then they share a vertex. For contrast, consider the unit disk intersection graph in Figure
1
(i). There is a unique shortest
a-b
path and a unique shortest
c-d
path. Even though these two paths cross twice, it is not possible to reroute any of the two paths to the other path without increasing its length (contrary to what we would expect in a planar graph). This difference invalidates the main principle behind the structural decompositions in the proof of Theorems
1.1
–
1.3
. This principle can be briefly formulated as follows (see Figure
1
(ii)). Let
C
be a cycle such that for any two vertices
x,y\in C
, the shorter of the two paths between
x
and
y
on
C
is a shortest
x-y
path. Then if
P
is a path with endpoints outside
C
and
P
enters
C
, then
P
can be rerouted, without increasing its length, to avoid the interior of
C
. Thus such a cycle can effectively make the interior irrelevant with respect to certain connections. In intersection graphs, the rerouting may incur additional cost, invalidating this argument. These additional costs have a cascading effect on the algorithm: they require extra layers of proof ideas to make certain steps more robust to such cost increases, which in turn require further extra layers of arguments in the later parts of the algorithm.

Figure 1:
(i) Two paths in a unit disk graphs that cannot be shortened: on the blue path from
a
to
b
, the distance of the disk from
a
is indicated with blue numbers, and on the red
c\rightarrow d
path the red number is the distance from
c
. (ii) In a planar graph, if
C
is a cycle where the distance of vertices on the cycle is less or equal to their graph distance, then we can shorten any path
P
that intersects
C
at least twice.

1.2
Organization

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
gives an overview of both previous techniques in planar graphs and our new techniques in intersection graphs to achieve the desired EPTASes. In Section
3
, we recall standard notation for graphs and geometric objects, and define new concepts introduced in the paper, such as wireframes and object frames.

Sections
4
–
7
contain basic results that are crucial for the algorithms. As they are mostly used in black-box fashion, the reader may decide to only skim these sections at first, and jump directly to the algorithmic results starting in Section
8
.
In more detail, Section
4
shows how a set of objects can be turned into an object frame while providing the same connectivity for terminal points. This result is crucial for the structural results for Steiner tree (Section
10
), but a special case with only two terminals is used already in the construction of the so-called skeleton and the spanner in Section
8
.
Section
5
presents our results on Lipschitz embeddings into planar graphs. These results are used, among other places, in Section
6
, where we prove our contraction decomposition results for geometric objects. Section
7
contains the sparsification results for both Subset TSP and Steiner Tree, showing that the clique size can be reduced to a function of
1/\varepsilon
.

Our main result for Subset TSP (Theorem
1.4
) is proved in Section
8
, by first constructing the subset spanner and then using it with contraction decomposition and a bounded-treewidth algorithm.

Theorem
1.5
for Steiner Tree is proved in Sections
9
and
10
: the so-called mortar graph is constructed in Section
9
and the main structural theorem for Steiner trees is stated in Section
10
.
The faster PTAS for Steiner Tree (Theorem
1.6
) is proved in Section
11
using this structural understanding.

Section
12
proves our APX-hardness results (Theorem
1.7
), as well as the connection to planar problems (Theorem
1.8
).

Most of our results are proved for so-called
\alpha
-standard intersection graphs of polygons. Appendix
A
discusses how intersection graphs of similarly sized connected fat objects can be converted to such a representation.

2
Overview

2.1
EPTAS for Subset TSP

We first review the main steps of the proof of Theorem
1.1
by Klein
[
68
]
, and then briefly explain the new ideas and concepts in our algorithm for the geometric intersection graph setting. In this and the following sections, we first go through the main algorithmic components of the previous algorithms and then introduce the new components that we developed.

2.1.1
Overview of Klein’s techniques
[
68
]
in planar graphs

Skeleton and subset spanner construction

The first step in the algorithm of Klein
[
68
]
is to build a
subset spanner:
as stated in the first part of Theorem
1.1
, this is a subgraph
G^{\prime}
, containing all the terminals, such that
|V(G^{\prime})|\leq\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
and
\mathrm{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(x,y)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)
for any
x,y\in T
. Let us start with any tree containing every vertex of
T
and having size
O(\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T))
; for example, a constant-factor approximate Steiner tree is suitable. This is likely not a correct subset spanner yet: a shortest
x-y
path
P
may use “shortcuts” outside the tree. To obtain a spanner, we need to extend the tree (with a factor of
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
increase in total size) in a way that the shortest path
P
can be rerouted (at the cost of at most a factor of
1+\varepsilon
increase in length). It is convenient for the description of the algorithm to change the setting: using the standard step of “cutting open the tree”, we can assume that the boundary cycle
C
of the infinite face contains all of
T
, and we want to extend
C
into a subset spanner, increasing its size by at most a factor of
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
.

As an intermediate step, we extend
C
into a so-called skeleton (a subgraph of
G
), satisfying a weaker property. Let
F
be a face of the skeleton; with slight abuse of notation, let
G[F]
be the subgraph of
G
containing every edge inside
F
or on the boundary of
F
. The boundary of each (finite) face
F
of the skeleton is divided into two paths, North and South, such that

•

For any two vertices
x,y
of North, the subpath
\textup{North}[x,y]
is a shortest
x-y
path in
G[F]
.

•

For any two vertices
x,y
of South, the subpath
\textup{South}[x,y]
is a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate shortest
x-y
path in
G[F]
.

We extend the cycle
C
with the following iterative procedure. Suppose that
C
has two vertices
x,y
such that there is a “shortcut”: an
x-y
path
P
inside
C
such that
|P|
is more than a factor of
(1+\varepsilon)
shorter than the
x-y
subpath
P^{\prime}
of
C
. Let us choose
P
such that
|P^{\prime}|
is minimum possible. If we extend
C
by adding
P
to it, then we are creating two faces (see Figure
2
):

•

A face bounded by
P
and
P^{\prime}
, which satisfies the requirements when we define
P
to be North (as it is a shortest path) and
P^{\prime}
to be South (where
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate minimality follows from the minimal choice of
P^{\prime}
).

•

A face bounded by the cycle
C^{\prime}
, which is obtained from
C
by replacing
P
with
P^{\prime}
.

Let us continue this process with the cycle
C^{\prime}
, stopping when no shortcut can be found in the cycle. A charging argument shows that the total length of all the added paths is
O(1/\varepsilon)
times the length of the initial cycle
C
.

Figure 2:
(i) Adding the shortcut
P
to the skeleton creates two faces inside
C
. (ii) The final skeleton after adding multiple shortcuts (red). The final orange face has no shortcuts. (iii) A face of the skeleton, with a North-North, a South-South, and a North-South path going through it.

Does the resulting graph have the subset spanner property? Consider a shortest
x-y
path
P
in
G
and consider a subpath
P^{\prime}
that intersects the skeleton only in the endpoints
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
, i.e.,
P^{\prime}
goes inside some face
F
(see Figure
2
(iii)). If
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are both on the North part of
F
, then the shortest path property shows that
P^{\prime}
can be rerouted along North without any increase of length. Similarly, if
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are both on South, then
P^{\prime}
can be rerouted along South with a
(1+\varepsilon)
factor increase of length. To handle the situation where
P^{\prime}
connects North and South, Klein
[
68
]
constructed a spanner connecting two paths, in the following sense:

Lemma 2.1

(Bipartite spanner, Klein
[
68
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph, and let
Q
be a shortest path in
G
from
a
to
b
, and
let
Q^{\prime}
be an arbitrary path from
a
to
b
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there is
a subgraph
H
with
|H|=O(1/\varepsilon^{3})|Q^{\prime}|
such that for any
q\in V(Q)
and

q^{\prime}\in V(Q^{\prime})
we have
\mathrm{dist}_{H\cup Q\cup Q^{\prime}}(q,q^{\prime})\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(q,q^{\prime})
.

Using Lemma
2.1
in every face of the skeleton with
Q=\textup{North}
and
Q^{\prime}=\textup{South}
allows us to extend the skeleton such that
P^{\prime}
can be rerouted with a factor of
1+\varepsilon
increase whenever it connects North and South. Overall, we create the required spanner having size
O(1/\varepsilon^{4}|V(C)|)
.

Contraction decomposition and bounded-treewidth algorithms

Using the subset spanner construction, we can assume that
|V(G)|,|E(G)|\leq\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
. Next we would like to decrease the treewidth of
G
by contracting an appropriate small set of edges. To find this set of edges, we use the following contraction decomposition result (a similar result was implicitely proved by Klein
[
69
]
).

Theorem 2.2

(Contraction Decomposition for Planar Graphs, Demaine
et al.

[
51
]
)
.

For any integer
k\geqslant 2
, the edge set of any planar graph
G
can be
partitioned into
k
sets
E_{1},\dots,E_{k}
such that
\mathrm{tw}(G/E_{i})=O(k)
for all

i\in[k]
. The partition can be found in
O(n^{3/2}\log n)
time.

The smallest set
E_{i}
has size at most
|E(G)|/k=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)/k
. Thus by setting
k
to be sufficiently large (but still polynomial in
1/\varepsilon
), we can ensure that
|E_{i}|\leq\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
. It can be shown that contracting a set
E
of edges changes the optimum of Subset TSP by at most
2|E|
, hence it is sufficient to approximate
\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G/E_{i},T)
, where we know that
\mathrm{tw}(G/E_{i})=O(k)=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
. Such an instance can be solved in time
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot n^{O(1)}}
using standard dynamic-programming techniques.

2.1.2
Solving Subset TSP in geometric intersection graphs

Cell partition

Intersection graphs can contain large cliques, making their structure significantly more complicated compared to planar graphs. However, it is well-known that in case of similarly sized fat objects, the intersection graph
G
can be partitioned into cliques such that each clique is adjacent to a bounded number of other cliques. For the description of this partition, it is convenient to define similarly sized fat objects differently. We say that an intersection graph of simple polygons is
\alpha
-standard if (i) each component of the intersection of two polygons is a simple polygon, (ii) no three polygon edges meet at one point, (iii) each polygon contains a closed disk of radius 1 and has diameter at most
\alpha
. We denote by
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
the set of all
\alpha
-standard intersection graphs. It can be shown that an intersection graph of
\delta
-similarly sized
\beta
-fat polygons is in
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha
depending on
\beta
and
\delta
(see Theorem
3.4
). From now on, we state the results for this class
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
.

Formally, we define the
cell partition

\mathcal{P}
as follows. Consider the grid of points in the plane with integer coordinates. By item (iii) in the definition of
\alpha
-standard, every object has one such point in its interior. Let us assign each object to the lexicographically first grid point that is in its interior; let
\mathcal{P}
be the partition of the objects according to which point they are assigned to. Clearly, every class of
\mathcal{P}
induces a clique in
G
. We denote by
G_{\mathcal{P}}
the graph obtained by contracting each class of
\mathcal{P}
into a single vertex. Often, it is usefully to think of the structure of
G
in terms of the graph
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, as this graph better reveals its close-to-planar structure. Observe that two objects can intersect only if they are assigned to lattice points at distance at most
O(\alpha)
. Thus a vertex of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
can be adjacent only to vertices that represent points at distance
O(\alpha)
in the plane; in particular
G_{\mathcal{P}}
has maximum degree
O(\alpha^{2})
.

(NEW) Sparsification

A clique in the partition
\mathcal{P}
can be large, but in Section
7
we show that the instance can be preprocessed to make the size of these cliques bounded. First, if a clique contains many terminals, then (as they all have to be visited anyways), we may assume that they are mostly visited after each other. This observation allows us to decrease the number of terminals and assume that
|C\cap T|=O(1/\varepsilon)
for every
C\in\mathcal{P}
. Let us select a distinguished vertex of each clique. A solution of Subset TSP consists of paths between terminals. If such a path is longer than
O(1/\varepsilon)
, then we can modify it in such a way that after every
O(1/\varepsilon)
steps it visits a distinguished vertex, and this modification increases the cost only by a factor of
1+O(\varepsilon)
. This means that we can assume that this
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution consists of paths of length
O(1/\varepsilon)
between terminals and distinguished vertices. Let us fix such a path for every pair. Consider a clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
. There are
O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
other cliques that are at distance
O(1/\varepsilon)
, thus there are
O(1/\varepsilon^{3})
terminals and distinguished vertices at distance
O(1/\varepsilon)
. This means that there are at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{6})
pairs of these vertices such that the fixed shortest path of length
O(1/\varepsilon)
between them go through
C
. As a shortest path can use only at most two vertices of a clique, this means that only
O(1/\varepsilon^{6})
vertices of the clique are used by these paths and the rest of the vertices can be removed from the graph without affecting the
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution. Therefore, we can assume from now on that every clique has size
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
.

(NEW) Wireframes and regions

Our goal is again to find a subset spanner, as required by the first part of Theorem
1.4
. In the planar case, the faces of the skeleton partitioned the instance into (internally) disjoint regions, and we could handle each region independently. The first problem we encounter in the case of intersection graphs is that the notion of faces, regions, being inside, being outside, etc. are much less clear. To tackle this difficulty, we introduce a formalism that turns out to be essential for the robust and formal description of the algorithms (especially for Steiner Tree).

Given an intersection graph
G
, a
wireframe
is a plane graph
\widetilde{H}
where vertices are points in the plane and edges are polygonal curves. Each vertex
v
of
\widetilde{H}
has a parent object
p(v)
of
G
such that
v
is located in the object
p(v)
. Moreover, for each edge
uv
of
\widetilde{H}
, the corresponding polygonal curve can be split into a part inside
p(u)
and a part inside
p(v)
(see Definition
3.6
for the precise definition). The graph
\widetilde{H}
can have much fewer edges and vertices than
G
: it can be thought of as a planar subgraph of the intersection graph, with a certain form of planar representation. Note, however, that it is not necessarily a subgraph, as multiple points can have the same parent object.

(NEW) Skeleton and subset spanner for intersection graphs.

In Section
8
, similarly to the planar algorithm, we first construct a 2-connected skeleton. In light of the sparsification result mentioned above, it is sufficient to bound by
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
the number of
cliques
of the cell partition
\mathcal{P}
that appear in the skeleton, as this also bounds the number of objects and hence proves Theorem
1.4
(1).

Formally, now this skeleton is a wireframe of the original intersection graph
G
. As before, we want to divide the boundary of each face into North and South such that any subpath of them is (approximately) shorter than any path connecting the endpoints through objects “inside” the face. We use the following definition to remove any potential ambiguity of what it means that a path of objects is inside the face.
Let
\mathsf{R}
be a region of the plane corresponding to a face of the skeleton. We define the intersection graph
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
, where for every object
v\in V(G)
, we introduce into
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
every component of
v\cap\mathsf{R}
as a separate object (so the objects in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
are also connected polygons). Note that, due to these smaller objects created by the intersection with the boundary, the polygons in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
are no longer similarly sized fat objects.
When constructing the skeleton, we want to ensure that the boundary of each face is divided into two parts, North and South, such that between any two vertices
x,y
on North, the subpath of North is not longer than the distance between
x
and
y
in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
(and similarly for South with a factor of
1+\varepsilon
).

We can build the skeleton with a similar iterative procedure as in the planar case. There is one technical challenge that appears when adding the shortcut and moving from a larger region
\mathsf{R}
to a smaller region
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
: because we are cutting the objects at the boundary, the total number/complexity of the polygons in
G|_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}}
may be larger than in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
. Thus repeated applications of this step may lead to an exponential number of objects. We overcome this issue by an appropriate (nontrivial) choice of the polygonal curve that we are cutting along.

After constructing the skeleton, for every face bounding some region
\mathsf{R}
, we use Theorem
2.1
on
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
to add connections between North and South (note that Theorem
2.1
works in arbitrary graphs). Does the resulting set of objects have the spanning property? Consider a path
P
between two terminals and let
P^{\prime}
be a subpath that enters and leaves region
\mathsf{R}
by crossing North of
\mathsf{R}
. Here is the point where the issue about crossing paths demonstrated in Figure
1
comes into play: it is possible that the subpath
P^{\prime}
is not using any object of the path North. This means that if we want to use a subpath of North for rerouting a path, then the length is increased by one when we “jump” to North, and then once more when we “jump” back to the original path.
Therefore, even though North has the required shortest path property, rerouting
P^{\prime}
on North may increase length by 2. If
P^{\prime}
is of length
\Omega(1/\varepsilon)
, this increase by 2 is introduces only
1+O(\varepsilon)
multiplicative error, and hence tolerable. However, if
|P^{\prime}|
is
o(1/\varepsilon)
, then the increase by 2 is significant.

Let us notice that if
|P^{\prime}|=O(1/\varepsilon)
, then
P^{\prime}
is fully contained in the distance-
O(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the skeleton. Thus if we include in the subset spanner the distance-
O(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the skeleton, then every such short path
P^{\prime}
is fully included in the subset spanner. Consequently, if the subpath
P^{\prime}
leaves the subset spanner, then it has to be of length
\Omega(1/\varepsilon)
, hence it can be rerouted along North with a factor
1+O(\varepsilon)
increase. We can argue similarly for subpaths entering and leaving by crossing South, or for subpaths connecting North and South. Notice that there are
O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
cliques in the cell partition that are at distance at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
from an object.
Thus adding the distance-
O(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the skeleton increases the number of cells only by a factor of
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
and hence we can ensure that the obtained subset spanner is the union of
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
cliques of the cell partition, as we wanted. Moreover, our earlier sparsification ensured that each clique contains
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
objects, hence in fact the subset spanner consists of
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
objects, proving the first part of Theorem
1.4
.

(NEW) Contraction decomposition via Lipschitz embedding

Having constructed the subset spanner, we can assume that
|V(G)|\leq\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)
.
To reduce the problem to an instance with bounded treewidth, we would like to use an analog of Theorem
2.2
for intersection graphs. Let us observe that the intersection graph itself can be a clique, thus it is impossible to reduce treewidth to a constant by contracting a small part of the graph. However, by the sparsification procedure described above, we can assume that there is a cell partition where every clique has size
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
. Therefore, in Section
6
, we prove the analog of Theorem
2.2
under a bound on the clique size. As a minor notational difference, instead of edge contractions, it will be convenient to consider contracting vertex sets. Given a set
X
of vertices, we let
G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

to be the graph obtained by contracting each component of
G[X]
into a single vertex. The following statement is proved by Theorem
6.1
(ii):

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
be an intersection graph of similarly sized fat objects with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
where each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
has size at most
|C|\leqslant\ell
.
Given an integer
k
, we can compute in polynomial time
k
sets
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}
of vertices such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|X_{i}|=O(|\mathcal{P}|\ell)
and
\mathrm{tw}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i})=O(k\ell)
.

We remark that in case of unit disk graphs, there are now known contraction decomposition theorems
[
74
,
10
]
that can handle cliques, but due to our more general objects these results are insufficient for our purposes. (In fact, they are insufficient for our Steiner tree algorithm even if the input graph is a unit disk graph, as we need to modify our starting objects via region restrictions and other changes.)

With Theorem
2.3
at hand and using the bounds on
|\mathcal{P}|
and on the maximum clique size, we can solve the instance in time
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
using a standard dynamic programming algorithm solving the problem on bounded-treewidth graphs.

It is natural to approach the proof of Theorem
2.3
via the graph
G_{\mathcal{P}}
: we would like to find sets of vertices
X^{\prime}_{i}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
such that

has treewidth

. For each

, there is a set

of at most

vertices in

, corresponding to the cliques in
X^{\prime}_{i}
. Furthermore, if we replace each vertex of

, then we get a supergraph of

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i}
. Thus the treewidth bound of
O(k)
on
G_{\mathcal{P}}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

would imply the required bound of
O(k\ell)
on
G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i}
.

How can we find these sets
X^{\prime}_{i}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
? As
G_{\mathcal{P}}
is somewhat close to being planar, we can try to approximate it with a genuinely planar graph
H
and then invoke Theorem
2.2
on
H
. However, the requirements of this approximation is fairly delicate and naive ideas (such as representing
G_{\mathcal{P}}
by an induced subgraph of a sufficiently fine grid) fail. The reason is that
H
has to strike a balance between two conflicting requirements:

•

H
should not have too many edges: otherwise, contracting a small set in
H
does not correspond to the contraction of a small set in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.

•

H
should not have too few edges: otherwise, small treewidth after contracting a set in
H
does not mean that contracting the corresponding set in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
results in small treewidth.

It turns out that the notion of Lipschitz embedding satisfies these requirements.
We say that a mapping
f:V(G)\rightarrow V(H)
is

c
-Lipschitz
for some constant
c\geqslant 1
if for any pair of vertices
x,y\in V(G)

we have that

\frac{1}{c}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(f(x),f(y))\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y).

For unit disk intersection graphs, a construction of a
c
-Lipschitz embedding into a planar graph is known for some constant
c
: in fact, they admit planar
c
-spanners
[
28
,
19
]
. In Section
5
, we construct
c
-Lipschitz embeddings for the intersection graphs of connected similarly sized fat objects. The fact that we are handling non-convex objects makes the construction of the embedding significantly more challenging and requires a different strategy. (See
Theorem

5.2
for a more detailed theorem statement.)

Theorem 2.4
.

For any
\alpha>0
, there exists a positive constant
c=O(\alpha^{32})
such that
for any
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
given by its representation the following hold. Let

\mathcal{P}
be the cell partition of
G
. Then in polynomial time one can construct
a plane graph
H
with the same vertex set as
G_{\mathcal{P}}
such that the identity mapping is
c
-Lipschitz from
G_{\mathcal{P}}
to
H
.

Very briefly, we want to represent edges of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
by polygonal curves that are covered by two adjacent objects. Then we introduce a new vertex at each point where two such curves intersect each other. If the objects are convex, then the curves can be chosen to be simple and it is easy to bound the number of these intersections. However, if the objects are not convex, then already two curves can have lots of intersections. In case two curves intersect multiple times, we can try to reroute one curve on the other to reduce the number of intersections, at the cost of making some curves longer. However, doing this iteratively can make curves arbitrarily longer. In order to avoid this, we split the curves into a constant number of nonconflicting sets, perform many reroutings in parallel, and then repeat this in a constant number of rounds. Theorem
2.4
requires that
H
and
G_{\mathcal{P}}
have the same vertex set, but our construction introduced many additional vertices at the intersections. To get rid of these intersection vertices, we can contract the Voronoi regions of the original vertices, retaining the Lipschitz propeperty and ensuring that the planar graph
H
has the same vertex set as
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.

The constructed planar graph
H
will be used as a proxy for
G_{\mathcal{P}}
when trying to reduce treewidth using contractions. We invoke Theorem
2.2
to find a small set
X
in
H
whose contraction reduces treewidth. To complete the proof of Theorem
2.3
, we need to argue that an analogous contraction reduces treewidth in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
as well. This requires a delicate argument that modifies a tree decomposition of a contraction of
H
to obtain a tree decomposition of a contraction of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. In particular, it is not sufficient to contract
X
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, but we need to contract a small neighborhood of
X
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
G
and
H
be graphs with the same vertex set
V
,
so that the identity map of
V
is
c
-Lipschitz from
G
to
H
. Let
X\subset V
be
arbitrary, and let
X^{r}
be the set of vertices whose endpoints have

H
-distance at most
r
from some vertex in
X
, i.e., we set
X^{r}=N_{H}(X,r)
. Assume moreover that
H
has degree at most
\Delta
. Then
\mathrm{tw}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X^{c^{2}})=O(\Delta^{c})\cdot\mathrm{tw}(H\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
