\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X)
.

2.2
EPTAS for Steiner Tree

2.2.1
Overview of the techniques of Borradaile et al.
[
24
]
in planar graphs

Let us review the EPTAS of Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu
[
24
]
for Steiner Tree on planar graphs and then explain what new algorithmic components are needed in case of intersection graphs (for example, techniques to obtain noncrossing structures of various types, which can be taken for granted in the case of planar graphs). The algorithm for planar graphs starts with the same idea as in the case of Subset TSP: constructing a
Steiner-tree spanner,
1
1

1

The term

banyan
is a different name for the same object
[
76
,
11
]
.
that is, a subgraph that admits a
(1+\varepsilon)
-optimal Steiner tree of the terminals in
T
. However, the size of the Steiner-tree spanner obtained by the algorithm can be bounded only by
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
, hence the contraction decomposition can reduce the treewidth only to
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
, resulting in
2^{2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}}\cdot n^{O(1)}
running time after performing contraction decomposition and solving the resulting bounded-treewidth instance.

Columns and the mortar graphs

The algorithm for Steiner Tree starts similarly to the Subset TSP algorithm: with the construction of the skeleton of size
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
. But then each face
F
is further partitioned, the following way. Consider a face of the skeleton, with its boundary partitioned into South and North. Let
s_{0}
be an endpoint of South and then for
i=1,2,\dots
, vertex
s_{i}
is defined to be the first vertex
s_{i}
on South such that the distance from
s_{i}
to North inside the face
F
is at most
|\textup{South}[s_{i-1},s_{i}]|/\varepsilon
. Let column
C_{i}
be a shortest path from
s_{i}
to North. In a planar graph, we can assume that the columns do not cross. It is easy to observe that the total length of the columns in a face is at most
|\textup{South}|/\varepsilon
. Thus the total length of all columns in all faces of the skeleton is at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
times the total size of the skeleton, hence it is also
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

We fix a constant
\kappa=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
, and in each face of the skeleton we extend the skeleton by every
\kappa
th column. These columns partition each face into multiple
bricks,
and the columns form the East and West sides of these bricks (see Figure
3
). The skeleton extended by these columns is called the
mortar graph.

Figure 3:
The brick
B
inside a skeleton face, bounded by columns starting at
s_{i}
and
s_{i+\kappa}
. The solution inside
B
(purple forest) includes
\mathrm{East}(B)
and
\mathrm{West}(B)
, and it is connected to the nearest portals (purple circled nodes) along
\mathrm{North}(B)
and
\mathrm{South}(B)
.

More precisely, we select an
i\in[\kappa]
and, starting with the
i
th column, we include every
\kappa
th column in the mortar graph . By an averaging argument, there is an
i
such that the total length of the added columns is at most a
1/\kappa
fraction of the total length of all columns. If
\kappa
is sufficiently large, then the total length is
O(\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T))
. Therefore, we can afford to “buy” these columns and can be assumed to be part of the solution. This can be expressed by contracting them, thus they will not play much role in the later arguments. The purpose of extending the skeleton this way is that now we can assume that the sequence
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
,
\dots
has length at most
\kappa
in each brick. This structural property is heavily exploited in the next step.

Simplifying a forest

We can think of the solution as having two parts: a subset of the mortar graph and a forest inside each face of the mortar graph. If a tree in a brick is attached only to the North or South side, then it can be replaced by a subpath of that side, increasing cost only by at most a
1+\varepsilon
factor. But, in general, a tree can connect several vertices of the North side with several vertices of the South side, and then it is not clear if the shortest path properties of North and South can be exploited for any simplification. Nevertheless, Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu
[
24
, Thm. 10.7]
showed by a careful simplification argument that parts of the tree can be replaced by subpaths of the boundary in such a way that the size of the solution increases only by at most a factor of
(1+\varepsilon)
and the forest in each brick is joined to the boundary of the brick only at
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
vertices.

Portals

We want to ensure that the inside of a brick has limited and well controlled interaction with the boundary, that is, the solution enters the inside of the brick via a limited number of portals. Let us consider a brick
B
an let us designate
\theta=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
portals on the boundary of
B
at roughly equal distance from each other. That is, if
\ell(B)
is the length of the boundary of
B
, then the portals are at distance roughly
\ell(B)/\theta
from each other. Whenever the solution has a tree inside a brick that is attached to a vertex
v
of the boundary, then let us extend the solution by connecting
v
to the closest portal. For each such vertex
v
, this modification increases the cost by at most
\ell(B)/\theta
. As we have assumed that the forest inside
B
is attached to the boundary at
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
vertices, the total increase incurred by brick
B
is at most
\ell(B)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)/\theta
. As the total size of the mortar graph created so far was
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
, the total increase of length is
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)/\theta
when summed over every brick
B
. If
\theta=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
is sufficiently large, then this increase is
\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
. Thus after some slight modification of the graph
G
(as demonstrated in Figure
4
), we can assume that the inside of each brick is connected only to the
\theta
portals on the boundary.

Figure 4:
The graph modification that restricts interactions between bricks to the portals.

Constructing the Steiner-tree spanner

Inside each brick
B
, the only role of the solution is to connect certain subsets of the
\theta
portals on the boundary of
B
. We need to ensure that the this can be done as efficiently in the Steiner-tree spanner as in the original graph. For this purpose, for every subset of the
\theta
portals of
B
, we compute an optimal Steiner tree in
B
and add this tree to the Steiner-tree spanner. Note that an optimal Steiner tree with at most
\theta
terminals can be computed in time
2^{O(\theta)}\cdot n^{O(1)}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
in any graph. Furthermore, in our case we can exploit that each brick is planar and the terminals are on the boundary, in which case the problem is polynomial-time solvable
[
55
]
. Clearly, the size of each such tree is at most the length
\ell(B)
of the boundary of
B
, as the boundary clearly connects all these terminals. Thus the total length we add for each brick
B
is at most
2^{\theta}\cdot\ell(B)=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot\ell(B)
. Summing for every brick
B
, this increases the total length of the mortar graph by a factor of
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
, resulting in a Steiner-tree spanner of length
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

2.2.2
Constructing a Steiner tree spanner in geometric intersection graphs

(NEW) Noncrossing columns via Lipschitz embedding

Section
9
is devoted to constructing an analog of the mortar graph in the intersection graph setting.
In the case of intersection graphs, if we define the columns as shortest paths from each
s_{i}
to North, then these paths may cross and therefore they are not suitable for defining the East/West sides of the brick. One could try to uncross these paths, but in intersection graphs jumping from one path to the other can increase the length, and it is hard to control the total increase of length after iteratively uncrossing paths. To overcome this issue in a robust way, we use a Lipschitz embedding of the union of the columns into a planar graph (more precisely, into a wireframe of the union of the columns). Using this embedding, we can find shortest paths that correspond to noncrossing curves in the wireframe, hence they can be used as the East and West sides of the brick. The Lipschitz property of the embedding ensures that the columns defined this way have the required length and distance properties.

There is a technical complication due to the fact that we have Lipschitz-embedding results only for similarly sized fat objects. However, the objects in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
can be non-fat and have smaller diameter, as
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
contains objects intersected with the boundary of
\mathsf{R}
. We solve this issue by considering the union of the first few objects in each column as a single object, ensuring that at least one of these objects is disjoint from the boundary of
\mathsf{R}
(and similarly for the last few objects). Note that the union of a constant number of similarly sized fat objects changes diameter and fatness only by a constant factor, thus we can still consider this set of objects as similarly sized fat. A further technical complication that appears here is that the created objects can have holes. However, we can modify them by “puncturing the holes” in a way that the intersection graph does not change.

Another technical difference compared to the case of planar graphs is that we have to be more careful about the columns going on or close to the boundary of the region. It is even possible that multiple columns overlap with the northern or southern boundary. In the clean world of planar graphs, such shared subpaths are inconsequential. On the other hand, for intersection graphs, handling this issue introduces additional technicalities. In particular, we need to allow that on some part of the boundary South and East overlap, complicating the definitions considerably.

(NEW) Sparsification

In Section
7
, we show that, similarly to Subset TSP, large cliques can be reduced in size with a factor of at most
(1+\varepsilon)
change in the optimum value. However, for Steiner Tree, we can guarantee only an exponential
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
upper bound on the reduced clique size. The crucial difference is that the role of a vertex in a clique is not just to be part of a path of length
O(1/\varepsilon)
connecting two vertices at distance at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
, but to be part of a subtree connecting a subset of up to
O(1/\varepsilon)
vertices at distance at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
. There are exponentially many such subsets and for each subset potentially a different vertex of the clique is needed, resulting in the exponential upper bound.

(NEW) Uncrossing trees and object frames

In Section
10
, we would like to use the forest simplification result
[
24
, Thm. 10.7]
from the planar algorithm as a black box. This seems reasonable: even though intersection graphs can be more complicated than planar graphs, the solution inside a brick is still just a forest. The forest simplification result considers only forests, so one could argue that it is irrelevant whether this forest is taken from a planar graph or from an intersection graph. However, it is more precise to say that the simplification is done on a planar graph that consists of a boundary cycle and a forest inside it; then the simplification argument replaces some parts of the forest with subpaths of the boundary. Therefore, in order to invoke this result, we need to define such a planar graph based on the solution in the intersection graph. The problem is that even if an intersection graph is a tree, the objects can cross each other and hence it might not be possible to draw the tree without crossing edges in the plane if we want to respect respect the topology of the original objects on the boundary (see Figure
5
(i)). Thus the union of the forest and the boundary can be highly nonplanar.

Figure 5:
(i) The intersection graph of the 15 red objects and the 3+3 objects on the boundary is a tree (shown by blue). However, the union of this tree and the green boundary is nonplanar, as it contains the subdivision of a
K_{3,3}
. (ii) Replacing the 15 red objects with an object frame of 18 subobjects. Union of the intersection graph of these objects and the boundary is now planar.

In order to be able to use
[
24
, Thm. 10.7]
, we create a “really planar” representation of the forest inside the brick. An
object frame
of an intersection graph
G
of polygons is an intersection graph
H
, where each object
v
of
H
is a subpolygon of a parent object
p(v)
of
G
and the polygons of
H
are internally disjoint (plus some further nondegeneracy conditions). Note that
p(u)=p(v)
is possible for
u,v\in V(H)
, that is, the object frame can use multiple subpolygons of a polygon. We show in Section
4
(Theorem
4.2
) that, given an intersection graph
G
that covers a set of
t
points in the plane, we can construct an object frame
H
of
G
such that (i)
H
is a forest, (ii)
|V(H)|\leq|V(G)|+O(t)
, (iii)
H
provides the same connectivity of the points as
G
, that is, if two of the
t
points are covered by a component of
G
, then it is also connected by a component of
H
. With this result at hand, we replace the forest of the solution inside a brick by an object frame (see Figure
5
(ii)). Now the forest defined by this object frame together with the boundary form a planar graph, hence
[
24
, Thm. 10.7]
can be invoked.

(NEW) Solution generators

In the case of planar graphs, we considered the solution to be composed of two disjoint parts: a subgraph of the mortar graph and forests inside the bricks. However, unlike in planar graphs, it is not straightforward to modify the instance such that the interior of a brick is connected only to the portals of the boundary. Moreover, the situation is even more complicated: as it was the case for Subset TSP, we need to include in the Steiner-tree spanner the distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the mortar graph, making it even less clear how the interior is supposed to be connected only to the portals.

For intersection graphs, we need to adopt a slightly different viewpoint. Instead of saying that the solution is partitioned into a subgraph of the mortar graph and forests, we say that the solution provides two things: a subgraph
S_{0}
in the neighborhood of the mortar graph and connections between some subsets of the portals. Of course, the connections between the portals are realized by trees and these trees have to go through the neighborhood of the mortar graph, but we do not care what exactly these trees do there. In particular, we do not care if these trees use objects that are already in
S_{0}
: we can achieve
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution size even if we are double counting such objects. In Section
10
, we prove our main structural result for Steiner Tree, which shows that there is an
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution that arises this way. Proving this requires modifying a solution in a way that components that connect portals are not needed to connect other parts of the boundary.

For the robust handling of the solutions inside bricks, we introduce a more abstract formalism, which will be particularly useful for the next result, the faster PTAS (Theorem
1.6
). A
portal-respecting generator
consists of a subset
S_{0}
of the distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the mortar graph and for each brick
B
a collection
\mathcal{F}
of subsets of portals. The generator is feasible if
S_{0}
connects all the terminals in
T
if we extend
S_{0}
with “virtual edges” connecting, for every
P\in\mathcal{F}
, the vertices of
P
to each other. Intuitively, a generator represents solutions that consist of
S_{0}
and for each set
P
of portals in
\mathcal{F}
, an arbitrary tree connecting
P
. If the generator is feasible, then any set obtained this way is indeed a solution. We define the cost of the generator to be
|S_{0}|
, plus, for every
P\in\mathcal{F}
, the cost of the minimum Steiner tree connecting
P
. Clearly, any solution represented by a feasible generator has size at most the cost of the generator. Our main structural result can be formulated as saying that there is a feasible generator whose cost is at most
(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

2.3
Faster PTAS for Steiner Tree

Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu
[
24
]
presented a PTAS for planar graphs that, instead of using the Steiner-tree spanner, uses dynamic programming on the mortar graph (Theorem
1.3
). Our proof of Theorem
1.6
uses a very different approach. Therefore, instead of giving an overview of the technique of Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu
[
24
]
, we first sketch a different, more streamlined algorithm for planar graphs. The ideas in this sketch form the basis for an analogous algorithm for intersection graphs presented in Section
11
. However, the difficulties arising in the intersection graph setting lead to major technical challenges and makes it necessary to substantially redesign this approach.

(NEW) PTAS via weighted tree-representative vertices in planar graphs

Using contraction decomposition, we can find a set
X
of
O(\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T))
vertices whose contraction reduces the treewidth of the mortar graph
M
to
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
. We can afford to include these vertices into the solution, which is effectively the same as contracting them in the input. Thus we may assume that the mortar graph has treewidth
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
. This of course does not mean that the input graph has bounded treewidth: the parts of the graph inside the bricks can still be complicated. However, this part of the graph is used only for trees connecting terminals; the idea is to represent each possible tree with a single vertex, making what appears inside each brick bounded.

It is convenient to switch at this point to a vertex-weighted problem, where each vertex
v
has a nonnegative weight
w(v)
and the task is to minimize the total weight of the solution. (Note that we are not aiming to solve the vertex-weighted version in general, but the specific instance appearing in the rest of the algorithm can be conveniently described and solved as a weighted instance.) We extend the mortar graph
M
the following way. Consider the
\theta=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
portals of a brick
B
and for any subset
P
of them, introduce a vertex
v_{P}
that is adjacent to every portal in
P
; let the weight of
v_{P}
be the size of the minimum Steiner tree containing
P
. We repeat this step for every brick
B
and every subset of the portals of
P
, this way we are introducing at most
2^{\theta}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
new vertices for each brick
B
(note that the resulting graph
M^{*}
can be highly nonplanar). It is clear that any solution of weight
W
of this new weighted instance
(M^{*},T)
can be turned into a solution of size
W
of the original unweighted instance
(G,T)
. Also, it should be clear that if the original instance
(G,T)
has a solution of size
W
that consists of a subgraph of the mortar graph
M
and trees connecting subsets of portals in the bricks, then the new instance
(M^{*},T)
has a solution of weight
W
. Thus it is sufficient to solve this new, weighted instance
(M^{*},T)
.

How can we bound the treewidth of
M^{*}
? It is known that introducing a new vertex into each face of a planar graph and connecting it arbitrarily with vertices of the face changes treewidth only by a constant factor. We are introducing at most
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
vertices per face, which then increases treewidth by at most a factor of
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
. To see this treewidth bound, let us first introduce a vertex
v_{F}
in each face
F
such that
v_{F}
is adjacent to every vertex of
F
, and consider a tree decomposition of this graph. Whenever
v_{F}
appears in a bag, let us replace it with a block of
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
copies. It is clear that the resulting tree decomposition has treewidth at most
O(2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)})
times the original treewidth.
The standard dynamic programming algorithm for Steiner Tree can be easily adapted to the vertex-weighted version. However, as this algorithm is exponential in treewidth, this would still give only a
2^{2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}}\cdot n^{O(1)}
time algorithm, which is weaker than our goal.

To improve the running time, let us observe that the dynamic programming can be made faster on such decompositions. In each bag of the decomposition, we have at most
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
original vertices of
M
, plus at most
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
blocks of tree-representative vertices, with each block having size at most
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
. The important observation is that if a solution for the original instance
(G,T)
uses at most
\theta=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
trees in each brick, then there is a corresponding solution of the new instance
(M^{*},T)
that uses at most
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
tree-representative vertices from each block. Therefore, there are
(\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\cdot 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)})^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
different subsets of a bag that can potentially appear in such a solution and we can restrict our search to solutions with this property. The running time of (weighted) Steiner tree mainly depends on the number of subproblems defined in the dynamic programming, which in turn mainly depends on the number of possible subsets of vertices that need to be considered at each bag. Given our bound on the number of relevant subsets, it follows that standard dynamic programming can solve the problem in
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
time.

Figure 6:
(i) Even if a subgraph (green) has constant treewidth, its neighborhood can induce a a graph of arbitrary large treewidth. (ii) Extending a single brick of the mortar graph with its distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood (gray shaded region) can create multiple “islands”.

(NEW) Contracting through bricks

If we try to follow the same strategy to speed up our PTAS for intersection graphs, then the main source of the difficulty is that the solution is not composed from a subgraph of the mortar graph plus trees in the bricks, but from a subgraph of the distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the mortar graph plus trees in the bricks. Let us observe that even if the mortar graph has bounded treewidth, it very well may be that the neighborhood of the mortar graph has large treewidth (see Figure
6
(i) for an example). Inside a brick, if we consider a distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the boundary, then this may create connections between different parts of the boundary and can split the inside of the brick into multiple “islands”. By their construction, the bricks have lots of structural properties and we may try to argue that including the neighborhood cannot create so many extra connections to increase treewidth significantly. While it may be possible to prove this, we found potential arguments very tedious and therefore chose a different strategy.

In Section
11
, instead of using contraction decomposition to find a set whose contraction decreases the treewidth of the mortar graph, we use contraction decomposition on the distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood of the mortar graph to reduce the treewidth of this neighborhood to
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
. Note that now the set
X
we contract can go through a brick (see Figure
7
). Therefore, it is difficult to determine where we should put a tree-representative vertex corresponding to a brick. Instead, we put tree-representative vertices in each island. As each island is in a single brick, these vertices should represent a part of one of the
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
possible trees that can appear in this brick. (Here it is a technical complication that the restriction of a tree to an island can consist of many components.)

Figure 7:
The mortar graph (green), its distance-
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
neighborhood (shaded gray), and the set
X
(red) whose contraction reduces the treewidth of this neighborhood.

If the graph were planar, then the scheme described in the previous paragraph could be made to work, with techniques similar to what we described in the planar case. In the case of intersection graphs, the set
X
for contraction needs to be found in a planar constant-Lipschitz representation of the graph (or more precisely, of the cell partition). Moreover, we need to insert the tree-representative vertices into the faces of this planar representation. In the end, we argue that certain properties (such as treewidth) of this planar representation has implications on the original graph (similarly to Lemma
2.5
above, which shows how the effect of contraction in the planar representation can be lifted back to the original graph). In this situation, this lifting is extremely delicate and nontrivial, especially if the set
X
is close to some of the islands. Nevertheless, we overcome these difficulties and manage to construct a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximately equivalent weighted instance that has a tree decomposition where the optimal solution has only
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}
different possible intersections with each bag. Then the
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot n^{O(1)}
running time follows by dynamic-programming techniques.

3
Preliminaries

Graphs, walks, paths, distances

We use standard graph notation
[
52
]
unless stated otherwise. The graph
G
has vertex set
V(G)
and edge set
E(G)
.
A
walk

P
is an alternating sequence of vertices and edges, starting and ending with a vertex. A walk is
closed
if its start- and endpoint are the same vertex, it is a
path
if each of its vertices appears exactly once in
P
, and it is a
cycle
if it is a closed walk where all vertices are non-repeating except for the start and end vertex being identical. The length of
P
is denoted by
|P|
, and it is the number of edges in the sequence of
P
. In particular, we allow for length-
0
paths that consist of a single vertex. For a cycle, path, or walk
P
the shortest path (or walk) from
u
to
v
along
P
is denoted by
P[u,v]
. We will also use
P(u,v)
to refer to the subpath/subwalk
P[u,v]
with the first and last vertex removed.

The shortest-path distance of
u,v\in V(G)
is denoted by
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)
and is simply the number of edges on a shortest path with endpoints
u
and
v
. For a vertex set
X\subset V(G)
let
N(X,k)
be the set of vertices within
distance
k
from
X
, that is,
N(X,k)=\{v\in V(G)\mid\exists x\in X:\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,v)\leqslant k\}
. We also use the notation
N(v,k)
when talking about the neighborhood of one vertex, and
N(X)
(resp.,
N(v)
) is a shorthand for
N(X,1)
(resp.,
N(v,1)
), i.e., the
closed neighborhood
of
X
(resp., the closed neighborhood of
v
).

Partitions, contractions, and consolidations

Let
G
be a graph and let
\mathcal{P}
be a partition of
V(G)
. For a vertex
v\in V(G)
or vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
, let
v_{\mathcal{P}}
or
X_{\mathcal{P}}
denote the partition class of
v
or the set of partition
classes in
\mathcal{P}
intersected by
X
, respectively.
Given a vertex set
X
and a partition
\mathcal{Q}
of some vertex set
Y\subseteq V(G)
, we denote by

\mathcal{Q}^{1}
is the partition of
V(H)
that extends
\mathcal{Q}
into a partition of
V(G)
by adding each vertex of
V(G)\setminus Y
as a singleton, and
\psi
is the consolidation map for

whose pre-image contains some vertex of

.

Treewidth and
\mathcal{P}
-flattened treewidth.

A tree decomposition of a graph
G
is a tree graph
\mathcal{T}
whose nodes are subsets of
V(G)
called
bags
, with the following properties:

(i)

\bigcup_{B\in V(\mathcal{T})}B=V(G)
, i.e., each vertex of
G
is represented in some bag of
\mathcal{T}

(ii)

For each edge
uv\in E(G)
there is some
B\in V(\mathcal{T})
with
u,v\in B
, that is, each edge of
G
is induced by some bag

(iii)

For each
v\in V(G)
the bags
\{B\in V(\mathcal{T})\mid v\in B\}
form a connected subtree of
\mathcal{T}
.

The width of the tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
is
\max_{B\in V(\mathcal{T})}|B|-1
, and the
treewidth
of
G
is the minimum width among its tree decompositions, and it is denoted by
\mathrm{tw}(G)
.

Let
\omega:V(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a weight function. The weighted width of a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
is
\max_{B\in V(\mathcal{T})}\omega(B)
. The weighted treewidth of
(G,\omega)
is the minimum weighted width among its tree decompositions.

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}
is a partition of
V(G)
. The
\mathcal{P}
-flattened treewidth

[
46
]
of
G
is the weighted treewidth of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
under the weight function
\omega:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
defined as
\omega(C):=\log(|C|+1)
for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
. We denote
\mathcal{P}
-flattened treewidth of
G
by
\mathrm{tw}_{\mathcal{P}}(G)
.

Note that in an
n
-vertex graph
G
and any partition
\mathcal{P}
of
V(G)
we have
\mathrm{tw}_{\mathcal{P}}(G)=O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{tw}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{P})
.

Observation 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a candidate tree decomposition of
G
where all vertices
and edges of
G
are represented in some bag of
\mathcal{T}
. Then
\mathcal{T}
is a valid tree decomposition of
G
if and only if for every
connected subgraph
H\subset G
the set of bags containing some vertex of
H

form a connected subtree of
T
.

Proof.

Clearly if any connected subgraph
H
has this porperty, then in particular one-vertex edgeless subgraphs also have this property, which directly yields property (iii) in the definition. On the other hand, if
\mathcal{T}
is a valid tree decomposition, then let us use induction on
V(H)
to prove that the vertices of
V(H)
form a connected subtree. The claim holds for
|V(H)|=1
by condition (iii) of tree decompositions. Suppose that
v\in V(H)
, and let
\mathcal{T}_{v}
be the subtree of
\mathcal{T}
where
v
appears in the bag. By induction, there is also a subtree
\mathcal{T}_{H-v}
that contains the bags where some vertex of
H-v
appears. Let
u\in N_{H}(v)
. By property (ii) of tree decompositions, there is some bag
B_{uv}
containing both
u
and
v
. Since
B_{uv}
is a vertex of both
\mathcal{T}_{v}
and
\mathcal{T}_{H-v}
, we get that the union of these trees is a connected subtree of
H
, concluding the proof.
∎

Subset TSP and Steiner tree.

We formally define our optimization problems as follows.

Subset
TSP

Input:
A graph
G
and a vertex set
T\subseteq V(G)
called
terminals
.

Output:
A shortest closed walk
W
of
G
where
T\subset V(W)
.

Steiner Tree

Input:
A graph
G
and a vertex set
T\subseteq V(G)
called
terminals
.

Output:
A minimum size vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
where
T\subseteq S
and
G[S]
is connected.

Notice that our definition of
Steiner Tree
is non-standard, as one normally has an edge-weighted graph and one is looking for a subgraph of edge weight
k
that contains all the terminals. In our unweighted setting these two optimization problems are equivalent from the perspective of approximation: indeed, a solution of size
|S|=k
yields has a spanning tree of size
k-1
, and a Steiner tree with
k-1
edges has
k
vertices. Thus (up to changing
\varepsilon
by a constant factor) an approximate solution for one problem is yields an approximate solution for the other with a linear time overhead.

Objects, regions, and curves

For a closed planar set (object or region)
\mathsf{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
we denote by
\partial\mathsf{R}
its boundary. We denote by
\inter\mathsf{R}
and
\exter\mathsf{R}
the interior and exterior of
\mathsf{R}
, respectively. For an arbitrary planar set
X
we denote by
\Clo(X)
its closure. A continuous function
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a
curve
between its endpoints
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)
. We will often abuse notation and refer to the picture of
\gamma
(i.e.,
\gamma([0,1])
) as
\gamma
. A curve
\gamma
is
piecewise linear
if it is the concatenation of closed segments at endpoints shared between consecutive segments. A curve is a
closed curve
when
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)
. The curve is
self-intersecting
if
\gamma(x)=\gamma(y)
for some
x\neq y
where
\{x,y\}\neq\{0,1\}
. A non-self-intersecting closed curve is a
Jordan curve
.

Polygons and intersection graphs

A
simple polygon
is a closed bounded region of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose boundary is a piecewise linear closed Jordan curve. In particular, a segment is not considered to be a simple polygon. A non-self-intersecting polygon is a connected bounded region of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose boundary consists of pairwise disjoint piecewise linear Jordan curves. If
P
is a non-self-intersecting polygon, then the closures of the bounded components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus P
are called the
holes
of
P
; since the holes are bounded by piecewise linear Jordan curves, they are simple polygons.
We observe that a component of the union of some non-self-intersecting polygons where no three polygon vertices are collinear is a non-self-intersecting polygon.

The
complexity
of a non-self-intersecting polygon is its number of vertices, and the complexity of an intersection graph that is represented by non-self-intersecting polygons is the total number of vertices in the representing polygons. For a polygon
v
and graph
G
let
\mathrm{compl}(v)
and
\mathrm{compl}(G)
denote their complexity, respectively. For a set of polygons the
arrangement
given by the polygons is the partition of the plane into a set of faces, edges and vertices given by the drawing of the polygon boundaries in the plane. The arrangement of an intersection graph
G
of polygons is simply the arrangement of its polygons, and it is denoted by
\mathcal{A}(G)
. The complexity of an arrangement is the complexity of its elements, i.e., the total number of vertices, edges and faces, and is denoted by
\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
. Notice that a set of
\mathrm{compl}(G)
segments can create at most
\binom{\mathrm{compl}(G)}{2}
intersections, so Euler’s formula implies that
\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))=O(\mathrm{compl}(G)^{2})
. We use
V(\mathcal{A}(G))
to refer to the set of vertices in the arrangement, i.e., this set includes both the polygon vertices and the intersection points of their edges.

An
intersection graph

G
is graph whose vertices are objects (sets) in some fixed metric space —typically the plane—, and its edges are the unordered pairs of intersecting objects. Objects are typically denoted by lowercase letters, and subgraphs in
G
are thought of as a collection of vertices and edges. The intersection graphs in this article are given via their representation, that is, by some straightforward description of the objects
V(G)
: in particular, unit disks are defined via the coordinates of their disk centers, and non-self-intersecting polygons are defined by listing the coordinates of the polygon vertices on each boundary component in clockwise order.

We will also work with graphs that are represented as plane graphs, i.e., vertices are points (or sometimes identical to points), and its edges are associated with piecewise linear curves between the corresponding endpoints. For a graph
G
with such a representation, we typically use the notation
\widetilde{G}
. If the curves representing the edges are piecewise linear, then
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{G})
denotes the total number of internal vertices on its edge curves plus the number of vertices in
G
; similarly, if
\gamma
is a piecewise linear curve, then
\mathrm{compl}(\gamma)
is the number of vertices and edges in
\gamma
.

Fair and
\alpha
-standard intersection graphs.

Definition 3.2

(Fair and
\alpha
-standard intersection graphs)
.

We say that an intersection graph
G
of simple polygons in the plane is
fair
if for any two polygons
v,w\in V(G)
each component of the intersection
v\cap w
is a simple polygon and no three polygon edges (from the polygons in
V(G)
) meet at one point.
If additionally each polygon
v\in V(G)
contains a closed disk of radius
1
and has diameter
\mathrm{diam}(v)\leqslant\alpha
for some fixed
\alpha>1
, then
G
is called
\alpha
-standard
.

We denote by
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
the class of intersection graphs with an
\alpha
-standard embedding. Notice that a polygon of an
\alpha
-standard graph contains a closed axis-aligned unit square in its interior, since this already holds for the disk of radius
1
contained in the polygon. Since an axis-aligned unit square contains some grid point from
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we can associate each object with the lexicographically smallest
2
2

2

The
lexicographic order
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is a total ordering where we order first by
x
-coordinates and then by
y
-coordinates, that is,
(a,b)<(c,d)
if and only if
a<c
or
a=c
and
b<d
.
point of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
contained in the interior of the polygon.

Definition 3.3

(Cell partition)
.

The
cell partition
or
clique partition
of a graph
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
(given by its representation) is the partition
\mathcal{P}
of
V(G)
where
u,v\in V(G)
are in the same class of
\mathcal{P}
if and only if the lexicographically smallest grid point contained in the interior of
u
and
v
are the same grid point.

In particular, all objects assigned to the same class are stabbed by a single point, thus for any
C\in\mathcal{P}
we have that
G[C]
is a clique. Occasionally, we will refer to the grid point associated with a class
C
of
\mathcal{P}
as
\dot{C}
, and the set of points associated with the classes of
\mathcal{P}
as
\dot{\mathcal{P}}
.

In Appendix
A
we show the following theorem about converting intersection graphs into
\alpha
-standard graphs.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of (a)
\delta
-similarly sized disks or (b)
\delta
-similarly sized
\beta
-fat polygons given by their representation. Then in polynomial time, we can construct an
\alpha
-standard intersection graph
G^{\prime}
that is isomorphic to
G
whose representation complexity is polynomial in
n
, where
\alpha=36/(\beta\delta)
and
\beta=1
for disks.

3.1
Object frames and wireframes

A
frame
can be thought of as a planar graph that relates to some underlying intersection graph but can use vertices of said graph multiple times. More precisely, we define two types of frames as follows.

Definition 3.5

(Object Frame)
.

An
object frame
of a fair intersection graph
G
is an intersection graph
H
that is planar together with a function
p:V(H)\rightarrow V(G)
that satisfies the following properties.

1.

Each vertex
v\in V(H)
is a simple polygon, and has a parent polygon
p(v)\in V(G)
such that
v\subseteq p(v)
.

2.

For any
v,w\in V(H)
they are either disjoint or
v\cap w
is a segment of positive length.

3.

The common intersection of any three distinct polygons
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in V(H)
is empty.

Definition 3.6

(Wireframe)
.

A
wireframe
of a fair intersection graph
G
is a plane graph
\widetilde{H}
and a function
p:V(\widetilde{H})\rightarrow V(G)
that satisfies the following properties.

1.

Each vertex
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{H})
is a point located in its parent polygon
p(\dot{v})\in V(G)
that is not a vertex of any polygon
w\in V(G)
.
3
3

3

We explicitly allow for
\dot{v}
to be on the boundary
\partial v
of its parent as long as it is not a vertex of any polygon.

2.

For any
\dot{u}\dot{v}\in E(\widetilde{H})
the edge is represented by a polygonal curve
\gamma_{\dot{u}\dot{v}}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and either
p(\dot{u})=p(\dot{v})
and
\gamma_{\dot{u}\dot{v}}\subset\inter p(\dot{u})
, or
p(\dot{u})\neq p(\dot{v})
and for some
t\in(0,1)
we have
\gamma_{\dot{u}\dot{v}}(0,t)\subset\inter p(\dot{u})
and
\gamma_{\dot{u}\dot{v}}(t,1)\subset\inter p(\dot{v})
.

3.

For each edge curve
\gamma
of
E(H)
and each
v\in V(G)
the intersection
\partial v\cap\gamma
is a finite point set (consequently, it does not contain any segments) and it contains no vertex of the arrangement
\mathcal{A}(G)
.

We note here that when constructing a wireframe
\widetilde{H}
for some subgraph or object frame
H
of
G
, it is convenient to disregard condition 3 of wireframes during the construction, as one can perturb the vertices of a wireframe (while staying in the same face of the arrangement
\bigcup_{v\in V(G)}\partial v
) so that the edges of the piecewise-linear curves in
E(\widetilde{H})
do not align with any of the edges of
\partial v
(when
v\in V(G)\setminus V(H)
or pass through any of the vertices of
\mathcal{A}(G)
. This insight can be summarized as follows.

Observation 3.7

(Perturbation principle)
.

Let
H
be a subgraph or object frame of
G
, and let
\widetilde{H}
be a wireframe of
H
. Then in
O(\mathrm{compl}(G)+\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{H}))
time we can perturb the vertices of the edge curves of
\widetilde{H}
to get a wireframe
\widetilde{H}^{\prime}
of both
H
and
G
that is isomorphic to
\widetilde{H}
and has the same complexity. Moreover, if the vertices of
\widetilde{H}
are disjoint from the vertices of the polygons of
V(G)
, then
V(\widetilde{H})=V(\widetilde{H}^{\prime})
, i.e., it is sufficient to perturb some of the internal vertices of the edge curves of
\widetilde{H}
.

We can convert between wireframe and object frame representations using the following lemmas.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex fair intersection graph hosting an object frame
H
, and for each
v\in V(H)
let
\dot{v}\in v
be a given designated point that is not a polygon vertex for any polygon of
V(H)\cup V(G)
, and not on the shared boundary of any pair of objects
v,w\in V(H)
. Then in polynomial time we can build a wireframe
\widetilde{H}
of
G
on the vertex set
\{\dot{v}\mid v\in V(H)\}
isomorphic to
H
where
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{H})\leqslant n^{2}\cdot\mathrm{compl}(H)
.

Proof.

For each edge
vw\in E(H)
we wish to fix a simple polygonal curve
\gamma(\dot{v}\dot{w})
with at most
\mathrm{compl}(v)+\mathrm{compl}(w)
vertices from
\dot{v}
to
\dot{w}
that is covered by
v\cup w
and intersects the segment
(\partial v\cap\partial w)
in exactly one internal point. The object frame properties ensure that the objects neighboring
v
will intersect
v
in exactly one segment somewhere along
\partial v
.

Figure 8:
The green polygon
v
is triangulated using the red edges. We place points on the segment
s=\dot{a}\dot{b}
according to the appearance of the neighbors of
v
along
\partial v
. The construction creates the blue dashed half-edge-curves: these will form the wireframe edges incident to
\dot{v}
together with their other halves in the neighboring polygons
w_{i}
.

Fix some triangulation of
v
, and consider each segment
s=\dot{a}\dot{b}
of this triangulation that is not on the boundary of
v
, see
Figure

8
. We will also assume that
\dot{v}\not\in s
and handle this case near the end of the proof. Note that
s
splits
v
into two polygons; let
v_{s}
denote the polygon among these that does not contain
\dot{v}
. Let
w_{1},\dots,w_{k_{s}}
be the sequence of neighbors of
v
in order of their appearance on
\partial v_{s}\setminus s
when traversing
\partial v_{s}\setminus s
from
\dot{a}
to
\dot{b}
.
We subdivide
s
into
k_{s}+1
equal length segments, and label the endpoints from
\dot{a}
to
\dot{b}
with
\dot{a},\dot{s}^{w_{1}},\dots,\dot{s}^{w_{k_{s}}},\dot{b}
. Furthermore, for each
w\in N_{H}(v)
we fix some point
\dot{x}_{vw}
that is an internal point of the segment
v\cap w
. We can now define the first half of the edge curve of
\dot{v}\dot{w}
from
\dot{v}
to
\dot{x}_{vw}
as the piecewise linear curve given by the vertex sequence
\dot{v},\dot{s}^{w}_{1},\dot{s}^{w}_{2},\dots,\dot{x}_{vw}
, where
s_{1},s_{2},\dots
are the unique sequence of triangulation edges separating
v
from the triangle containing
v\cap w
. (Note that when
v
is a triangle, then this curve consists of a single segment from
\dot{v}
directly to
\dot{x}_{vw}
)
Notice that there are at most
\mathrm{compl}(v)-3
internal edges in the triangulation, thus the half-edge described above has at most
\mathrm{compl}(v)-1
vertices; together with the other half of the edge in
\dot{w}
, this gives at most
\mathrm{compl}(v)+\mathrm{compl}(w)-3
vertices (as
\dot{x}_{vw}
appears on both half-edges).

It remains to show that this results in a plane graph, i.e., that there are no intersections between edges. Suppose the contrary, there is an intersection point. Notice that no segment of an edge curve will align with a triangulation edge, and the edge curves use different vertices. We can also notice that each half-edge-curve consists of segments in distinct triangles, thus no self-intersection occurs on these curves. Thus an intersection can only happen between two edges connecting distinct boundary points of some triangle
T=\Delta(\dot{a}\dot{b}\dot{c})
of the triangulation of
v
. Let
s=\dot{a}\dot{b}
be the edge of
T
that separates
\dot{v}
from the the interior of
T
. Let
w_{1},w_{2}
be the neighbors of
v
whose half-edge-curves intersect in
T
, and assume without loss of generality that
w_{1}
appears before
w_{2}
on
\partial v_{s}\setminus s
when traversing
\partial v_{s}\setminus s
from
\dot{a}
to
\dot{b}
.
If both
v\cap w_{1}
and
v\cap w_{2}
are separated from
\dot{v}
by
s^{\prime}:=\dot{a}\dot{c}
, then the segments
\dot{s}^{w_{1}}(\dot{s}^{\prime})^{w_{1}}
and
\dot{s}^{w_{2}}(\dot{s}^{\prime})^{w_{2}}
cannot intersect as
w_{1}\cap v
and
w_{2}\cap v
will appear in the same order on
\partial v_{s^{\prime}}\setminus s^{\prime}
as it did on
\partial v_{s}\setminus s
, so these points appear in the same order on the segments
\dot{a}\dot{b}
and
\dot{a}\dot{c}
. The analogous argument works if both
v\cap w_{1}
and
v\cap w_{2}
are separated from
\dot{v}
by
s^{\prime\prime}:=\dot{c}\dot{b}
. Finally, due to the order of appearance of
v\cap w_{1}
and
v\cap w_{2}
on
\partial v
the only remaining option is that
v\cap w_{1}
is separated from
\dot{v}
by
s^{\prime}=\dot{a}\dot{c}
and
v\cap w_{2}
is separated from
\dot{v}
by
s^{\prime\prime}=\dot{c}\dot{b}
. Again this means that
\dot{s}^{w_{1}}(\dot{s}^{\prime})^{w_{1}}
and
\dot{s}^{w_{2}}(\dot{s}^{\prime\prime})^{w_{2}}
are disjoint.

Recall that the above proof assumed that
\dot{v}
does not fall on any internal edge of the triangulation of
v
. If this happens, then we can simply remove the edge of the triangulation containing
\dot{v}
and proceed with the proof unchanged: if there is some
w\in N_{H}(v)
that intersects
\partial v
on one of the triangles incident to
\dot{v}
, then
\dot{v}
will be directly connected to
\dot{x}_{vw}
inside that triangle, and such half-edge-curves cannot introduce intersections with other edges starting from
\dot{v}
.

The resulting plane graph
\widetilde{H}
is a wireframe of
H
. We apply the perturbations of
3.7
to ensure that the result also satisfies condition 3 of wireframes with respect to
G
. This results in a wireframe of complexity at most
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{H})<E(H)\cdot\mathrm{compl}(H)<n^{2}\cdot\mathrm{compl}(H)
.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex fair intersection graph hosting a wireframe
\widetilde{H}
. Then in polynomial time we can construct an object frame
H
isomorphic to
\widetilde{H}
of complexity
\mathrm{compl}(H)\leqslant n^{2}\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{H})
.

Proof.

Consider cutting the curve of each edge
\gamma(\dot{v}\dot{w})
of
\widetilde{H}
at some point
\dot{x}_{\dot{v}\dot{w}}
such that the portion of the edge curve from
\dot{v}
to
\dot{x}_{\dot{v}\dot{w}}
is covered by
p(\dot{v})
and the rest is covered by
p(\dot{w})
; let us call these parts half-edge curves. The star at
\dot{v}
is defined as the union of the half-edge curves incident to
\dot{v}
. Since
\deg(\dot{v})\leqslant n-1
and
\dot{v}\in\inter p(\dot{v})
, we can construct a polygon
u_{\dot{v}}
of complexity
n\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{H})
for
\dot{v}
in some small
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of the star of
\dot{v}
such that
u_{\dot{v}}\subset p(\dot{v})
and for each of the points
\{\dot{x}_{\dot{v}\dot{w}}\mid\dot{w}\in N_{\widetilde{H}}(\dot{v})\}
there is a side
s_{\dot{v}\dot{w}}\subset p(\dot{v})\cap p(\dot{w})
of
u_{\dot{v}}
which contains
\dot{x}_{vw}
as an inner point. It is straightforward to check that if
\varepsilon
is small enough, then these polygons are either disjoint or intersect in segments
s_{\dot{v}\dot{w}}
, thus they form an object frame of
G
that is isomorphic to
\widetilde{H}
. The resulting object frame
H
has complexity at most
\mathrm{compl}(H)\leqslant n^{2}\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{H})
.
∎

Finally, we will often restrict our intersection graphs to various regions as follows.

Definition 3.10

(
\mathsf{R}
-restriction)
.

Let
G
be a fair interection graph, and let
\mathsf{R}
be a simple polygon such that for any
v\in V(G)
each component of the intersection
v\cap R
is a simple polygon. Then the
\mathsf{R}
-restriction
of
G
is the intersection graph induced by the simple polygons
\bigcup_{v\in V(G)}\mathrm{CComp}(v\cap\mathsf{R})
, where
\mathrm{CComp}(X)
is the set of connected components of
X
. We denote the
\mathsf{R}
-restriction of
G
by
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
. For a vertex
v\in G|_{\mathsf{R}}
we denote by
\hat{v}
the vertex of
G
such that
v\in\mathrm{CComp}(\hat{v}\cap R)
.

For a wireframe
\widetilde{H}
the
drawing
of
\widetilde{H}
, denoted by
\gamma(\widetilde{H})
, is the union of all curves in the fixed plane representation of
\widetilde{H}
.

If
\mathsf{R}
is a region that is bounded by a wireframe cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
of
G
, then there is a natural restriction of the parent relation
p(.)
to
\mathsf{R}
: for
\dot{v}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
we define
p|_{\mathsf{R}}(\dot{v})
to be the unique object
v\in V(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
such that
\dot{v}\in v
and
\hat{v}=p(\dot{v})
. We say that a wireframe
\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}
of
G
is a
boundaried wireframe
of
R=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
if its representation is in the closed region
\mathsf{R}
and it has no vertices on
\partial\mathsf{R}
except the vertices of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
.

4
Constructing a connectivity-preserving object frame

We start by defining an intersection graph that is a mix between a fair intersection graph and an object frame.
An intersection graph is
fair-frame
if its objects are simple polygons, and for any pair
u,v
of objects, either (
i
) each component of
u\cap v
is a simple polygon or (
ii
)
u\cap v
is a segment of positive length.

In order to prove our next theorem, we need the following technical lemma, which can be considered as a conversion step from a fair graph to an object frame.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
G
be a fair-frame graph with a fixed vertex
v
, and consider the connected components of
G-v
. Let
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
denote the unions of the objects in these connected components, and assume that
k\geqslant 2
. Then
v
has a collection
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
of subpolygons for some
t\leqslant k-1
such that
V(G-v)\cup\{v_{1},\dots,v_{t}\}
induces a connected fair-frame graph where all
v_{i}
are pairwise disjoint, and for each
v_{i}
and
u\in V(G)-v
the intersection
v_{i}\cap u
is either empty or a segment of positive length. Finally, the new objects have total complexity
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathrm{compl}(v_{i})\leqslant 3\mathrm{compl}(v\setminus(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}U_{k}))
.

Proof.

Consider the drawing of the boundaries of
\partial v
and
\partial U_{i}
for all
i
. These boundaries slice the plane into several connected regions, see
Figure

9
. Notice moreover that in the fair-frame graph it is possible that
\partial U_{i}\subset\partial v
; in this case we think of the shared segment as a distinct degenerate region. We label each region with the set of objects among
v,U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
that cover the region. Thus there are at most
2k+2
distinct labels. These regions then define a planar graph
H
whose vertices correspond to regions and edges correspond to region pairs whose shared boundary contains a segment of positive length. Since
v
is connected, we have that the planar graph
H
restricted to the regions inside
v
is also connected; let
H_{v}
denote this graph. Notice that since each
U_{i}
intersects
v
, the graph
H_{v}
must contain a vertex of label
\{v,U_{i}\}
for each
U_{i}
. Next, for each
U_{i}
, we identify the set of vertices in
H_{v}
that have the label
\{v,U_{i}\}
into a single vertex, and denote by
u_{i}
the newly created vertex. We remove any multiple edges created by the operation. (We note that loops are not created by the identification as regions of equal label are not adjacent.) Consider some spanning tree of the resulting graph, and remove all leaves of label
\{v\}
, resulting in the tree
T
.

Figure 9:
(i) The setup of
Lemma

4.1
: the connected components of
G-v
cover the regions
U_{i}
. (
ii
) The graph
H_{v}
labelled with the regions covering each vertex. Vertices of label
{v}
are denoted by empty green circle nodes. (
iii
) The tree
T
obtained by identifying labels
\{U_{i},v\}
for each
i
and removing leaves of label
\{v\}
. (
iv
) The subpolygons
v_{1},v_{2}
replacing
v
and keeping connectivity.

We claim that
T
has at most
k-1
vertices that are labeled with
\{v\}
. Indeed,
T
has at most
k
leaves (namely, each vertex
u_{i}
may be a leaf), and a vertex of label
\{v\}
is adjacent only to new vertices, while new vertices are only adjacent to vertices of label
\{v\}
, as the regions
U_{i}
are pairwise disjoint. Consequently,
T
is bipartite where one part consists of the vertices of label
\{v\}
, and each of these vertices have degree at least two, while the other part consists of the
k
vertices
u_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
. This means that if there are
t
vertices with label
\{v\}
, then the edge count is
t+k-1\geqslant 2t
, which implies
t\leqslant k-1
, as claimed.

Let
w
be a vertex of
T
with label
\{v\}
, and for each adjacent vertex
u_{j}
we fix a positive length segment
s_{j}
that is on the intersection of the region of
w
and (one of) the region(s) corresponding to
u_{j}
. (More precisely, we choose
s_{j}
to be a segment inside
v
that is part of the boundary of a single object in
U_{j}
.) Notice that the region of
w
, denoted by
\mathsf{R}(w)
is a connected component in
(v\setminus(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}U_{k}))
. Observe that
\mathsf{R}(w)
contains a subpolygon
w^{\prime}
that has the middle third of each of these
s_{j}
segments on its boundary, and it is otherwise disjoint from the boundary of the region of
w
. We will select
w^{\prime}
so that it follows the boundary of
\mathsf{R}(w)
in the interior of
\mathsf{R}(w)
, while its complexity is at most three times the complexity of the region of
w
. We select at most one vertex for
w^{\prime}
for each vertex of
\mathsf{R}(w)
and at most two vertices for
w^{\prime}
for each edge of
\mathsf{R}(w)
, as illustrated in
Figure

9
. It is routine to check that the subpolygons
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
defined this way for each of the
t
vertices of
T
labeled with
\{v\}
are pairwise disjoint, their intersections with all vertices of
G-v
satisfy object frame property 2, and the resulting intersection graph is fair-frame. Moreover, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathrm{compl}(v_{i})\leqslant 3\mathrm{compl}(v\setminus(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}U_{k}))
.
∎

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
G
be a fair intersection graph, and let
X\subset V(G)
be a set of terminals. Moreover, each terminal
x\in X
has a designated point
\dot{x}\in x
in the plane such that the designated points of distinct terminals do not coincide. Then there exists an object frame
G^{\prime}
of
G
where for each
x\in X
there is a corresponding terminal vertex
x^{\prime}
in
V(G^{\prime})
(where
p(x^{\prime})=x
), and the following hold:

1.

For each
x\in X
we have
\dot{x}\in x^{\prime}
.

2.

Terminals
u,v\in X
are in the same connected component of
G
if and only if
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
are in the same connected component of
G^{\prime}
.

3.

|V(G^{\prime})|-|V(G)|\leqslant 16|X|
.

4.

The object frame
G^{\prime}
is a forest.

5.

\mathrm{compl}(G^{\prime})=O(\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G)))
.

Finally, when
|X|\leqslant 2
, then
|V(G^{\prime})|\leqslant|V(G)|
.

Proof.

If
|X|=1
, then the object frame consisting of the single terminal

x\in X
satisfies all the conditions. For the rest of the proof, assume
|X|\geqslant 2
.

We will define a procedure that creates the desired graph
G^{\prime}
using several modifications to
G
; for convenience, we refer to the current graph as
G
, and assume without loss of generality that
G
is a connected fair-frame graph, as we can do the following procedure on each of its connected components. (In turn, we will show that the resulting object frame
G^{\prime}
is a tree.)

It will be convenient to assume in the proof that
X
consists of pairwise disjoint objects. This can be achieved the following way. If
|X|=2
and the two objects
x,y\in X
intersect, then the object frame satisfying the statement of the theorem can be found the following way. If
\dot{x}\in y
, then we set
x^{\prime}
to be a triangle in a small neighborhood of
\dot{x}
(so that the triangle is in
x
and it does not contain
\dot{y}
), and we set
y^{\prime}
to be a subpolygon of
y
that contains
\dot{y}
and shares some boundary segment with this triangle. Otherwise, let
y^{\prime}=y
and let
x^{\prime}
be a subpolygon of
x
that contains
\dot{x}
, internally disjoint from
y
, and shares a boundary segment with
y
. (This is possible as
x\cap y
cannot be a single point in a fair-frame graph.) In both cases, we get the required object frame.

If
|X|\geq 3
and some terminals intersect, then we modify
X
the following way. Let us place a new infinitesimally small polygon
x^{\prime}
in each polygon
x
, so that the created polygons are pairwise disjoint, and
x^{\prime}
covers
\dot{x}
.
We redefine
X
to be the set of these new polygons, and consider them the new terminals; note that this increases the vertex count of
G
by
|X|
and retains the fairness of the representation, as well as the connectivity between terminals.

We define the rank of a vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus X
(during any time in this procedure) as the number of connected components of
G-v
that contain some vertex from
X
; we denote this number by
\rank(v)
. Furthermore, we fix some root vertex
r\in X
.
We then apply the following modifications exhaustively:

M1

If for some
v\in V(G)\setminus X
the graph
G-v
has a component that does not contain any vertex of
X
, then delete all vertices in this component.

M2

If
\rank(v)=1
for some
v\in V(G)\setminus X
, then we remove
v
.

M3

If
\rank(v)\geqslant 2
for some
v\in V(G)\setminus X
and some vertex
w\in V(G)-v
intersects
\inter v
, then let
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
be the components of
G-v
, where
U_{1}
is the component containing the root
r
. By
Lemma

4.1
, there exists a collection of at most
k-1
subpolygons

v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{t}\quad(t\leqslant k-1)
of
v
, which are pairwise disjoint, do not intersect the interior of any object of
G-v
, and these polygons
can be used to connect the components
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
. We then replace
v
with
v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{t}
in
G
to retain connectivity and the fair-frame property.

M4

For any point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is on the boundary of least three polygons
v\in V(G)
, we remove a small triangle or concave quadrilateral around
p
from all but one polygon that contains it; note that this does not change the realized intersection graph.

After exhausting modifications M1, M2, and M3, the realizing polygons are interior-disjoint. After M4, the resulting intersection graph is an object frame of the original graph. Moreover, each polygon in
V(G)\setminus X
is a cut vertex, as otherwise it could be removed by M2.

We now continue the procedure with the following modifications.

M5

If there is a cycle in the graph, then let
u
and
v
be two neighboring vertices along the cycle. We remove a small neighborhood of their shared boundary
u\cap v
from
v
. (Note that if the segment
u\cap v
contained a terminal point
\dot{x}
, then
\dot{x}
is still covered by
u
.)

Note that after applying M5 exhaustively we get a tree. Finally, we do our final modifications:

M6

If there are polygons
v^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{j}\in V(G)\setminus X
that are subpolygons of the same original object
v
such that the unique shortest path between them
u_{0}=v^{\prime}_{i},u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{t}=v^{\prime}_{j}
has the property that
u_{1},u_{2}\in V(G)\setminus X
and
\rank(u_{1})=\rank(u_{2})=2
, then we do the following operation. Let
C_{u_{0}}
and
C_{u_{3}}
be the two components (as sets of polygons) that we get after removing
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
from the graph. Let
U_{0}=\bigcup C_{u_{0}}
and
U_{3}=\bigcup C_{u_{3}}
denote the unions of these components, and apply
Lemma

4.1
on
v
and the components
U_{0}
and
U_{3}
. We get that
v
has a subpolygon
v^{\prime}
that connects
U_{0}
and
U_{3}
. We then remove
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
and add
v^{\prime}
. Notice that
v^{\prime}
may be incident to
v_{i}^{\prime}
or
v_{j}^{\prime}
(or even both); in such a case we take
v^{\prime}
and all incident subpolygons of
v
and replace them with their union (as a single subpolygon of
v
).

Figure 10:
Modification M6, where polygons of
X
are red and other polygons of the tree
G
are blue. (i) The setup of M6: the path between subpolygons
v^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{j}
of
v
passes through
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
that are of rank
2
(thus not branching vertices) and not polygons of
X
. (
ii
) The union-objects
U_{0}
and
U_{3}
, with a subpolygon
v^{\prime}
of
v
connecting them. (
iii
) The final polygon
v^{\prime}
after incorporating the incident
v^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{j}
. The connectivity is preserved after
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are deleted.

Let
G^{\prime}
denote the resulting object frame.
Consider the planar subdivision given by the union of the original polygon boundaries. Modifications M1 and M2 do not increase the complexity. Each polygon created by the modifications M3 and M6 is a subpolygon of some connected collection of regions of this subdivision, in particular, each created polygon has at most three times as many vertices as the union of the corresponding set of regions by the complexity bound of
Lemma

4.1
. Modification M4 may double the number of sides of any given polygon, and M5 can be executed without increasing the complexity of the polygons in question. As a result, we have that
\mathrm{compl}(G^{\prime})\leqslant\mathrm{compl}(G)+2\cdot 3\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))=O(\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
.

Recall that after applying M5 exhaustively we get a tree. Each leaf of this tree must be a terminal from
X
. Thus, the tree has at most
|X|-2
vertices of degree at least
3
, and there are at most
2|X|-2
edge-disjoint paths whose internal vertices have degree
2
and whose endpoints are leaves or branching in the tree. Consequently, there are at most
8|X|-8+2|X|-2<10|X|
vertices that are within distance
2
from a branching point. Furthermore, if
x\in X
is not a branching point, then there are at most
5
vertices that are within distance
2
from
x
and not within distance two of a branching, so in total there are at most
15|X|
vertices in
G^{\prime}
that are within distance
2
of a vertex of
X
or a branching. Thus, after applying M6 exhaustively, each of the
n
original objects has at most one subpolygon that is at distance at least
3
from all branching vertices and all vertices from
X
. Consequently, there are at most
n+|X|+15|X|
polygons. Thus the resulting object frame is a tree with at most
n+16|X|
vertices, as required.

Finally, notice that when
|X|=2
, then either the terminals intersect and
|G^{\prime}|=2
, or the the two terminals are disjoint. In this latter case, we did not introduce any new terminals, and by definition the rank of any object is at most
2
. In particular, M2 can replace a vertex
v
of rank
k=2
with
k-1=1
new object, so the number of objects is not increased by the modifications.
∎

If we apply the above theorem on a shortest path (with the endpoints being the terminals) then we get an isomorphic representation.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
P
be a shortest path from
u
to
v
in
G
, where
\dot{u}\in u
and
\dot{v}\in v
. Then there exists a wireframe
\widetilde{P}
in
G
of complexity
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{P})=O(\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(P)))
inducing a path from
\dot{u}
to
\dot{v}
where the parent relation
p(.)
gives an isomorphism to
P
.

Proof.

We consider the intersection graph given by the objects along
P
and set
X=\{u,v\}
with designated points
\dot{u}
and
\dot{v}
. Then
Theorem

4.2
gives an object frame of complexity
O(\mathcal{A}(P))
where
|V(G^{\prime})|\leqslant|V(G_{P})|
where the end objects are connected with an object frame. Since all edges of the object frame must correspond to edges of
P
, the only way for this connection to occur is if there is a bijection between
E(P)
and
E(G^{\prime})
, which in turn implies that the graphs are isomorphic under the parent relation. This isomorphism is preserved when we switch to a wireframe representation using
Lemma

3.8
. Finally, we apply
3.7
to make this wireframe of
P
into a wireframe of
G
without increasing its complexity.
∎

We will use the above corollary several times to define wireframe paths, but we will usually need a finer control over the complexity of the resulting paths. This is provided by the following lemma for a more specific setting. Essentially, we want to say that when we move from a region
\mathsf{R}
to a smaller region
\mathsf{R}_{2}
by cutting
\mathsf{R}
into two parts
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2}
with a shortest path
P
, then the increase of the complexity of the boundary of
\mathsf{R}_{2}
compared to
\mathsf{R}
can be bounded by the complexity inside
\mathsf{R}_{1}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
be a wireframe of
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
bounding the region
\mathsf{R}
, let
\dot{a},\dot{b}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
and
P
be a shortest path of
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
from
p|_{\mathsf{R}}(\dot{a})
to
p|_{\mathsf{R}}(\dot{b})
. Let
V(\partial\mathsf{R})
denote the set of vertices in
\mathcal{A}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
that appear on
\partial\mathsf{R}
, and let
V(\inter\mathsf{R})
be the set of vertices of
\mathcal{A}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
in
\inter\mathsf{R}
.

If
\dot{a}
and
\dot{b}
splits
\partial\mathsf{R}
into wireframe paths
\widetilde{Q}_{1}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{2}
, then there exists a wireframe path
\widetilde{P}
from
\dot{a}
to
\dot{b}
isomorphic to
P
under the parent function
p|_{\mathsf{R}}(.)
such that

•

\widetilde{P}
intersects
\partial\mathsf{R}
only at
\dot{a}
and
\dot{b}
, and

•

the regions
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2}\subset\mathsf{R}
bounded by
\widetilde{Q}_{1}\cup\widetilde{P}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{2}\cup\widetilde{P}
, respectively, satisfy
|V(\partial R_{2})|\leqslant|V(\partial R)|+8|V(\inter\mathsf{R}_{1})|
.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
Corollary

4.3
more carefully to establish a better complexity bound on
\widetilde{P}
and the boundaries of the regions
\mathsf{R}_{i}
.

Consider the intersection graph given by the objects along
P
and set
X=\{p|_{\mathsf{R}}(\dot{a}),p|_{\mathsf{R}}(\dot{b})\}
with designated points
\dot{a}
and
\dot{b}
. Then
Theorem

4.2
gives an object frame of complexity
O(\mathcal{A}(P))
and
|V(G^{\prime})|\leqslant|V(G_{P})|
, where the end objects are connected with an object frame, converted to a wireframe using
Lemma

3.8
, where the parent relation gives an isomorphism to
P
. We apply
3.7
to make this wireframe of
P
into a wireframe of
G
without increasing its complexity. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{k}
be the sequence of faces of the arrangement
\mathcal{A}(P)
traversed by
\widetilde{P}
such that
\dot{a}\in\partial F_{1}
and
\dot{b}\in\partial F_{k}
.

Let
\dot{p}_{i}
be a point of
\partial F_{i}\cap\partial F_{i+1}
that is not a vertex of
\mathcal{A}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
for each
i=1,\dots,k-1
, and set
\dot{p}_{0}:=\dot{a}
and
\dot{p}_{k}:=\dot{b}
. Notice that we have freedom in choosing a path
\widetilde{P}
connecting the points
\dot{p}_{0},\dots,\dot{p}_{k}
in this sequence, as long as the relative interior of
\gamma(\widetilde{P})[\dot{p}_{i-1},\dot{p}_{i}]\subset\inter F_{i}
, we preserve the isomorphism and the wireframe properties. We may also assume without loss of generality that each face occurs at most once in the sequence
F_{i}
, because we can directly traverse from the first entry into the face to the last exit of the face within
F_{i}
. See Figure
11
for an illustration.

Figure 11:
Finding a path
\widetilde{P}
(green) through the faces
F_{1},\dots,F_{k}
(blue background) of an arrangement. The path
\widetilde{P}
slices the shaded (red and blue) region
\mathsf{R}
into
\mathsf{R}_{1}
and
\mathsf{R}_{2}
. The curve
\widetilde{P}
is defined to follow the curve
\gamma
(gray, dashed). The curve
\gamma^{\prime}
is denoted by a magenta, dashed curve.

Let
\partial F
denote the cycle of
\partial\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}
that contains
\dot{p}_{0}
and
\dot{p}_{k}
: such a cycle exists as
\dot{p}_{0}=\dot{a}
and
\dot{p}_{k}=\dot{b}
are on the closed curve
\partial R
that is disjoint from the connected set
\inter\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}
. Notice that
\dot{p}_{0}
and
\dot{p}_{k}
splits the closed curve
\partial F
into two subpaths:
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
.

We claim now that
\dot{p}_{i-1}
and
\dot{p}_{i}
are on the same component of
\partial F_{i}
. To see why, assume the contrary. Since
\widetilde{P}
passes through
F_{i}
only once, it follows that some boundary component
\mu
of
\partial F_{i}
which is disjoint from
\partial\mathsf{R}
is crossed by
\widetilde{P}
exactly once, and thus
\mu
separates
\dot{a}
and
\dot{b}
. This would imply however that the closed curves
\mu
and
\gamma(\widetilde{P})\cup\gamma(\widetilde{Q}_{1})
intersect each other exactly once, which is a contradiction.

The component of
\partial F_{i}
containing
\dot{p}_{i-1}
and
\dot{p}_{i}
is split into two parts by
\dot{p}_{i-1}
and
\dot{p}_{i}
. Let
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
denote these parts. Observe that
\gamma_{i}
cannot intersect both
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
(and the same holds for
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
) as in the union of the faces
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}
the curve
\gamma(\widetilde{P})
separates
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, and it also separates
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
. We can therefore define
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
in such a way that
\gamma_{i}\cap\gamma^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\cap\gamma=\emptyset
.

Assume without loss of generality that
\gamma
is inside
\mathsf{R}_{1}
. We define
\widetilde{P}
to follow the curve
\gamma_{i}
inside each
\inter F_{i}
from
\dot{p}_{i-1}
to
\dot{p}_{i}
. As a result, the complexity of
\widetilde{P}
is
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{compl}(\gamma_{i})
. Notice that
\widetilde{P}
intersects
\partial\mathsf{R}
only at
\dot{a}
and
\dot{b}
by definition.

Consider now the plane graph
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
given by the vertices and edges of
\mathcal{A}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
, that is
V(\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}})=V(\partial\mathsf{R})\cup V(\inter\mathsf{R})
. Since
G
is
\alpha
-standard, we have that no three polygon edges of
G
meet at one point, thus for any
v\in V(\inter\mathsf{R})
we have
\deg_{\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}}(v)\leqslant 4
.

Charge each edge of
\widetilde{P}
to the corresponding edge of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
. Notice that each edge of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
can be followed by at most two edges of
\widetilde{P}
(one edge in each adjacent face of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
), except edges that are on
\partial\mathsf{R}
as such edges can be followed only once. Indeed, for an edge of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
that is in
\partial\mathsf{R}
only one of the incident faces of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
will lie in
\mathsf{R}
, while
\widetilde{P}
lies in
\mathsf{R}
and consequently can follow along only on the side of the edge covered by
\mathsf{R}
.
More precisely, each edge of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
that is incident to a vertex inside
\inter\mathsf{R}_{1}
is charged at most twice, while each edge of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
that is on
\gamma(\widetilde{Q}_{1})
is charged at most once; no other edges of
\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}
are charged. Denoting the former edge set by
E_{\inter}
and the latter by
E_{\partial}
, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\leqslant|E_{\partial}|+2|E_{\inter}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{Q}_{1})+2\sum_{v\in V(\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}})\cap\inter\mathsf{R}_{1}}\deg_{\widetilde{A}_{\mathsf{R}}}(v)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{Q}_{1})+8|V(\inter\mathsf{R}_{1})|

by our earlier degree bound. Adding
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{Q}_{2})
to both sides concludes the proof.
∎

5
Lipschitz embeddings into plane graphs

Let
G,H
be graphs. We say that a mapping
f:V(G)\rightarrow V(H)
is

c
-Lipschitz
for some constant
c\geqslant 1
if for any pair of vertices
x,y\in V(G)

we have that

\frac{1}{c}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(f(x),f(y))\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y).

(1)

Clearly any
c
-Lipschitz mapping is injective.

Observation 5.1
.

The natural mapping
f
from
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
to
G_{\mathcal{P}}
satisfies

\frac{1}{3}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(f(x),f(y))\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)

for all
x,y
with
f(x)\neq f(y)
.

Proof.

The upper bound of
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(f(x),f(y))\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)
follows from the fact that
G_{\mathcal{P}}
is obtained from
G
by edge contractions, which cannot increase distances.

For any edge
f(u)f(v)\in E(G_{\mathcal{P}})
there is a path of length at most
3

connecting
u
and
v
in
G
, and therefore
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(f(u),f(v))\leqslant 3\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)
holds. The same inequality follows for any
u,v
where
f(u)\neq f(v)
by applying the above inequality on all edges of the shortest
f(u)-f(v)
path in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

The goal of this section is to show that
G_{\mathcal{P}}
can be Lipschitz-mapped to a
planar graph on the same vertex set as
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, and then to use this to show
that
G
itself can be Lipschitz-mapped to a planar graph with the same
vertex set as
G
. For a clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\dot{C}
denote the stabbing
point of
C
, and let
\dot{\mathcal{P}}
be the set of centers of
non-empty cells. Recall that we can also think of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
as an intersection graph, where the object corresponding to a clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
is the union of the objects in
C
. The core theorem of this section is the following.

Theorem 5.2
.

For any
\alpha>0
, there exists a positive constant
c=O(\alpha^{32})
such that
for any
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
given by its representation the following holds. Let

\mathcal{P}
be the cell partition of
G
. Then in polynomial time one can construct
a plane graph
H=(\dot{\mathcal{P}},E^{\prime})
such that natural mapping from
\mathcal{P}
to

\dot{\mathcal{P}}
is
c
-Lipschitz from
G_{\mathcal{P}}
to
H
. Moreover,
H
has maximum degree
O(\alpha^{34})
, its edges have geometric diameter at most
O(\alpha^{17})
and each edge of
H
can be covered by at most
O(\alpha^{34})
objects of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Before we begin the proof, note that the theorem implies the analogous
statement for
G
itself.

Corollary 5.3
.

For any
\alpha>0
there exists a positive constant
c=O(\alpha^{32})
such that
for any
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
given by its representation, one can in polynomial
time construct a plane graph
G^{\prime}=(V(G),E^{\prime})
such that the identity mapping of

V(G)
is
c
-Lipschitz from
G
to
G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
H
be the plane graph on
\dot{\mathcal{P}}
created by

Theorem

5.2
, and to each vertex
\dot{C}
of
H
we attach

|C|-1
leaves, and assign the original vertex and the leaves arbitrarily to
distinct vertices of
C
. It is routine to check that with the resulting planar graph
G^{\prime}
the above map is
3c
-Lipschitz, where
c
is the Lipschitz-constant guaranteed by

Theorem

5.2
.
∎

The following lemma will be used much later to help us get rid of unwanted
vertices using a contraction, while maintaining a Lipschitz map.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
G
be a graph with vertex set
V
, and let
H
be a graph with vertex
set
W
such that
V\subseteq W
. Suppose moreover that the identity map

f:V\rightarrow V
is
c
-Lipschitz from
G
to
H
. Then in polynomial time
(in terms of
|W|
) one can construct a set of edges
S
in
H
such that the
vertices of the contraction
H/S
correspond to the vertices of
V
and this
map is
c^{2}
-Lipschitz from
G
to
H/S
.

Moreover, if
H
is a plane graph whose edges are piece-wise linear curves of geometric diameter at most
\delta
, then for any
\mu>0
the graph
H/S
can be realized as a plane graph whose edges have geometric diameter at most
\delta\cdot(c+2)
, and each edge of
H/S
is contained in the geometric
\mu
-neighborhood of some path of
H
.

Proof.

Let us show first that we can assume that each vertex
w\in W
has distance at
most
c/2
from some vertex in
V
.
We claim that the identity map remains

c
-Lipschitz from
G
to
H
if we remove every vertex of
H
that is at distance more than
c/2
from every vertex in
V
. Indeed, each edge of
G
can be realized with a
path of length at most
c
in
H
connecting two vertices of
V
, and no vertex on such a path was removed. Consequently, any shortest path of length
k
in
G

can be covered by the union of at most
k
such paths of length
c
. On the
other hand, deleting vertices from
H
can only increase pairwise distances,
so the inequality
\frac{1}{c}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(u,v)
remains true
after the deletion. Therefore, in the following we can assume that each vertex
w\in W
has distance at
most
c/2
from some vertex in
V
.

Let
V=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
, and define the following vertex sets in
H
. The

Voronoi cell

\Vor(v_{i})
of a vertex
v_{i}\in V
contains the vertices

w\in W
that are closest to
v
in
H
-distance among all the vertices of

V
(breaking ties by the order of
i
). More precisely, we define

\displaystyle\Vor(v)=\big\{w\in W\mid

\displaystyle\phantom{\text{ and }}\forall j<i:\mathrm{dist}_{H}(v_{i},w)<\mathrm{dist}_{H}(v_{j},w)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\forall j>i:\mathrm{dist}_{H}(v_{i},w)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(v_{j},w)\big\}.

We claim that
H[\Vor(v_{i})]
is connected. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
H[\Vor(v_{i})]
is disconnected, and let
u\in\Vor(v_{i})
be in a different connected component of
H[\Vor(v_{i})]
than
v_{i}
. Let
P_{u}
be a shortest path from
v_{i}
to
u
, and let
zz^{\prime}
