be an edge of
P_{u}
where
z\not\in\Vor(v_{i})
and
z^{\prime}\in\Vor(v_{i})
(where
z
is closer to
u
than
z^{\prime}
). Now
z\in\Vor(v_{j})
; suppose that
j<i
. Then by the definition of Voronoi diagrams we have (a)
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z,v_{i})\geqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z,v_{j})
and (b)
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z^{\prime},v_{i})<\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z^{\prime},v_{j})
. On the other hand, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z^{\prime},v_{i})=\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z,v_{i})+1
and the edge
zz^{\prime}
ensures
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z^{\prime},v_{j})\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(z,v_{j})+1
; substituting these to (b) gives a contradiction with (a). The case when
j>i
can be argued the same way, one just needs a strict inequality in (a) and a non-strict inequality in (b). This concludes the proof that
H[\Vor(v_{i})]
is connected.

Notice that since each vertex in
W
has distance at most
c/2
from some
vertex in
V
, we have that
\Vor(v)
has diameter at most
c
. Let
E_{i}

denote the set of edges of the subgraph of
H
induced by
\Vor(v_{i})
, and
let
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}
.

Next, we contract all edges of each Voronoi cell in
H
and remove any
multiple edges to get the graph
H^{\prime}=H/S
, where we identify the vertex that
results from the contraction of
E_{i}
with
v_{i}
.
Since contractions can only decrease the distances between vertices, we have

\mathrm{dist}_{H^{\prime}}(v_{i},v_{j})\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{H}(v_{i},v_{j})\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v_{i},v_{j})
. On the other hand, if
\varrho
is a shortest path from

v_{i}
to
v_{j}
in
H
of length
\ell
, then it intersects at least

\ell/c
distinct Voronoi cells (as each cell has diameter at most
c
).
Consequently,
\mathrm{dist}_{H^{\prime}}(v_{i},v_{j})\geqslant\frac{1}{c}\mathrm{dist}_{H}(v_{i},v_{j})\geqslant\frac{1}{c^{2}}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v_{i},v_{j})
.

Planar realizations.

Suppose now that
H
is a plane graph whose edges have geometric diameter at most
\delta
, and
\mu>0
. We assume without loss of generality that
\mu
is small enough, i.e., we assume that
\mu<\delta/2
, and in the plane realization of
H
all vertices have pairwise distance at least
3\mu
, and all non-incident vertex-edge pairs have pairwise distance at least
3\mu
. Our first goal is to construct pairwise non-crossing short paths
P_{ij}
for each edge
v_{i}v_{j}
of
H/S
.

For each edge
v_{i}v_{j}
of
H/S
with
i<j
let
P^{0}_{ij}
be a path of length at most
c+1
in
H
constructed as follows: we fix some edge
w_{i}w_{j}
where
w_{i}\in\Vor(v_{i})
and
w_{j}\in\Vor(v_{j})
. Then we fix a shortest path from
v_{i}
to
w_{i}
and from
w_{j}
to
w_{j}
. The concatenation of these two paths and the edge
w_{i}w_{j}
forms a path
P^{0}_{ij}
of length at most
c/2+1+c/2=c+1
.

Next, we
uncross
the paths
P^{0}_{ij}
. Consider two distinct paths
P^{0}_{ij}
and
P^{0}_{k\ell}
. Observe that when the paths do not share endpoints, then their vertices are covered by pairwise disjoint voronoi cells, thus they are vertex-disjoint. Suppose now that
P^{0}_{ij}
and
P^{0}_{k\ell}
share one endpoint, and assume without loss of generality that
i=k
. (Since the paths are distinct, they cannot share both their endpoints, as they would correspond to the same edge
v_{i}v_{j}
of
H/S
.) It follows that
P^{0}_{ij}
and
P^{0}_{i\ell}
can only share vertices inside
\Vor(v_{i})
. Let
w
be their shared vertex that is at the largest
H
-distance from
v_{i}
. We then modify
P^{0}_{i\ell}
by replacing
P^{0}_{i\ell}[v_{i},w]
with
P^{0}_{ij}[v_{i},w]
. We do these modifications exhaustively, until any pair of paths are either vertex disjoint or they share some prefix/suffix; let
P_{ij}
denote the collection of paths at the end of the procedure.

It is now routine to construct the desired planar realization of
H/S
. For each vertex
v_{i}
and each neighbor
v_{j}
with
v_{i}v_{j}\in E(H/S)
consider the paths
P_{ij}
from
v_{i}
. These paths form a plane tree in
H
rooted at
v_{i}
whose leaves are
N_{H/S}(v_{i})
, i.e., the other endpoints of the paths
P_{ij}
. We create a new plane tree consisting of the root-to-leaf paths to remove all overlaps but keep all paths within distance
\mu
of the original root-to-leaf paths. Since the overlapping parts are restricted to
\Vor(v_{i})
, these modifications can be done in each Voronoi cell separately. The resulting collection of plane paths
P^{\prime}_{ij}
are pairwise disjoint other than possible shared endpoints, they are within distance
\mu
of the original path
P_{ij}
in
H
of length at most
c+1
, and their geometric diameter is at most
(c+1)\delta+2\mu\leqslant(c+2)\delta
. Thus their curves form a plane realization of
H/S
with the desired properties.
∎

5.1
Approximate plane graph with extra vertices

In order to prove
Theorem

5.2
, we need to start with an
intermediate step, which resembles known contraction decomposition theorems for unit disks
[
74
,
10
]
: initially, we also allow additional vertices in the plane graph,
and later we will remove the extra vertices so that only the clique centers

\dot{\mathcal{P}}
remain.

Let us fix a graph
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
and its representation, as well as a
cell-partition
\mathcal{P}
based on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Each edge
CC^{\prime}
of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
can be associated with some
edge
uu^{\prime}
of
G
where
u\in C
and
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
. If
\widetilde{P}
is a single-edge wireframe of
G
with endpoints
\dot{C}
and
\dot{C}^{\prime}
, where
p_{\gamma}(\dot{C})\in C
and
p_{\gamma}(\dot{C}^{\prime})\in C^{\prime}
, then we say that the curve
\gamma=\gamma(\widetilde{P})
is an
edge curve
of
G
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{P}
, which corresponds to the edge
CC^{\prime}
of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. An edge curve can be constructed by applying
Corollary

4.3
to the single-edge shortest path
uu^{\prime}
of
G
and the fixed endpoints
\dot{C}\in\inter u
and
\dot{C}^{\prime}\in\inter u^{\prime}
. We construct for each edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
a corresponding edge curve, and denote by
\Gamma
the resulting set of edge curves. We denote by
\mathcal{I}(\Gamma)
the intersection
graph induced by
\Gamma
. Observe that the curves are drawn in an open region, thus —similarly to
3.7
— they can be defined in such way to satisfy the following general position requirements: (i) the
curves of
\Gamma
avoid
\dot{\mathcal{P}}
, that is, no curve passes through any
point
a\in\dot{\mathcal{P}}
other than at its endpoints, (
ii
) distinct edge curves intersect each other in
O(\mathcal{A}(G))
points, and (
iii
) no three edge curves meet at any point of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\dot{\mathcal{P}}
.

Since each object of
G
has diameter at most
\alpha
, and an edge curve can be covered by the union of two objects that intersect, we have the following observations.

Observation 5.5
.

The edge curves of
\Gamma
satisfy the following properties.

(i)

Each point
\dot{C}
is incident to at most
O(\alpha^{2})
edge curves.

(ii)

Each edge curve
\gamma\in\Gamma
has geometric diameter at most
2\alpha
.

(iii)

Each edge curve intersects at most
O(\alpha^{4})
other edge curves.

We can now prove the following.

Theorem 5.6
.

For any
\alpha>0
there exists a positive constant
c=c_{\alpha}
such that
for any
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
given by its representation the following hold. Let

\mathcal{P}
be the cell partition of
G
. Then in polynomial time one can construct
a plane graph
H
with
\dot{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq V(H)
such that the natural mapping from
\mathcal{P}
to
\dot{\mathcal{P}}

is
c
-Lipschitz from
G_{\mathcal{P}}
to
H
for some
c=O(\alpha^{16})
. Moreover, (i)
H
has maximum degree at most
O(\alpha^{2})
, (ii) for each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
the union
\bigcup_{v\in C}v
covers at most
O(\alpha^{8})
vertices of
H
, and (iii) each edge of
H
is a section of an edge curve of
G
and thus each edge of
H
has geometric diameter at most
2\alpha
.

Proof.

Consider the curve set
\Gamma
in the plane, and add each
intersection of pairs of curves as new vertices, resulting in a plane graph, where each vertex is either a point
\dot{C}\in\dot{\mathcal{P}}
of degree at most
O(\alpha^{2})
(by
5.5
(
i
)) or it is an intersection thus a vertex of degree
4
. The graph
H
will be defined as a subgraph of this graph, so it will automatically satisfy conditions (i) and (
iii
). Unfortunately, each curve of
\Gamma
could intersect any other curve of

\Gamma
in
O(\mathcal{A}(G))
many points, therefore we have to decrease the number
of intersections involved. We partition
\Gamma
into classes so that edge curves that are in the same class are pairwise distant in
\mathcal{I}(\Gamma)
, and we will add the classes of curves one by one in our construction.

Claim 5.7
.

For any positive integer
d
there is a partition of
\Gamma
into

O(d^{4}\alpha^{4})
sets such that if
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
are in the same partition
class then
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{I}(\Gamma)}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\geqslant d
and

\min_{x\in\gamma,y\in\gamma^{\prime}}\mathrm{dist}(x,y)\geqslant d\alpha
.

Proof of claim.

5.5
implies that if
\mathrm{dist}(\gamma(0),\gamma^{\prime}(0))>2\alpha\cdot(d+1)
, then
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{I}(\Gamma)}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\geqslant d
. It also follows that any point of
\gamma
and any point of
\gamma^{\prime}
has distance more than
d\alpha
. Set

t=\lceil 2(d+1)\alpha\rceil
, and let us now define the partition class

\Gamma_{i,j,k,\ell}
for
i,j\in[t]\times[t]
, as follows:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i,j,k,l}=\big\{\gamma\in\Gamma\mid

\displaystyle(\gamma(0))_{x}\equiv i\mod t,

\displaystyle(\gamma(0))_{y}\equiv j\mod t

\displaystyle(\gamma(1))_{x}\equiv k\mod t,

\displaystyle(\gamma(1))_{y}\equiv\ell\mod t\big\}

This is a partition of size
t^{2}=O(d^{4}\alpha^{4})
that satisfies
the desired property.
∎

Let
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\dots
be a partition of

\Gamma
according to
5.7
into
O(\alpha^{4})

classes so that curves in the same class have distance at least

d=3
in
\mathcal{I}(\Gamma)
. Our strategy is to add these classes one by one, reducing
the number of newly created intersections after each step. On the planar graph
H
that we build,
each edge will be labeled by the curve
\gamma\in\Gamma
that the edge came from, and we also
maintain a set
\Pi
of paths: for each previously introduced curve
\gamma
with endpoints
\gamma(0)
to
\gamma(1)
we ensure that
\Pi
contains a path
\pi(\gamma)
connecting these endpoints in the current graph.

In each phase
i
we will add the curves of
\Gamma_{i}
one by one to the existing planar graph, updating the graph
H
with new vertices and edges, as well as updating the path collection
\Pi
. When adding a new curve
\gamma\in\Gamma_{i}
, we add the endpoints
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)
as vertices if they are not yet
H
-vertices, and we add
\gamma
as the realization of the edge between
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)
. We also add the single-edge path
\pi(\gamma)
realized by this edge to
\Pi
. Next, we need to address the intersections of
\gamma
with the existing graph
H
to maintain planarity. Suppose that
\gamma
intersects some existing edge of
H
, and label each intersection on
\gamma
with the curve of
\Gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Gamma_{i-1}
that is intersected there. If there is a label that repeats, then we use the following
slicing
procedure recursively on
\gamma
.

Let
\gamma(a)
and
\gamma(b)
be a pair of
maximally distant intersections (along
\gamma
) of the same label
\gamma^{\prime}
, that is, choose
\gamma^{\prime}
so that for any label
\hat{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Gamma_{i-1}
that appears on
\gamma
at least twice at
\gamma(\hat{a})
and
\gamma(\hat{b})
,
it holds that
[a,b]\setminus[\hat{a},\hat{b}]\neq\emptyset
. We add
\gamma(a)
and
\gamma(b)
as new vertices, and remove the edge represented by
\gamma|_{(a,b)}
. We update the part of
\pi(\gamma)
between
\gamma(a)
and
\gamma(b)
to detour along
\pi(\gamma^{\prime})
and apply the slicing procedure recursively on the curves
4
4

4

Technically, we have
\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined as

\gamma^{1}(x)=\gamma(ax)
and
\gamma^{2}(x)=\gamma(b+(1-b)x)
.

\gamma^{1}=\gamma|_{[0,a]}
and
\gamma^{2}=\gamma|_{[b,1]}
. By the end of the slicing procedure, the remaining subcurves of
\gamma
will be intersected with distinct labels; for each such intersection we add a new vertex as described above.

Let
H
denote the final graph.
We note here that at the end of the procedure, for any given pair of edge curves
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
there are at most two intersections among
\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}
that appear as vertices in
H
. Thus property (
ii
) follows: all objects of a given clique can be covered by a square of area
O(\alpha^{2})
that intersects
O(\alpha^{4})
edge curves, so there can be at most
O(\alpha^{8})
pairs of edge curve pairs intersecting in this region, each contributing at most
2
vertices to
H
.

Let
\Pi
denote the final paths in
H
, and let
\pi(\gamma)
be the path of
\Pi
corresponding to
\gamma
. We denote by
H_{i}
the graph and by
\Pi_{i}
the set of paths after all curves of
\Gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Gamma_{i}
have been introduced.
For a curve
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Gamma_{i}
, let
\pi_{i}(\gamma)
denote the corresponding path in
\Pi_{i}
. We denote the embedded curve of
\pi_{i}(\gamma)
by
\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\gamma)
.

Claim 5.8
.

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{i}
the path
\pi_{i}(\gamma)
has at most
i+1
edges that are represented by subcurves of
\gamma
.

Proof of claim.

The curve
\gamma
can intersect at most one curve from
each set
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{i}
, as otherwise
there would be two curves in some
\Gamma_{j}
whose graph distance in
\mathcal{I}(\Gamma)
is at most
2
. Therefore,
\gamma
will have at most
i

disjoint maximally distant intersection pairs in the slicing procedure. Consequently,
\pi_{i}(\gamma)
makes at most
i
detours from
\gamma
, thus
\pi_{i}(\gamma)
retains at most
i+1
subcurves of
\gamma
.
∎

Claim 5.9
.

The maximum (Euclidean) distance of a curve point
x\in\gamma
and a curve point

y\in\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\gamma)
is
O(i\alpha)
.

Proof of claim.

We use induction on
i
. Clearly the statement holds for
i=1
since for any

\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}
we have
\gamma=\tilde{\pi}_{1}(\gamma)
, and thus the maximum
distance between them is
\mathrm{diam}(\gamma)\leqslant 2\alpha
by
5.5
(ii).
Suppose now that
\max\{\mathrm{dist}(x,y)\mid x\in\gamma,y\in\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\gamma)\}\leqslant 4i\alpha
.

Notice that if
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Gamma_{i}
, then the
curves
\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\gamma)
and
\tilde{\pi}_{i+1}(\gamma)
are identical, so it is
sufficient to prove that for any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{i+1}
it holds that

\max\{\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(x,y)\mid x\in\gamma,y\in\widetilde{\pi}_{i+1}(\gamma)\}\leqslant 2(i+1)\alpha
. Each
detour that
\pi_{i+1}(\gamma)
takes from
\gamma
will be along a portion
of some curve
\widetilde{\pi}_{i}(\gamma^{\prime})
for some

\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\Gamma_{i}
. By induction, we have that

\widetilde{\pi}_{i}(\gamma^{\prime})
has maximum distance
2i\alpha
from
\gamma^{\prime}
, and

\gamma^{\prime}
intersects
\gamma
, therefore the maximum distance from
\gamma

is at most
4i\alpha+\mathrm{diam}(\gamma)+\mathrm{diam}(\gamma^{\prime})\leqslant 4i\alpha+4\alpha=4(i+1)\alpha
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We occasionally refer to the curves corresponding to the paths in

\Pi
by
\tilde{\Pi}
. Since there are
O(\alpha^{4})
phases,
5.9
implies that the geometric diameter of each path

\tilde{\pi}\in\tilde{\Pi}
is
O(\alpha^{4}\cdot\alpha)=O(\alpha^{5})
. Moreover, we have the following.

Claim 5.10
.

Each curve
\tilde{\pi}\in\tilde{\Pi}
has diameter
O(\alpha^{5})
, and the corresponding path
\pi(\gamma)
in
H
has at most
O(\alpha^{16})
edges.

Proof of claim.

Consider a path
\pi\in\Pi
in
H
and its edge labels. Recall that by
definition any pair of distinct curves in
\Gamma_{i}

have minimum geometric distance at least
3\alpha
. Since the geometric diameter of
\tilde{\pi}

is
O(\alpha^{5})
, it may intersect other curves within a region of size
O(\alpha^{5})\times O(\alpha^{5})
, which contains at most
O(\alpha^{10})
endpoints. Each endpoint has
O(\alpha^{2})
edge curves ending there, so altogether at most
O(\alpha^{12})
distinct labels can occur in

\pi
.

We now bound the number of occurrences for a fixed label
\gamma\in\Gamma_{i}
along
\pi
.
By
5.8
we have that
\gamma
will create at most
i+1=O(\alpha^{4})
subcurves
(i.e. edges labeled with
\gamma
) after its slicing in phase
i
. The later phases
j>i
can however add further subdividing vertices on these edges. In each new phase
j>i
, the union of the label-
\gamma
subcurves of
\pi
can receive at most two new subdividing vertices, as at most one curve among
\Gamma_{j}

can intersect
\gamma
from each later phase
j>i
, and after its slicing it will intersect
\gamma
at most twice.

Thus any fixed curve label can
occur on at most
O(\alpha^{4})
edges of
\pi
. Consequently, there are
O(\alpha^{12})
distinct edge labels on
\pi
, each occurring
O(\alpha^{4})
times, so the length of
\pi
in
H
is
O(\alpha^{16})
.
∎

It remains to show the
c
-Lipschitz property. Since each edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
is
represented by a curve
\gamma\in\Gamma
that has a corresponding path

\pi\in\Pi
in
H
, our bound on the paths in
\Pi
imply that
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(\dot{u},\dot{v})\leqslant O(\alpha^{16})\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(u,v)
. For the other
inequality, observe that each edge of
H
can be covered by at most two
objects, and therefore any path of length
k
in
H
corresponds to a path in
G
of length at most
2k
, which directly maps
to a path of length at most
2k
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. Consequently,

\mathrm{dist}_{H}(\dot{u},\dot{v})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(u,v),

(2)

which concludes the proof of
Theorem

5.6
.
∎

We are now ready to finish the proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

Let
H_{0}
be the planar graph with extra vertices given by
Theorem

5.6
. We set
\mu
small enough so that for any edge curve
\gamma
of
H
covered by a minimal collection
T
of objects it holds that the geometric
\mu
-neighborhood of
\gamma
is also covered by
T
. We apply
Lemma

5.4
to
G_{\mathcal{P}}
,
H_{0}
and
\mu
. This yields an edge set
S
and the plane graph
H_{0}/S
so that
H_{0}/S
and
G_{\mathcal{P}}
are
O(\alpha^{16})
-Lipschitz, each edge of
H_{0}/S
is covered by objects from
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, and each edge of
H_{0}/S
has at most
O(\alpha^{16})
times the geometric diameter of the maximum edge diameter of
H_{0}
, which —as an edge curve section— had geometric diameter at most
O(\alpha)
. Thus
H:=H_{0}/S
is a plane graph that can be created in
polynomial time and has the desired Lipschitz property with the Lipschitz-constant
\big(O(\alpha^{16})\big)^{2}=O(\alpha^{32})
, its edges have geometric diameter at most
O(\alpha^{17})
and they are covered by objects of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. Since there are
O(\alpha^{34})
objects in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
within distance
O(\alpha^{17})
of any given point in the plane, we have that
O(\alpha^{34})
objects of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
are sufficient to cover any edge of
H
.

To prove the degree bound on
H
, recall that
V(H)=\dot{\mathcal{P}}
is a subset of the integer grid, so we have that the number of points within distance
O(\alpha^{17})
from a fixed vertex
\dot{C}\in V(H)
is at most
O(\alpha^{34})
, thus the maximum degree of
H
is at most
O(\alpha^{34})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

5.2
Constructing a wireframe from a Lipschitz embedding

By modifying the graph obtained in
Theorem

5.6
we get the following theorem.

Theorem 5.11
.

For any
\alpha>0
there exists a positive constant
c=c(\alpha)
such that
for any
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
given by its representation in polynomial time one can construct a wireframe
\widetilde{G}
such that
p(V(\widetilde{G}))=V(G)
and for any
\dot{u},\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{G})
where
p(\dot{u})\neq p(\dot{v})
we have

\frac{1}{c}\mathrm{dist}_{G}\big(p(\dot{u}),p(\dot{v})\big)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{u},\dot{v})\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}\big(p(\dot{u}),p(\dot{v})\big).

Moreover, for each
v\in V(G)
we have
|p^{-1}(v)|\leqslant c
, and the maximum degree of
\widetilde{G}
is at most
c+\Delta(G)
where
\Delta(G)
is the maximum degree of
G
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the cell partition of
G
, and apply
Theorem

5.6
to obtain a plane graph
H
. We will use the drawing of
H
to construct
\widetilde{G}
. For each
C\in\mathcal{P}
fix a vertex
v_{C}\in C
, and set
p(\dot{C})=v_{C}
. We note that the vertices
v_{C}
are distinct for different cliques
C
. Consider now a vertex
\dot{a}\in V(H)\setminus\dot{\mathcal{P}}
, which is an intersection of the edge curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. Since
\dot{a}\in\gamma
, we have that it must be contained either in the interior of
p_{\gamma}(\gamma(0))
or in the interior of
p_{\gamma}(\gamma(1))
. Similarly, it is in the interior of at least one among
p_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime}(0))
and
p_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime}(1))
. We set
p(\dot{a})
to be one of the objects
u\in\{p_{\gamma}(\gamma(0)),p_{\gamma}(\gamma(1)),p_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime}(0)),p_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime}(1))\}
where
\dot{a}\in\inter u
.

Doing this assignment for all vertices of
H
results in a planar graph that satisfies the first property of wireframes. We will need further modifications to satisfy the second property. Consider an edge
e=\dot{x}\dot{y}\in E(H)
, and suppose that the edge is represented as a subcurve of the edge curve
\gamma
where
p_{\gamma}(\gamma(0))=a
and
p_{\gamma}(\gamma(1))=b
. Let
\gamma^{e}
denote the restriction of
\gamma
to the part between
\dot{x}
and
\dot{y}
, re-parameterized so that
\gamma^{e}(0)=\dot{x}
and
\gamma^{e}(1)=\dot{y}
. The definition of
\gamma
implies that
\gamma^{e}[0,1]
is either entirely contained in the interior of some object
w\in\{a,b\}
, or there is some
t\in(0,1)
such that
\gamma^{e}[0,t)\subset\inter a
and
\gamma^{e}(t,1]\subset\inter b
. If
\gamma^{e}
is contained
in
\inter w
where
w\in\{a,b\}
, then we place a new vertex at its middle whose parent is
w
. In the latter case, let
\dot{z}\in\gamma^{e}
be a point such that
\dot{z}\in\inter a\cap\inter b
. Then we place two new points
\dot{z}_{a}
and
\dot{z}_{b}
in a small neighborhood of
\dot{z}
so that they are both in
\dot{z}\in\inter a\cap\inter b
, the initial part of
\gamma_{a}
ending at
\dot{z}_{1}
is covered by
\inter a
, and the final part of
\gamma^{e}
from
\dot{z}_{2}
is covered by
\inter b
. We set their parents as
p(\dot{z}_{1})=a
and
p(\dot{z}_{2})=b
. Notice that we need to subdivide each edge of
H
at most twice.

Next, to ensure that
p(V(\widetilde{G}))=V(G)
, consider some vertex
u\in V(G)
that is not a parent of any vertex so far, and let
C
be the clique of
u
. Then we add a new point
\dot{u}
with
p(\dot{u})=u
to our planar graph in a small neighborhood of
\dot{C}
(and inside
\inter u
) and connect
\dot{u}
to
\dot{C}
.

At the end of this procedure, the mapping
p(.)
defined above gives a valid wireframe of
G
that we denote by
\widetilde{G}
. We note here the following about the above construction.

(
\star
)

If
\gamma^{e}
is an edge of
H
that is part of the edge curve
\gamma
, then
\gamma^{e}
contains at least one vertex of
V(\widetilde{G})
whose parent is either
p_{\gamma}(\gamma(0))
or
p_{\gamma}(\gamma(1))
.

We will prove the degree bound and the bound on
p^{-1}(v)
next. Recall that by
Theorem

5.6
the maximum degree of
H
is
O(1)
, and our subdivisions introduced only degree-
2
vertices. We furthermore added up to
|C|-1
neighbors to some of the vertices
\dot{C}
; it follows that the maximum degree of
\widetilde{G}
is at most
O(1)+\max_{C\in\mathcal{P}}|C|\leqslant O(1)+\Delta(G)
.
Theorem

5.6
created a graph where the objects in any clique of
G
contain
O(1)
vertices of
H
in total, and
H
has degree
O(1)
. We have subdivided each edge of
H
at most twice, thus there are
O(1)
vertices of
\widetilde{G}
intersected by any given clique (and thus by any given object) of
G
. Since an object
v\in V(G)
can only be assigned as parent to vertices of
\widetilde{G}
that fall inside
v
, we have that
|p^{-1}(v)|=O(1)
.

It remains to show the distance property. Let
\dot{x},\dot{y}\in V(\widetilde{G})
with
x=p(\dot{x})
and
y=p(\dot{y})
, where
x\neq y
. First, notice that since
\widetilde{G}
is a wireframe in
G
, we have that each edge of
\widetilde{G}
corresponds to an edge (between the parents) in
G
; in particular, a shortest path in
\widetilde{G}
can be mapped to a walk of equal length in
G
connecting
x
and
y
. Therefore

\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{y}).

Next, we need to upper bound
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{y})
. Notice that it is sufficient to prove that
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{y})
is bounded by a constant whenever
x
and
y
are adjacent in
G
; the general bound then follows from applying this bound on all edges of a shortest path.
Let
\gamma_{u}^{e}
be the curve of an edge of
H
containing (or incident to)
\dot{x}
, and let
\gamma_{u}
be the edge curve of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{P}
that
\gamma^{e}_{u}
is a subcurve of. By (
\star
) we have that
\gamma^{e}_{u}
contains a vertex
\dot{u}
such that
p(\dot{u})\in\{p_{\gamma_{u}}(\gamma_{u}(0)),p_{\gamma_{u}}(\gamma_{u}(1))\}
. Let
u:=p(\dot{u})
and let
C_{u}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
u\in C_{u}
(and thus
\dot{C}_{u}\in\{\gamma_{u}(0),\gamma_{u}(1)\}
). We define
\dot{v}
,
v
and
C_{v}
symmetrically based on
\dot{y}
.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{y})&\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{u})+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{u},\dot{C}_{u})\\
&\phantom{\leqslant}+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{C}_{u},\dot{C}_{v})\\
&\phantom{\leqslant}+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{C}_{v},\dot{v})+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{v},\dot{y}).\end{split}

(3)

Recall from the proof of
Theorem

5.6
that
\gamma^{e}_{u}
is a part of some curve
\tilde{\pi}_{u}\in\tilde{\Pi}
with endpoints
\gamma_{u}(0)
and
\gamma_{u}(1)
. The corresponding path
\pi_{u}
of
H
has length at most
c_{\Pi}=O(\alpha^{16})
by
5.10
. Since
\widetilde{G}
was defined by subdividng each edge of
H
at most twice, all distances are at most tripled; consequently the path in
\widetilde{G}
corresponding to the curve
\tilde{\pi}_{u}
has length at most
3c_{\Pi}
. In particular, we get that
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{u},\dot{C}_{u})\leqslant 3c_{\Pi}
. The analogous argument gives
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{v},\dot{C}_{v})\leqslant 3c_{\Pi}
. Since
\gamma^{e}_{u}
contains both
\dot{x}
and
\dot{u}
, and
\gamma^{e}_{u}
was subdivided at most twice in
\widetilde{G}
, we also have
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{u})\leqslant 3
. Applying the same arguments to the symmetric terms in (
3
), we obtain

\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{y})\leqslant 6+6c_{\Pi}+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{C}_{u},\dot{C}_{v}).

Next, we bound
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{C}_{u},\dot{C}_{v})
. Since
\widetilde{G}
is obtained by subdividing each edge of
H
at most twice, we have

\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{C}_{u},\dot{C}_{v})\leqslant 3\mathrm{dist}_{H}(\dot{C}_{u},\dot{C}_{v})\leqslant 3c_{H}\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(C_{u},C_{v}),

where
c_{H}
is the Lipschitz-constant of the mapping from
G_{\mathcal{P}}
to
H
that is guaranteed in
Theorem

5.6
.

Recall that
u\in C_{u}
and
v\in C_{v}
. We also have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{u})\leqslant 3
which implies that
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,u)\leqslant 3
; analogously,
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(y,v)\leqslant 3
. Since
x
and
y
are adjacent in
G
, we have that

\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(C_{u},C_{v})\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,x)+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(y,v)\leqslant 7.

Putting it all together, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{x},\dot{y})

\displaystyle\leqslant 6+6c_{\Pi}+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}}(\dot{C}_{u},\dot{C}_{v})

\displaystyle\leqslant 6+6c_{\Pi}+3c_{H}\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(C_{u},C_{v})

\displaystyle\leqslant 6+6c_{\Pi}+21c_{H}

\displaystyle=O(1),

which concludes the proof.
∎

6
Contraction decompositions for Lipschitz-mapped graphs

In order to create our algorithms, we will need to use Baker’s
technique
[
9
]
to be able to work on a bounded-treewidth graph.
Our starting point is the following
contraction decomposition
from
[
51
]
.

See
2.2

Based on the above theorem and our machinery for planarizing intersection graphs via Lipschitz embeddings, we get a contraction decompostion for intersection graphs. However, we need the following key lemma first, which allows us to compare the treewidth of a contraction of a Lipschitz-mapping of
H
of
G
to the treewidth of some (larger) contraction of
G
.

and a vertex set

, that is,

We claim that if
a,b\in V
are contracted to the same vertex
\breve{x}\in\breve{X}
, then they are also contracted to the same vertex
\breve{x}^{c^{2}}\in\breve{X}^{c^{2}}

a

H
,

a

in
H[X]
. By the Lipschitz property,
each edge
uv
of this path can be represented by a path
P_{uv}
of length

c
in
G
. The vertices of
P_{uv}
are in
N_{G}(\{u,v\},c/2)
, so by

N_{H}(\{u,v\},c^{2}/2)\subset X^{c^{2}}
and
thus the concatenation of the paths
P_{uv}
forms a connected subgraph of
H[X^{c^{2}}]
that contains both
u
and
v
. This concludes the proof of the
claim. For a given
\breve{x}\in\breve{X}
, let
\phi(\breve{x})
be the
corresponding contracted vertex in
\breve{X}^{c^{2}}
. The previous claim implies
that
\phi:\breve{X}\rightarrow\breve{X}^{c^{2}}
is well-defined and surjective.

of width
w
. Using this

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X^{c^{2}}
. Consequently,
v\in\psi_{G}(N_{H}(u^{\prime},c))
and the edge is represented in any bag
B_{G}
where the bag
B_{H}

contained
u^{\prime}
.

Finally, we need to show that the set of bags that contain a given vertex

v\in V(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X^{c^{2}})
form a connected subtree of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
Suppose first that
v\in V\setminus X^{c^{2}}
. Then
v
appears in a bag

B_{G}
if and only if the corresponding bag
B_{H}
contains some vertex of
N_{H}(v,c)\setminus X
. Note that since
v\not\in X^{c^{2}}
, we have that
N_{H}(v,c)
is disjoint from
X
, so the condition simplifies to
B_{H}
containing
some vertex of
N_{H}(v,c)
. By
3.1
, the
vertices of
\psi_{H}(N_{H}(v,c))
appear in a connected subtree of

\mathcal{T}
as they induce a connected subgraph of
H\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X
, thus the same holds for the set of bags where
v
appears in.

Suppose now that
v\in\breve{X}^{c^{2}}
. Let
Y
be the set of vertices that are
contracted to
v
by the contraction
G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X^{c^{2}}
. Clearly
G[Y]
is
connected, so by the Lipschitz property, we have that the connected
components of
H[Y]
can be connected by length
c
paths, hence,
H[N_{H}(Y,c)]
is connected. By
3.1
we have that the
vertices
\psi_{H}(N_{H}(Y,c))
appear in a connected subtree of
\mathcal{T}
as they induce a connected graph. Notice however that
v
is added to a bag
B_{G}
if and
only if some vertex of
\psi_{H}(N_{H}(Y,c))
appears in the corresponding bag

B_{H}
. Consequently, the set of vertices containing
v
also form a connected
subtree in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
∎

We are now ready to state our contraction decomposition theorem.

Theorem 6.1

(Contraction Decomposition for Intersection Graphs)
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of similarly sized fat objects with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
where each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
has size at most
|C|\leqslant\ell
.
Then for any
k
there is a collection of sets
\mathcal{X}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{X}_{k}\subset\mathcal{P}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(|\mathcal{P}|)
and the corresponding vertex sets
X_{i}:=\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{X}_{i}}C
satisfy the following bounds about treewidth and
\mathcal{P}
-flattened treewidth.

(i)

\mathrm{tw}(G_{\mathcal{P}}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{X}_{i})=O(k)

(ii)

\mathrm{tw}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i})=O(k\ell)

(iii)

\mathrm{tw}_{\mathcal{P}\mathord{\hbox to3.85pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.175pt\lower-0.175pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.23 5.81 M 4.2 5.81 C 4.2 6.35 3.76 6.78 3.23 6.78 C 2.69 6.78 2.26 6.35 2.26 5.81 C 2.26 5.28 2.69 4.84 3.23 4.84 C 3.76 4.84 4.2 5.28 4.2 5.81 Z M 3.23 5.81}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i}}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i})=O(k\log(\ell+1))
, where
\mathcal{P}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i}
is the partition of
V(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X_{i})
containing the cliques of
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{i}
, and the contracted vertices are added as singletons.

Proof.

Apply
Theorem

5.2
to construct a plane graph
H
with
the same vertex set as
G_{\mathcal{P}}
(namely,
V(H)=V(G_{\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{P}
) such that
the identity map is
c
-Lipschitz from
G_{\mathcal{P}}
to
H
. We apply

Theorem

2.2
on
H
with
k
classes. We get a
partition
E_{1},\dots,E_{k}
of
E(H)
where
\mathrm{tw}(H/E_{i})=O(k)
. For each

E_{i}
let
\mathcal{X}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
denote the vertex set in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
that is
within
H
-distance
c^{2}
from
V(E_{i})
, that is, set
\mathcal{X}_{i}=N_{H}(V(E_{i}),c^{2})
. Let
X_{i}
be the set of vertices in
V(G)
that appear in the
cliques
\mathcal{X}_{i}
. Since
H
has constant-bounded degree
\Delta_{H}
, each

c^{2}
-neighborhood in
H
has constant size, thus

|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(|E_{i}|\cdot\Delta_{H}^{c^{2}+1})=O(|E_{i}|).

(5)

Consequently,

\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|E_{i}|)=O(E(H))=O(V(H))=O(|\mathcal{P}|).

By
Lemma

2.5
each of

, the maximum clique size is obtained on some clique of

-flattened treewidth of

can be bounded as
\mathrm{tw}_{\mathcal{P}}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
