Remark 6.2
.

The bounds (
4
) and (
5
) are the only reason why our running time is doubly (or triply, in case of the slower Steiner tree algorithm) exponential as a function of
\alpha
. To get single-exponential dependence on
\alpha
, one can observe that the size of a
c
-neighborhood in
H
is only quadratic in
c
, as all the graphs
H
where
Lemma

2.5
is applied as well as the graph
H
in
Theorem

6.1
are not only of degree at most
c
, but have further porperties: their vertices are grid points and its edges have length bounded by some polynomial of
\alpha
. Thus the size of the
c
-neighborhood of a vertex of
H
is
\mathrm{poly}(\alpha)c^{2}
.

7
Sparsifying cliques

The goal of this section is to simplify the underlying graph so that each
clique has
f(1/\varepsilon)
vertices, which will be required for our
algorithms. The simplification is done in several steps, and uses some
problem-specific arguments.

7.1
Sparsifying cliques for
Subset
TSP

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
and let

T\subset V(G)
be a set of terminals. Then there is an optimum subset
TSP walk
W
for
T
that uses
O(1)
non-terminal vertices from each
cell
C\in\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,
|(V(W)\setminus T)\cap C|=O(1)
for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
.
Moreover, all vertices are used
O(1)
times by the walk, and all but
O(1)
vertices of
T\cap C\cap V(W)
are incident only to edges induced by
C
.

Proof.

We start by simplifying the tour so that no pair of neighboring cells
5
5

5

The cells
C
and
C^{\prime}
are neighboring if they are neighbors in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, that is, they may not be “grid-neighbors”.
has more
than two edges of
W
going between them. Let
C,C^{\prime}
be two cells such that
there are at least three edges of
W
going between them. We pick an
orientation on
W
to make it a directed closed walk. As a result, there are at
least two arcs of
W
going in the same direction between
C
and
C^{\prime}
;
suppose without loss of generality that
uu^{\prime}
and
vv^{\prime}
are edges of
W
that
both go from
C
to
C^{\prime}
. We can then replace the edges
uu^{\prime}
and
vv^{\prime}
in

W
with the edges
uv
and
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
(and reverse the direction of the
u^{\prime}
to

v
subpath of
W
) to get a closed walk
W^{\prime}
of the same length that has fewer inter-cell steps. Repeated applications of this step result in a tour

W
that has at most two edges going between any pair of neighboring cells.

If a vertex
x\in C\setminus T
has all incident edges ending in
C
, then

x
could be bypassed and we would get a shorter walk spanning all the
terminals; therefore, each vertex of
C\setminus T
must be incident to at
least one edge that goes to a different cell. Since there are only
O(1)
cells that a given vertex can be connected to, and at most two edges in

W
lead to each of these cells, there are only
O(1)
non-terminals in
C

that are on
W
. The last claim also follows.
∎

Next, we remove some vertices from the terminal set
T
(but not from the
graph) so that in the new instance each cell has only a few terminals from
the new terminal set.

Lemma 7.2
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and an instance
(G,T)
of
Subset
TSP, we can construct a vertex set

X\subset T
to be removed from the set of terminals in
\mathrm{poly}(n)
time such
that the following hold for
X
and the new terminal set
T^{\prime}=T\setminus X
.

(i)

Each
C\in\mathcal{P}
contains only a few terminals:

|C\cap T^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon

(ii)

If
(G,T)
has a subset TSP of length
\ell
then
(G,T^{\prime})
has
a subset TSP of length
\big(1+O(\varepsilon)\big)\ell-|X|
.

(iii)

Given a subset TSP of
(G,T^{\prime})
of length
\ell^{\prime}
, a subset TSP
of
(G,T)
of length
\ell^{\prime}+|X|
can be constructed in
O(n^{2})

time.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a cell partition of
G
. In each clique
C
, we select up to

1/\varepsilon
arbitrary terminals per clique to be put into
T^{\prime}
; in cliques with
at most
1/\varepsilon
terminals, all terminals will be put into
T^{\prime}
. We let

X=T\setminus T^{\prime}
bet he set of terminals to be deleted. Clearly this
satisfies (
i
).

To prove (
ii
), let
W
be a subset TSP walk of
(G,T)
of length
\ell
that is
simplified with respect to
\mathcal{P}
in the sense of

Lemma

7.1
. Consequently, there are
O(1)
edges of

W
leaving each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
; let us call such edges
external
, and let

W_{ext}
denote their multiset. Edges of
W
whose endpoints are in the same
clique of
\mathcal{P}
are called
internal
, and their multiset is denoted by

W_{int}
. Note that if a vertex of
W
is not incident to any external
edges, then it must be a terminal and it is visited only once in
W
. Indeed,
a non-terminal that is incident to only internal edges can be removed from

W
, and multiple visitations of a terminal with internal edges can also be
simplified to one visitation. Since we have
O(1)
external edges incident to
any clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
, we have that there are at most
|C\cap T|
internal
edges incident to
C
.

We say that
C
is
light
if
|C\cap T|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
, and otherwise we
say that
C
is
heavy
.
We can now construct a subset TSP walk
W^{\prime}
for the instance
(G,T^{\prime})
as follows:
we follow the walk
W
, and if in some heavy clique
C
we have that both the arc entering and exiting
v\in X\cap V(W)\cap C
is internal, then we skip
v
. Note that the skipping is possible since the predecessor and successor of
v
are connected in
C
.

|W^{\prime}|=|W|-\sum_{C\text{ heavy}}(|X\cap C|-O(1))=|W|-|X|+O(\varepsilon|T|)=\big(1+O(\varepsilon)\big)\ell-|X|.

To prove (
iii
), let
W^{\prime}
be an optimum tour for the instance
(G,T^{\prime})
that is
simplified using
Lemma

7.1
. We construct a tour

W
. First, let
W_{ext}=W^{\prime}_{ext}
. To construct the internal edges of
W
,
consider a clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
. If
C
is light, then we use the same internal
edges from
G[C]
for
W^{\prime}
as we did in
W
. If
C
is heavy, then notice
that
W^{\prime}
must have an internal edge in
C
. Indeed,
C
has
1/\varepsilon

terminals from
T^{\prime}
and there are only
O(1)
edges of
W^{\prime}
leave
C
. Let
(u,v)\in W^{\prime}_{int}[C]
be a an edge of
W^{\prime}
. We replace the edge
(u,v)
with
a
u\rightarrow v
path whose internal vertices are
C\cap X
, which lengthens
W^{\prime}

by
|C\cap X|
. We apply this modification to each clique
C
, and the
resulting tour
W
is a subset TSP tour of all the terminals in
T=T^{\prime}\cup X
, and has length

|W|=|W^{\prime}|+\sum_{C\text{ heavy}}(|C\cap X|)=\ell^{\prime}+|X|.

Finally, notice that both
Lemma

7.1
and the
modification above is algorithmic and can be done in
O(n^{2})
time.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
and terminal set

T
such that
|C\cap T|\leqslant k
for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0

there is a vertex set
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
such that for any
C\in\mathcal{P}
we have

|V^{\prime}\cap C|=O(k^{2}/\varepsilon^{4})
, and for any
u,v\in T
we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G[V^{\prime}]}(u,v)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,v^{\prime})
. Moreover, given
G
and its cell
partition
\mathcal{P}
, the set
V^{\prime}
can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof.

From each
C\in\mathcal{P}
choose a single vertex
a_{C}
to be the
hub
of
C
,
and collect the hubs in the vertex set
A
. Then for any pair of vertices

u,v\in T\cup A
of distance at most
4/\varepsilon+2
we add to
V^{\prime}
all vertices
of a shortest
u\rightarrow v
path.

If a vertex
w\in C
is added to
A
, then it is either a hub, a terminal, or
it is an internal vertex of a shortest path connecting some
u,v\in T\cup A

with
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant 4/\varepsilon+2
. Consequently,
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(w,u)
and
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(w,v)
are both less than
4/\varepsilon+2
. The number of vertices in
T\cup A
at
distance at most
4/\varepsilon+2
from
w
is at most
O(k/\varepsilon^{2})
, therefore the
number of such shortest paths is
O(k^{2}/\varepsilon^{4})
, and thus
|V^{\prime}\cap C|=O(k^{2}/\varepsilon^{4})
.

To show the second property, let
u,v\in T
be vertices whose distance is

\ell\geqslant 4/\varepsilon
. Let
u=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{k}=v
be a sequence of vertices on
a shortest
u\rightarrow v
path such that
k=\lceil\ell\varepsilon/4\rceil
and
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u_{i},u_{i+1})\leqslant 4/\varepsilon
. Let
a_{i}
be the hub of the cell
C_{i}\in\mathcal{P}

which contains
u_{i}
. Since
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u_{i},u_{i+1})\leqslant 4/\varepsilon
, we have that

\mathrm{dist}_{G}(a_{i},a_{i+1})\leqslant 4/\varepsilon+2
, therefore
V^{\prime}
contains a shortest path
from
a_{i},a_{i+1}
. Concatenating these paths we get a
u\rightarrow v
path whose
internal vertices are in
V^{\prime}
and has length at most
\ell+2k=\ell+2\lceil\ell\varepsilon/4\rceil<\ell+2\cdot(\ell\varepsilon/2)=(1+\varepsilon)\ell
.

Finally, notice that the above construction can be repeated using a
polynomial algorithm.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and an instance
(G,T)
of
Subset
TSP with a fixed cell partition

\mathcal{P}
of
G
, in polynomial time we can construct an instance
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
that
satisfies the following conditions.

•

V(G^{\prime})\subseteq V(G)
,
T^{\prime}\subseteq T

•

Each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
satisfies

|C\cap V(G^{\prime})|\leqslant O(1/\varepsilon^{6})
and
|C\cap T^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
.

•

The shortest subset TSP
tour of
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is within a
1+O(\varepsilon)
factor from the optimum of
(G,T)
, and given a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
we can in
polynomial time create a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour for
(G,T)
.

Proof.

Fix a cell partition
\mathcal{P}
of
G
. By
Lemma

7.2
we
can create a terminal set
T^{\prime}
where for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
the number of
terminals is at most
|C\cap T^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
, and the length of the optimum
tour increases by at most a
1+O(\varepsilon)
factor. We can then apply
Lemma

7.3
on
(G,T^{\prime})
with
k=1/\varepsilon
to get a graph
G^{\prime}
where each
clique
C^{\prime}
has size at most
|C^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon^{6}
. Note that any subset TSP walk

W
can be regarded as a sequence of shortest paths between its consecutive
terminal visits, so by
Lemma

7.3
the shortest subset TSP
tour of
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is within a
1+O(\varepsilon)
factor from the optimum of
(G,T)
, and given a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
we can in
polynomial time create a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour for
(G,T)
.
∎

7.2
Sparsifying cliques for
Steiner Tree

The next two lemmas are similar to those that we have seen for
Subset
TSP.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
and let
T\subseteq V(G)
be a set of terminals. Then given any Steiner tree
H_{0}
for
(G,T)
we can construct in polynomial time a Steiner tree
H
for
(G,T)
where
V(H)\subset V(H_{0})
, and

H
uses
O(1)
non-terminal vertices from each cell
C\in\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,
|(V(H)\setminus T)\cap C|=O(1)
for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
. Moreover, there are at most
O(1)
vertices of
T\cap C\cap V(H)
that are incident to some edge of
H
that has an endpoint outside
C
.

Proof.

We start by simplifying
H_{0}
into a tree
H_{1}
so that no pair of neighboring cells has more than
one edge of
H_{1}
going between them. Let
C,C^{\prime}
be two cells such that there
are at least two edges of
H_{0}
going between them, let
(u,u^{\prime})
and
(v,v^{\prime})
be these edges (where
u,v
and
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
are not necessarily
distinct). We remove the edge
(u,u^{\prime})
from
H_{0}
, and as a result the tree falls
apart into two components, one of which contains the edge
(v,v^{\prime})
. Without
loss of generality,
u
and
v
are in different components. Therefore we can
add the edge
(u,v)
to form a tree of the same length, but which has fewer
inter-clique edges.

By applying the above modification exhaustively, we get a a tree
H_{1}
where any
pair of neighboring cells have at most one edge going between them.
If a vertex
x\in C\setminus T
has all its incident edges in
H_{1}
ending in
C
, then
we bypass
x
by adding a path connecting its neighbors, and remove
x
from
H_{1}
.
Once no more such modifications are possible, the resulting tree
H
has the property that each vertex of
H
in
C\setminus T
must be incident to at
least one edge that goes to a different cell. Since there are only
O(1)
cells that a given vertex can be connected to, and at most one edge in

H
lead to each of these cells, there are only
O(1)
non-terminals in
C

that are on
H
. Thus
H
has the desired properties and it was obtained in polynomial time.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and an instance
(G,T)
of
Steiner Tree
, we can construct a
vertex set
X\subset T
to be removed from the set of terminals in

\mathrm{poly}(n)
time such that the following hold for
X
and the new terminal set

T^{\prime}=T\setminus X
.

(i)

Each
C\in\mathcal{P}
contains only a few terminals:

|C\cap T^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon

(ii)

If
(G,T)
has a Steiner tree of length
\ell
then
(G,T^{\prime})
has
a Steiner tree of length
\big(1+O(\varepsilon)\big)\ell-|X|
.

(iii)

Given a Steiner tree of
(G,T^{\prime})
of length
\ell^{\prime}
, a Steiner
tree of
(G,T)
of length
\ell^{\prime}+|X|
can be constructed in

O(n^{2})
time.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a cell partition of
G
. In each clique
C
, we select up to

1/\varepsilon
arbitrary terminals per clique to be put into
T^{\prime}
; in cliques with
at most
1/\varepsilon
terminals, all terminals will be put into
T^{\prime}
. We let

X=T\setminus T^{\prime}
be the set of terminals to be deleted. Clearly this
satisfies (
i
).

To prove (
ii
), let
H
be an optimum Steiner tree of
(G,T)
that is
simplified with respect to
\mathcal{P}
in the sense of

Lemma

7.5
. As in

Lemma

7.2
, edges of
H
leaving a clique
C\in\mathcal{P}

are external and denoted by
H_{ext}
, and the rest of the edges are
H_{int}
. Since there are at most
O(1)
external edges incident to
C
, and
each non-terminal in
C
must be incident to at least one such edge, we have
that there are at most
|C\cap T|+O(1)
internal edges incident to
C
.

We can now construct a Steiner tree
H^{\prime}
for the instance
(G,T^{\prime})
. To
construct the internal edges of
H^{\prime}
, let
C
be some clique. We say that
C

is
light
if
|C\cap T|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
, and otherwise we say that
C
is
heavy
.
To construct a shorter tree, in each heavy clique
C
we remove from
H
any
v\in X\cap C
that is only incident to internal edges. (We can connect the neighbors of
v
inside the clique with a star after the removal.) Since there are only
O(1)
external edges incident to
C
, this removes at least
|X\cap C|-O(1)
vertices. Thus

|H^{\prime}|=|H|-\sum_{C\text{ heavy}}(|X\cap C|-O(1))=|H|-|X|+O(\varepsilon|T|)=\big(1+O(\varepsilon)\big)\ell-|X|.

To prove (
iii
), let
H^{\prime}
be an optimum tree for the instance
(G,T^{\prime})
. We can
then construct the desired tree
H
by simply connecting each terminal of
X

to some terminal of
T^{\prime}
in the same clique.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
and terminal set

T
such that
|C\cap T|\leqslant k
for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0

there is a vertex set
V^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
such that for any
C\in\mathcal{P}
we have

|V^{\prime}\cap C|=2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(k/\varepsilon))}
, and for any
T^{\prime}\subset T
we
have
|\mathrm{StTree}(G[V^{\prime}],T^{\prime})|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)|\mathrm{StTree}(G,T^{\prime})|
. The set
V^{\prime}
can be computed
in
2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(k/\varepsilon))}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time.

Proof.

Similarly to
Lemma

7.3
, we define a hub vertex
a_{C}
in each
clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
, and collect the hubs in the set
A
. Next, for each subset

T^{\prime}
of
T\cup A
we want to find a minimum Steiner tree for the terminal
set
T^{\prime}
, and take the union of the vertices of
\mathrm{StTree}(G,T^{\prime})
if

|\mathrm{StTree}(G,T^{\prime})|\leqslant 2/\varepsilon
. More precisely, we set

V^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{\prime}\subseteq A\cup T\\
|\mathrm{StTree}(G,T^{\prime})|\leqslant 2/\varepsilon\end{subarray}}V(\mathrm{StTree}(G,T^{\prime})).

First, we show that
V^{\prime}
satisfies the desired properties. Let
H
be an
optimum Steiner tree for
(G,T)
. We can partiton
H
into
k
edge-disjoint subtrees

H_{1},\dots,H_{k}
, each of size between
t\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}{=}}\lfloor 1/(2\varepsilon)\rfloor
and

2t
. To see this, root
T
at some arbitrary vertex
r
, and let
v
be the
lowest vertex (i.e., most distant from
r
) whose subtree has at least

t
vertices. We order the children of
v
in decreasing order of their
subtree sizes, and take the shortest prefix of the children that together
with
v
induce a subtree of size at least
t
. Note that this subtree
cannot exceed
2t
edges, since that would mean that the subtree of the first
child and
v
would induce a subtree of size at least
t
. We say that a
vertex of
H
is chopped if there are at least two distinct trees
H_{i}

incident to it.

Note that the number of trees
k
is between
|H|/t
and
|H|/2t
, thus

k=\Theta(\varepsilon|H|)
. For each tree
H_{i}
let
\mathcal{P}_{i}
be the set of cliques
where
H_{i}
has a chopped vertex. The terminal set

T_{i}
is defined as the terminals
T\cap V(H_{i})
together with the hubs in the
cliques of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, that is, let

T_{i}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny def}}}{=}}(T\cap V(H_{i}))\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{P}_{i}}a_{C}.

Note that since
|\mathcal{P}_{i}|\leqslant|V(H)|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
, we have

|T_{i}|\leqslant 2/\varepsilon
, therefore
V^{\prime}
induces a minimum Steiner tree of
T_{i}
.
Consequently,
|\mathrm{StTree}(G[V^{\prime}],T_{i})|=\mathrm{StTree}(G,T_{i})\leqslant|H_{i}|+|\mathcal{P}_{i}|
.

We claim that the tree
H^{\prime}
defined as the spanning tree of the union of the
trees
\mathrm{StTree}(G[V^{\prime}],T_{i})
is a suitable approximation. To prove this, we can sum
up the inequalities above for all
i
:

|H^{\prime}|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathrm{StTree}(G[V^{\prime}],T_{i})|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}(|H_{i}|+|\mathcal{P}_{i}|)\leqslant|H|+\!\!\sum_{v\text{ chopped}}\!\!\deg_{H}(v)=|H|+k-1\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)|H|,

since the total degree of the chopped vertices is one less than the number

k
of trees that
H
was chopped into, and since each tree has at most

2t\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
edges, we have
k\leqslant\varepsilon|H|
at most the number of trees that
we have chopped into.

In order to compute
V^{\prime}
, we enumerate all sets of
S\subset T\cup A
with

|S|\leqslant 2/\varepsilon
and with diameter at most
2/\varepsilon
(where the diameter is
measured in the metric space induced by
G
). Since a given clique
C\in\mathcal{P}

has
O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
cliques within distance
2/\varepsilon
, we have that there are at
most
O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
cliques from which
S
can be built if its Steiner tree
contains some vertex of
C
. Each clique has at most
k
terminals and one
hub, thus there are at most
((k+1)/\varepsilon^{2})^{O(1/\varepsilon)}=2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(k/\varepsilon))}
sets
S
whose Steiner tree intersects
C
. For each
such set
S
, we use the algorithm of Dreyfus and Wagner
[
53
]
to compute
the minimum spanning tree of
S
in
3^{|S|}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time, and we add the
vertices of the computed tree to
V^{\prime}
. Since there are at most
n

neighborhoods in which we run the enumeration and the Steiner tree
computation, the running time is
2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(k/\varepsilon))}\mathrm{poly}(n)
, as required.
∎

The equivalent of
Lemma

7.4
for Steiner tree follows analgously, using
Lemma

7.6
and
Lemma

7.7
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and an instance
(G,T)
of
Steiner Tree
with a fixed cell partition

\mathcal{P}
of
G
, in
2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time we can construct an instance
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
that satisfies the following conditions.

•

V(G^{\prime})\subseteq V(G)
,
T^{\prime}\subseteq T

•

Each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
satisfies

|C\cap V(G^{\prime})|\leqslant 2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))}
and
|C\cap T^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
.

•

The shortest Steiner tree of
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is within a
1+O(\varepsilon)
factor from the optimum of
(G,T)
, and given a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
we can in polynomial time create a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour for
(G,T)
.

8
A subset spanner for intersection graphs

The main goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 8.1

(Subset spanner)
.

Given
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
with cell partition
\mathcal{P}
, a vertex set
T\subset V(G)
of terminals, and some
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a family
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of size
|\mathcal{S}|=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\mathrm{StTree}(G,T))
such that for any pair of vertices
x,y\in T
we have

\mathrm{dist}_{G[\bigcup\mathcal{S}]}(x,y)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,y)
, and
\mathcal{S}
can be computed in polynomial time.

The first two steps of our proof mirror the subset spanner proof in weighted
planar graphs given by Klein
[
68
]
. In Klein’s construction, the spanner is created in three
phases: first, a single source spanner is created, which only
concerns distances from a single vertex
v
to the vertices of a shortest
path
Q
not containing
v
. Next, a bipartite spanner is defined that tracks
distances from a shortest
uv
path
Q_{2}
to a different
uv
path
Q_{1}

disjoint from
Q_{2}
. Finally, a spanner is made that approximates distances
between any pair of vertices on the outer face of a planar graph. It turns
out that the first two steps work in arbitrary, not necessarily planar graphs as well. More precisely Klein’s proof of the first step (Lemma
8.2
below) does use planarity, but it can be avoided, while the proof of the second step (Lemma
2.1
) can be used verbatim for nonplanar graphs.

Lemma 8.2

(Single source spanner)
.

Let
G
be a graph, and let
Q
be a shortest path in
G
between its
endpoints. Then for
any
\varepsilon>0
there is a subgraph
H
with
|H|=O(1/\varepsilon^{2})|\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,Q)|
such that for
any
x\in V(Q)
we have
\mathrm{dist}_{H\cup Q}(v,x)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x)
. Moreover,
H
can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof.

Let
R
be a shortest path from a vertex
v
to
Q
.
Let
x_{0}
be the endpoint of
R
on
Q
, and first we consider the portion of

Q
that follows
x_{0}
. Let
Q^{\prime}
be the portion of
Q
whose vertices are at
distance at most
(1/\varepsilon)|R|
distance from
x_{0}
. Starting at
x_{0}
we
define a sequence of vertices
x_{1},\dots
on
Q^{\prime}
the following way. For

i\geqslant 1
, let
x_{i}
be the earliest vertex on
Q^{\prime}
where
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i-1})+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x_{i-1},x_{i})>(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i})
. Analogously, we do the
same procedure moving backwards on
Q^{\prime}
from
x_{0}
, defining the points
x_{-1},x_{-2},\dots
. Let
H
be the union of the shortest paths
v\rightarrow x_{i}
.

Let
x_{k}
be the vertex of largest index assigned by the procedure. We
estimate the total length of paths from
v
to
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
; the paths
whose endpoint has a negative index can be estimated analogously. By summing the inequalities
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i-1})+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x_{i-1},x_{i})>(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i})
defining the
x_{i}
’s, we get

(1+\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,w)<\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{j})+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x_{j},x_{j+1})\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{j})+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x_{0},x_{k})

Substracting
\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i})
from both sides and using that
\mathrm{dist}(x_{0},x_{k})\leqslant(1/\varepsilon)|R|
holds by construction, we get:

\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i})+\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{k})<\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{0})+(1/\varepsilon)|R|=(1+1/\varepsilon)|R|,

which yields
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,x_{i})=O(|R|/\varepsilon^{2})
.
∎

Klein
[
68
]
shows that applying the single source spanner several
times can yield a bipartite spanner.

See
2.1

Perimeters in region-restricted graphs

Let
G
be an intersection graph, and let
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
be a wireframe of
G
that is a cycle, where
\mathsf{R}
is either the bounded or unbounded region defined by
\gamma(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
. Consider now the graph
R:=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
: for each vertex
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
there is a natural corresponding polygon
v_{\mathsf{R}}\in V(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
, namely, the component of
p(\dot{v})\cap\mathsf{R}
that contains the point
\dot{v}
.
Consider the polygon set
\{v_{\mathsf{R}}\mid\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})\}
. Notice that because of the definition of wireframes, whenever
\dot{v}\dot{v}^{\prime}
is an edge of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
, then
v_{\mathsf{R}}v^{\prime}_{\mathsf{R}}
is an edge induced by
\{v_{\mathsf{R}}\mid\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})\}
. Let
\mathrm{Perim}(R)
denote the walk on
\{v_{\mathsf{R}}\mid\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})\}
corresponding to
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
.

Object duplication

Let us discuss a technical issue that can be avoided using a simple trick.
Starting here, we will often use a path
\widetilde{P}
in some wireframe
\widetilde{W}
of
G
to define a path in
G
. This is not straightforward, since
\widetilde{P}
would normally give rise to a
walk
in
G
through the parent relation: indeed, if
\dot{u}\dot{v}
is an edge of
\widetilde{P}
, then
p(\dot{u})p(\dot{v})
is an edge in
G
, but different vertices along
\widetilde{P}
may have the same parent in
G
.
To avoid technicalities involving walks using an object multiple times, we pretend that we have multiple copies of an object available. In all cases where
v\in G
appears
k
times as a parent along the walk corresponding to
\widetilde{P}
, we add
k-1
new copies of the polygon
v
to
G
. This is also done in a restricted intersection graph
R:=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
whose boundary is a wireframe cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
: we think of the objects of
\mathrm{Perim}(R)
as objects of
R
, each of which has been copied as many times as they occur as parents of
V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
-vertices.
Consequently
\mathrm{Perim}(R)
forms a cycle in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
rather than a closed walk. Finally, we remark that adding such objects to
G
does not impact the optimum of
Steiner Tree
(or even
Subset TSP
). To see why, notice that (sub)polygon(s) of an original object
v
that appear in the optimum of the modified instance can be substituted by a single copy of
v
in any feasible solution, whose total size can therefore not increase, while any feasible solution of the original instance is also feasible in the modified instance.

Building a skeleton

Every subpath of a shortest path is a shortest path between its endpoints, but if a path is, say, twice as long as the distance between its endpoints, then a subpath can be still much longer than the distance between the endpoints of the subpath. We will often encounter paths that are approximately short in a sense that extends to subpaths as well:

Definition 8.3
.

A path
P
in a graph
G
from
u
to
v
is called an
internally
c
-approximate shortest path
if for any
a,b\in V(P)
where
a,b
are not both endpoints of
P
6
6

6

We do not include the full path
P
for technical reasons; we will need this to ensure that the southern boundary paths of the faces in the skeleton are internally
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate rather than talking about their subpaths. See
Definition

8.5
and
Sk5
.
, we have
|P[a,b]|\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(a,b)
.

The following lemma relates the graphs obtained by restricting
G
to smaller regions. Essentially, it says in various forms that distances can only increase with further restrictions. It will be used much later.
Recall that for a cycle
W
and
u,v\in V(W)
we denote by
W[u,v]
the shorter subpath of
W
connecting
u
and
v
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}},\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
be wireframe cycles in
G
where
\mathsf{B}\subset\mathsf{R}
, and let
B=G|_{\mathsf{B}}
and
R=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
. Then the following hold.

(i)

Let
u,v\in V(B)
such that
u\in\mathrm{CComp}(\hat{u}\cap\mathsf{B})
and
v\in\mathrm{CComp}(\hat{v}\cap\mathsf{B})
for some
\hat{u},\hat{v}\in V(R)
. Then
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)\geqslant\mathrm{dist}_{R}(\hat{u},\hat{v})
.

(ii)

If
\widetilde{P}\subset\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
is path such that the corresponding path
P_{R}
in
R
is a internally
c
-approximate shortest path for some
c\geqslant 1
, then the path of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
corresponding to
\widetilde{P}
is a internally
c
-approximate shortest path in
B
.

(iii)

If
\widetilde{W}
is a boundaried wireframe of
B
then for any
\dot{u},\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{W})
we have

\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{W}}(\dot{u},\dot{v})\geqslant\mathrm{dist}_{B}(p|_{\mathsf{B}}(\dot{u}),p|_{\mathsf{B}}(\dot{v})).

Moreover, each of the above statements hold when replacing
R
with
G
.

Proof.

(
i
) For
v\in V(B)
let
\hat{v}\in V(R)
denote the unique corresponding object such that
v\in\mathrm{CComp}(v\cap\mathsf{B}
. Note that for any edge
ab
of
B
we have that
\hat{a}\hat{b}
is an edge in
R
. Thus any path in
B
from
u
to
v
has a corresponding walk in
R
of the same length, which concludes the proof.

(
ii
) Let
P_{B}
denote the path on
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
corresponding to
\widetilde{P}
. By definition, we have that for any proper subpath
Q_{B}=P_{B}[u,v]
the corresponding path
Q_{R}=P_{R}[\hat{u},\hat{v}]
satisfies

|Q_{B}|=|Q_{R}|\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{R}(\hat{u},\hat{v})\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v),

where the last inequality used (
i
).

(
iii
) Observe that for any wireframe edge
\dot{a}\dot{b}\in E(\widetilde{W})
the objects
p|_{\mathsf{B}}(\dot{a}),p|_{\mathsf{B}}(\dot{b})
form an edge in
B
. Thus a shortest path in
\widetilde{W}
has a corresponding walk of the same length in
B
connecting the
p|_{B}
-parents of its endpoints.
∎

Our final ingredient in the proof of
Theorem

8.1
is the creation of a wireframe
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
of
G
called the
skeleton
. The skeleton will be created by iteratively adding so-called
shortcuts
to a starting graph.

Definition 8.5

(Shortcut and its reach)
.

A
shortcut
of
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
is a shortest path
P
in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}

between some vertices
\breve{u},\breve{v}\in V(\mathrm{Perim}(R))
, such that
|P|<\frac{|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}[\dot{u},\dot{v}]|}{1+\varepsilon}
. The
reach
of a shortcut
from
\breve{u}
to
\breve{v}
is the length of the shorter subpath of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
from
\dot{u}
to
\dot{v}
, that is,
|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}[\dot{u},\dot{v}]|=|\mathrm{Perim}(R)[\breve{u},\breve{v}]|
.

We need a skeleton with the following properties.

Lemma 8.6

(Skeleton properties)
.

Given
G,T,
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, a wireframe
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
of
G
of complexity
\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})=O(n^{3}/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
can be constructed in polynomial time with the following properties.

Sk1

The graph
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
is
2
-connected.

Sk2

The wireframe
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
has a unique irrelevant face with region
\mathsf{F}_{\textup{irr}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that any object of
V(G)
intersecting
\mathsf{F}_{\textup{irr}}
also intersects
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathsf{F}_{\textup{irr}}
. (Other faces are called
relevant
.)

Sk3

The cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
on the boundary of any relevant face region
\mathsf{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the concatenation of two paths,
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
, with corresponding paths
\mathrm{North}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
and
\mathrm{South}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
in
\mathrm{Perim}(R)
.

Sk4

For each
v\in T
there is at least one point
\dot{v}\in V(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{R}))
with
p(\dot{v})=v
for some relevant face
\mathsf{R}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
.

Sk5

For any relevant face
\mathsf{R}
the paths
\mathrm{North}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
is a shortest path and
\mathrm{South}(G|_{\mathsf{R}})
is an internally
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate shortest path in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
.

Sk6

The total length of the northern and southern paths of all relevant faces is
O(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

Proof.

First we build a wireframe that is a cycle going through every terminal, as follows.
Consider a 2-approximate Steiner tree
W_{0}
of the terminal set
T
in
G
. Such a tree can be computed by finding a minimum spanning tree of
T
in the graph metric
\mathrm{dist}_{G}
in polynomial time. We use
Theorem

4.2
on
W_{0}
with terminal set
T
to get an object frame that is a tree. We have that
\mathrm{compl}(W_{0})=O(\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G)))
.
Use
Lemma

3.8
to obtain the corresponding wireframe
\widetilde{W}_{0}
that is a plane tree of complexity
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{W}_{0})=O(n^{2})\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
.

Figure 12:
(i) The objects of the approximate Steiner tree, terminals are shaded red. In a small neighborhood of the black wireframe
\widetilde{W}_{0}
we create a wireframe cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
. (ii) The unbounded region of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
is sliced into smaller regions with paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. The region bounded by
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
(shaded) is the irrelevant face.

We can create a wireframe cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
that is realized as a closed curve that goes around the plane drawing of
\gamma(\widetilde{W}_{0})
inside an infinitesimally small neighborhood of
\gamma(\widetilde{W}_{0})
; see
Figure

12
(i).
That is, any object of
V(G)
intersecting the bounded region of this curve also intersects the unbounded region. When
\dot{v}\in V(W_{0})
has degree
k
, then the plane cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
has
k
vertices
\dot{v}_{1},\dots\dot{v}_{k}
corresponding to
\dot{v}\in V(W_{0})
placed between consecutive edges in a small neighborhood of
\dot{v}
in
\widetilde{W}_{0}
.
Notice that
|V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1})|\leqslant 2|V(W_{0})|\leqslant 4\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
. We define
\mathsf{R}_{1}
to be the unbounded region defined by the closed curve
\gamma(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1})
, and
F_{\textup{irr}}
is the bounded region; it satisfies Property
Sk2
.
The above operation increases the complexity only by a constant factor compared to that of
\widetilde{W}_{0}
, thus
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1})=O(n^{2})\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
.

Let
G_{1}=G|_{\mathsf{R}_{1}}
be our initial intersection graph and
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
be our initial wireframe constructed above, and we add the vertices of

p(V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}))
to a set
S
, and furthermore we also add
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}
to an auxiliary

skeleton

\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
that is constructed as a wireframe in
G
that we will build along the way. Then we will recursively
define shortcuts
P_{i}
and wireframe cycles
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}
around regions
\mathsf{R}_{i}
. See
Figure

12
(ii) for an illustration.

While there is a shortcut
P_{i}
between two vertices of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}
in
G|_{\mathsf{R}_{i}}
, we find a shortcut with the smallest reach that satisfies
(1+\varepsilon)|P_{i}|<|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}[u,v]|
, where
u
and
v
are the endpoints of
P_{i}
. Note that the vertices of
P_{i}
form a shortest path in
G|_{\mathsf{R}_{i}}
, thus a wireframe path corresponding to
P_{i}
can be created using
Lemma

4.4
on the object set
V(P_{i})
with terminals
u,v
and designated points
\dot{u}\in u
and
\dot{v}\in v
(where
\dot{u},\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i})
); let
\widetilde{P}_{i}
be the wireframe corresponding to this object frame.
Notice that since
P_{i}
is a shortest path, its vertices induce a path in
G|_{\mathsf{R}_{i}}
, and thus there is a one-to-one correspondence between the vertices and edges in
P_{i}
and
\widetilde{P}_{i}
.
Adding
\widetilde{P}_{i}
to
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
generates a new wireframe, where
\mathsf{R}_{i}
is split into two sub-regions by
\gamma(\widetilde{P}_{i})
, where one region
\mathsf{R}_{i+1}\subset\mathsf{R}_{i}
does not contain the edges of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}[\dot{u},\dot{v}]
. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i+1}
be the wireframe cycle given by the concatenation of
\widetilde{P}_{i}
and
E(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i})\setminus E(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}[u,v])
.
By
Lemma

4.4
we have that

\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i+1})\leqslant\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i})+8|V(\mathcal{A}(G))\cap(\inter(\mathsf{R}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{i+1}))|.

(6)

The procedure stops when there are no more shortcuts satisfying
(1+\varepsilon)|P_{i}|<|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}[u,v]|
in
G|_{\mathsf{R}_{i}}
. Let
G|_{\mathsf{R}_{k}}
be this last face, and let
P_{k}
be a shortest path in
G|_{\mathsf{R}_{k}}
connecting two opposite vertices, or if the cycle
\mathrm{Perim}(R_{k})
has odd length, then let
P_{k}
be a shortest path connecting two vertices cutting
\mathrm{Perim}(R_{k})
into paths whose lengths differ by
1
. The corresponding path
\widetilde{P}_{k}
divides
\mathsf{R}_{k}
into two new faces.

Since
(1+\varepsilon)|P_{i}|<|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}[u,v]|
for
i<k
, we get that

|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i+1}|=|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}|-|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}[u,v]|+|P_{i}|<|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}|-\varepsilon|P_{i}|.

Let
P_{k-1}
be the last shortcut added by the procedure. Summing the previous
inequality for all
i\leqslant k-1
gives

\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|P_{i}|<\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i}|-|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i+1}|)=|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}|-|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{k}|.

Since
|P_{k}|<|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{k}|
, we get that
\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{k}|P_{i}|<|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}|
and thus

|\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}|=|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|P_{i}|=O(|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1}|/\varepsilon),

(7)

which proves
Sk6
.

To prove the complexity bound on
\mathcal{A}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})
, notice first that the regions
\mathsf{R}_{i+1}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{i}
are pairwise interior-disjoint and in particular the sets
V(\mathcal{A}(G))\cap(\inter(\mathsf{R}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{i+1}))
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
V(\mathcal{A}(G))
. Thus iterating (
6
) implies

\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{j})\leqslant\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}8|V(\mathcal{A}(G))\cap(\inter(\mathsf{R}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{i+1}))|\leqslant O(n^{2})\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))+8\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))

for all
j=1,\dots,k
. By (
7
) we have that
k=O(n/\varepsilon)
, thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{1})+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{P}_{i})

\displaystyle<\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{i})

\displaystyle<k\cdot O(n^{2})\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))

\displaystyle=O(n^{3}/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G)),

which concludes the proof of the complexity bound.

Let
\mathsf{F}_{i}=\Clo(\mathsf{R}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{i+1})
denote the face of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
created in step
i
of the above iteration, and let
\mathsf{F}_{k}
and
\mathsf{F}_{k+1}
be the final faces obtained by the cutting of
\mathsf{R}_{k}
. For
i\leqslant k-1
define
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{i})=\widetilde{P}_{i}
, and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{i})
to be the path given by the rest of the edges on the face. For the final faces
\mathsf{F}_{k}
and
\mathsf{F}_{k+1}
we define
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{k})=\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{k+1})=\widetilde{P}_{k}
, and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{k})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{k+1})
are the paths formed by the remaining edges on the face
\mathsf{F}_{k}
and
\mathsf{F}_{k+1}
, respectively.

Observe that
Sk3
holds. Let us denote by
\mathrm{South}(F_{i})
and
\mathrm{North}(F_{i})
the paths of
\mathrm{Perim}(G|_{\mathsf{F}_{i}})
corresponding to
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{i})
, respectively.

Since
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
was constructed by repeatedly adding shortcuts to an initial cycle, the constructed graph remains
2
-connected throughout the iteration, and in particular, Property
Sk1
holds. As the initial cycle contained all terminals Property
Sk4
also holds.

Notice that the faces
\mathsf{F}_{1},\dots\mathsf{F}_{k+1}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
are created using a path
P_{i}
that is a shortest path in
G|_{R_{i}}
, consequently, it is also shortest in
G|_{F_{i}}
. Observe that no proper subpath of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{i})
admits a shortcut in
R_{i}
, as in case of
i<k
that would give a shortcut with a shorter reach than what was chosen, or for
i=k
it contradicts the non-existence of a shortcut in
\mathsf{R}_{k}
. Hence

|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{i})[\dot{a},\dot{b}]|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G|_{R_{i}}}(a,b)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G|_{F_{i}}}(a,b)

for any pair
a,b
on
V(\mathrm{South}(F_{i}))
that are not both endpoints of
\mathrm{South}(F_{i})
. This concludes the proof of Property
Sk5
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
be the skeleton for
(G,T)
constructed according to
Lemma

8.6
.
To construct our spanner
\mathcal{S}
, for each vertex
v
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
we add
p(\dot{v})
to the set
S
. Next, we use the following procedure on each relevant face
\mathsf{R}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
. We apply the bipartite spanner construction of
Lemma

2.1
on

V(\mathrm{North}(R))
, and
V(\mathrm{South}(R))
in
R
, and for each vertex
v
of this spanner we add
v^{\mathrm{orig}}
(i.e., the original object of
V(G)
that
v
is a restriction of containing
v
) to
S
.
Once the above procedure is done, the final spanner consists of the cliques of
\mathcal{P}
intersecting
S
or having a vertex within distance
1/\varepsilon
from the parent of some vertex of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
. Formally, we set
\mathcal{S}=(V(S)\cup N_{G}(p(V(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})),1/\varepsilon))_{\mathcal{P}}
. It remains to bound
|\mathcal{S}|
and to show that it has the desired spanner property.

Bounding
|\mathcal{S}|
.

Since for any vertex
v
there are
O(r^{2})
cliques within distance
r

from
v
, we can bound
(N(p(V(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})),1/\varepsilon))_{\mathcal{P}}
by
O(|\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}|/\varepsilon^{2})=O(\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)/\varepsilon^{3})
by (
7
).

To bound
S_{\mathcal{P}}
, we observe that on the cycle corresponding to each face of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
we applied the bipartite
spanner once (except for the irrelevant face). Consequently, the total length of the cycles on which we
have applied the bipartite spanner is at most
2|\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}|
, and thus
by Property
Sk6
we get
|S|=O(|\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}|/\varepsilon^{3})=O(|\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)|/\varepsilon^{4})
.
Therefore
|S_{\mathcal{P}}|=O(|\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)|/\varepsilon^{4})
and
|\mathcal{S}|=O(|\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)|/\varepsilon^{4})
, as required.

The spanning property for
\mathcal{S}
.

Consider a shortest path
Q
in
G
connecting two vertices of
T
. Notice that if
V(Q)\subseteq N_{G}(p(V(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})),1/\varepsilon)
, then clearly
Q
is contained in
G[\bigcup\mathcal{S}]
and the claim follows. Suppose the contrary, that there is some vertex
x\in V(Q)
outside
N_{G}(p(V(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})),1/\varepsilon)
. Notice that
x
must be outside
F_{\textup{irr}}
as all of its vertices are in the
1
-neighborhood of
p(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})
. Let
\mathsf{F}

be the relevant face of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
that contains
x
, and let
F=G|_{\mathsf{F}}
. We find a wireframe
\widetilde{Q}
corresponding to the shortest path
Q
using
Corollary

4.3
, and let
\dot{x}
be the vertex of
\widetilde{Q}
whose parent is
x
. Fix an orientation of
Q
, and let
u\in V(Q)
be the last vertex intersecting
\partial\mathsf{F}
before
Q
arrives in
x
. Let
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Perim}(F)
be some neighbor of
u
(more precisely, some vertex of
N_{B}(u\cap\mathsf{F})
). Such a vertex exists since
\mathrm{Perim}(F)
covers
\partial\mathsf{F}
. Analogously, let
v\in V(Q)
be the first vertex on
Q
after
x
whose object intersects
\partial\mathsf{F}
, and let
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Perim}(F)\cap N_{B}(v\cap\mathsf{F})
.
Observe that
u^{\prime}
is either a neighbor of
u
or
u^{\prime}
is a connected component of
u\cap\mathsf{F}
. The analogous observation holds for
v^{\prime}
and
v
.

It is sufficient to show that we can replace
Q[u,v]
with a path
Q^{\prime}
in

G[\bigcup\mathcal{S}]
such that
|Q^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))|Q[u,v]|
. Since the internal vertices of
Q[u,v]
are inside the skeleton face, they cannot be terminals, thus
Q[u,v]
is a shortest path, and in particular,
|Q[u,v]|=\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)
.
Let
Q^{\prime}=(u,u^{\prime})\cup P\cup(v^{\prime},v)
, where
P
is the shortest

u^{\prime}\rightarrow v^{\prime}
path in
G[\bigcup\mathcal{S}]|_{\mathsf{F}}
.
We claim that
|Q^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+4
. We distinguish three cases.

Case 1.

Both
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
are in
\mathrm{North}(F)
.

Then by
Sk5
we know that
\mathrm{North}(F)
is a shortest path in
F
. Thus the triangle inequality implies

|Q^{\prime}|\leqslant|\mathrm{North}(F)[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]|+2=\mathrm{dist}_{F}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+2\leqslant|Q|+4=\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+4,

where the second inequality uses that the internal vertices of
Q
are inside
F
.

Case 2.

Both
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
are outside
\mathrm{North}(F)
, i.e., they are internal vertices of
\mathrm{South}(F)
.

Consider the path
\bar{Q}:=(u^{\prime},u)\cup Q[u,v]\cup(v,v^{\prime})
. By
Sk5
we have that
\mathrm{South}(F)
is a internally
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate shortest path in
F
, thus
(1+\varepsilon)|\bar{Q}|>|\mathrm{South}(F)[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]|
.
Therefore

|Q^{\prime}|\leqslant|\mathrm{South}(F)[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]|<(1+\varepsilon)|\bar{Q}|=(1+\varepsilon)(\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+2)<(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+4.

Case 3.

Exactly one of
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
lies in
\mathrm{North}(F)
.

By the spanning property of the bipartite spanner, we
have that

|Q^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)(\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+2)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+4.

Recall that
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\geqslant 2/\varepsilon-2
since
x
is on the
shortest path from
u
to
v
and has distance at least
1/\varepsilon
from both

u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
. Since
\varepsilon<0.5
implies
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)>1/\varepsilon
, we get

|Q^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)+4<(1+5\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v).

This concludes the proof.
∎

8.1
Algorithm for Subset TSP

It can be shown that contracting an edge can decrease the optimum value of Subset TSP only by at most 2: a closed walk in the modified instance can be extended to a closed walk of the original graph by traversing the contracted edge at most twice. Thus consolidating a set
X
of vertices decreases the optimum by at most
2|X|
. To obtain more efficient algorithms, we show that contracting a bounded number of cliques has only a bounded effect on the value of the optimum solution.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
T\subset V(G)
be a set of terminals and
X\subset V(G)
an arbitrary
set, and fix a clique partition
\mathcal{P}
of
G
. Then
\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G,T)\leqslant\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}

, and fix a

as claimed. Substituting in (

) now yields the desired bound:

.

because contractions can only decrease the optimum cost of
a subset TSP instance.

By
Lemma

8.7
in polynomial time we can construct tours

\tau_{i}
for
(G^{*},T)
of length at most

\mathrm{SubsetTSP}(G^{*},T)+O(|\mathcal{X}_{i}|)\leqslant\mbox{{\sc opt}}^{*}+O(|\mathcal{X}_{i}|).

Since
Theorem

6.1
guarantees
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(|\mathcal{S}|)
, there exists some
i
where
|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(|\mathcal{S}|/k)=O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}
. As a result, the shortest tour among the tours

\tau_{i}
has length at most
\mbox{{\sc opt}}^{*}+O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}=(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}
.
∎

9
The mortar graph

This section aims to construct a so-called mortar graph for a given intersection graph
G
and terminal set
T
. Mortar graphs were introduced by Borradaile
et al.

[
24
]
for the planar Steiner tree problem. We also only need them for our Steiner tree algorithms. Our construction is much more involved as we need to avoid unwanted crossings between columns.

Definition 9.1

(Mortar graph and bricks)
.

A
2
-connected wireframe
\widetilde{M}
of
G
is a
mortar graph
if
\widetilde{M}
has a unique
irrelevant
face with region
\mathsf{F}_{\textup{irr}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, such that any object of
V(G)
intersecting
\mathsf{F}_{\textup{irr}}
also intersects
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathsf{F}_{\textup{irr}}
. We call all other faces of
\widetilde{M}
(as closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
)
brick regions
, and each brick region
\mathsf{B}
defines a corresponding
brick

B:=G|_{\mathsf{B}}
.

Definition 9.2

(Compass)
.

Given a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
, a
compass
of
\widetilde{M}
is a partition of the boundary cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
for each brick region
\mathsf{B}
into six paths:

\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),

so that these subpaths of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
are in anticlockwise order if
\mathsf{B}
is a bounded brick region and in clockwise order if
\mathsf{B}
is the unique unbounded brick region. We further define

\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}):=\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}):=\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}).

For a mortar graph with a compass and a brick
B
, let

\dot{v}^{B}_{SSE},\dot{v}^{B}_{SE},\dot{v}^{B}_{NE},\dot{v}^{B}_{NW},\dot{v}^{B}_{SW},\dot{v}^{B}_{SSW}

denote the shared vertices among the consecutive paths

\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),

where
\dot{v}^{B}_{SSW}
is shared between
\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
. We set
v^{B}_{SSE}:=p|_{B}(\dot{v}^{B}_{SSE})
to denote the object of
B
corresponding to
\dot{v}^{B}_{SSE}
, and we define the objects
v^{B}_{SE}
,
v^{B}_{NE}
,
v^{B}_{NW}
,
v^{B}_{SW}
, and
v^{B}_{SSW}
analogously.

An important property we will need of the columns is that they behave similarly to shortest paths toward the northern path of their bricks. Let
G
be a graph, and suppose that
P
is a path and
Q
is a path or vertex set that contains exactly one endpoint
y
of
P
. We say that
P
is
c
-approaching

Q
if for any vertex
x
of
P
we have that
|P[x,y]|\leqslant c\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(x,Q)
.

Recall that the object duplication introduced on Page
8
means that we can use the correspondence of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
with
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
to define paths in
B
corresponding to the compass of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
: let

\mathrm{South}(B)
,
\mathrm{East}^{-}(B)
,
\mathrm{East}^{|}(B)
,
\mathrm{North}(B)
,
\mathrm{West}^{|}(B)
,
\mathrm{West}^{-}(B)
, as well as
\mathrm{East}(B)
and
\mathrm{West}(B)
be these paths.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, we set
\kappa=1/\varepsilon^{3}
and
\lambda=1/\varepsilon^{7.5}
. Let
\mathsf{B}
be a brick region with corresponding graph
B
. If
B
has no vertex
v
where
\mathrm{dist}(v,\mathrm{Perim}(B))>\lambda
and for each
v\in V(B)
where
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\mathrm{South}(B))\leqslant\lambda
and
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\mathrm{North}(B))\leqslant\lambda
it holds that
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\{v^{B}_{SE},v^{B}_{SW}\})\leqslant 2\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
, then
\mathsf{B}
and
B
are called
small
, otherwise they are called
large
.
Our goal for the rest of this section is to construct a mortar graph with the following properties.

Theorem 9.3

(Mortar graph properties)
.

Given the intersection graph
G
, a terminal set
T
, and a number
\varepsilon\in(0,0.1)
, we can construct a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
of complexity
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{M})=O(n^{4}/\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
with a compass such that the following properties hold.

M1

For each
v\in T
the mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
contains at least one vertex
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
with
p(\dot{v})=v
for some brick region
\mathsf{B}
of
\widetilde{M}
.

M2

In each brick
B
the path
\mathrm{North}(B)
is a shortest path in
B
, and
\mathrm{West}^{-}(B)\cup\mathrm{South}(B)\cup\mathrm{East}^{-}(B)
is a internally
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate shortest path in
B
.

M3

There is a constant
c
depending only on
\alpha
such that in each brick
B
the path
\mathrm{West}^{|}(B)
and
\mathrm{East}^{|}(B)
are
c
-approaching
\mathrm{North}(B)
. Moreover,
\mathrm{West}^{|}(B)
and
\mathrm{East}^{|}(B)
are internally
c
-approximate shortest paths in
B
.

M4

The total length of the paths
\mathrm{North}(B),\mathrm{South}(B),\mathrm{West}^{-}(B),\mathrm{East}^{-}(B)
for all bricks
B
is
O(1/\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

M5

The total length of the eastern and western paths of all bricks is
\bigcup_{B}|\mathrm{East}(B)\cup\mathrm{West}(B)|=O(\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

M6

For each large brick
B
and for some
k\leqslant\kappa
there is a set of vertices
s_{0},\dots,s_{k}
ordered from west to east on
\mathrm{South}(B)
such that for any vertex
x
of
\mathrm{South}(B)[s_{i},s_{i+1})
we have
|\mathrm{South}(B)[s_{i},x]|\leqslant\varepsilon\mathrm{dist}_{B}(x,\mathrm{North}(B))
.

Given
G,T
, and
\varepsilon
, such a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
and its compass can be computed in polynomial time.

We start proving
Theorem

9.3
by invoking
Lemma

8.6
to construct a skeleton
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
. Consider now a relevant face of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
with region
\mathsf{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
be the cycle of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
bounding this face, consisting of north and south boundary paths
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
, respectively. We will define so-called
bridges
and
columns
on the graph
R:=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
to slice the region
\mathsf{R}
into smaller brick regions. Let
\mathrm{North}(R)
denote the walk in
R
corresponding to
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
, that is,
\mathrm{North}(R)
is the subpath of
\mathrm{Perim}(R)
that corresponds to
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
. We define the path
\mathrm{South}(R)
analogously based on
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
. Recall that the northern paths are always shortest paths in
R
by
Sk5
.

9.1
Defining bridges

A shortest path of
R
is a
bridge
if it is a shortest path of length at most
7
connecting
\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}))
to
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}))
, that is, a
shortest path in
R
with vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
where
k\leqslant 8
,
v_{1}
intersects
\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}))
, and
v_{k}
intersects
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}))
. We note that the parents of the endpoints of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
are
considered to be length-
0
bridges. Consider a bridge
P
. Since it intersects the northern and southern boundary of
\mathsf{R}
, its first vertex must also
intersect some
u\in V(\mathrm{North}(R))
and its last vertex must intersect some
u^{\prime}\in V(\mathrm{South}(R))
(where
v_{1}=u
and
v_{k}=u^{\prime}
are possible), creating a path of length at most
9
connecting
u
and
u^{\prime}
. Let
\dot{u},\dot{u}^{\prime}
be the vertices of

\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
such that
p(\dot{u})=u
and
p(\dot{u}^{\prime})=u^{\prime}
. We invoke
Corollary

4.3
to make a shortest path connecting
u
and
u^{\prime}
as terminals such that the corresponding objects of the object frame contain
\dot{u}
and
\dot{u}^{\prime}
. The corresponding wireframe is a
bridge path
connecting

\dot{u}
and
\dot{u}^{\prime}
, and it splits the region
\mathsf{R}
into two parts: a western and eastern subregion. (Note that the wireframe
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}
could now be extended with the edges and new vertices of the bridge path.)

Next, we define a greedy collection of bridges as follows. Consider the vertices of
\mathrm{South}(R)
in a west-to-east order, denoting its first and last vertex by
v
and
w
, and let
P_{1}
be the trivial bridge made up by
v
. Suppose that
P_{i}
is a bridge already defined, then let
P_{i+1}
be a bridge whose starting point is the first on
\mathrm{South}(R)[v,w]
whose objects are disjoint from the objects of
P_{i}
. This defines some greedy collection of pairwise disjoint bridges indexed according to their west-to-east order with the property that if
u\in\mathrm{South}(R)
is between the starting points of
P_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
, then any bridge starting at
u
has an object intersecting either some object of
P_{i}
or some object of
P_{i+1}
. For each of these bridges let us fix corresponding bridge paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
, which subdivides
\mathsf{R}
into a sequence of smaller regions. In such a smaller region
R^{\prime}
we define
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime})
to be the parts of
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}^{\prime}
that fall on
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
and
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
, respectively.

A vertex
v
of
R^{\prime}=G|_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}}
is called
boundary-distant
if
\mathrm{dist}_{R^{\prime}}(v,V(\mathrm{South}(R^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{North}(R^{\prime}))>\lambda
. (Recall that
\lambda=1/\varepsilon^{7.5}
). Consider now two consecutive small regions
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{R}^{\prime\prime}
divided by some bridge path
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. If neither of
R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime\prime}
have a boundary-distant vertex, then we remove the bridge path
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. We claim that the resulting region
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{R}^{\prime\prime}
still does not contain a boundary-distant vertex. First, if a vertex
v\in V(R^{\prime})
disjoint from
\gamma(\widetilde{P}_{i})
has distance
k_{v}
to
V(\mathrm{South}(R^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{North}(R^{\prime})
in
R^{\prime}
, then its distance to the same set of vertices is not larger in
R^{\prime\prime\prime}:=G|_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{R}^{\prime\prime}}
, and notice that
V(\mathrm{South}(R^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{North}(R^{\prime})\subset V(\mathrm{South}(R^{\prime\prime\prime})\cup\mathrm{North}(R^{\prime\prime\prime})
. The analogous property holds for any
v\in V(R^{\prime\prime})
disjoint from
\gamma(\widetilde{P}_{i})
. Thus if
R^{\prime\prime\prime}
has a boundary-distant vertex
v
, then it must intersect
\gamma(\widetilde{P}_{i})
. This however implies that
v\in N_{R^{\prime\prime\prime}}p(\widetilde{P}_{i})
and thus
v
is within distance
10
from the starting vertex of
P_{i}
in
R^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Since
\lambda>10
, we conclude that
R^{\prime\prime\prime}
cannot have a boundary-distant vertex.

We continue eliminating bridges between consecutive regions lacking a boundary-distant vertex. As a result, we have a collection of regions
\mathsf{L}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{L}_{t}
indexed in west-to-east order such that the following properties hold.

Bdg1

If
L_{i}=G|_{\mathsf{L}_{i}}
has a boundary-distant vertex, then any bridge in
L_{i}
intersects some object of the eastern or western bridge of
R_{i}
.

Bdg2

For any
i\in[t-1]
at least one among
L_{i}
and
L_{i+1}
contains a boundary-distant vertex.

Lemma 9.4
.

If
\mathsf{R}
is decomposed with
t-1
bridges into sub-regions
\mathsf{L}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{L}_{t}
as described above, then the total length of the bridges separating them is
O(t)=O\left(\frac{|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}|}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|)
.

Proof.

Let us denote by
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
the portion of
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i}
that falls on
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
. We define
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
analogously. Let
L_{i}=G|_{\mathsf{L}_{i}}
, and the paths of
\mathrm{Perim}(L_{i})
corresponding to
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
are denoted by
\mathrm{North}(L_{i})
and
\mathrm{South}(L_{i})
, respectively.

If there are no boundary-distant vertices in
\mathsf{R}
, then
t=1
and the claim holds; suppose now that this is not the case.
Notice that there are at least
\lfloor t/2\rfloor
regions among
\mathsf{L}_{i}
that have boundary-distant vertices. We claim that each region
\mathsf{L}_{i}
with a boundary-distant vertex has two points on its boundary of (Euclidean) distance at least
\Omega(\sqrt{\lambda})
. Indeed, if
v
is boundary-distant in
L_{i}
, then let
P
be a shortest path from
v
to
V(\mathrm{North}(L_{i})\cup\mathrm{South}(L_{i}))
. Then the vertices that are at an odd integer distance from
v
among the internal vertices of
P
are pairwise disjoint, thus by fatness they must cover an area of at least
\Omega(\lambda)
. All of these objects are contained in the bounded area defined by the curve
\partial\mathsf{L}_{i}
, thus by the isodiametric inequality of the Euclidean plane we have that
\mathsf{L}_{i}
has diameter at least
\Omega(\sqrt{\lambda})
, as claimed.

Recall that objects of
\mathrm{Perim}(L_{i})
are subpolygons of objects of
V(G)
, thus each object has diameter at most
2\alpha=O(1)
. Moreover, the bridges on the east and west of
L_{i}
contribute at most
O(1)
edges to
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i}
, so the diameter of a bridge path is at most
\beta=O(\alpha)=O(1)
.
Since the vertices of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
correspond to disjoint objects of
\mathrm{Perim}(L_{i})
, each of diameter at most
2\alpha
, and by the definition of wireframes these objects cover
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})
, we have that there must be at least
\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\partial L_{i})-2\beta}{2\alpha}=\Omega(\sqrt{\lambda})
vertices on
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})\cup\mathrm{South}(wf(L_{i}))
. Let
I_{bdv}
denote the set of indices
i
where
\mathsf{L}_{i}
has a boundary-distant vertex. We have that
|I_{bdv}|\geqslant\lfloor t/2\rfloor
. The total bridge length is
O(t)
, while
|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}|
can be bounded as follows:

|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}|=|\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|>\sum_{i\in I_{bdv}}|\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{i})|=\lfloor t/2\rfloor\cdot\Omega(\sqrt{\lambda}).

Thus we have
O(t)=O\left(\frac{|E(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)
.

Recall that
|E(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|=|\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|+|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|
and denoting
R=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
we have
|\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|=|\mathrm{North}(R)|
and
|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|=|\mathrm{South}(R)|
by definition. Moreover,
\mathrm{North}(R)
is a shortest path in
R
by
Sk5
, while
\mathrm{South}(R)
is a path between the same endpoints in
R
. Therefore
|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|\geqslant|\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|
, and consequently
E(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|)\geqslant|E(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|/2
. Now substituting
\lambda=1/\varepsilon^{7.5}
yields that

O(t)=O\left(\frac{|\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}|}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)=O\left(\varepsilon^{3.75}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|\right).\qed

9.2
Region slicing with columns

Consider now some region
\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{L}_{i}
with the wireframe
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
surrounding
\mathsf{L}
, which consists of a part of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
denoted by
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, a part of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
denoted by
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, and the western and eastern bridge paths denoted by
\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
and
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, respectively, so that the anticlockwise
7
7

7

when
\mathsf{L}
is the unbounded face, then clockwise
order traverses the paths in the order
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}),\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}),\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}),\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
. We define
L=G|_{\mathsf{L}}
and define the subpaths
\mathrm{South}(L),\mathrm{East}(L),\mathrm{North}(L),\mathrm{West}(L)
from
\mathrm{Perim}(L)
as the paths corresponding to
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
,
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
,
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
,
\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, respectively. Note that
\mathrm{North}(L),\mathrm{East}(L),\mathrm{West}(L)
are in fact shortest paths by
Sk5
and by the definition of bridges. A region
\mathsf{L}
that contains a boundary-distant vertex is called a
lens
, but first we will slice non-lens regions into small bricks as follows.

Slicing non-lens regions into small bricks

Consider a region
\mathsf{L}
with graph
L=G|_{\mathsf{L}}
that does not contain any boundary-distant vertex. Recall that a brick region
\mathsf{B}
with a compass is small if
B=G|_{\mathsf{B}}
has no vertex
v
where
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\mathrm{Perim}(B))>\lambda
and for each
v\in V(B)
where
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\mathrm{South}(B))\leqslant\lambda
and
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\mathrm{North}(B))\leqslant\lambda
, it holds that
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\{v^{B}_{SE},v^{B}_{SW}\})\leqslant 2\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
. We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
\mathsf{L}
be a region where for each
v\in L
it holds that
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,\mathrm{Perim}(L))\leqslant\lambda
. Then there is a collection of vertex-disjoint wireframe paths
\widetilde{P}_{0}=\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}),\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{P}_{k}=\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
from
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
to
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, indexed in their West-to-East order such that the brick regions
\mathsf{B}_{i}
enclosed between
\widetilde{P}_{i-1}
and
\widetilde{P}_{i}
(for
i=1
to
k-1
) can be assigned a compass with the following properties:

•

\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
is the concatenation of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{k})
, and
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
is the concatenation of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{k})
.

•

\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{0})=\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
,
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{k})=\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, and for each
i\in[k]
we have
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i-1})=\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
.

•

Each
\mathsf{B}_{i}
is small.

•

The paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
for
i=1
to
k-1
have total length
O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{South}(L)|)
.

Moreover, given
G
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
, the paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
(and the bricks
B_{i}
) can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof.

We will greedily build a collection of paths
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
connecting some vertices of
\mathrm{South}(L)
to some vertex of
\mathrm{North}(L)
; they will later be used to slice
\mathsf{L}
into small bricks. Initially set
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}=\{\mathrm{West}(L),\mathrm{East}(L)\}
, and for a path
P\in\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
let
s_{P}
denote its starting object on
\mathrm{South}(L)
and
t_{P}
its ending object on
\mathrm{North}(L)
. We will maintain throughout the procedure that (i) the objects of distinct paths of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
are pairwise disjoint and their staring points on
\mathrm{South}(L)
have distance at least
\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
, and (
ii
) for each
P\in\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
we have
|P|\leqslant 2\lambda
. Both conditions hold initially: recall that
\mathrm{West}(L)
and
\mathrm{East}(L)
are built from bridges and thus have length at most
9
.

Suppose that
v\in V(L)
is within distance
\lambda
from both
\mathrm{South}(L)
and
\mathrm{North}(L)
, but its distance is at least
\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}+\lambda
from every vertex
\{s_{P}\mid P\in\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}\}
. Let
s\in\mathrm{South}(L)
and
t\in\mathrm{North}(L)
be endpoints of shortest paths from
v
to
\mathrm{South}(L)
and
\mathrm{North}(L)
, respectively. Consequently,
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,s)\leqslant\lambda
and
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s,t)\leqslant 2\lambda
. Let
Q
be a shortest path in
L
connecting
s_{Q}=s
to
t_{Q}=t
. Notice that
s_{Q}
is within distance
\lambda
from
v
, and
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s_{Q},t_{Q})\leqslant 2\lambda
implies that
|Q|\leqslant 2\lambda
. Let
P\in\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
be arbitrary. Then by the triangle inequality

\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s_{Q},s_{P})\geqslant\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s_{P},v)-\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,s_{Q})\geqslant\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}+\lambda-\lambda=\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}.

Notice that since both
P
and
Q
are of length at most
2\lambda
and their starting points have distance at least
\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
, they must be disjoint.
Thus the objects of
Q
are disjoint from the objects in paths of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
, and we can insert
Q
into
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
and maintain properties (i) and (
ii
).

The above greedy procedure can be applied on a given
v\in V(L)
at most once and can be executed in polynomial time. Since
L
has less than
n
vertices, the procedure terminates in polynomial time. Let
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
denote the obtained path collection. Let
k=|\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}|-1
and index the paths of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{P}
from
0
to
k
according to their starting points in
\mathrm{South}(L)
in West-to-East order. We apply
Corollary

4.3
on each path
P_{i}
and the fixed points
\dot{s}_{P_{i}}\in\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
and
\dot{t}_{P_{i}}\in\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
to gain pairwise disjoint wireframe paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
that can extend the wireframe
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
.

Given the paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
we can define the regions
\mathsf{B}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
Define

\displaystyle\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})\cap\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i},

\displaystyle\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})\cap\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i},

\displaystyle\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i}):=\widetilde{P}_{i-1},

\displaystyle\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i}):=\widetilde{P}_{i}.

The path
\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
is defined to be a single-vertex edgeless path given by the shared vertex of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
.
We define
\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
analogously as the shared vertex of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
.

The total length of the paths is at most
(k-1)\cdot 2\lambda<2k\lambda
. On the other hand the distance between
s_{P_{i}}
and
s_{P_{i+1}}
is at least
\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
fore each
i=0,\dots,k-1
, thus the triangle inequality gives

|\mathrm{South}(L)|\geqslant\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s_{P_{i}},s_{P_{i+1}})\geqslant k\cdot\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}.

Consequently, the total length of the paths in question is at most
2k\lambda\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{South}(L)|
, as required.
It remains to show that the region
\mathsf{B}_{i}
is small.

We will start with the following claim.

Claim 9.6
.

For any
v\in V(B_{i})
and any
j\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
we have
\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,v^{B_{i}}_{SW})\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{L},s_{P_{j}})+2\lambda+2
if
j<i
and
\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,v^{B_{i}}_{SE})\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{L},s_{P_{j}})+2\lambda+2
if
j\geqslant i
, where
v_{L}
is the object of
V(L)
that creates the subpolygon
v
when restricted to
\mathsf{B}_{i}
.

Proof of claim.

Recall that
v^{B_{i}}_{SE}
is a subpolygon of
s_{P_{i}}
and
v^{B_{i}}_{SW}
is a subpolygon of
s_{P_{i-1}}
. We will show that
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,v^{B_{i}}_{SE})\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,s_{P_{j}})+2\lambda+1
when
j\geqslant i
; the other statement can be proven analogously.

Let
Q
be a shortest path from
v
to
s_{P_{j}}
in
L
, and let
w
be the first vertex of
Q
(starting from
v
) whose object intersects the curve
\gamma(\widetilde{P}_{i})
. (Such an object exists as
j\geqslant i
implies that either
j=i
and
s_{P_{j}}
thus intersects the curve, or
j>i
and the curve separates
v
from
s_{P_{j}}
inside
\mathsf{L}
.

Then there is some
w^{\prime}\in V(B_{i})
such that
w^{\prime}
is a subpolygon of
w
and there is a path
Q_{B}
from
v
to
w^{\prime}
of length
|Q[v,w]|
. Moreover,
w^{\prime}
is a neighbor of some object
x^{\prime}\in V(\mathrm{West}(B_{i}))
. Let
x\in V(L)
be the polygon that
x^{\prime}
is a subpolygon of. Note that
\mathrm{West}(B_{i})
is a shortest path in
B_{i}
as
P_{i}
is a shortest path in
L
and by
Lemma

8.4
(
ii
) with
c=1
implies the same for
\mathrm{West}(B_{i})
in
B_{i}
.
Consequently,

\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(x^{\prime},v^{B_{i}}_{SE})=|\mathrm{West}(B_{i})[x^{\prime},v^{B_{i}}_{SE}]|=|P_{i}[x,s_{P_{i}}]|=\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x,s_{P_{i}}).

Consider the concatenation of
Q[v,w^{\prime}]
, the edge
w^{\prime}x^{\prime}
, and the path
\mathrm{West}(B_{i})[x^{\prime},v^{B_{i}}_{SE}]
.
Thus by the triangle inequality and the bound
|P_{i}|=|\mathrm{West}(B_{i})|\leqslant 2\lambda
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,v^{B_{i}}_{SE})

\displaystyle\leqslant|Q[v,w^{\prime}]|+1+|\mathrm{West}(B_{i})[x^{\prime},v^{B_{i}}_{SE}]|

\displaystyle=|Q|+1+|\mathrm{West}(B_{i})|

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,s_{P_{j}})+2\lambda+1,

which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

To show that the region
\mathsf{B}_{i}
is small, notice first that
B_{i}
has no vertex whose distance from
\mathrm{Perim}(B_{i})
is more than
\lambda
, as such a vertex would have distance more than
\lambda
from
\mathrm{Perim}(L)
.

To show the second condition of smallness, let
v\in V(B_{i})
be any vertex such that

\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,\mathrm{South}(B_{i}))\leqslant\lambda\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,\mathrm{North}(B_{i}))\leqslant\lambda.

We will show that
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,\{v^{B}_{SE},v^{B}_{SW}\})\leqslant 2\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}
. Let
v_{L}\in V(L)
denote the object such that
v
is a subpolygon of
v_{L}
. Since paths in
B_{i}
can be realized with (potentially shorter) paths in
L
and
\mathrm{South}(B_{i})
is a subpath of
\mathrm{South}(L)
, we have that
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{L},\mathrm{South}(L))\leqslant\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,\mathrm{South}(B_{i}))\leqslant\lambda
, and similarly
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{L},\mathrm{North}(L))\leqslant\lambda
. Moreover,
v
was not picked in the the greedy procedure, thus
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,s_{P_{j}})<\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}+\lambda
for some
j\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
. Thus
9.6
implies that

\min\big(\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,v^{B_{i}}_{SW}),\mathrm{dist}_{B_{i}}(v,v^{B_{i}}_{SE})\big)<\lambda/\varepsilon^{2}+2\lambda+1<2\lambda/\varepsilon^{2},

since
\lambda>10
and
\varepsilon<1/2
.
∎

Columns and distinguished columns in lens regions

Consider a lens region
\mathsf{L}
and the corresponding graph
L
.
We define a set of vertices
s^{0}_{i}
in
\mathrm{South}(L)
to serve as the starting points of columns. Let
C_{0}
be the path
\mathrm{West}(L)
and let
s^{0}_{0}\in V(\mathrm{South}(L))
its starting vertex. Then for
i=1,2,\dots,
let
s^{0}_{i}
be the earliest vertex on
\mathrm{South}(L)
(in west-to-east order) where

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{South}(L)}(s^{0}_{i-1},s^{0}_{i})>\varepsilon(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s^{0}_{i},\mathrm{North}(L)).

Let
C^{0}_{i}
be a shortest path from
s^{0}_{i}
to
\mathrm{North}(L)
in
L
.
When we get to the end of
\mathrm{South}(L)
, notice that we will add the path
\mathrm{East}(L)
as the final column
C^{0}_{i}
. This is because
\mathrm{East}(L)
is either of length
0
or it is a bridge of length at most
10
, thus when
s^{0}_{i}
is the starting point of
\mathrm{East}(L)
, then
1>\varepsilon(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s^{0}_{i},\mathrm{North}(L))
. We call these paths the
columns
.

Now let us consider the column set
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{j}=\{C^{0}_{j},C^{0}_{j+\kappa},C^{0}_{j+2\kappa},\dots\}
for
j\in[\kappa]
where
\kappa=\kappa(\varepsilon)
will be defined later. Let
j^{*}\in[\kappa]
be the index that minimizes the total length of the columns in
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{j^{*}}
, i.e., minimizes
\sum_{t}|C^{0}_{j^{*}+(t-1)\kappa}|
. Notice that the columns in
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{j^{*}}
have total length
O(|\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}|/(\varepsilon\kappa))
. We call the columns of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{j^{*}}

distinguished
.

Lemma 9.7
.

The total number of edges in the union of all distinguished columns in the lens region
\mathsf{L}
is
O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|)
.

Proof.

First, we claim that the total length of all the columns defined in
L
is at most
O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|/\varepsilon)
.

By definition we have that

|C^{0}_{i}|=\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s^{0}_{i},\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}))<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})}(s^{0}_{i-1},s^{0}_{i}).

Summing this for all
i
and using the fact that the last column
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
is either empty or a bridge (and thus constant length), we get that

\sum_{i}|C^{0}_{i}|<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|)+|\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}|\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}|+O(1)=O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|/\varepsilon).

Thus the total length of the distinguished columns in
\mathcal{C}_{j^{*}}
is at most

O\left(\frac{|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|}{\varepsilon\kappa}\right)=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})|).\qed

Uncrossing the distinguished columns

Unfortunately, the distinguished columns may still cross each other, so we will eventually need to define different
uncrossed
columns. Let
C^{1}_{i}=C^{0}_{j^{*}+(i-1)\kappa}
, and we denote by
s^{1}_{i}
the starting vertex of
C^{1}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{C}^{1}=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{j^{*}}
, and let
S^{1}
denote the corresponding set of starting points. Consider now the last subpath of
C^{1}_{i}
obtained by removing all objects that intersect
\partial\mathsf{L}
.
If this path has length at most
5
, then we say that
C^{1}_{i}
is
short
; otherwise it is
long
. Moreover, if
C^{1}_{i}
intersects
V(\mathrm{West}(L))\cup V(\mathrm{East}(L))
, then it is called
diverting
. In particular, any short column is a bridge, so
Bdg1
implies that all short columns are diverting. If
C^{1}_{i}
is non-diverting (and thus long), then let
\mathrm{Top}(C^{1}_{i})
denote its final maximal subpath of length at least
5
that is disjoint from
\partial L
.

Suppose that
C^{1}_{i}
is non-diverting. Let
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
