denote the first and last vertex on
\mathrm{Top}(C^{1}_{i})
, respectively. Observe that
x_{i}
has a neighbor in
C_{i}^{1}
that intersects
\mathrm{South}(\mathsf{R})
, and
y_{i}
has a neighbor that intersects
\mathrm{North}(\mathsf{R})
. (Note that
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
is undefined when
C^{1}_{i}
is diverting.)

Now consider the
x_{i}
vertices. For each
x_{i}
, there is a path of length at most two connecting
x_{i}
to some vertex in
V(\mathrm{South}(L))
.

We introduce the object
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
that consists of the union of the objects on a shortest path connecting
x_{i}
to
V(\mathrm{South}(L))
.
Crucially, the objects
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
are still fat with a slightly worse constant, and they are still similarly sized as the original objects with a worse constant (fatness and diameter both change by a factor of
3
).
Let
X^{\mathrm{South}}
denote the objects created here. Similarly, we introduce the objects
Y^{\mathrm{North}}
extending the
y_{i}
vertices to objects
y^{\mathrm{North}}_{i}
with shortest paths to
\mathrm{North}({\mathsf{L}})
, having the same fatness and diameter ratio guarantees as
X^{\mathrm{South}}
.
We note that since
C^{1}_{i}
is not short, we have that
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
cannot intersect any
y^{\mathrm{North}}_{j}
. Indeed, if such an intersection would occur, then
x_{i}
would be at distance at most
5
from
\mathrm{North}(L)
, contradicting the fact that the suffix of
C^{1}_{i}
from
x^{1}_{i}
is a shortest path to
\mathrm{North}(L)
of length at least
|\mathrm{Top}(C^{1}_{i})|+1\geqslant 6
.

Let
\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
be a fixed maximal independent set in
X^{\mathrm{South}}
and let
\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
be a fixed maximal independent set in
Y^{\mathrm{North}}
. We denote by
I
the set of indices
i
such that
s^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}\in\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
. Let
\mathrm{Cols}_{L}
denote the set of objects
v\in V(L)
that appear on the top of columns from index set
I
, or more precisely, we set

\mathrm{Cols}_{L}:=\bigcup_{i\in I}\big(V(C^{1}_{i}[x_{i},y_{i}])\setminus\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\big).

Recall that objects of
\mathrm{Top}(C^{1}_{i})=C^{1}_{i}[x_{i},y_{i}]
are disjoint from
\partial\mathsf{L}
, thus we have
\mathrm{Cols}_{L}\subset V(G)
. Let
\mathrm{Cols}=\mathrm{Cols}_{L}\cup\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})\cup\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
. Notice that
\mathrm{Cols}
consists of similarly sized fat objects.
We apply
Lemma

A.3
to obtain a fair polygon collection
\mathrm{Cols}^{\prime}
that induces the same intersection graph, consists of similarly sized connected fat objects, is
\alpha^{\prime}
-standard after scaling, and for any object
p\in\mathrm{Cols}
we have that the corresponding object
p^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cols}^{\prime}
is a subpolygon of
p
. It follows that
\mathrm{Cols}^{\prime}
is an object frame of the intersection graph induced by
\mathrm{Cols}
.

We apply
Theorem

5.11
on
\mathrm{Cols}^{\prime}
, which results in a wireframe
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
; notice that due to the subpolygon property and
3.7
it can be perturbed into a wireframe of
\mathrm{Cols}
. For a vertex
v\in\mathrm{Cols}
let
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{D})
be its picture under the Lipschitz embedding of the intersection graph of
\mathrm{Cols}
to
\widetilde{D}
. Let
D^{\mathrm{North}}
denote the vertices of
V(\widetilde{D})
that correspond to
\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
, that is,
D^{\mathrm{North}}=\{\dot{u}\mid u\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})\}
. Consider a vertex
v\in V(C^{1}_{i}(x_{i},y_{i}])\cup\{x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}\}
, and let
v_{L}=v
if
v\in V(C^{1}_{i}(x_{i},y_{i}])
, and let
v_{L}
be the parent of
\dot{v}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
when
v=x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
, i.e., the object of
\mathrm{South}(L)
that is one of the objects making up
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
. By the Lipschitz property, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{D}}(\dot{v},D^{\mathrm{North}})

\displaystyle=O\Big(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{Cols}}\big(v,\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})\big)\Big)

(9)

\displaystyle=O\big(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{Cols}}(v,y^{\mathrm{North}}_{i})+1\big)

\displaystyle=O\big(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{L},y_{i})\big)

\displaystyle=O\big(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{L},\mathrm{North}(L))\big),

Here the second equality follows from the fact that when
y^{\mathrm{North}}_{i}\not\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
then it intersects some
y^{\mathrm{North}}_{j}\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
. To show the third equality we need that any non-empty path on
\mathrm{Cols}
can be followed with a path in
L
that is at most constant times longer: indeed, any object of
\mathrm{Cols}
is the union of at most
3
objects of
V(L)
, so for any non-empty shortest path in
\mathrm{Cols}
we can build a corresponding path in
L
which is at most
7
times longer.

Consider now shortest paths
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
in
{\widetilde{D}}
connecting each
\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
to
D^{\mathrm{North}}
for each
i\in I
. Since
{\widetilde{D}}
is planar, when the curve of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
intersects the curve of some
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{j}
, then they must share a vertex. We can choose the first shared vertex
v
of these paths, and replace the part of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{j}
starting at
v
with the part of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
starting at
v
. Since both
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
and
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{j}
were shortest paths to
D^{\mathrm{North}}
, we have that the new
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{j}
has the same length as the original one. Using such modifications we can find paths
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
that are pairwise
non-crossing
, that is, when
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
and
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{j}
share a vertex, then all edges after the first shared vertex are also shared. Let
\Gamma
denote the planar curves given by the edges of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
for each
i\in I
. Notice that for each curve
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
\gamma\subset\inter(\mathsf{L})
.

Let
{\mathsf{L}}^{XY}
denote the unique connected component of the region

{\mathsf{L}}^{XY}:={\mathsf{L}}\setminus\bigcup\{v\mid v\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})\cup\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})\}

that is adjacent to each object in
\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
and
\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
; indeed such a component exists as
\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
and
\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
all intersect
\partial\mathsf{L}
and the objects in
\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})\cup\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
are pairwise disjoint: if some
x^{\mathrm{South}}\in\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
would intersect some
y^{\mathrm{North}}\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
, then they would form a bridge in
\mathsf{L}
, contradicting the definition of
\mathsf{L}
. Notice that when walking
\partial\mathsf{L}^{XY}
from the Southeast corner of
{\mathsf{L}}^{XY}
in a counter-clockwise direction, then the objects
\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
appear along
\partial\mathsf{L}^{XY}
in the order of their indices, followed by the objects
\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
in reverse order of their indices, and each of these objects is encountered exactly once.

The region
{\mathsf{L}}^{XY}
cuts each
\gamma\in\Gamma
into subcurves. Let
\mu_{i}
denote the subcurve among these that connects some point of
\partial x_{i}^{\mathrm{South}}
to some
\partial y^{\mathrm{North}}
for some
y^{\mathrm{North}}\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
if such a subcurve of
\gamma
exists. If there are multiple curves from a fixed
\partial x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
, then we dispose of all but one of them. Let
I_{\mu}\subseteq I
denote the indices
i
where
\mu_{i}
is defined. We remark that when
j\in I\setminus I_{\mu}
then the path
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{j}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
for some
i\in I_{\mu}
.

Notice that the curves
\mu_{i}
for
i\in I_{\mu}
are pairwise non-crossing (although some suffix of
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
may be equal), and when
\mu_{i}
ends at
\partial y^{\mathrm{North}}_{i^{\prime}}
and
\mu_{j}
ends at
\partial y^{\mathrm{North}}_{j^{\prime}}
for some
i<j
, then
i^{\prime}\leqslant j^{\prime}
due to the order in which these boundaries appear along
\partial\mathsf{L}^{XY}
. Let us now extend both ends of each
\mu_{i}
inside
\bigcup\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
and inside
\bigcup\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})
to
\partial\mathsf{L}
.
Let
\dot{v}
denote the vertex of the wireframe
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
when
v\in V(\mathrm{South}(L))\cup V(\mathrm{North}(L))
, that is,
\dot{v}\in\partial\mathsf{L}
. When
\mu_{i}
connects
x^{\mathrm{South}}
to
y^{\mathrm{North}}
, then there is some
\dot{u}\in V(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}))\cap x^{\mathrm{South}}
and
\dot{v}\in V(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}))\cap y^{\mathrm{North}}
where we will connect the ends of
\mu_{i}
to.
These extensions can be done without introducing any new intersections among
\mu_{i}
: that is, distinct
\mu_{i}
remain disjoint except when extending some
\mu_{i}
with a shared suffix inside some
y\in\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
that get extended with the same suffix inside
y
.
This is possible due to the fact that the polygons of
\mathrm{MIS}(X^{\mathrm{South}})\cup\mathrm{MIS}(Y^{\mathrm{North}})
are connected polygons, each of which covers some subcurve of
\partial\mathsf{L}
.
Let
\mu^{*}_{i}
denote the resulting curves (for each
i\in I_{\mu}
). In fact, in each of these extensions we can introduce at most two vertices (for the objects making up
x^{\mathrm{South}}
or
y^{\mathrm{North}}
to create a valid wireframe path
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
.
Since
\mu^{*}_{i}
stays inside
\mathsf{L}
, it corresponds to a walk of
L
of length at most

|\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}|=|Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}|+O(1)=\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{D}}(\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{i},D^{\mathrm{North}})+O(1)=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},y_{i}))

by (
9
).

Next, for later convenience we need to ensure that the paths
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
have no shared suffixes other than perhaps their endpoints. If some non-endpoint vertex
\dot{v}
is shared between
b
paths
(\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i_{1}},\dots,\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i_{b}})
(whose indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{b}
are necessarily consecutive in
I^{\mu}
), then we replace
\dot{v}
with
b
points
\dot{v}_{i_{1}},\dots,\dot{v}_{i_{b}}
in a small neighborhood of
\dot{v}
. An edge
\dot{v}\dot{w}
that has been shared between
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i_{1}},\dots,\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i_{b}}
can now be represented by
b
curves that are disjoint, but each of the new edges run in an infinitesimally small neighborhood of the original edge curve of
\dot{v}\dot{w}
. Finally, when
(\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i_{1}},\dots,\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i_{b}})
share their last edge
\dot{v}\dot{w}
, then
\dot{w}
is not duplicated, but it becomes the center of a star, with edges
\dot{v}_{i_{j}}\dot{w}
for
j\in[b]
, where the edge curves go in a small neighborhood of the original edge curve of
\dot{v}\dot{w}
. One can verify that these modifications can be done while maintaining the wireframe property for each
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
.

Let
\widetilde{D\mathsf{L}}
denote the wireframe realized by
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
and the paths
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
(as modified above to avoid shared suffixes), that is, the vertices are
V(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, the new vertices inside the extensions, and the vertices of
\widetilde{D}
that fall on
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
. The edges are realized by the drawing
\partial\mathsf{L}\cup\bigcup_{i}\mu^{*}_{i}
. We denote by
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{0}
and
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{|\mathcal{C}|+1}
the paths
\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
and
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, respectively. Consequently, the paths
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
are defined only for the index set
\bar{I}_{\mu}:=I_{\mu}\cup\{0,|\mathcal{C}^{1}|+1\}
. For each
j\in\bar{I}_{\mu}
we denote by
\dot{v}_{j}
the southern starting vertex of
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{j}
.

We say that a column
C^{1}_{i}

leans west
if one of the following hold:

•

i\in I_{\mu}
and
\dot{v}_{i}
is to the west of (or equal to)
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}

•

i\in I\setminus I_{\mu}
and the curve of
Q_{i}^{\widetilde{D}}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
with
j\in I_{\mu}
where
\dot{v}_{j}
is to the west of (or equal to)
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
,

•

i\not\in I
, because
C^{1}_{i}
is a non-diverting column where
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i^{\prime}}
for
i^{\prime}\in I
, and the curve of
Q_{i^{\prime}}^{\widetilde{D}}
intersects
8
8

8

We note that it is possible that
i^{\prime}\in I_{\mu}
and consequently
j^{\prime}=i^{\prime}
, so
Q_{i^{\prime}}^{\widetilde{D}}
intersects
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i^{\prime}}
by the fact that its starting point
\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{i^{\prime}}
is inside
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i^{\prime}}
.
some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}\in I_{\mu}
where
\dot{v}_{j^{\prime}}
is to the west of (or equal to)
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
,

•

i\not\in I
because
C^{1}_{i}
is diverting, and it intersects some
v\in\mathrm{West}(L)
.

If
C^{1}_{i}
does not lean west then it
leans east
. It follows that if
C_{1}^{i}
leans east, then one of the following hold:

•

i\in I_{\mu}
and
\dot{v}_{i}
is to the east of (and not equal to)
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}

•

i\in I\setminus I_{\mu}
and the curve of
Q_{i}^{\widetilde{D}}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
with
j\in I_{\mu}
where
\dot{v}_{j}
is to the east of (and not equal to)
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
,

•

i\not\in I
, because
C^{1}_{i}
is a non-diverting column where
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i^{\prime}}
for
i^{\prime}\in I
, and the curve of
Q_{i^{\prime}}^{\widetilde{D}}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}\in I_{\mu}
where
\dot{v}_{j^{\prime}}
is to the east of (and not equal to)
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
,

•

i\not\in I
because
C^{1}_{i}
is diverting, and it intersects some
v\in\mathrm{East}(L)
.

For a fixed
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
, let
j,k\in\bar{I}_{\mu}
be the consecutive indices of
\bar{I}_{\mu}
where
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\in\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}_{j},\dot{v}_{k}]
and
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\neq\dot{v}_{k}
. We define the paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
according to increasing indices
i
. We set
\widetilde{P}_{0}=\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
. For general
i
, if
\widetilde{P}_{i-1}
contains
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
, then let
\widetilde{P}_{i}
be the suffix of
\widetilde{P}_{i-1}
starting at
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
. Otherwise, let
\widetilde{P}_{i}
be the concatenation
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]\cup Q^{*}_{j}
if
C^{1}_{i}
leans west and
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{k}]\cup Q^{*}_{k}
if it leans east. Finally, let
\widetilde{P}_{|\mathcal{C}^{1}|+1}=\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
. Observe that under this definition two paths
\widetilde{P}_{i},\widetilde{P}_{j}
are pairwise non-crossing, but they may have overlaps along some subpath of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
. Moreover,
\widetilde{P}_{i}
is a subpath of
\widetilde{P}_{i-1}
if and only if
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\in V(\widetilde{P}_{i-1})
if and only if there is no face of
\widetilde{D\mathsf{L}}
between
\widetilde{P}_{i-1}
and
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. We call the resulting paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
the
uncrossed columns
.

Uncrossed column properties

For the lens
\mathsf{L}
let
\prec
denote the west-to-east order of vertices along
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
and
\mathrm{South}(L)
.
We prove the following lemmas for later use.

Lemma 9.8
.

Suppose that
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\not\in V(\widetilde{P}_{i-1})
, and let
\widetilde{P}_{i}=\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]\cup\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{j}
for some
j\in\bar{I}_{\mu}
. Then there is a path
\tau
in
L
from
s^{1}_{i}\in\mathrm{South}(L)
to
\mathrm{North}(L)
such that

\tau
is
c
-approaching
\mathrm{North}(L)
for some constant
c=O(1)
and it intersects
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{j}
.

Proof.

Suppose without loss of generality that
C^{1}_{i}
leans west and thus
\dot{v}_{j}\preceq\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
; the east leaning case with
s^{1}_{i}\preceq\dot{v}_{j}
can be handled analogously.
For the rest of the proof we will follow closely the notations in the definition of a west-leaning column.

Notice that when
C^{1}_{i}
is diverting then
\tau:=C^{1}_{i}
intersects
\mathrm{West}(L)
, and since
Q^{*}_{j}
separates
\mathrm{West}(L)
and
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
in
L
we have that
C^{1}_{i}
must intersect
Q^{*}_{j}
. If
i\in I_{\mu}
then
j=i
and
C^{1}_{i}
contains
x^{i}
, thus
\tau:=C^{1}_{i}[s^{1}_{i},x_{i}]
followed by a constant length diversion to
v_{i}
and the suffix of
C^{1}_{i}
from
x_{i}
intersects
Q^{*}_{i}
, and it is
c
-approaching as
C^{1}_{i}
is a shortest path to
\mathrm{North}(L)
and the constant length diversion from
x_{i}
to
v_{i}
and back weakens this to a
c
-approaching path for some constant
c
.

If
i\in I\setminus I_{\mu}
, then
Q_{i}^{\widetilde{D}}
intersects
x_{j}^{\mathrm{South}}
, thus it has a vertex within distance
3
of
v_{j}
(as
x_{j}^{\mathrm{South}}
is a union of at most
3
objects, one of which is
v_{j}
). Let
\tau
be the concatenation of
C_{1}^{i}[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},x_{i}]
, the path corresponding to the wireframe path
Q_{i}^{\widetilde{D}}
from
x_{i}
to its vertex intersecting
x_{j}^{\mathrm{South}}
, the constant length path connecting this neighbor of
x_{j}
to
v_{j}
, and the path corresponding to
Q^{*}_{j}
. Notice that
C^{1}_{i}
is a shortest path to
\mathrm{North}(L)
, and
Q_{i}^{\widetilde{D}}
is
c
-approaching
\mathrm{North}(L)
by
9
, the same holds for
Q_{j}^{\widetilde{D}}
, and the concatenation we defined adds constant length paths to these in constantly many places, which still leaves a
c
-approaching path to
\mathrm{North}(L)
for some constant
c
.

If
i\not\in I
and
C^{1}_{i}
is not diverting, then we again define
\tau
as a concatenation. From
s^{1}_{i}
we use
C^{1}_{i}
to get to
x_{i}
, then in constant steps we get to
x_{i^{\prime}}
for some
i^{\prime}\in I
, then continue on
Q_{i^{\prime}}^{\widetilde{D}}
to
x_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}\in I_{\mu}
, then go along
Q^{*}_{j}
to
\mathrm{North}(L)
. Again, this is a concatenation of
c
-approaching paths to
\mathrm{North}(L)
with constantly many constant-length paths between them, so it is
c
-approaching
\mathrm{North}(L)
for some
c=O(1)
.
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

Suppose that
\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\not\in V(\widetilde{P}_{i-1})
.
Then
\widetilde{P}_{i}
defined above satisfies

|\widetilde{P}_{i}|=\begin{cases}\displaystyle O\Big(\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\bar{I}_{\mu}\\
\dot{v}_{k}\preceq\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\end{subarray}}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{k}]\cup Q^{*}_{k}|\Big)&\text{when $C^{1}_{i}$ leans west,}\\
\displaystyle O\Big(\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\bar{I}_{\mu}\\
\dot{v}_{k}\succ\dot{s}^{1}_{i}\end{subarray}}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{k}]\cup Q^{*}_{k}|\Big)&\text{when $C^{1}_{i}$ leans east.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
C^{1}_{i}
leans west; the east-leaning case can be handled symmetrically.
Fix some
k\in\bar{I}_{\mu}
with
\dot{v}_{k}\preceq s^{1}_{i}
, and we will show that
|\widetilde{P}_{i}|=O(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{k}]\cup Q^{*}_{j}|)
. Let
\widetilde{P}_{i}=\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]\cup\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{j}
. See
Figure

13
(i). The claim clearly holds if
k=j
. Otherwise
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{k}]
must pass through
\dot{v}_{j}
. Since
Q^{*}_{j}
is a shortest path from
\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
to
D^{\mathrm{North}}
in
\widetilde{D}
, we get by (
9
) that
|Q^{*}_{j}|=\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{D}}(\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{j},D^{\mathrm{North}})=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{j},\mathrm{North}(L)))
, where the last step uses that
v_{j}
and the objects making up
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
are within constant distance of each other in
L
.
Using the Lipschitz property from
Corollary

5.3
, we can write:

\displaystyle|\widetilde{P}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]|+|Q^{*}_{j}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]|+O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v_{j},\mathrm{North}(L)))

\displaystyle\leqslant|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]|+O(|\mathrm{South}(L)[v_{j},v_{k}]|+|Q^{*}_{k}|)

\displaystyle\leqslant O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{k}]\cup Q^{*}_{k}|),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
\widetilde{P}_{i}
be the path defined above. Then
|\widetilde{P}_{i}|=O(|C^{1}_{i}|)
.

Proof.

Recall that
v_{i}\in\mathrm{South}(L)
is some fixed vertex within distance
2
of
x_{i}
for each
i
. We will assume without loss of generality that
C^{1}_{i}
leans west.

We distinguish several cases based on the index
i
.

Case 1.

i\in I_{\mu}

Since
v_{i}\preceq s^{1}_{i}
, Lemma
9.9
gives that
|\widetilde{P}_{i}|=O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{i}]|+|Q^{*}_{i}|)
. Using
M2
and that
x_{i}
is within constant distance of
v_{i}
in
L
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{i}]|

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s^{1}_{i},v_{i})

(10)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)(|C^{1}_{i}[s^{1}_{i},x_{i}]|+\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},v_{i}))

\displaystyle=O(|C^{1}_{i}[s^{1}_{i},x_{i}]|).

On the other hand, (
9
) implies that
|Q^{*}_{i}|=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},\mathrm{North}(L))
. Since
C^{1}_{i}
is a shortest path in
L
, this gives

|P_{i}|\leqslant O(|C^{1}_{i}[s^{1}_{i},x_{i}]|)+O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},\mathrm{North}(L))=O(|C^{1}_{i}|)

(11)

as desired.

Case 2.

i\in I\setminus I_{\mu}

It follows that the curve of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
intersects some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
where
j\in I_{\mu}
, and
v_{j}\preceq s^{1}_{i}
. Thus there is some
\dot{x}^{\prime}_{j}\in V(Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i})
such that
x^{\prime}_{j}:=p(\dot{x}^{\prime}_{j})
intersects
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
, i.e.,
x_{j}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
are within constant distance of each other in
L
. By
Lemma

9.9
and the triangle inequality we have that

|P_{i}|\leqslant O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]|+|Q^{*}_{j}|)\leqslant O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{i}]|+|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]|+|Q^{*}_{j}|),

(12)

where the first term of the right hand side expression can be bounded as in (
10
) by
O(|C^{1}_{i}[s^{1}_{i},x_{i}]|)
. Using the fact that
x_{i}
and
v_{i}
are neighbors as well as
x_{j},v_{j}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
are within constant distance of each other, we get by the Lipschitz property of
Corollary

5.3
that these distances are distorted by a constant factor in
\widetilde{D}
for the corresponding vertices
\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
and
\dot{v}_{i}
, as well as among
\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{j}
,
\dot{x}^{\prime}_{j}
and
\dot{v}_{j}
. Thus we can easily switch among such vertices and incur only a constant length cost.

Let
\dot{y}^{\mathrm{North}}
denote the endpoint of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
.
The second and third terms of (
12
) can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}_{i},\dot{v}_{j}]|+|Q^{*}_{j}|

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},x_{j})+O(1)+\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{D}}(\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{j},D^{\mathrm{North}})

(13)

\displaystyle\leqslant O(|Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}[\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{i},\dot{x}^{\prime}_{j}]|)+|Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}[\dot{x}^{\prime}_{j},\dot{y}^{\mathrm{North}}]|+O(1)

\displaystyle=O(|Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}[\dot{x}^{\mathrm{South}}_{i},\dot{y}^{\mathrm{North}}]|

\displaystyle=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},\mathrm{North}(L))

where line 1 used
M2
, line 2 used the Lipschitz property of
Corollary

5.3
, and line 4 used (
9
). We can thus conclude this case again with (
11
).

Case 3.

i\not\in I
and
C^{1}_{i}
is not diverting.

It follows that
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i}
was not included in
I
because it intersected some
x^{\mathrm{South}}_{i^{\prime}}
where
i^{\prime}\in I
. Let
j^{\prime}\in\bar{I}_{\mu}
be the index such that
P_{i^{\prime}}
is using
Q^{*}_{j^{\prime}}
, i.e., it is preceding or succeeding
i^{\prime}
. We have
v_{j^{\prime}}\preceq s^{1}_{i}
.
By
Lemma

9.9
, the triangle inequality and the fact that
|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}_{i},\dot{v}_{i^{\prime}}]|=O(1)
we can write

|P_{i}|=O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{i}]|+|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}_{i^{\prime}},\dot{v}_{j^{\prime}}]|+|Q^{*}_{j^{\prime}}|).

The first term can again be bounded as in Case 1 formula (
10
) by
O(|C^{1}_{i}[s^{1}_{i},x_{i}]|)
, and the last two terms can be bounded by
O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i^{\prime}},\mathrm{North}(L))
using (
13
), concluding with (
11
) and the fact that
\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x_{i},x_{i^{\prime}})=O(1)
.

Case 4.

i\not\in I
and
C^{1}_{i}
is diverting.

Suppose that
C^{1}_{i}
intersects some object of
\mathrm{West}(L)
(the proof is analogous when it intersects
\mathrm{East}(L)
. By
Lemma

9.9
, property
M2
, and the fact that
Q^{*}_{0}=\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
has constant length, we have

\displaystyle|P_{i}|

\displaystyle=O(|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{v}_{0}]|+|Q^{*}_{0}|)

\displaystyle=O(|\mathrm{South}(L)[s^{1}_{i},v_{0}]|)

\displaystyle=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(s^{1}_{i},\mathrm{West}(L)))

\displaystyle=O(|C^{1}_{i}|).\qed

9.3
Defining bricks and constructing the mortar graph

The
mortar graph

\widetilde{M}
is the wireframe that consists of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
, and in each skeleton face its bridge decomposition, and the union of all graphs
\widetilde{D\mathsf{L}}
in the lenses of the decomposition. Notice that each relevant face
\mathsf{R}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
is now sliced into smaller regions with south-north paths that are either bridges or the paths
Q^{*}_{i}
in the lenses of
\mathsf{R}
. We also include the eastern and western corner of
\mathsf{R}
as length-0 south-north paths.

Figure 13:
(i) A lens
\mathsf{L}
sliced into bricks with paths
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. (ii) A brick and its compass. The path
\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
consists of a single vertex
\dot{v}^{B}_{SW}=\dot{v}^{B}_{SSW}
.

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a face in the drawing of
\widetilde{M}
that is located inside a relevant face
\mathsf{R}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
, and it is bounded by consecutive south-north paths inside
\mathsf{R}
. (See
Figure

13
(ii)). Recall that the brick
B
corresponding to
\mathsf{B}
is the intersection graph
G|_{\mathsf{B}}
.

Next, we define the compass for each brick region
\mathsf{B}
. Observe that in the above definition, either
\mathsf{B}
is inside some lens
\mathsf{L}\subseteq\mathsf{R}
, or
\mathsf{B}
is not contained in any lens. If
\mathsf{B}
is not contained in any lens, then we use the definitions guaranteed by
Lemma

9.5
.

If
\mathsf{B}
is inside the lens
\mathsf{L}
, then it is some region between the paths
\widetilde{P}_{i},\widetilde{P}_{i+1}
for some
i\in\{0,\dots,|\mathcal{C}^{1}|\}
.
We let
\dot{v}^{B}_{SE}
denote the southeast corner of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
, defined as the easternmost vertex of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
that is on
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
. Similarly, we define
\dot{v}^{B}_{SW}
, the southwest corner as the westernmost vertex of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
that is on
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
. Let
\dot{v}^{B}_{NE}
and
\dot{v}^{B}_{NW}
denote the easternmost and westernmost vertices of
\widetilde{B}
that are on
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})
, respectively. Finally, let
\dot{v}^{B}_{SSW}:=\dot{s}^{1}_{i}
and
\dot{v}^{B}_{SSE}:=\dot{s}^{1}_{i+1}
denote the first vertex on
\widetilde{P}_{i}
and
\widetilde{P}_{i+1}
.
We can now define the compass of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}^{B}_{NE},\dot{v}^{B}_{NW}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{P}_{i}[\dot{v}^{B}_{SW},\dot{v}^{B}_{NW}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{P}_{i+1}[\dot{v}^{B}_{SE},\dot{v}^{B}_{NE}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}^{B}_{SW},\dot{v}^{B}_{SSW}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}^{B}_{SSE},\dot{v}^{B}_{SE}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{v}^{B}_{SSW},\dot{v}^{B}_{SSE}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})

Note that each of these paths may only consist of a single vertex.

Let
\mathrm{South}(B),\mathrm{North}(B),\mathrm{East}(B)
and
\mathrm{West}(B)
denote the paths of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
corresponding to
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
and
\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
, respectively.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

9.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

9.3
.

The graph
\widetilde{M}
is
2
-connected because, according to
Sk1
, its construction started from a
2
-connected wireframe
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
, and
\widetilde{M}
was created by adding paths between vertices of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
. The irrelevant face of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
is inherited by
\widetilde{M}
and has the desired property by
Sk2
. Thus,
\widetilde{M}
is a mortar graph of
G
. It is routine to check that the compass of
\widetilde{M}
is well-defined by starting from
Sk3
, and following the construction of the eastern and western brick sides in
Lemma

9.5
and above.

Property
M1
holds because by
Sk4
we have that
\widetilde{M}
is a supergraph of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
where all but a single face are brick regions.

Next, we prove property
M2
. Notice first that if
\mathsf{B}
is inside a relevant face
\mathsf{R}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
, then the distances of vertices in
B=G|_{\mathsf{B}}
are greater or equal than the distance of the corresponding vertices in
G|_{\mathsf{R}}
by Lemma
8.4
(
i
). It is therefore sufficient to prove that these paths are
t
-approxiamte shortest paths in
R=G|_{\mathsf{R}}
, or possibly just in some lens graph
L=G|_{\mathsf{L}}
where
\mathsf{L}
is a lens of
\mathsf{R}
containing
\mathsf{B}
.

The path
\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{South}(B)\cup\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
is a subpath of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
, and
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
is a subpath of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
. Switching to objects of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
and
\mathrm{Perim}(R)
, the path
\mathrm{South}(B)
is a path consisting of some subpolygons on a subpath of
\mathrm{South}(R)
, and either
\mathrm{North}(B)
is a single vertex, or it is a is a path consisting of some subpolygons on
\mathrm{North}(R)
. Consequently,
Sk5
implies that
\mathrm{West}^{-}(B)\cup\mathrm{South}(B)\cup\mathrm{East}^{-}(B)
is a internally
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate shortest path in
R
, and
\mathrm{North}(B)
is a shortest path in
R
.

Next, we prove
M3
; first we consider only
\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
, and later we will extend the proof to
\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
. (The proofs for
\mathrm{East}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
and
\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
are analogous). Consider a path
\mathrm{West}^{|}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
. Such a path is either a single vertex edgeless path (and thus satisfies
M3
), or it came about as the walk inside some lens
\mathsf{L}
corresponding to a path
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
in
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
where
i\in\bar{I}_{\mu}
. When
i=0
or
i=|\mathcal{C}^{1}|+1
, then this is a constant length bridge path and the claim follows. Othewise, since
\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}
extends some path
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
with at most two vertices on both ends, we have that the corresponding path
Q^{*}_{i}
in
L
is
c
-approaching
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
and is a internally
c
-approximate shortest path if and only if the same holds for the path given by the internal vertices of
Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}
. Then (
9
) implies that for any internal vertex
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{D}}_{i})
we have

\widetilde{Q}^{\widetilde{D}}_{i}[\dot{v},\dot{v}^{B}_{NW}]=\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{D}}(\dot{v},D^{\mathrm{North}})+O(1)=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(v,\mathrm{North}(L)),

(14)

where
v=p|_{\mathsf{L}}(\dot{v})
is an object in
V(L)
. Since the hidden constant above only depends on
\alpha
, the claim of
M3
about
c
-approaching follows. Simiarly, to show that it is internally
c
-approximate shortest, we can similarly pick any vertex pair
u,v\in V(\mathrm{West}^{|}(B))
and corresponding vertices
\dot{u},\dot{v}\in V(Q^{\widetilde{D}}_{i})
and use the Lipschitz property:

\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{i}[\dot{u},\dot{v}]=\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{D}}(\dot{u},\dot{v})=O(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{Cols}}(u,v))=O(\mathrm{dist}_{L}(u,v)),

which concludes the proof of
M3
.

Property
M4
follows from
Sk6
and the fact that the edges of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}),\mathrm{West}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
, and
\mathrm{East}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
correspond to edges of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
, and each edge of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
is listed in at most two bricks.

We can now prove the complexity bound on
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{M})
. Notice that
\widetilde{M}
is obtained from
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
by adding at most
O(|E(\widetilde{M})|)
shortest paths to various faces
\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
using
Corollary

4.3
. In each case the addition is based on some path
P
of objects where
\mathcal{A}(P)
can be bounded by
O(\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))+\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}))=O(\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))+\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}))=O(n^{3}/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
, where the last step used
Lemma

8.6
. By
M4
add at most
O(|E(\widetilde{M})|)=O(n/\varepsilon)
such paths, so we get
\mathrm{compl}(\widetilde{M})=O(n^{4}/\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\mathrm{compl}(\mathcal{A}(G))
, as required.

Next, we prove property
M5
. By
Lemma

9.4
,
Lemma

9.5
, and
Lemma

9.7
we have that the total length of all bridges, slicing paths and columns used for the relevant face region
\mathsf{R}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
is
O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})|)
. Since the paths
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{R}})
cover each skeleton edge at most twice, this is at most
O(\varepsilon^{2}|E(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})|)
edges in total. Notice that all edges on these paths appear on the boundary of at most two bricks (once on the east, once on the west), so the same bound holds for the total length of all
\mathrm{West}(B)
and
\mathrm{East}(B)
together. Finally,
Sk6
implies that
O(\varepsilon^{2}|E(\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}})|)=O(\varepsilon\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}})
, which concludes the proof of
M5
.

It remains to prove
M6
. The large brick in question is part of some lens
\mathsf{L}
; suppose that it is between the paths
\widetilde{P}_{i},\widetilde{P}_{i+1}
for some
i\in\{0,\dots,|\mathcal{C}^{1}|\}
. It follows that
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\subset\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{L}})[\dot{s}^{1}_{i},\dot{s}^{1}_{i+1}]
. Consequently, there are at most
\kappa
vertices
s^{0}_{j},s^{0}_{j+1},\dots,
on
\mathrm{South}(B)
that are starting points of the consecutive columns
C^{0}_{j},\dots,C^{0}_{j+1}
. By the definition of these columns, we have that
s^{0}_{j},s^{0}_{j+1},\dots,
are at most
\kappa
points ordered west to east on
\mathrm{South}(B)
such that for any vertex
x
of
\mathrm{South}(B)[s^{0}_{k},s^{0}_{k+1})=\mathrm{South}(L)[s^{0}_{k},s^{0}_{k+1})
we have
|\mathrm{South}(L)[s^{0}_{k},x]|\leqslant\varepsilon\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x,\mathrm{North}(L))
. Notice however that (i)
|\mathrm{South}(L)[s^{0}_{k},x]|=|\mathrm{South}(B)[s^{0}_{k},x]|
, (
ii
)
\mathrm{North}(B)\subset\mathrm{North}(L)
, and (
iii
) the distance of vertices in
B
is greater or equal to the distance of corresponding vertices in
L
by
Lemma

8.4
(
i
). Thus

|\mathrm{South}(B)[s^{0}_{k},x]|\leqslant\varepsilon\mathrm{dist}_{L}(x,\mathrm{North}(L))\leqslant\varepsilon\mathrm{dist}_{B}(x,\mathrm{North}(B)),

which concludes the proof of
M6
.

Finally, we note that all of the construction works on polynomial-sized graphs and the construction steps can be followed by a polynomial algorithm.
∎

10
The structure theorem and spanner for Steiner tree

Let
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
be a graph, and suppose
T\subset V(G)
and
\varepsilon>0
are given. Suppose that we have constructed a mortar graph
\mathcal{M}
in
G
using
Theorem

9.3
. We will set
G^{\mathrm{orig}}:=G
, and then extend
G
with all restricted objects in all bricks
B
of
\mathcal{M}
. As a result, the optimum solution in
G
is unchanged (see also the discussion on Page
8
around object duplication), but the size of
G
might be much bigger than the original graph
G^{\mathrm{orig}}
, and we occasionally need to refer to only original fat objects, as
G
now typically contains non-fat subpolygons of objects.

10.1
Simplifying a forest in a large brick

Recall that every large brick is a part of some lens.
We call the vertices of
N_{B}(V(\partial B)),\lambda)

outer
vertices, and vertices of
V(B)\setminus N_{B}(V(\partial B),\lambda)
will be called
inner
vertices of
B
. Recall that small bricks do not have boundary-distant vertices and thus they do not have inner vertices.

Suppose that
A
is a vertex set in a thick brick
B
. Based on
A
, we will define a wireframe
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
, whose outer face is the concatenation of the paths
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}),\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}),\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}),\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
. The graph
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
will consist of the paths
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
(equal to
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
),
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
(equal to
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
), and a forest
\widetilde{F}_{A}
. We define the wireframe
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
such that it satisfies the following conditions.

(P1)

The path
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
is a shortest path in
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
.

(P2)

For some
k\leqslant\kappa
there is a set of vertices
s_{0},\dots,s_{k}
ordered from west to east on
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
such that for any vertex
x
of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})[s_{i},s_{i+1})
we have

|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})[s_{i},x]|\leqslant\varepsilon\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{Q}_{A}}(x,\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})).

(P3)

For any pair of vertices
a,b
of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
where
a,b
are not both endpoints of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
, the path along
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
is an approximate shortest path, that is,
|\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})[a,b]|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{Q}_{A}}(a,b)
.

We can therefore apply the following theorem in a black-box fashion.
Given a subgraph
\widetilde{F}
of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
a
joining vertex
of
\widetilde{F}
is a vertex that has an incident edge both in
\widetilde{F}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{A}\setminus\widetilde{F}
.

Theorem 10.1

(Borradaile
et al.
, Thm. 10.7
[
24
]
)
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
be a plane graph satisfying conditions
9
9

9

In the setting of Borradaile
et al.

[
24
]
there is one more condition, namely that every terminal of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
is either on
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
or
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
. Observe that this condition is immaterial to their Theorem 10.7, as terminals do not appear in its statement.
(P1)-(P3). Let
\widetilde{F}_{A}=E(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\setminus E(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}))
. Then there is a forest
\widetilde{F}
of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
with the following properties.

(F1)

If two vertices of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
are connected in
\widetilde{F}_{A}
then they are connected in
\widetilde{F}
.

(F2)

The number of
joining vertices
of
\widetilde{F}
on
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
is
o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5})
.

(F3)

The total length of
\widetilde{F}
is at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))|\widetilde{F}_{A}|
.

Remark 10.2
.

The forest
\widetilde{F}
in
Theorem

10.1
joins the northern and southern boundary of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
at only
o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5})
vertices. Assume without loss of generality that we remove any component from
\widetilde{F}
whose intersection with
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
is disjoint from the
\widetilde{F}_{A}\cap\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
. This preserves the properties required of
\widetilde{F}
. Consider a maximal subpath
\widetilde{I}
of
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
that is also a subpath of
\widetilde{F}
. Either there is a joining vertex of
\widetilde{F}
on
\widetilde{I}
, or the component of
\widetilde{F}
containing
I
must be
I
itself. Moreover, if two such disjoint maximal intervals
I_{1},I_{2}
of
\widetilde{F}\cap E(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}))
are in different components of
\widetilde{F}
, then no vertex pair
u\in I_{1},v\in I_{2}
can be connected in
\widetilde{F}_{A}
: indeed, such a connection would also have to be present in
\widetilde{F}
. Thus the number of maximal intervals of
\widetilde{F}
in
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
is at most
o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5})
plus the number of connected components of
\widetilde{F}_{A}
.

In what follows, we are generally concentrating on a single brick
B
. Let
\partial_{0}=V(\mathrm{South}(B)\cup\mathrm{North}(B))
and we use the shorthand
\partial_{1}
for the set of objects in
V(B)
that intersect the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
or the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
. Let
\partial_{2}=N_{B}(\partial_{1}\cup\partial_{0})
. Consequently,
\partial_{0}\subseteq\partial_{1}\subseteq\partial_{2}
.

10.2
The wireframe
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
.

Let
A
be a vertex set in
V(B)\setminus\partial_{1}
where each component of
A
has size at least
\lambda
, and
A
contains the east and west of
B
, or more precisely, we require that

\big(V(\mathrm{East}(B))\cup V(\mathrm{West}(B))\big)\setminus\partial_{1}\subset A\subset V(B)\setminus\partial_{1}.

Let
a\in V(A)\cap\partial_{2}
be a vertex neighboring to some vertex of
\partial_{1}
. Then there is a path
P_{a}
of length at most
2
connecting
a
to
\partial_{0}
in
B
; we fix one such path for each such vertex
a
. Let
A^{\partial}
denote the set of vertices on these paths
P_{a}
.
In each component
K
of
B[A\cup A^{\partial}]
, we greedily select a maximal number of vertices from
V(K)\cap\mathrm{South}(B)
whose pairwise distance is at least
1/\varepsilon
in
B
, and do the same in
V(K)\cap\mathrm{North}(B)
. Moreover, for each selected vertex
y
, there is some path
P_{a}
ending there. For each selected
y
we add the vertices of one such path
P_{a}
to a set
Z_{K}
. Note that
Z_{K}\cap\mathrm{South}(B)
consists of vertices that have pairwise distance at least
1/\varepsilon
in
B
, and all vertices of
K\cap\mathrm{South}(B)
are within distance
1/\varepsilon
from some vertex in
Z_{K}\cap\mathrm{South}(B)
. The analogous property holds for
Z_{K}\cap\mathrm{North}(B)
. Let
Z
be the union of the sets
Z_{K}
for each component
K
of
B[A\cup A^{\partial}]
.

We apply
Theorem

4.2
to each connected component of
B[A\cup Z]
where the point terminals are the vertices of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
corresponding to the objects of
Z_{K}\cap\partial_{0}
. Let
\widetilde{F}_{A}
be the resulting plane forest with its leaves on
\mathrm{South}(\mathsf{B})\cup\mathrm{North}(\mathsf{B})
. We add to
\widetilde{F}_{A}
the edges of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
. This results in a wireframe that we denote by
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
.

In order to simplify a forest in a brick via
Theorem

10.1
, we will need the following.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
A
be a vertex set in
B\setminus\partial_{1}
. Then the wireframe
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
defined above satisfies the conditions (P1)-(P3).

Proof.

Condition (P1) can be easily proven using the construction of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
. Recall that
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
is a (boundaried) wireframe of
B
, so by
Lemma

8.4
(
iii
) the distance between two vertices of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
are at least as big as the distance of their parents in
B
. On the other hand,
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})=\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
is a path that corresponds to a shortest path in
B
by
M2
, thus
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
is a shortest path in any wireframe inside
\mathsf{B}
that contains it.

We can analogously argue that Condition (P2) follows from
M6
and Condition (P3) follows from
M2
.
∎

We will also need the following simple lemma.

Lemma 10.4
.

The forest
\widetilde{F}_{A}=E(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\setminus E(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}))
has
(1+O(\varepsilon))|A|
vertices.

Proof.

By
Theorem

4.2
the forest
|\widetilde{F}_{A}|
has
|A|+O(t)
vertices, where
t
is the number of point terminals in the construction of
|\widetilde{Q}_{A}|
.
Thus it is sufficient to show that
t=O(\varepsilon|A|)
.

Recall from the definition of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
that the point terminals of a connected component
K
of
B[A\cup A^{\partial}]
are placed on the boundaries of polygons in
Y
that have pairwise distance at least
1/\varepsilon
in
B
. Consequently, if
u,v\in\mathrm{South}(B)\cap K
then
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{South}(B)}(u,v)\geqslant 1/\varepsilon
. If
u,v
have corresponding point terminals
\dot{u},\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{F}_{A})
, then
d_{\widetilde{F}_{A}}(u,v)\geqslant 1/\varepsilon-2=\Omega(1/\varepsilon)
, as otherwise there would be a valid shortcut in
B
between
u
and
v
formed by the parent objects along the shortest
\widetilde{F}_{A}
path from
\dot{u}
to
\dot{v}
, contradicting the definition of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Skel}}
. (In case of
\mathrm{North}(B)
, any path along
\mathrm{North}(B)
and thus along
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})
is a shortest path.)

Suppose that
K
has
t_{K}
point terminals attached, with
t^{\mathrm{South}}_{K}
on
\mathrm{South}(B)
and
t^{\mathrm{North}}_{K}
on
\mathrm{North}(B)
. We will show that
t_{K}\leqslant 2+O(\varepsilon|K|)
. Without loss of generality assume that
t^{\mathrm{North}}_{K}\geq 2
. Consider an Euler tour of the doubled spanning tree of
K
, i.e., an Euler tour of the graph where each edge of some spanning tree of
K
is doubled.

Mark one occurrence of each object that contains a point terminal in this Euler tour. The Euler tour has length at least
\Omega(t_{K}/\varepsilon)
, since it has distance at least
\Omega(1/\varepsilon)
between at least
t^{\mathrm{South}}_{K}-1+t^{\mathrm{North}}_{K}-1=t_{K}-2
pairs of consecutive marked objects. As the length of the Euler tour is
2|K|-2=\Omega(t_{K}/\varepsilon)
, we have
t_{K}=O(\varepsilon|K|)
. Summing over all components, we get that

t=\sum_{K}t_{K}\leqslant\sum_{K}\big(2+O(\varepsilon|K|)\big)=O(\varepsilon|A\cup A^{\partial}|)+2\mathrm{cc}(A\cup A^{\partial})=O(\varepsilon|A|)+O(\mathrm{cc}(A)),

as
|A^{\partial}|=O(|A|)
and removing
A^{\partial}
from
B[A\cup A^{\partial}]
only increases the number of connected components. Notice that
\mathrm{cc}(A)\leqslant\varepsilon|A|
since each component of
A
has size at least
\lambda=1/\varepsilon^{7.5}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

10.3
Stating and proving the structure theorem

We will now state and prove our structure theorem for Steiner trees. Roughly, our theorem states that there exists a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution where in each brick either no inner points are used, or when they are used, then they are from an optimum Steiner forest of some portal family of the brick. We need some definitions first.

Definition 10.5

(Portals)
.

Given
G,\varepsilon>0
and a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
, we designate
2/\varepsilon^{7.5}=2\lambda
vertices of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
as
portals
in each brick
B
of
\widetilde{M}
. The portals
p_{1},p_{2},\dots
on
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
are placed at (almost) equal distances along the cycle
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
, that is, the distances between consecutive portals along
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
differ by at most
1
.

It follows that in any brick
B
and any vertex
v
of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
there is a portal
p_{i}
such that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{Perim}(B)}(v,p_{i})\leqslant\frac{1}{\lambda}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
. We will usually denote by
P_{B}
the set of portals assigned in
B
.

Definition 10.6

(Portal-respecting generator)
.

Given
G^{\mathrm{orig}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
, a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
and portals
P_{B}
in each brick
B
of
\widetilde{M}
, a portal-respecting generator is a pair
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
where
S_{0}\subset N_{G}(p(V(\widetilde{M})),\lambda)
, and
\mathcal{F}
is a sequence of portal families
\mathcal{F}_{B}
, one for each brick
B
of
\widetilde{M}
, such that each
\mathcal{F}_{B}
consists of pairwise disjoint subsets of
P_{B}
of size at least
2
. The pair
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
generates all sets
S
that arise as the union of
S_{0}
and for each brick
B
and each portal set
U\in\mathcal{F}_{B}
an arbitrary optimum Steiner tree of
(B,U)
. The
cost
of the generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is

|S_{0}|+\sum_{B}\sum_{U\in\mathcal{F}_{B}}\mathrm{StTree}(B,U).

Definition 10.7

(Feasible portal-respecting generator)
.

Given
G^{\mathrm{orig}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
, a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
, and a terminal set
T\subset V(G)^{\mathrm{orig}}
, the
feasibility graph
of the portal-respecting generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is a graph
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
that consists of
G[S_{0}\cup\bigcup_{B\text{ brick}}\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{F}_{B}}U]
together with the complete graphs on each
U
(where
U\in\mathcal{F}_{B}
and
B
is a brick). The portal-respecting generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is
feasible
if
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is connected and its vertex set (more precisely,
10
10

10

Recall that for a set
U
of objects in
V(G)
possibly containing subpolygons, the set
U^{\mathrm{orig}}
consists of the corresponding original objects of
V(G^{\mathrm{orig}})
.

V(G(S_{0},\mathcal{F}))^{\mathrm{orig}}
) contains
T
.

We note that in the above definition the size of every generated set is at most the cost of the generator. We are now ready to state our main structure theorem for Steiner Trees.

Theorem 10.8

(Structure theorem for Steiner Trees)
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of similarly sized connected fat objects, and suppose a terminal set
T\subset V(G)
and
\varepsilon>0
are given. Then in polynomial time we can construct a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
of
G
for the terminal set
T
and corresponding portal set, such that there exists a feasible portal-respecting generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
of cost at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

Proof.

We use
Theorem

9.3
to compute a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
that satisfies the properties
M1
-
M6
.
Let
S_{\mathrm{opt}}
denote the vertex set of an optimum Steiner tree for
(G,T)
, and let
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\widetilde{M}}
denote the vertices in
S_{\mathrm{opt}}
whose object intersects the drawing of
\widetilde{M}
. Let us now fix a brick
B
. We denote by
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}
the set of vertices in
S_{\mathrm{opt}}
whose object lies in the interior of
\mathsf{B}
, i.e., those that intersect
\mathsf{B}
but are disjoint from the boundary curve
\partial\mathsf{B}
. Let
A_{\mathrm{in}}
denote the vertices of the connected components of
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}
that contain at least one inner vertex, and we denote by
A_{\mathrm{out}}
the other connected components.

Recall that
\partial_{1}
denotes the set of objects in
V(B)
that intersect the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
or the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
.
If
A_{\mathrm{in}}\neq\emptyset
, then for each connected component
K
of
A_{\mathrm{in}}
that has a neighboring vertex
v
in
S_{\mathrm{opt}}
which intersects the eastern boundary
\gamma(\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
but it is disjoint from
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))\cup\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
there exists some
v^{\prime}\in V(B)\setminus\partial_{1}
such that
v^{\prime}
connects
K
to
\mathrm{East}(B)
. Let
A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}\subset V(B)\setminus\partial_{1}
denote the set that we get by adding to
A_{\mathrm{in}}
such vertices, i.e., connecting components of
A_{\mathrm{in}}
to
\mathrm{East}(B)
and
\mathrm{West}(B)
in this manner whenever possible. Observe that
|A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}|\leqslant|A_{\mathrm{in}}|+2\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}})
.

Define the planar graph
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
as above as defined in
Section

10.2
based on the set
A:=A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}\cup V(\mathrm{East}(B))\cup V(\mathrm{West}(B))\setminus\partial_{1}
. By
Lemma

10.3
the graph
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
satisfies the properties (P1), (P2) and (P3), therefore by
Theorem

10.1
applied to
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
and
\widetilde{F}_{A}=\widetilde{Q}_{A}\setminus(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}))
, there is a forest
\widetilde{F}
satisfying the properties (F1), (F2), and (F3).
For each connected component
\widetilde{K}
of
\widetilde{F}
and each joining vertex
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{K})\cap N(V(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{Q}_{A}))\cup V(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{Q}_{A})))
, we add the portal of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
closest to
p(\dot{v})
along
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
to a set
P_{\widetilde{K}}
. Finally, we add the set
P_{\widetilde{K}}
to the family
\mathcal{F}_{B}
. Note that if
A_{\mathrm{in}}=\emptyset
, then
\widetilde{F}
is the empty graph and
\mathcal{F}_{B}=\emptyset
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the sequence of the created portal families.

Next, we add the following sets to
S_{0}
.

1.

We add all vertices of
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\widetilde{M}}
to
S_{0}
.

2.

For each brick
B
, if
X
is the vertex set of a connected component of
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}
where
X\subseteq A_{out}
, then we add
X
to
S_{0}
.

3.

For each brick
B
, if
X
is the vertex set of a maximal subpath of
\mathrm{South}(B)
or
\mathrm{North}(B)
such that the corresponding path
\widetilde{X}
is a subpath of
\widetilde{F}
, then we extend
X
with
6/\varepsilon
edges in both directions along
\mathrm{South}(B)
or
\mathrm{North}(B)
, and add all of these vertices (i.e., both
X
and the extension) to
S_{0}
.

4.

For each brick
B
, we add
V(\mathrm{West}(B))
and
V(\mathrm{East}(B))
to
S_{0}
.

5.

For each brick
B
and for each joining vertex
\dot{v}\in V(\widetilde{F})\cap V(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))\cup V(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
we add to
S_{0}
the subpath of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
connecting
p(\dot{v})
to the nearest portal.

We claim that
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is a feasible portal-respecting generator of the desired cost.

Proving that
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is feasible.

To prove the feasibility, we must ensure that the feasibility graph
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is connected and contains a subpolygon of each object in
T
.

Since terminals have corresponding vertices in
\widetilde{M}
, a path connecting two (subpolygons of) terminals in
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
can be decomposed into subpaths whose internal vertices are in the interiors of individual bricks, each of which starts and ends with an object that intersects the curve of the brick boundary
\partial\mathsf{B}
. Thus it would be sufficient to prove that the endpoint pairs of such subpaths remain connected in in
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
. In fact, in order to show connectivity, within
B
, we simply consider all the subpolygons of each object of
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
that fall in the region
\mathsf{B}
as part of
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
: this does not influence the connectedness of
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
. Let
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
denote the resulting graph; we note that
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
contains a clique on each
U\in\mathcal{F}_{B}
. (Note that we will not use these objects when we will bound the cost of
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
.)

Consider a maximal subpath
W
of
S_{\mathrm{opt}}
whose internal vertices do not intersect the boundary curve. Let
W=(a=a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{t},a_{t+1}=b)
be the corresponding path in
\mathsf{B}
where we have restricted the first and last vertex to
\mathsf{B}
, i.e.,
a,b\in V(B)
intersect
\partial B
while
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{t}\}\subseteq S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}
. Our goal now is to prove that
a
and
b
are connected by
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
. We note here that the subpolygons
a,b
are included in
B[S_{0}]
as the original
S_{0}
contains the original polygons containing
a
and
b
by Addition
1
. If
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{t}\}
is contained in a connected component of
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}
that has no inner vertex, then by Addition
2
we have
W\subseteq S_{0}
, thus
a
and
b
are connected in
S
. Otherwise the component of
B[A]
containing
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{t}\}
must contain some inner vertex. Let
K_{W}
be this a component of
S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}
.

If
a
intersects
\gamma(\mathrm{East}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
but it is disjoint from
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))\cup\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
, then by Addition
4
it is connected to the brick corners on
\mathrm{East}(B)
in
S
, and by definition, it is also connected to these corners in its component of
A
. Thus by extending
W
to a brick corner, we will henceforth assume that
a\in\partial_{1}
. The case where
a
intersects
\gamma(\mathrm{West}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
is handled analogously. Similarly, we will assume
b\in\partial_{1}
. It remains to show that
a
and
b
are connected in
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
.

Suppose now that
a
intersects the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{B}))
. Recall from the definition of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
that
A
is extended by connections to
\partial_{0}
with the set
A^{\partial}\subseteq\partial_{1}
. Let
K
be the connected component of this extended set which contains
K_{U}
. By the definition of
\widetilde{Q}_{A}
, there is a vertex
z_{a}\in Z_{K}\cap\mathrm{South}(B)
within distance at most
1+1/\varepsilon
from
a
in
B
, as otherwise the connection from
a
to a neighbor in
\mathrm{South}(B)
could have been added to
Z_{K}
to obtain a larger independent set. Analogously, if
a
intersects the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{B}))
, then there is a vertex
z_{a}\in Z_{K}\cap\mathrm{North}(B)\cap N_{B}(a,1+1/\varepsilon)
. We define a vertex
z_{b}
in the same manner within distance
1+1/\varepsilon
from
b
.

Consequently,
z_{a}
and
z_{b}
are connected in
K
. Recall that they have corresponding vertices
\dot{z}_{a},\dot{z}_{b}\in V(\widetilde{F}_{A})
which are connected in
\widetilde{F}_{A}
.
By property (F1) in
Theorem

10.1
, we have that
\dot{z}_{a}
and
\dot{z}_{b}
are also connected also in
\widetilde{F}
, i.e., they are in the same component
\widetilde{K}
of
\widetilde{F}
. If
\widetilde{K}
is a subpath of
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
or
\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
, then
z_{a}
and
z_{b}
are connected in
S_{0}
(and thus in
S
) by Addition
3
.
Otherwise, there is a joining vertex on the path of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}\cap\widetilde{F}
containing
\dot{z}_{a}
, thus
z_{a}
is connected to some portal in
P_{\widetilde{K}}
by additions
3
and
5
; similarly,
z_{b}
is connected to some portal in
P_{\widetilde{K}}
.
These portals are contained in the set
P_{\widetilde{K}}\in\mathcal{F}_{B}
and thus they are directly connected by the clique on
P_{\widetilde{K}}
inside
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
. We conclude that
z_{a}
and
z_{b}
are connected in
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
.

It remains to show that
z_{a}
is connected to
a
in
B(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
. An analogous argument shows that
z_{b}
is connected to
b
. Suppose again that
a
intersects the curve
\gamma(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{B}))
, as the case when it intersects
\gamma(\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{B}))
can be proven with the same arguments. Since
a\in S
, it is sufficient to show that it has a neighbor
\bar{a}\in\mathrm{South}(B)
such that
z_{a},\bar{a}
are connected in
S
. Notice that
\dot{z}_{a}
is on some maximal subpath of
\widetilde{F}\cap\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
. Then by Addition
3
we have that any vertex of
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
that is within distance
4/\varepsilon
from
z_{a}
along
\mathrm{South}(B)
is connected to
z_{a}
in
S
. In particular, we have that
z_{a}
is connected to
\bar{a}
, since by property
M2
we have
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{South}(B)}(z_{a},\bar{a})\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{dist}_{B}(z_{a},\bar{a})\leqslant 2\cdot(2+1/\varepsilon)\leqslant 6/\varepsilon
. This concludes the proof of feasibility.

Bounding the cost of
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
.

We prove that the cost of
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mbox{{\sc opt}}
.
In the brick
B
we have

|V(S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B})|=|A_{\mathrm{in}}|+|A_{\mathrm{out}}|.

(15)

Let
\mathrm{opt}(\mathcal{F}_{B})
denote the total number of vertices in the optimal trees for the portal family
\mathcal{F}_{B}
, and let
\bar{F}=\widetilde{F}\cap(\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}))
. We denote by
\mathrm{join}(\widetilde{F})
the number of joining vertices of
\widetilde{F}
to
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
.
We can bound the cost of
(S_{0},\mathcal{F}_{B})
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathrm{cost}(S_{0},\mathcal{F})\leqslant\;|S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\widetilde{M}}|+\sum_{B}\big(

\displaystyle\mathrm{opt}(\mathcal{F}_{B})+|S_{0}\cap B|\big)

\displaystyle<|S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\widetilde{M}}|+\sum_{B}\Big(

\displaystyle|V(\widetilde{F}\setminus\bar{F})|+\mathrm{join}(\widetilde{F})\cdot\varepsilon^{7.5}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|

\displaystyle+|A_{\mathrm{out}}|

\displaystyle+2\cdot\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F})+|V(\bar{F})|

\displaystyle+|V(\mathrm{West}(B))|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B))|

\displaystyle+\mathrm{join}(\widetilde{F})\cdot\varepsilon^{7.5}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|\Big)

Let
\Sigma_{B}
denote the summand for
B
above.
Notice that
|V(\widetilde{F}\setminus\bar{F})|+|V(\bar{F})|=V(\widetilde{F})+\mathrm{join}(\widetilde{F})
, and
\mathrm{join}(\widetilde{F})\leqslant\min(\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F}),o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5}))
, thus we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{B}

\displaystyle<|A_{\mathrm{out}}|

\displaystyle\quad+|V(\widetilde{F})|+\frac{13}{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F})

\displaystyle\quad+|V(\mathrm{West}(B))|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B))|

\displaystyle\quad+O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|)

Claim 10.9
.

|V(\widetilde{F})|+\frac{13}{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F})\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))(|A_{\mathrm{in}}|+|V(\mathrm{West}(B)|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B))|)
.

Proof of claim.

The claim is trivial if
A_{\mathrm{in}}=\emptyset
, as that implies that
\widetilde{F}
is the empty graph and
\bar{F}
is also empty.
Suppose now that
A_{\mathrm{in}}\neq\emptyset
. To show the claim, consider first a component
K_{A}
of
\widetilde{F}_{A}
. Then any pair of vertices on
\mathrm{South}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})\cup\mathrm{North}(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
that are connected by
\widetilde{F}_{A}
are also connected by some component
\widetilde{K}
of
\widetilde{F}
. We assign to
K_{A}
the component
\widetilde{K}
; notice that this is an injective assignment. Since a component of
\bar{F}
can contain at most one joining vertex, we have that

\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F})\leqslant\mathrm{cc}(\widetilde{F})+\mathrm{join}(\widetilde{F})\leqslant\mathrm{cc}(\widetilde{F}_{A})+o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5})\leqslant\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime})+2+o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5})\leqslant\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}})+2+o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5}),

since
\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime})\leqslant\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}})
and
\widetilde{F}_{A}
contains
\mathrm{East}(B)
and
\mathrm{West}(B)
which may form connected components disjoint from those of
A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}
. Recall that each connected component of
A_{\mathrm{in}}
has size at least
\lambda-O(1)
, since each of them must connect an inner vertex to
\partial_{1}
. Thus each connected component of
A_{\mathrm{in}}
has size
\Omega(\lambda)=\Omega(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
, and we get

\frac{13}{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F})\leqslant\frac{13}{\varepsilon}\cdot(\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}})+2+o(1/\varepsilon^{5.5}))\leqslant O(\varepsilon^{6.5}|A_{\mathrm{in}}|)+o(\varepsilon|A_{\mathrm{in}}|)\leqslant O(\varepsilon|A_{\mathrm{in}}|).

(16)

Next, we bound
|V(\widetilde{F})|=|E(\widetilde{F})|+\mathrm{cc}(\widetilde{F})
. By
Theorem

10.1
and
Lemma

10.4
we have

|E(\widetilde{F})|\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))|E(\widetilde{F}_{A})|<(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot(|A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B)|+|V(\mathrm{West}(B))|)+O(\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}}).

Recall that
|A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}|\leqslant|A_{\mathrm{in}}|+2\mathrm{cc}(A_{\mathrm{in}})=(1+O(\varepsilon^{7.5}))|A_{\mathrm{in}}|)
, thus
(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot|A_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot|A_{\mathrm{in}}|
.
Since (
16
) implies
\mathrm{cc}(\widetilde{F})\leqslant\mathrm{cc}(\bar{F})=O(\varepsilon|A_{\mathrm{in}}|)
, we can bound
|V(\widetilde{F})|
as

|V(\widetilde{F})|=|E(\widetilde{F})|+\mathrm{cc}(\widetilde{F})=(1+O(\varepsilon))(|A_{\mathrm{in}}|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B)|+|V(\mathrm{West}(B))|).

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Substituting this bound, we get

\displaystyle\Sigma_{B}

\displaystyle<|A_{\mathrm{out}}|+(1+O(\varepsilon))|A_{\mathrm{in}}|

\displaystyle\quad+(2+O(\varepsilon))\cdot(|V(\mathrm{West}(B))|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B))|)

\displaystyle\quad+O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|)

By Equation (
15
) and simplifying further, we have

\Sigma_{B}<(1+O(\varepsilon))|S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}|+O\big(|V(\mathrm{West}(B))|+|V(\mathrm{East}(B))|+\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|\big).

(17)

Since
\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)=|S_{\mathrm{opt}}|=|S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\widetilde{M}}|+\sum_{B}|S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{B}|
, we get the following for the cost:

\displaystyle\mathrm{cost}(S_{0},\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\leqslant\;|S_{\mathrm{opt}}^{\widetilde{M}}|+\sum_{B}\Sigma_{B}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)+\sum_{B}O\big(|V(\mathrm{West}(B)\cup\mathrm{East}(B))|+\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|\big).

Property
M5
bounds the length of the eastern and western paths, that is,
\sum_{B}|V(\mathrm{West}(B)\cup\mathrm{East}(B))|=O(\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
. Together with Property
M4
they bound the perimeter of
B
by

\sum_{B}\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|\leqslant\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{B}|V(\mathrm{West}(B)\cup\mathrm{North}(B)\cup\mathrm{East}(B)\cup\mathrm{South}(B))|=O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mathrm{StTree}(G,T).

Thus
\mathrm{cost}(S_{0},\mathcal{F})\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Next, we simplify our structure theorem further by slicing the Steiner trees generated by our portal-respecting generator into shortest paths whose vertices come from a restricted vertex set. First, we define the variant of a generator based on shortest paths.

Definition 10.10

(Path generator)
.

Given
G^{\mathrm{orig}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
and a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
a path generator is a pair
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
where
S_{0}\subset N_{G}(p(V(\widetilde{M})),\lambda)
, and
\mathcal{W}
is a sequence of triples
(B,u,v)
where
B
is a brick of
\widetilde{M}
and
u,v\in B
. The pair
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
generates all sets
S
that arise as the union of
S_{0}
and for each triplet
(B,u,v)
an arbitrary shortest path from
u
to
v
in
B
. Let
V_{\mathcal{W}}
denote the set of vertices appearing in
\mathcal{W}
.
The
cost
of the generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{Q})
is

|S_{0}|+|V_{\mathcal{W}}|+\sum_{(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)-1).

We observe that the size of a set
S
generated from
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
is at most the cost of the generator.

Definition 10.11

(Feasible path generator)
.

Let
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
be a path generator for
G^{\mathrm{orig}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
, a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
, and let
T\subset V(G^{\mathrm{orig}})
be a given terminal set. The
feasibility graph
of the path generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
is a graph
G(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
that consists of
G[S_{0}\cup V_{\mathcal{W}}]
together with the edges
uv
for each
(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}
. The path generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
is
feasible
if
G(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
is connected and its vertex set (more precisely,
11
11

11

Recall that for a set
U
of objects in
V(G)
possibly containing subpolygons, the set
U^{\mathrm{orig}}
consists of the corresponding original objects of
V(G^{\mathrm{orig}})
.

V(G(S_{0},\mathcal{W}))^{\mathrm{orig}}
) contains
T
.

With this definition we can state a modified structure theorem for path generators.

Theorem 10.12

(Structure theorem with path generators)
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of similarly sized connected fat objects, and suppose a terminal set
T\subset V(G)
and
\varepsilon>0
are given. Then in
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time we can construct a mortar graph
\widetilde{M}
of
G
for the terminal set
T
and a vertex set
W\subset V(G)
with the following properties:

(i)

|W\cap B|=2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
for each brick
B
of
\widetilde{M}

(ii)

For each
w\in W\cap B
we have
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(w,\mathrm{Perim}(B))\leqslant|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
.

(iii)

there exists a feasible path generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
with

(a)

cost at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)

(b)

V_{\mathcal{W}}\subset W

(c)

|V_{\mathcal{W}}\cap B|=O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
for each brick
B
of
\widetilde{M}
.

(d)

For each brick
B
there are at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
triplets in
\mathcal{W}
involving
B
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{M}
be a mortar graph of
G
for the terminal set
T
that is computed in polynomial time using
Theorem

9.3
. For each brick
B
, let
P_{B}
denote its portal set, which we compute in polynomial time based on
\widetilde{M}
. For any subset
Q\subset P_{B}
let
\mathrm{Can}(Q)
be a
canonical tree
which is an optimal Steiner tree for the instance
(B,Q)
that we compute using the algorithm of Dreyfus and Wagner
[
53
]
. Note here that
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
is a feasible solution to the instance
(B,Q)
and thus
|\mathrm{Can}(Q)|\leqslant|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
. The algorithm of Dreyfus and Wagner
[
53
]
finds a solution in
3^{|Q|}\mathrm{poly}(|V(B)|)=2^{O(\lambda)}\mathrm{poly}(|V(B)|)
time. Computing all the canonical trees for each of the
2^{O(\lambda)}
portal sets in each brick thus takes
2^{O(\lambda)}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time.

Next, for each canonical tree
\mathrm{Can}(Q)
fix a spanning tree
SP(Q)
of
B[\mathrm{Can}(Q)]
. Let
V_{\neq 2}(Q)
denote the set of vertices in
SP(Q)
whose degree is not
2
(i.e., this is the set of leaves and branching vertices of the spanning tree). Let
W
be the union of the sets
V_{\neq 2}(Q)
for all sets
Q\subset P_{B}
with
|Q|\geqslant 2
over all bricks
B
of
\widetilde{M}
. Since each tree
SP(Q)
has at most
|Q|\leqslant|P_{B}|\leqslant 2\lambda
leaves, we have that
|V_{\neq 2}(Q)|\leqslant 2\lambda+2\lambda-1<4\lambda
. Since there are at most
2^{2\lambda}
subsets
Q
, there are altogether
4\lambda\cdot 2^{2\lambda}=2^{O(\lambda)}
vertices of
B
that have been added to
W
, i.e.,
|W\cap B|=2^{O(\lambda)}
and
W
satisfies (
i
). Notice moreover that
\mathrm{Can}(Q)
has a subpath connecting each
w\in W\cap B
to
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
, so
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(w,\mathrm{Perim}(B))\leqslant|\mathrm{Can}(Q)|\leqslant|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
, thus (
ii
) holds.

Theorem

10.8
implies that there exists a feasible portal-respecting generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
of cost at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
. Let
S
be a set generated by
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
. Recall that
|S|
is at most the cost of
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
and its connectivity directly follows from the fact that
G(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
is feasible, as the canonical trees added to
S
ensure the connections among the vertices of each set
U\in\mathcal{F}_{B}\in\mathcal{F}
. Moreover, since
S^{\mathrm{orig}}\supseteq V(G(S_{0},\mathcal{F}))^{\mathrm{orig}}
, we have that
T\subset S^{\mathrm{orig}}
. Thus
S^{\mathrm{orig}}
is a feasible solution to the Steiner tree instance
(G,T)
of cost at most
|S^{\mathrm{orig}}|\leqslant|S|=(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

Consider now a canonical tree
\mathrm{Can}(Q)
for some
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{B}
in a brick
B
. We can decompose
SP(Q)
into maximal paths
\pi^{Q}_{1},\pi^{Q}_{2},\dots
whose internal vertices have degree
2
. Consequently, the endpoints of each
\pi^{Q}_{i}
are from
V_{\neq 2}(Q)
. The number of paths created is
|V_{\neq 2}(Q)|-1
and recall that
|V_{\neq 2}(Q)|<4\lambda
, thus there are less than
4\lambda
paths. Notice moreover that each path
\pi^{Q}_{i}
is a shortest path in
B
: indeed, otherwise it could be exchanged for a strictly shorter path in
SP(Q)
and would create a Steiner tree for the instance
(B,Q)
with fewer vertices, contradicting the minimality of
\mathrm{Can}(Q)
. For each
\pi^{Q}_{i}
, we add the triple
(B,u_{i},v_{i})
to the set
\mathcal{W}
where
u_{i},v_{i}\in V_{\neq 2}(Q)
are the end vertices of
\pi^{Q}_{i}
. Observe that the construction implies (
ii
)(b), (
ii
)(c) and (
ii
)(d).

To prove (
ii
)(a), notice that the path of
SP(Q)
connecting
u_{i}
to
v_{i}
contains
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u_{i},v_{i})
internal vertices. Consequently,

\mathrm{StTree}(B,Q)=|V(\mathrm{Can}(Q)|=|V_{\neq 2}(Q)|+\sum_{i}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u_{i},v_{i})-1).

Thus the cost of the constructed path generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
is

\displaystyle|S_{0}|+|V_{\mathcal{W}}|

\displaystyle+\sum_{(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)-1)

\displaystyle\leqslant|S_{0}|+\sum_{B}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{B}}\Big(|V_{\mathcal{W}}\cap V_{\neq 2}(Q)|+\sum_{i}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u_{i},v_{i})-1)\Big)

\displaystyle=|S_{0}|+\sum_{B}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{B}}|V(\mathrm{Can}(Q))|

\displaystyle=(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T),

where the inequality comes from the fact that each vertex of
V_{\mathcal{W}}
appears as a vertex of
V_{\neq 2}(Q)
for at least one set
Q
(possibly more than one), and the last step follows because it is the cost of the portal-respecting generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{F})
guaranteed by
Theorem

10.8
. This concludes the proof.
∎

As a corollary, we can find an approximate solution in the union of the mortar graph, of the set of outer vertices, and some fixed set of shortest paths.

Theorem 10.13

(Steiner tree spanner for intersection graphs)
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of similarly sized connected fat objects with terminal set
T
and
\varepsilon>0
given. Then in time
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
we can compute an induced subgraph
G^{\prime}
of
G
such that
G^{\prime}
has a Steiner tree spanning
T
of size at most
(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
, and
G^{\prime}
consists of at most
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
cell cliques of
G
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{M}
be a mortar graph of
G
for the terminal set
T
that is computed in polynomial time using
Theorem

9.3
. Use Theorem
10.12
to construct a vertex set
W
where
|W\cap B|=2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
. For all vertex pairs
u,v\in W\cap B
we consider a shortest path from
u
to
v
in
B
. By Theorem
10.12
(
ii
), we have that there exist vertices
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Perim}(B)
such that
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,u^{\prime})\leqslant|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
and
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(v,v^{\prime})\leqslant|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
. Moreover, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leqslant|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
, so by the triangle inequality, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)\leqslant 3|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
. Thus every shortest path added inside
B
has length at most
3|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
G
consisting of
N_{G}(V(p(\widetilde{M})),\lambda)\cup\bigcup_{B}\bigcup_{\emptyset\neq Q\subseteq P_{B}}\mathrm{Can}(Q)
. Observe that by Theorem
10.12
(
iii
)
G^{\prime}
contains a connected subgraph inducing all vertices of
T
of size
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mbox{{\sc opt}}
. Moreover, in each brick we have added
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
shortest paths, and they have been computed in
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time per brick. Since
\widetilde{M}
has
\mathrm{poly}(n)
bricks, the computation takes
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time.

To bound the number of cell cliques of outer vertices, recall that the number of cells within distance
k
of a vertex of
G
is
O(k^{2})
, thus
|N_{G}(p(V(M)),\lambda)_{\mathcal{P}}|=O\left(\frac{|V(\widetilde{M})|}{(\varepsilon^{7.5})^{2}}\right)
. Since
\widetilde{M}
is connected planar, we have
|V(\widetilde{M})|=\Theta(|E(\widetilde{M})|)
, and applying
M4
and
M5
yields
|N_{G}(p(V(\widetilde{M})),\lambda)_{\mathcal{P}}|=O(E(\widetilde{M})/\varepsilon^{15})=O(\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)/\varepsilon^{16})
. To bound the number of cliques required to cover the shortest paths, observe that the sum of brick perimeters is twice the edge count of
\widetilde{M}
, so by
M4
and
M5
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{B}\sum_{u,v\in B\cap W}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)-1)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{B}2^{O(\lambda)}\cdot 3|\mathrm{Perim}(B)|

\displaystyle=2^{O(\lambda)}\cdot 6|E(\widetilde{M})|

\displaystyle=2^{O(\lambda)}\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)\qed

10.4
Algorithm for Steiner Tree via contraction decomposition

We start by proving the following lemma, which is analogous to
Lemma

8.7
.

(18)

Let

.
Lemma

implies that without loss of generality,

of

, and as a result, get a set

1.5

Proof.

We apply
Theorem

3.4
to obtain an
\alpha
-standard graph
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
whose representation has polynomial complexity. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the corresponding cell partition.
By
Lemma

7.8
we may assume without loss of generality that
for each
C\in\mathcal{P}
we have
|C\cap T|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
and
|C|\leqslant 2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))}
(after spending
2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time to compute this).

Let
opt
denote the number of vertices in the
optimal Steiner tree of
(G,T)
. We use
Theorem

10.13
to compute a subgraph
G^{*}
with at most
2^{\gamma\lambda}\mbox{{\sc opt}}
cell cliques for some constant
\gamma
in time
2^{O(\lambda)}\mathrm{poly}(n)
that contains a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution. This is a Steiner tree spanner of the desired size, and thus proves the first part of the theorem.

Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the set of at most
2^{\gamma\lambda}\mbox{{\sc opt}}
cell cliques in
G^{*}
. We have that the optimum
\mbox{{\sc opt}}^{*}
of
G^{*}
satisfies
\mbox{{\sc opt}}^{*}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\mbox{{\sc opt}}
. It is now sufficient
to compute a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate tour in
G^{*}
for the terminal set
T
.

We apply

Theorem

6.1
on
G^{*}
and the cell partition
\mathcal{A}
for
k=\frac{2^{\gamma\lambda}}{\varepsilon}=2^{O(\lambda)}
, which yields a collection
\mathcal{X}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{X}_{k}\subset\mathcal{S}
and corresponding vertex sets
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}
. Note that
\ell=\max_{C\in\mathcal{S}}|C|\leqslant 2^{O((1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))}
.
