because contractions can only decrease the optimum cost of
a subset TSP instance.

By
Lemma

10.14
in polynomial time we can construct trees

\tau_{i}
for
(G^{*},T)
of length at most

\mathrm{StTree}(G^{*},T)+O(|\mathcal{X}_{i}|)=\mbox{{\sc opt}}^{*}+O(|\mathcal{X}_{i}|).

Theorem

6.1
guarantees that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(|\mathcal{A}|)
, and therefore there exists some
i
where

|\mathcal{X}_{i}|=O(|\mathcal{A}|/k)=O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}
.
As a result, the shortest among the trees

\tau_{i}
has length at most
\mbox{{\sc opt}}^{*}+O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}=(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}
.
∎

11
A more efficient approximation scheme for Steiner Tree

The main goal of this section is to prove a theorem that transforms our input instance into vertex-weighted instance that is easier to handle algorithmically.

Formally, the vertex-weighted variant is defined as follows.

Vertex-Weighted Steiner Tree

Input:
A graph
G
, a vertex weighting
\omega:V(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and a vertex set
T\subset G
called
terminals
.

Output:
The minimum
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exist a vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
of weight
\omega(S)\leqslant k
where
T\subset S
and
G[S]
is connected.

Most of this section is devoted to proving the following theorem.

Theorem 11.1
.

Given
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
,
T\subset V(G)
and
\varepsilon>0
, in
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time we can construct a graph
G^{\prime}
, a terminal set
T^{\prime}\subset V(G^{\prime})
, a vertex weighting
\omega:V(G^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, and a partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
V(G^{\prime})
such that the following hold.

(i)

For each class
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
we have
|C^{\prime}|\leqslant 2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
.

(ii)

\mathrm{tw}(G^{\prime}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

, and a tree decomposition of

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of width
O(1/\varepsilon^{18})
can be constructed in polynomial time.

(iii)

Given a Steiner tree of
(G^{\prime},\omega,T^{\prime})
of weight
\kappa
, we can construct a Steiner tree of
(G,T)
of size
\kappa+O(\varepsilon)\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
in polynomial time.

(iv)

There exists a Steiner tree
S^{\prime}
of
(G^{\prime},\omega,T^{\prime})
with the following properties:

(a)

The weight of
S^{\prime}
is at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

(b)

For each
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
we have
|S^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime}|\leqslant O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
.

To prove
Theorem

11.1
, we start by applying
Lemma

7.8
to get an instance that is equivalent to
(G,T)
where all classes in
\mathcal{P}
have size at most
2^{O(1/\varepsilon\log(1/\varepsilon))}
, and each class of
\mathcal{P}
contains at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
terminals from
T
.

Our proof proceeds throughout this section. For our input graph
G^{\mathrm{orig}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
recall that
\mathcal{P}
denotes its standard partition into cliques and
G_{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the graph obtained by contracting each partition class of
\mathcal{P}
. We apply
Lemma

7.8
to get an instance that is equivalent to
(G,T)
where all classes in
\mathcal{P}
have size at most
2^{O(1/\varepsilon\log(1/\varepsilon))}
; we will keep using the notation
(G,T)
for this modified instance. Let
\mbox{{\sc opt}}=\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.

Let
H
denote the plane graph obtained via
Theorem

5.2
of vertex set
\mathcal{P}
that
12
12

12

In this section we will have the plane graph
H
have the same vertex set
\mathcal{P}
as
G_{\mathcal{P}}
; we will use
\dot{\mathcal{P}}
only when we specifically need to refer to the corresponding realization.
is
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
-Lipshitz with
G_{\mathcal{P}}
under the identity map, where
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}=O(\alpha^{32})
.

We say that a clique
C\in\mathcal{P}
and the corresponding vertex
C\in V(H)
are
\tau
-outer
if there is some
v\in C
such that
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,p(V(\widetilde{M})))\leqslant\tau
. Non-
\tau
-outer cliques of
\mathcal{P}
are called
\tau
-inner
cliques or vertices, respectively. Let
\mathrm{Inn}(\tau)
denote the set of
\tau
-inner vertices.

We define
\tau=\lambda+\max(\beta^{*},6c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}})
where
\beta^{*}
is set in the next lemma (
Lemma

11.2
), and we define
Y_{\tau}=\mathrm{Inn}(\tau)
as the set of
\tau
-inner cliques.
Let
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
be the partition of
Y_{\tau}
where two connected components of
H[Y_{\tau}]
belong to the same class of
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
if and only if they are located in the same face of the plane graph
H-Y_{\tau}
. By this definition, the classes of
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
do not necessarily induce connected graphs in
H
. However, it will be crucial for our arguments that
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
is defined this way: we want to ensure that, after consolidating the classes of the partition
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
, at most one consolidated vertex appears in a face of the graph induced by the vertices outside
Y_{\tau}
, hence the existence of these consolidated vertices change treewidth only by constant factor.

11.1
Relating the classes of
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
to brick regions

Lemma 11.2
.

There exists a number
\beta^{*}=O(c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2.1})
such that for any
\tau>\beta^{*}
and the above definition of
Y_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
the following holds. Each class of
\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}
consists of cliques that are located in the interior of a single brick region.

Proof.

The lemma is implied by the following: if
u,v\in Y_{\tau}
are in the same face of
H-Y_{\tau}
, then the vertices of
G
inside the cliques
u
and
v
are in the same brick of
G
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
u,v\in Y_{\tau}
are in the same face of
H-Y_{\tau}
but in distinct bricks
B_{u}
and
B_{v}
. Since the vertices of
G
inside
u
and
v
are far from the brick boundaries (at distance more than
\tau
), we have that all objects in
u
are in the interior of the brick region
\mathsf{B}_{u}
and similarly all objects in
v
fall in the interior of
\mathsf{B}_{v}
.

Without loss of generality, assume that
\mathsf{B}_{u}
is a bounded face of
\widetilde{M}
(as otherwise the proof can work on
\mathsf{B}_{v}
, since
\widetilde{M}
has exactly one unbounded face). Let
\gamma_{B}=\gamma(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{u})
be the Jordan curve given by the cycle
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{u}
of
\widetilde{M}
around the face
\mathsf{B}_{u}
. Notice that
\gamma_{B}
has a unique bounded region (
\mathsf{B}_{u}
) containing
u
and its unbounded region contains
v
. We will need the following claim.

In what follows, we will think of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
as an intersection graph whose objects are given by the unions of objects in each class of
\mathcal{P}
. Our strategy will be to consider
\gamma
and gradually change it into a closed walk of
H-Y_{\tau}
—another closed curve— that “follows”
\gamma
. We will show that in each gradual change of
\gamma
, the current closed curve must still separate
u
and
v
. We will need the following two claims.

Claim 11.3
.

Let
\varrho
be a closed curve that can be covered by the objects of some closed walk
W
of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
of length
|W|\leqslant\frac{\tau}{5\alpha}
and suppose that there is some vertex
v\in V(W)\cap(p(V(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{u}))_{\mathcal{P}}
. Then both
u
and
v
are in the unbounded component of
\varrho
.

Proof of claim.

We will show that
u
is in the unbounded component; the proof for
v
is analogous. Notice first that
u
cannot be intersected by any object of
W
, since all vertices of
W
are within distance
|W|
from
\mathrm{Perim}(B)
in
B
and thus they are
3|W|
-outer; in particular, they cannot be
\tau
-inner as
\tau>3|W|
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
u
is in some bounded component
\mathsf{R}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\varrho
, and consider a shortest path from
u
to
\mathrm{Perim}(B_{u})
. Since
u
is
\tau
-inner, the shortest path has at least
\tau-3|W|
vertices that are
3|W|
-inner, thus they are disjoint from the objects of
W
. Consequently, there are at least
(\tau-3|W|)/2
vertices on this shortest path that are pairwise disjoint, and all of them are in a bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\varrho
. Since these are original objects of
G
, each of them contains a disk of diameter at least
\sqrt{2}
, thus each of these objects has area at least
\pi/2>1
. Consequently,
\mathsf{R}
has to cover these pairwise disjoint objects must have area more than
(\tau-3c_{W})/2
.

On the other hand, observe that
\mathrm{diam}(\varrho)\leqslant 3\alpha|W|
(as each object of
V(W)
has diameter at most
3\alpha
). By the isodiameteric inequality, we have
\mathrm{Area}(\mathsf{R})\leqslant\mathrm{diam}(\mathsf{R})^{2}\cdot\frac{\pi}{4}
, thus

\frac{\tau-3|W|}{2}\leqslant 3\alpha|W|\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad|W|\geqslant\frac{\tau}{3\alpha+3/2}.

On the other hand, we have

c_{W}\leqslant\frac{\tau}{5\alpha}<\frac{\tau-1}{3\alpha+3/2},

as
\alpha>1
. Therefore, we have arrived at a contradiction.
∎

Consider a closed curve
\gamma
that is potentially self-intersecting. Notice that
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\gamma
has one or more connected components, with exactly one unbounded component and
0
or more bounded components.
Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a closed curve and let
\sigma
be a continuous subcurve of
\gamma
, i.e.,
\sigma=\gamma|_{[a,b]}
. We denote by
\gamma-\sigma
the remaining closed curve including the endpoints, i.e.,
\gamma-\sigma=\gamma|_{[0,a]}\cup\gamma|_{[b,1]}
.

Claim 11.4
.

Let
\varrho
be a closed curve whose unbounded component contains
u
and
v
, and let
\gamma
be a closed curve where the unbounded component contains
v
and some bounded component contains
u
. Assume moreover that there is some continuous subcurve
\sigma
of
\gamma
such that
\sigma
also appears as a continuous subcruve of
\varrho
. Suppose that
\varrho
and
\gamma
are both piecewise linear curves of finite complexity. Then the closed curve
\gamma^{\prime}
obtained by concatenating
\varrho-\sigma
and
\gamma-sigma
has
u
in one of its bounded components, and
v
in its unbounded component.

Proof of claim.

Observe that the intersection of the unbounded components of
\gamma
and
\sigma
is disjoint from both curves and thus it is covered by the unbounded component of
\gamma^{\prime}
. Since
v
is in this intersection, it must be in the unbounded component of
\gamma^{\prime}
. It remains to show that
u
is in some bounded component of
\gamma^{\prime}
. Suppose the contrary:
u
is in the unbounded component of
\gamma^{\prime}
.

Then
u
and
v
are both in the intersection of the unbounded components of both
\varrho
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, thus they can be connected by a curve inside the intersection of these components. In particular, the curve connecting
u
and
v
is disjoint from
\gamma^{\prime}
and disjoint from
\varrho
. Notice that since
\gamma
can be covered by
\gamma^{\prime}\cup\varrho
, we have that the curve connecting
u
and
v
is disjoint from
\gamma
. This contradicts the fact that
u
and
v
are in different components of
\gamma
.
∎

Let
\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{B}
and we extend
\gamma_{0}
in several steps using
11.4
.
For each vertex
\dot{x}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
let
\gamma_{\dot{x}}
be a closed piecewise linear curve connecting
\dot{x}
to
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}
and back inside
x:=p(\dot{x})
. (Recall that
x_{\mathcal{P}}
is the clique of
\mathcal{P}
containing
x
and
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the grid point assigned to
x_{\mathcal{P}}
that stabs all objects in
x_{\mathcal{P}}
.) Clearly we can choose
\gamma_{\dot{x}}
to have complexity at most
2\mathrm{compl}(x)
. The curve
\gamma_{\dot{x}}
can be covered by a single object
x_{\mathcal{P}}
of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
and thus a trivial walk. We will ensure that
\tau>5\alpha
, thus by
11.3
we have that
\gamma_{\dot{x}}
does has both
u
and
v
in its unbounded face. Consequently, if we extend
\gamma_{0}
to also include
\gamma_{\dot{x}}
(notice that this can be done as
\dot{x}
appears on both curves), then by
11.4
the resulting curve
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
still has the property that
u
is in one of its bounded components while
v
is in its unbounded component.

We repeat the above procedure for each
\dot{x}\in V(\widetilde{\mathsf{B}})
, to obtain a curve
\gamma_{1}
with
u
in one of its bounded components and
v
in its unbounded components.

Now for each edge
\dot{x}\dot{y}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
, consider the subcurve
\sigma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
of
\gamma_{1}
given by the curve from
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}
to
\dot{x}
(a part of
\gamma_{\dot{x}}
), the
edge
\dot{x}\dot{y}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
, and the path from
\dot{y}
to
\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}
(a part of
\gamma_{\dot{y}}
).

Consider the shortest path in
H
connecting
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}
in
H
. Notice that there is such a path: indeed,
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}},\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}\in V(H)
; if
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}=\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}
, then a path of length
0
connects them.
Suppose that
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}\neq\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
x
and
y
are neighboring by the wireframe property of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
, thus
x_{\mathcal{P}}
and
y_{\mathcal{P}}
are neighboring cliques, i.e.,
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}
is an edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.
By the Lipschitz property of
H
, there must be a path of at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
edges connecting
\dot{x}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\dot{y}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let
P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
be this path in
H
, and we set
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
to be the concatenation of
P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
and
\sigma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
, which results in a closed curve. We will now bound the geometric diameter of
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
and show that it can be covered by constantly many cliques of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Note that if
\dot{w}
is a vertex of
H
on
P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
, then it is within
H
-distance
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
from
\dot{x}
and
\dot{y}
, thus it is within
G_{\mathcal{P}}
-distance
c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}
from
x_{\mathcal{P}}
and
y_{\mathcal{P}}
by the Lipschitz property. By
Theorem

5.2
we have that each edge of
P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
can be covered by at most
O(\alpha^{34})=O(c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{1.1})
objects of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. Thus
P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
can be covered by cliques that are within
G_{\mathcal{P}}
-distance
c^{G_{\mathcal{P}}}_{\gamma}=O(c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2.1})
from
x_{\mathcal{P}}
. Since
\sigma
can be covered by two objects of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, we conclude that
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
can be covered by
c^{\mathrm{cover}}=O(c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2.1})
objects of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.

We can now set

\beta^{*}:=c^{\mathrm{cover}}\cdot 5\alpha+1=O(c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2.1}).

Observe that the vertices of
H
(and of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
) participating in this cover are
\beta^{*}
-outer, and in particular, we have that
V(P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}})
does not have any
\tau
-inner vertices, thus it is disjoint from
Y_{\tau}
.

Consequently,
11.3
implies that
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
has both
u
and
v
in its unbounded component.
Since the curve
\sigma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
is a shared subcurve of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
, we can use
11.4
to show that the curve
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
obtained as the concatenation of
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}-\sigma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
and
\gamma_{1}-\sigma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
has
u
in one of its bounded components and
v
in its unbounded component.
Notice that the subcurves
\gamma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
from a partition of
\gamma_{1}
, thus we can apply the above procedure repeatedly on each edge
\dot{x}\dot{y}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}
.

The resulting curve
\gamma_{2}
does not contain any subcurve
\sigma_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
, i.e., it is the concatenation of the paths
P_{\dot{x}\dot{y}}
, which are paths of
H-Y_{\tau}
. Thus,
\gamma_{2}
is a walk of
H-Y_{\tau}
that has
u
in a bounded face and
v
in the unbounded face. In particular, there is some cycle in this walk (and in
H-Y_{\tau}
) where
u
is inside the cycle and
v
is outside. This contradicts the fact that
u,v
are in the same face of
H-Y_{\tau}
, concluding the proof.
∎

11.2
A contraction decomposition that avoids
Y_{\tau}

We will need a bound on the treewidth of certain plane graphs. The
radial graph

\mathrm{Radial}(G)
of a plane graph
G
is the bipartite graph where vertices are either in
V(G)
or they are obtained by adding a vertex
v_{F}
to each face
F
of
G
. A vertex
v_{F}
is then connected to
v\in V(G)
in
\mathrm{Radial}(G)
if
v
appears on the face
F
. Let
V_{F}
denote the set of vertices corresponding to faces. The dual graph
G^{\mathrm{dual}}
of
G
is a plane multigraph on
V_{F}
where two vertices are connected by
k
edges if and only if the corresponding faces have
k
shared edges in
G
.
We note that for a connected graph
G
we have that
(G^{\mathrm{dual}})^{\mathrm{dual}}
is
isomorphic to
G
and
\mathrm{Radial}(G^{\mathrm{dual}})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Radial}(G)
. A result of Demaine
et al.

[
50
]
gives the following bound.

Lemma 11.5

(Corollary of
[
50
]
)
.

For any plane graph
G
we have that
\mathrm{tw}(G\cup\mathrm{Radial}(G))=O(\mathrm{tw}(G))
.

Proof.

Demaine
et al.

[
50
]
proved that
\mathrm{tw}(H^{\mathrm{dual}}\cup\mathrm{Radial}(H))=\mathrm{tw}(H^{\mathrm{dual}}\cup\mathrm{Radial}(H^{\mathrm{dual}}))=O(\mathrm{tw}(H^{\mathrm{dual}}))
for any plane graph
H
. For each connected component
G_{i}
of
G
let
H=G_{i}^{\mathrm{dual}}
, and apply the above result to get
\mathrm{tw}(G_{i}\cup\mathrm{Radial}(G_{i}))=O(\mathrm{tw}(G_{i}))
. Now a tree decomposition of
G\cup\mathrm{Radial}(G)
of width
O(\max_{i}\mathrm{tw}(G_{i}))=O(\mathrm{tw}(G))
can be obtained as follows. Remove the vertex of
\mathrm{Radial}(G_{i})
corresponding to the outer face of
G_{i}
from each bag, and add the vertex
v_{G}
corresponding to the outer face of
G
to every bag; let
\mathcal{T}_{i}
be the obtained tree decomposition. Finally, a singleton bag containing only
v_{G}
is connected to an arbitrary bag of each tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}
. This is a valid tree decomposition of
G\cup\mathrm{Radial}(G)
of width
1+O(\max_{i}\mathrm{tw}(G_{i}))=O(\mathrm{tw}(G))
.
∎

We have by
Theorem

9.3

M4
and
M5
that there are
O(\mbox{{\sc opt}}/\varepsilon)
vertices of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
containing vertices of
p(V(\widetilde{M}))
. Recall that all vertices of
\mathcal{P}-Y_{\tau}
are within distance
O(\lambda)=O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
to some vertex of
p(V(\widetilde{M}))
. Since
G_{\mathcal{P}}
connects grid points within constant distance of each other, the
r
-neighborhood of a vertex has size at most
O(r^{2})
, so in particular we have that

|V(H-Y_{\tau})|=|\mathcal{P}-Y_{\tau}|\leqslant|N_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(p(V(\widetilde{M}))\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{P},\lambda)|\leqslant O(\lambda^{2})\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}/\varepsilon=O(\mbox{{\sc opt}}/\varepsilon^{16}).

Next, apply
Theorem

2.2
on
H-Y_{\tau}
to get a partition
E_{1},E_{2},\dots,E_{k_{X}}
consisting of
k_{X}:=1/\varepsilon^{18}
edge sets. Notice that since
V(E_{i})\leqslant 2|E_{i}|
, the planarity of
H-Y_{\tau}
implies that

\sum_{i=1}^{k_{X}}|V(E_{i})|\leqslant 2\sum_{i=1}^{k_{X}}|E_{i}|\leqslant 6|V(H-Y_{\tau})|.

In particular, there is some index
i
such that
X:=V(E_{i})
has size at most
6|V(H-Y_{\tau})|/k_{X}
.
Moreover, we have

\mathrm{tw}((H-Y_{\tau})\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X)\leqslant\mathrm{tw}((H-Y_{\tau})/E_{i})=O(k_{X})=O(1/\varepsilon^{18}).

(19)

We can bound the size of
|X|
as follows:

|X|\leqslant|6|V(H-Y_{\tau})|/k_{X}=O(\mbox{{\sc opt}}/\varepsilon^{16})/k_{X}=O(\varepsilon^{2}\mbox{{\sc opt}}).

(20)

Notice that
X
is disjoint from
Y_{\tau}
, and we denote by
\mathcal{X}
the partition of
X
to its connected components induced in the graph
H-Y_{\tau}
(or, equivalently in
H
).

that contains some point from

, and let

∎

Define
Y:=N_{H-X}(Y_{\tau},c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
, thus, by definition,
Y
is disjoint from
X
.
Let

.

Lemma 11.7
.

We have
Y\subset\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda+3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}})
, and for any
u,v\in Y
if
u,v
are in the same class of
\mathcal{Y}
, then they are in the interior of the same brick of
\widetilde{M}
.

Proof.

Notice that
Y=N_{H-X}(Y_{\tau},c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
is a subset of
N_{H}(Y_{\tau},c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
. So, by the Lipschitz property we have:

Y\subset N_{H}(Y_{\tau},c_{\mathrm{Lip}})\subset N_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(Y_{\tau},c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}).

Recall that
Y_{\tau}=\mathrm{Inn}(\tau)
, and since each edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
can be simulated in
G
by a path of length at most
3
, we have that each vertex of
Y
is
(\tau-3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}})
-inner, thus —as
\tau\geqslant\lambda+6c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}
— we have that
Y\subset\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda+3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}})
.

To prove the second claim, let
P
be a path in
(H\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{Y}_{\tau})[\xi_{0}(Y)]
connecting
\xi_{0}(u)
to
\xi_{0}(v)
. It is sufficient to prove that for each edge
\xi_{0}(a)\xi_{0}(b)
of this path the vertices
a,b
fall in the same brick of
B
. Consider the original edge
ab\in E(H[Y])
. By the Lipschitz property, there is a path
P_{ab}
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
connecting
a
to
b
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. Moreover, since
Y\subset\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda+3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}})
, we have that the vertices of this shortest path
P_{ab}
are in
\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda+3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}-3c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
. Thus the path
P_{ab}
can never leave its starting brick, as it cannot reach its boundary, i.e.,
a
and
b
are in the same brick.
∎

The graphs

,
G^{*}
, and their properties

Notice that

and

.
Consequently, for each

denote the new consolidation map.

We define

. Now (
20
) and the fact that

connects cliques corresponding to integer grid points that are within constant distance, we have

|Z|=O(|X|c^{4}_{\mathrm{Lip}})=O(|X|)=O(\varepsilon^{2}\mbox{{\sc opt}}).

(21)

as follows.

If
v\in B
is a consolidated vertex, i.e.,
v\in\xi_{0}(X\cup Y)
, then we add the corresponding vertex of

to
B^{*}
, or formally, we include the vertex
\xi(\xi_{0}^{-1}(v))
to
B^{*}
. We observe that
\xi(\xi_{0}^{-1}(w))
is always a single vertex: indeed,
\xi_{0}^{-1}(w)
can only be of size more than one if
w\in\xi_{0}(X)
or
w\in\xi_{0}(Y)
.
In the former case we have
\xi(\xi_{0}^{-1}(w))=w
as
\xi_{0}
and
\xi
are the same over
X
. In the latter case, we have that there is a unique
Y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}
containing
Y_{j}=\xi_{0}^{-1}(w)
thus
\xi(\xi_{0}^{-1}(w))=\xi(Y_{i})
is also a single vertex.

Now let
v\in B
be some non-consolidated vertex of

, i.e., let

. Consider the set of paths

in

starting at

that have length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
whose internal vertices are not consolidated vertices. (That is, the starting vertex
v
and every internal vertex of these paths is from
\mathcal{P}\setminus(X\cup Y)
, while the end vertex of the path may or may not be consolidated.)
For each vertex
w\in\xi_{0}(\mathcal{P})
that appears as an endpoint of some path in
\mathcal{Q}_{v}
we add
\xi(\xi_{0}^{-1}(w))
to the bag
B^{*}
. As seen earlier,
\xi(\xi_{0}^{-1}(w))
is always a single vertex. Let
\mathcal{T}^{*}
be the constructed tree decomposition.

Let us now bound the size of the bag
B^{*}
based on
|B|
. Notice first that
B^{*}
contains at most one vertex for each consolidated vertex of
B
. Additionally, for each non-consolidated vertex
v\in B\setminus\xi_{0}(X\cup Y)
we consider all paths of length
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
starting at
v
whose internal vertices are also non-consolidated. Recall that
H
was obtained via
Theorem

5.2
, which guarantees that the maximum degree of
H
is bounded by a constant
c_{\Delta(H)}
. Consequently, the number of paths starting at
v
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
H
can be bounded as
c_{\Delta(H)}^{c_{\mathrm{Lip}}}=O(1)
. Notice that the same bound holds for
|\mathcal{Q}_{v}|
as we allowed only the last vertex of a path of
\mathcal{Q}_{v}
to be consolidated. Thus, for each non-consolidated vertex
v
of
B
we have added at most
O(1)
vertices to
B^{*}
. We conclude that
|B^{*}|=O(|B|)
and the width of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
is at most constant times the width of
\mathcal{T}
, thus the width of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
is
O(1/\varepsilon^{18})
. Notice that the construction of
G^{*}
and
\mathcal{T}^{*}
can be obtained in polynomial time when a tree decomposition of
H\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

of width
O(1/\varepsilon^{18})
is given, which we have also obtained in polynomial time using

11.6
.

Next, we verify that each vertex

of

appears in a non-empty connected subtree of

.

We are now ready to define the edges of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
with vertex set
\mathcal{P}
as follows:

•

If
uv
is an edge in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\xi(u),\xi(v)
appear together in some bag of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
, then include the edge
uv
in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

•

If there are
u,v\in Z
and there is a path
Q_{uv}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}[Z]
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
such that
\xi(u),\xi(v)
appear in some bag of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
, then include the edge
uv
in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Notice that the definition of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
implies properties (i) and (
iii
). It is also straightforward to verify that
\mathcal{T}^{*}
is a valid tree decomposition of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

as the edges of

were defined in such a way to ensure that they are represented in some bag

of

.

It remains to prove Property (
ii
). Let
uv\in E(G_{\mathcal{P}})
, and
uv\not\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
, and let
Q
be a path of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
H
connecting
u
and
v
. We need to prove that there exists a path of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}[Z]
.

We distinguish three cases based on the interaction of the vertices of
Q
with
X
and
Y
.

•

The path
Q
has a vertex
x\in X
.

We will show that each edge
ab
of
Q
is also an edge of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
, which will prove that
Q
has the desired properties. Note that
ab\in E(H)
implies that either
\xi_{0}(a)=\xi_{0}(b)
or there is an edge
\xi_{0}(a)\xi_{0}(b)\in H\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}_{\tau})
, thus
\xi_{0}(a)
and
\xi_{0}(b)
appear in some bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
. Moreover, there is a path
Q_{ab}
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
connecting
a
and
b
by the Lipschitz property. The concatenation of these paths for each edge
ab
of
Q
is a walk
Q^{G_{\mathcal{P}}}
of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
of length at most
c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}
that still contains some
v\in V(Q)\cap X\subseteq V(Q^{G_{\mathcal{P}}})\cap X
. Recall that
Z=N_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(X,c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}})
, thus
V(Q^{G_{\mathcal{P}}})\subset Z
, and in particular, the vertices of
Q_{ab}
are in
Z
. The definition of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
now yields that
ab\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
, as claimed.

•

The path
Q
has a vertex
y\in Y
, but no vertex from
X
.

It follows that
V(Q)\subseteq N_{H-X}(y,c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
, and thus it is in a single connected component of
(H-X)[Y]
. This connected component of
(H-X)[Y]
falls in a single class
Y_{i}
of
\mathcal{Y}
by the definition of
\mathcal{Y}
. Thus
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
contains the edge
uv
because
\xi(u)=\xi(v)=\xi(Y_{i})
. The edge
uv
is therefore a single-edge path with the desired property.

•

The path
Q
has no vertex from
X\cup Y
.

If
u=\xi_{0}(u)
appears in some bag
B
, then both
\xi(u)
and
\xi(v)
appear in
B^{*}
as
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{u}
. It follows that the edge
uv
is in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
and serves as a single-edge path with the desired property.∎

For a vertex
u\in V(G)
recall that
u_{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the corresponding class of
\mathcal{P}
containing
u
. Let
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
be the graph from
Lemma

11.8
.

Definition 11.9
.

We define
G^{*}
to be the graph on the vertex set
V(G^{\mathrm{orig}})
where we include an edge
uv
if and only if:

(i)

u_{\mathcal{P}}=v_{\mathcal{P}}
or

(ii)

u_{\mathcal{P}}v_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
and either a)
uv\in E(G)
or b)
u_{\mathcal{P}}v_{\mathcal{P}}\not\in E(G_{\mathcal{P}})

Observation 11.10
.

G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}=G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

.

Proof.

First, we show that
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a subgraph of
G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{P}
.
Suppose that
u_{\mathcal{P}}v_{\mathcal{P}}
is an edge in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
. We need to show that there is an edge between some vertex
u\in u_{\mathcal{P}}
and
v\in v_{\mathcal{P}}
in
G^{*}
. Notice that if there is some
uv\in E(G)
, then
uv
is included in
G^{*}
by part (
ii
) of the definition. On the other hand, if there is no such edge, then
u_{\mathcal{P}}
and
v_{\mathcal{P}}
are not connected in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, thus
u_{\mathcal{P}}v_{\mathcal{P}}\not\in G_{\mathcal{P}}
implies that all edges between any
u\in u_{\mathcal{P}}
and
v\in v_{\mathcal{P}}
have been added to
G^{*}
.

To show that
G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}

is a subgraph of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
, notice that any edge
uv
to be included in
G^{*}
where

requires that
u_{\mathcal{P}}v_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
. Thus

}

can only contain edges that are present in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

We can define an analogous consolidation on
V(G^{*})
via a new notation. For a set
A\subset\mathcal{P}
define
X^{\cup}:=\bigcup_{C\in X}C
. For a subpartition
\mathcal{Z}
over
\mathcal{P}
(i.e., a partition of a subset of
\mathcal{P}
) we define
\mathcal{Z}^{\cup}=\{Z^{\cup}\mid Z\in\mathcal{Z}\}
to be the corresponding subpartition of
V(G)
. We will now consider the consolidation
G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

, and we denote by
\psi:V(G)\rightarrow V(G)\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

the consolidation map.

Lemma 11.11
.

Let
u,v\in V(G)
whose objects intersect the same brick region
\mathsf{B}
of
G
, and let
P
be a path from
u
to
v
in
G
. Then in polynomial time we can construct a walk
P^{*}
in
G^{*}
from
u
to
v
with the following properties:

1.

V(P^{*})\subset N_{G^{*}}(V(P_{B}),3c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
.

2.

For any
a,b\in V(P)
we have
V(P^{*}[a,b]\setminus Z^{\cup})\subset V(P[a,b])\subset V(P^{*}[a,b])
.

Proof.

Consider the edges of
P
in order from
u
to
v
. Let
pq
be the next edge of
P
in this order. If
pq\in E(G^{*})
, then we add
pq
to
P^{*}
. Suppose that
pq\not\in E(G^{*})
.
By the definition of
G^{*}
(
Definition

11.9
) this implies that
p
and
q
are in different cliques of
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,
p_{\mathcal{P}}\neq q_{\mathcal{P}}
, and
p_{\mathcal{P}}q_{\mathcal{P}}\not\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
. By
Lemma

11.8
(
ii
) we have that
p_{\mathcal{P}}
and
q_{\mathcal{P}}
can be connected by some path of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}[Z]
. Such a path corresponds to a path of length at most
3c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G^{*}[Z^{\cup}]
from
p
to
q
by
5.1
. We add this path to
P^{*}
. Once all edges of
P
are processed, the resulting walk
P^{*}
satisfies the above properties.
∎

11.4
Finishing the proof of
Theorem

11.1

Next, we define a new partition
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
of
Y
that is a coarsening of
\mathcal{Y}
. The classes
Y_{i},Y_{j}\in\mathcal{Y}
will be added to the same class of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
if and only if they are in the same connected component of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}[Y]
, that is, we unify two classes of
\mathcal{Y}
while there is an edge in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
connecting them.

Lemma 11.12
.

If
\psi(a)=\psi(b)
for some
a,b\in Y^{\cup}
then
a,b
are in the same brick.

Proof.

Lemma

11.7
implies that
Y\subset\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda+3c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2})
, thus any edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
induced by
Y
is an edge connecting a pair of inner cliques; in particular, any edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
incident to
Y
has both its endpoints in the inner clique of the same brick. Now if the corresponding cliques
a_{\mathcal{P}}
and
b_{\mathcal{P}}
are in the same class of
\mathcal{Y}
, then they are in the same brick by the second part of
Lemma

11.7
. Suppose that this is not the case, but
a_{\mathcal{P}}
and
b_{\mathcal{P}}
are in the same class of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Recall that the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
was based on a coarsening of
\mathcal{Y}
where two partition classes were unified if and only if they were connected by some edge in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Consider now some edge
uv
of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
. By property (
iii
) of
Lemma

11.8
we have that either
uv
is also an edge of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
—and by the argument above we automatically have that
u
and
v
are
\lambda
-inner cliques of the same brick—, or there exists a path
P_{uv}
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
from
u
to
v
. By
5.1
every vertex of this path are within distance
3c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
from
u
, thus they are
(\lambda+3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}-3c_{\mathrm{Lip}})
-inner vertices of the brick containing
u
. Thus
u
and
v
must be in the same brick. Since each class of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
can be connected by such paths, we have that each class of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
is contained in a single brick, and in particular, the objects in these cliques are original objects in the same brick of
\widetilde{M}
.
∎

Finishing the proof of
Theorem

11.1
.

Recall that we have applied
Lemma

7.8
, thus all classes in
\mathcal{P}
have size at most
2^{O(1/\varepsilon\log(1/\varepsilon))}
.

We apply
Theorem

10.12
on
(G,T)
to obtain a vertex set
W
where
|W\cap B|=2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
for each brick
B
of
\widetilde{M}
. Consider the consolidation
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}:=G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathcal{X}^{\cup},\hat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\cup})
defined above. We
will modify
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}
around the vertices of
\psi(Y^{\cup})
in order to construct
G^{\prime}
.

The construction of
G^{\prime}
.

As seen in
Section

10
we will occasionally need to refer to a subpolygon
u\in B
and the corresponding original object
u^{\mathrm{orig}}
from the original graph
G^{\mathrm{orig}}
.

Consider a vertex
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
, and let
B
be the brick containing the class
A_{y}:=\psi^{-1}(y)\in\hat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\cup}
(there exists such a brick by
Lemma

11.12
). We will add new vertices and edges to
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}
related to
y
.
Next, for each vertex pair
u,v\in W\cap B
we fix a shortest path in
B
from
u^{\mathrm{orig}}
to
v^{\mathrm{orig}}
; let
P_{uv}
be the corresponding path in
G
(using original objects of
V(G)
, from
u^{\mathrm{orig}}
to
v^{\mathrm{orig}}
). We will now work exclusively on
V(G^{\mathrm{orig}})
, so for the sake of brevity we will use
u,v
to refer to
u^{\mathrm{orig}},v^{\mathrm{orig}}
throughout the definition of
G^{\prime}
. Our goal is to model the behavior of this path in each set
A_{y}
it passes through; in order to do that, we will identify entry and exit points
\bar{a}^{uv}_{i},\bar{b}^{uv}_{i}
for each path that pass through some sets
A_{y}
. Due to the small difference between
G
and
G^{*}
we need to define several paths.
For each path
P_{uv}
we define the corresponding walk
P^{*}_{uv}
in
G^{*}
as in
Lemma

11.11
.

Next, we slice
P^{*}_{uv}
as we walk from
u
to
v
as follows. Let
a_{1}
be the first vertex on
P^{*}_{uv}\in Y^{\cup}
when starting from
u
such that
a_{1}\in Y^{\cup}
. We set
y_{1}=\psi(a_{1})
, and let
b_{1}
be the last vertex from
A_{y_{1}}
in
P_{uv}
; consequently,
V(P^{*}_{uv})\setminus V(P^{*}_{uv}[a_{1},b_{1}])
is disjoint from
A_{y_{1}}
. See
Figure

14
. (We remark that when
u,v\in A_{y_{1}}
, then
a_{1}=u
and
b_{1}=v
, and no slicing will take place.)
Assuming some sequence of vertices
a_{1},b_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},b_{i-1}
along
P^{*}_{uv}
is already defined, let
a_{i}
be the first vertex of
P^{*}_{uv}
after
b_{i-1}
that falls into
Y^{\cup}
. We define
y_{i}=\psi(a_{i})
, and let
b_{i}
be the last vertex of
P^{*}_{uv}
that falls into
A_{y_{i}}
. The procedure stops once the suffix of
P^{*}_{uv}
after
b_{i}
is disjoint from
Y^{\cup}
.

Figure 14:
Slicing a path
P^{*}_{uv}
that can behave differently than
P_{uv}
inside
Z
(gray shaded region). We slice
P^{*}_{uv}
according to its interaction with
A_{y_{i}}\subset Y^{\cup}
(red shaded regions).

For a given
a_{i}\neq u
let
(a^{\prime})^{uv}_{i}=a^{\prime}_{i}
be the vertex preceding
a_{i}
along
P_{uv}
. Similarly, let
(b^{\prime})^{uv}_{i}=b^{\prime}_{i}
be the vertex succeeding
b_{i}
. We note that it may happen that
b^{\prime}_{i}=a^{\prime}_{i+1}
. Observe that by the definition of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}=G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathcal{X}^{\cup},\hat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\cup})
and the fact that
a^{\prime}_{i}a_{i}
and
b_{i}b^{\prime}_{i}
are edges of
G^{*}
we have that
\psi(a^{\prime}_{i})y_{i}
and
y_{i}\psi(b^{\prime}_{i})
are edges in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}
. Moreover, we have that
a^{\prime}_{i},b^{\prime}_{i}
are not in
Y^{\cup}
.
To see why, consider first the edge
a^{\prime}_{i}a_{i}\in E(G^{*})
. Since
a^{\prime}_{i}
is not in
A_{y_{i}}
, we have that
a^{\prime}_{i}
is not in the same class of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\cup}
as
a_{i}
, and in particular, they are in different classes of
\mathcal{P}
. Thus there is a corresponding edge
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{\mathcal{P}}(a_{i})_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
where
v_{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the class of
\mathcal{P}
containing
v\in V(G)
.
Recall that by the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
we have that no two classes of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
can be adjacent in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}
(as such classes would have been unified), thus
(a_{i})_{\mathcal{P}}\in Y
implies
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{\mathcal{P}}\not\in Y
and thus
a^{\prime}_{i}\not\in Y^{\cup}
.
As a result, each path
P^{*}_{uv}
is sliced into several subwalks using the vertices
a^{\prime}_{i},b^{\prime}_{i}
. For a subwalk
Q^{*}
whose endpoints are consecutive among
u,a^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{k},b^{\prime}_{k},v
we say that it is a
Y
-slice
if its internal vertices are in
V(Q^{*})\subset Y^{\cup}
and otherwise (when
V(Q^{*})\subset B\setminus Y^{\cup}
) it is called an
ordinary slice
. For a fixed brick
B
let
\mathcal{Q}_{B}^{Y}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{B}^{\mathrm{ord}}
denote these collections of
Y
-slices and ordinary slices, and let
\mathcal{Q}^{Y}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{ord}}
denote the set of all slices in all bricks.

With the above definitions at hand, we are ready to define
G^{\prime}
based on
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}
. As a result of the above definitions, each
\psi(a^{\prime}_{i}),\psi(b^{\prime}_{i})
are vertices of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}-\psi(Y^{\cup})
, and the edges
\psi(a^{\prime}_{i})y_{i}
and
y_{i}\psi(b^{\prime}_{i})
exist in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}
.
Consider now some
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
. Let
\mathrm{AW}(y)
denote a collection of
|A_{y}\cap W|
new vertices, and fix an arbitrary bijection
\nu:\mathrm{AW}(y)\rightarrow A_{y}\cap W
.

For any
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
, we define
\mathrm{NAW}(y)\subset V(G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}})
the following way.
Consider every brick
B
and every pair
u,v\in B\cap W
and, as above, define the sequences
y_{i}
,
a^{\prime}_{i}
,
b^{\prime}_{i}
based on
P_{uv}
. For every
i
such that
y=y_{i}
, let us introduce
\psi(a^{\prime}_{i}),\psi(b^{\prime}_{i})\in V(G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}})
into
\mathrm{NAW}(y)
.
We extend
\nu
to
\mathrm{NAW}(y)
by setting
\nu=\psi^{-1}
on this set, i.e.,
\nu(\psi(a^{\prime}_{i}))=a^{\prime}_{i}
. Notice that as a result,
\nu
may not be a bijection, but it remains a bijection on
W\cap\bigcup_{y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})}A_{y}
.

Consider the vertex set
\mathrm{AW}(y)\cup\mathrm{NAW}(y)
, and for each pair
a,b\in\mathrm{AW}(y)\cup\mathrm{NAW}(y)
such that there exists some
Y
-slice
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{Y}_{B}
between
a
and
b
, we add an an edge from
a
to
b
and subdivide it with a new vertex denoted by
m_{Q}
. Let
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
denote the resulting graph. The role of this vertex will be to represent traversing the
Y
-slice
Q
.

We can now define
G^{\prime}
as
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}-\big(\psi(Y^{\cup})\big)\cup\bigcup_{y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})}\mathrm{Jump}(y)
, i.e., the graph that we get by replacing each vertex
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
with the corresponding graph
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
. Notice that
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
is glued to the existing graph along the shared vertices
\mathrm{NAW}(y)=V(\mathrm{Jump}(y))\cap V(G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}})
.

Let
\psi^{\prime}:(V(G)\setminus Y^{\cup})\cup W\rightarrow V(G^{\prime})
be defined as follows:

\psi^{\prime}(v)=\begin{cases}\psi(v)&\text{if }v\in V(G)\setminus Y^{\cup}\\
\nu^{-1}(v)&\text{otherwise, i.e., if }v\in Y^{\cup}\cap W.\end{cases}.

Defining
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
\omega
and the proof of (
i
).

We define
\omega(t)
for each
t\in V(G^{\prime})
as follows.

\omega(t):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }t\in\psi(Z^{\cup}\setminus Y^{\cup})\\
|(V(Q[a,b])\setminus\{a,b\})\setminus Z^{\cup}|&\text{if $t=m_{Q}$ for some $Y$-slice $Q$ from $a$ to $b$}\\
1&\text{otherwise, (i.e.}v\in(V(G)\setminus(Z^{\cup}\cup Y^{\cup}))\cup\bigcup_{y\in Y^{\cup}}\mathrm{AW}(y)\text{)}\end{cases}

We can observe by
Lemma

11.7
that
T
is disjoint from
Y^{\cup}
, thus we can set
T^{\prime}:=T\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

as the desired terminal set in
G^{\prime}
.

All vertices in
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
can be associated with vertices or vertex pairs of
|W\cap B|
. By
Theorem

10.12
we have
|W\cap B|\leqslant 2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
so there are at most
O(|W\cap B|^{2})=2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
vertices in
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
.

To define the partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, for each clique
C\in\mathcal{P}\setminus Y
we add
\psi(C)
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, that is,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}
are the same when restricted to
V(G)\setminus(X^{\cup}\cup Y^{\cup})
, and the vertices of
\psi(X^{\cup})
appear as singletons in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Moreover, for each
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
we add the set
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(y):=V(\mathrm{Jump}(y))\setminus\mathrm{NAW}(y)
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. This concludes the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Notice that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is indeed a partition of
V(G^{\prime})
, i.e., it consists of pairwise disjoint sets and its union covers
V(G^{\prime})
. Moreover, a partition class of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{P}
has size at most
2^{O(1/\varepsilon\log(1/\varepsilon))}
, and any other class in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
has size at most
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
, thus (
i
) holds.

Consolidations from

.

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}))
.
Lemma

11.8
implies that
\mathrm{tw}(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

. The same lemma guarantees that a tree decomposition of

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of width
O(1/\varepsilon^{18})
can be constructed in polynomial time, and in polynomial time we can adapt it into a tree decomposition of
G^{\prime}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
as the latter is obtained from the former via a series of contractions and deletions.

Relating Steiner trees in
G^{\prime}
to
G
, the proof of (
iii
).

Let
S^{\prime}
be a Steiner tree for the instance
(G^{\prime},\omega,T^{\prime}=T\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{X}^{\cup})
of weight
\kappa=\omega(S^{\prime})
. We first remove vertices from
S^{\prime}
in order to make it a minimal feasible solution. Notice that each vertex in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(y)
that is subdividing some edge can only be included in
S^{\prime}
if both of its neighbors are in
S^{\prime}
: indeed,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(y)
contains no terminals and including a non-terminal vertex of degree
2
without both of its neighbors would contradict the minimality of
S^{\prime}
.

Now, for each vertex
v\in S^{\prime}\cap V(G)
we add
v
to the set
S
. Consider a vertex
x\in S^{\prime}\cap\psi(X^{\cup})
and the corresponding vertex set
A_{x}\in\mathcal{X}^{\cup}
where
\psi(A_{x})=x
. We add the set
A_{x}
to
S
. Now we process the remaining vertices, which is
V(\mathrm{Jump}(y)\setminus\mathrm{NAW}(y))\cap S^{\prime}
for each
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
.
For each vertex
v\in S^{\prime}\cap(\mathrm{AW}(y))
we simply add the corresponding
\nu(v)\in V(G)
to
S
. If
v=m_{Q}
for some
Y
-slice
Q
from
a
to
b
, then we add all vertices of
Q(a,b)
into
S
. Notice that
S
gains at most
\omega(m_{Q})
internal vertices that are not in
Z^{\cup}
, and all weight 1 vertices of
S^{\prime}
grew
S
by at most one vertex.

Finally, we extend
S
with all vertices of
Z^{\cup}
. Notice that
G[S]
is not necessarily connected, and it may be very large, as
Z^{\cup}
is significantly larger than
Z
. However, we observe that
|S\setminus Z^{\cup}|\leqslant\omega(S^{\prime})
. Next, we will show that there is a single connected component of
G[S]
that contains all of
T
, and later we will modify
S
so that feasibility is maintained and the size is decreased below the desired upper bound.

For now, we need to prove that all pairs of terminals are connected in
G[S]
. Notice that we can decompose any terminal-to-terminal path in
G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]
into edges that are outside of the subgraphs
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
as well as minimal subpaths inside
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
that start and end in
\mathrm{NAW}(y)
. Consequently, the connectivity of terminals in
G[S]
can be proven via the following statements:

(1)

For every edge
st
of
G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]
outside the subgraphs
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
there is a path connecting some vertex of
G[\psi^{-1}(s)]
to some vertex of
G[\psi^{-1}(t)]
in
G[S]
.

(2)

For every
s\in S^{\prime}
that is not in any set
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(y)
(i.e.,
s\in(V(G^{\prime})-Y^{\cup})\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{X}^{\cup}
) we have that
G[\psi^{-1}(s)]
is connected in
G[S]
.

(3)

If
G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]
connects
s,t\in\mathrm{NAW}(y)
inside
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
, then
G[S]
connects some vertex of
\psi^{-1}(s)
and some vertex of
\psi^{-1}(t)
.

To prove (1), notice that
st
is an edge of
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}
, and consider the original edge
s^{*}t^{*}\in E(G^{*})
that gave rise to the edge
st
after the consolidation of
G^{*}
into
G^{*}_{\mathcal{X}\hat{\mathcal{Y}}}=G^{*}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathcal{X}^{\cup},\hat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\cup})
. (Notice here that
s^{*}\in\psi^{-1}(s)
and
t^{*}\in\psi^{-1}(t)
). Let
s_{\mathcal{P}},t_{\mathcal{P}}
be the cliques of
\mathcal{P}
containing
s^{*}
and
t^{*}
, respectively.
Observe that each of
s_{\mathcal{P}}
and
t_{\mathcal{P}}
can be in
X
or
\mathcal{P}\setminus(X\cup Y)
, and by
11.10
either
s_{\mathcal{P}}t_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
, or
s_{\mathcal{P}}=t_{\mathcal{P}}
. If they coincide, then
s^{*},t^{*}
are in the same clique of
G
and thus they are connected in
G[S]
. Otherwise, we have that
s_{\mathcal{P}}t_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
, so by
Lemma

11.8
(
iii
) either
s_{\mathcal{P}}t_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G_{\mathcal{P}})
or there is a path of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
connecting them in
G_{\mathcal{P}}[Z]
. In the latter case, we have that
s^{*}
and
t^{*}
are connected in
G[Z^{\cup}]
and since
Z^{\cup}\subset S
they are connected in
G[S]
.

To prove (2), if
s_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus(X\cup Y)
, then
\psi^{-1}(s)=s
and it is trivially connected, so assume that
s
is the consolidation of some set
A_{x}\in\mathcal{X}^{\cup}
. Let
A_{\mathcal{P}}
be the corresponding class in
\mathcal{X}
, i.e., the one where
A_{x}=A_{\mathcal{P}}^{\cup}
. Recall that by the definition of
\mathcal{X}
we have that
A_{\mathcal{P}}
is connected in
H
. Thus, by the Lipshitz property, each edge of
H
can be represented as a path of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
. Since both endpoints of this path are in
X
(as
A_{\mathcal{P}}\subset X
), all path vertices must be in
N_{G_{\mathcal{P}}}(X,c_{\mathrm{Lip}})\subset Z
. Thus
A_{\mathcal{P}}
is connected in
G_{\mathcal{P}}[Z]
and therefore
A_{x}
is connected in
G[Z^{\cup}]
as well as in
G[S]
.

To prove (3), consider some path in
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
connecting
s
to
t
, and without loss of generality assume that the internal vertices of this path are not from
\mathrm{NAW}(y)
. Such a path has the following form due to the structure of
\mathrm{Jump}(y)
:

s:=v_{0},m_{Q_{1}},v_{1},m_{Q_{2}},v_{2},\dots,v_{k-1},m_{Q_{k}},v_{k},s^{\prime}=v_{k}

where
v_{1},\dots,v_{k-1}\in\mathrm{AW}(y)
.
For each subdividing vertex
m_{Q_{i}}
there is a corresponding vertex pair
\nu(v_{i}),\nu(v_{i+1})
in
G
that we can connect with
Y
-slice
Q
. The concatenation of these slices gives a walk from
\nu(s)
to
\nu(t)
in
G^{*}
. Each edge of this walk that is not an edge of
G
can be substituted with a path of length at most
3c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G^{*}
. Moreover, recall that by the definition of
\nu
we have
\nu(s)\in\psi^{-1}(s)
and analogously
\nu(t)\in\psi^{-1}(t)
. This concludes the proof of connectivity.

We now know that
G[S]
has a connected component that includes
T
. Remove all other connected components from
S
, and apply the simplification of
Lemma

7.5
to obtain a solution
\hat{S}
that is a subset of
S
and contains at most
O(1)
vertices from each clique of
\mathcal{P}
. It follows that
|\hat{S}\cap Z^{\cup}|\leqslant|T\cap Z^{\cup}|+O(|Z|)
. We apply (
21
) and recall that our application of
Lemma

7.8
at the beginning of
11
ensured that each clique of
\mathcal{P}
contains at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
terminals of
T
. Therefore,

|\hat{S}|\leqslant\omega(S^{\prime})+|T\cap Z^{\cup}|+O(|Z|)=\omega(S^{\prime})+O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}},

concluding the proof of (
iii
).

Relating Steiner trees in
G
to
G^{\prime}
, the proof of (
iv
).

Consider the path generator
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
with
V_{\mathcal{W}}\subset W
for
(G,T)
guaranteed by
Theorem

10.12
, with cost at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
.
Let
S
be the solution that
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
generates where for each
(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{W}
we use the shortest path
P_{uv}
that we fixed earlier in the definition of
G^{\prime}
. Recall that
|S|
is at most the cost of
(S_{0},\mathcal{W})
and thus
|S|\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T)
. Let
W_{0}\subset W
denote the vertices of
V_{\mathcal{W}}
that are not
\lambda
-outer, i.e.,
S_{0}\cup W_{0}
is disjoint from
\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda)^{\cup}
. (More precisely,
W\subset V(G^{\mathrm{orig}})
consists of the original objects in
G
rather than any of their subpolygons in
B
.)

Consider now
S_{0}\cup W_{0}
as a vertex set in
G^{*}
, and extend it with
Z^{\cup}\setminus Y^{\cup}
to get the vertex set
S^{*}_{0}
. We will show that edges induced by
G[S_{0}\cup W_{0}]
remain connected in
G^{*}[S^{*}_{0}]
. The subgraph
G^{*}[S_{0}\cup W_{0}]
is not necessarily connected, but all edges of
G[S_{0}\cup W_{0}]
induced by some clique of
\mathcal{P}
are also present in
G^{*}[S^{*}_{0}]
by the definition of
G^{*}
(see
Definition

11.9
). We claim that for any edge
ab\in E(G[S_{0}\cup W_{0}])
where
a,b\in S_{0}\cup W_{0}
it holds that
a,b
are in the same component of
G^{*}[S^{*}_{0}]
.

Consider the cliques
a_{\mathcal{P}}
and
b_{\mathcal{P}}
If
a_{\mathcal{P}}=b_{\mathcal{P}}
, then they are directly connected in
G^{*}
. Otherwise, notice that
a_{\mathcal{P}}b_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G_{\mathcal{P}})
. If
a_{\mathcal{P}}\not\in Z
or
b_{\mathcal{P}}\not\in Z
, then by
Lemma

11.8
(
ii
) we have that
a_{\mathcal{P}}b_{\mathcal{P}}\in E(G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}})
, so by the definition of
G^{*}
(see
Definition

11.9
) we have that
ab\in E(G^{*})
. Suppose now that
a,b\in(S\cap Z^{\cup})\setminus Y^{\cup}
and thus
a_{\mathcal{P}},b_{\mathcal{P}}\in Z
.
Lemma

11.8
(
ii
) we have that

a_{\mathcal{P}},b_{\mathcal{P}}
are connected in
G^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}[Z]
with a path
R^{*}
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}
. This corresponds to a path
R
of length at most
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}
in
G_{\mathcal{P}}
where
V(R^{*})\subset V(R)
, and thus by
5.1
a path of length at most
3c^{2}_{\mathrm{Lip}}
in
G
. Since both endpoints of this path are in
B\setminus\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda)
, we have that
V(R^{*})
(and thus also
V(R)
) is disjoint from
\mathrm{Inn}(\lambda+3c_{\mathrm{Lip}}^{2})
. In particular, by
Lemma

11.7
we conclude that
V(R)
is disjoint from
Y
, and thus
a,b
can be connected within
G^{*}[(S_{0}\cup W_{0}\cup Z^{\cup})\setminus Y^{\cup}]
as claimed.

Next, we build the desired solution
S^{\prime}
to
(G^{\prime},\omega,T^{\prime}=T\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{X}^{\cup})
. Include all vertices of
\psi^{\prime}(V_{\mathcal{W}}\cup S^{*}_{0})=\psi^{\prime}(V_{\mathcal{W}}\cup(S_{0}\cup W_{0}\cup Z^{\cup})\setminus Y^{\cup})
in
S^{\prime}
. For each triplet
(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{W}
let
P_{uv}
be the shortest path in the brick
B
of
G
we have fixed; more precisely,
P_{uv}
is the path where we replace each subpolygon on this shortest path with their orignal object from
G^{\mathrm{orig}}
. As before, we will use
u
and
v
to refer to
u^{\mathrm{orig}},v^{\mathrm{orig}}
. For each
Y
-slice
Q
of the walk
P^{*}_{uv}
we add
m_{Q}
to
S^{\prime}
. Similarly, for each ordinary slice
Q
we add
\psi^{\prime}(V(Q))
to
S^{\prime}
. Observe that for a given triplet
(B,u,v)
we have added some consolidation of the corresponding
G^{\mathrm{orig}}
-path’s endpoints (as we have added
\psi^{\prime}(V_{\mathcal{W}})
as well as consolidations of paths for each of the slices of
P^{*}_{uv}
. Since consolidations preserve connectivity, one can verify that
\psi^{\prime}(V_{\mathcal{W}}\cup S^{*}_{0})
retains its connected components in
G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]
, thus in particular the vertices
T^{\prime}=T\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{X}^{\cup}
are in the same connected component of
G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]
.

To prove the weight bound, recall that
V(P_{uv})\subset V(P^{*}_{uv})
, and by
Lemma

11.11
property 2, all vertices of
V(P^{*}_{uv})
that are outside
V(P_{uv})
are in
Z^{\cup}
. Notice in the definition of
\omega
that vertices of
\psi(Z^{\cup}\setminus Y^{\cup})
have weight
0
, and vertices of
Z^{\cup}
that are also internal vertices of some
Y
-slice
Q
within
P^{*}_{uv}
are also subtracted from
\omega(m_{Q})
. Thus, the only internal vertices of
P^{*}_{uv}
that can contribute weight are from
V_{\mathcal{W}}\cup V(P_{uv})
. In total, the contribution because from the ordinary and
Y
-slices of
P^{*}_{uv}
as well as all internal vertices separating these is at most
|V(P_{uv}\setminus\{u,v\})\setminus Z^{\cup}|=\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)-1
.

It follows that the weight contribution from the triplets is at most
|V_{\mathcal{W}}|+\sum_{(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)-1)
. The contribution from
\psi^{\prime}(Z^{\cup}\setminus Y^{\cup})
is
0
, and the contribution from
|S_{0}|
is at most
|S_{0}|
. Thus we get:

\omega(S^{\prime})\leqslant|S_{0}|+|V_{\mathcal{W}}|+\sum_{(B,u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}}(\mathrm{dist}_{B}(u,v)-1)\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\mathrm{StTree}(G,T),

as required for (
iv
)(a).

To prove (
iv
)(b), let
S^{\prime\prime}
be the minimum weight solution for
(G^{\prime},\omega,T\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
