\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{X})
among the subsets of
S^{\prime}
. Consdier a class
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. We have three cases.

Case 1.

C^{\prime}=\mathrm{Jump}(y)\setminus\mathrm{NAW}(y)
for some
y\in\psi(Y^{\cup})
.

We have by
Theorem

10.12
(
iii
)(d) that there are at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
triplets for any given brick
B
in the path generator. Since each path
P_{uv}
will have at most one corresponding slice of
P^{*}_{uv}
going through any fixed class of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
(by the slicing procedure), we have that
S^{\prime}
contains at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
vertices from
C^{\prime}
.

Case 2.

C^{\prime}
is a singleton containing some consolidated vertex.

Then
|C^{\prime}\cap S^{\prime}|\leqslant 1
.

Case 3.

C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
is an original clique of
G
.

Recall that
|T\cap C^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
due to our usage of
Lemma

7.8
. Moreover,
Theorem

10.12
(
ii
)(c) implies
|V_{\mathcal{W}}\cap C^{\prime}|\leqslant O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
. Recall that
C^{\prime}
has
O(1)
cliques of
\mathcal{P}
that are neighboring in
G
, and each of those cliques may remain in
G^{\prime}
, but they might also become part of distinct classes of
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
. For a given class
y\in\psi(\hat{\mathcal{Y}}^{\cup})
we have already shown that there are at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
vertices selected from
\mathrm{Jump}(y)\setminus\mathrm{NAW}(y)
, thus
C^{\prime}
has at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
edges induced between itself and such classes. For every other neighboring class of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
the neighboring class is a clique, thus it is again sufficient to have at most one edge induced between
C^{\prime}
and such neighbors; see
Lemma

7.7
for the argument. By the minimality of
S^{\prime}
, each vertex in
C^{\prime}\cap S^{\prime}
must either be a terminal or it must induce an edge going into neighboring classes of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Consequently, there are at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
vertices in
C^{\prime}\cap S^{\prime}
.

Thus in each case there
O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
vertices in
C^{\prime}\cap S^{\prime}
, concluding both the proof of (
iv
) and of the theorem.
∎

11.5
A faster EPTAS for Steiner Tree

The following lemma presents a general way to compute Steiner trees in a graph with a partitioned vertex set.
Let
G
be graph and let
\mathcal{R}
be a partition of
V(G)
. We say that a vertex set
S
is
(k,\mathcal{R})
-bounded
if for each
C\in\mathcal{R}
we have
|C\cap S|\leqslant k
.

Lemma 11.13
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph with nonnegative vertex weighting
\omega
and let
\mathcal{R}
be a partition of
V(G)
where each class
C
of
\mathcal{R}
has at most
r
vertices. Let
K
be a given set of terminals, and suppose that we are given a tree decomposition of
G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{R}
of treewidth
w
. Then we can find the minimum weight
(\kappa,\mathcal{R})
-bounded Steiner tree of
(G,K)
in
(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time.

Proof.

We modify the standard treewidth-based dynamic programming for Steiner tree to this setting. Observe that, based on the tree decomposition, we can make a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of
G
by considering each bag
X_{t}
of the given decomposition, and substituting each class
C\in X_{t}
of
G\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{R}
with all vertices inside
C
. The resulting tree decomposition is a valid decomposition of
G
and has width
rw
. Observe that the trees we need to consider intersect each class in at most
\kappa w
vertices.

The standard dynamic programming algorithm for Steiner tree
[
43
,
29
]
proceeds bottom-up on a (slightly tweaked)
nice
tree decomposition. We will rely on the notation of
[
43
, Section 7.3.3.]
. For a bag
X_{t}
let
G_{t}
denote the subgraph of
G
induced by the subtree rooted at the bag
X_{t}
. Then, for each
X\subset X_{t}
and partition
\mathcal{P}
of
X
, there is a subproblem
c[X_{t},X,\mathcal{P}]
, which is the minimum number of edges in a forest
F
in
G_{t}
such that:

1.

X_{t}\cap V(F)=X
.

2.

Every terminal from
K\cap V(G_{t})
is in
V(F)
.

3.

The forest
F
has
|\mathcal{P}|
connected components, and for each class
X^{i}
of
\mathcal{P}
there is a corresponding connected component
F^{i}
of
F
such that
X^{i}=V(F_{i})\cap X_{t}
.

First, we compute a nice tree decomposition based on
\mathcal{T}_{0}
in polynomial time using standard techniques
[
43
]
; notice that the resulting nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
has bags that are subsets of existing bags, so in particular, it still holds that for each bag
X_{t}
of
\mathcal{T}
there are at most
w
classes of
\mathcal{R}
intersecting
X_{t}
.

We can use the same algorithm with two small modifications.
First, we will only iterate over sets
X\subset X_{t}
where
|X\cap C|\leqslant\kappa w
, as any
(k,\mathcal{R})
-bounded Steiner tree will intersect
X_{t}
in at most
\kappa w
vertices. Second, instead of
c[X_{t},X,\mathcal{P}]
denoting the number of edges in
F
, it will denote the minimum vertex weight of
V(F)
satisfying the same properties.

One can verify that the same dynamic programming algorithm works to compute the minimum weight
(\kappa,\mathcal{R})
-bounded Steiner tree. The original algorithm has
|X_{t}|^{O(|X_{t}|)}
states for the bag
X_{t}
, as it needs to consider all
2^{|X_{t}|}
subsets
X
and up to
|X_{t}|^{O(|X_{t}|)}
partitions on each subset.
The dynamic programming needs to consider all pairs of states from two child bags to compute the value for a state, so the running time is
\mathrm{tw}^{O(\mathrm{tw})}n
for an
n
-vertex graph with a tree decomposition of width
\mathrm{tw}
. In our case, we only have
|X_{t}|^{O(kw)}=(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}
subsets to consider, each of which has
(\kappa w)^{O(\kappa w)}\leqslant(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}
partitions. Thus the number of states is
(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}
and the running time is
(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
∎

See
1.6

Proof.

We apply
Theorem

3.4
to obtain an
\alpha
-standard graph
G\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
whose representation has polynomial complexity.
Apply
Theorem

11.1
to obtain a graph
(G^{\prime},\omega,T^{\prime})
and vertex partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
Theorem

11.1
(
ii
) gives a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}\mathord{\hbox to5.5pt{\vbox to9.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.92 12.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.31 4.15 M 3.69 4.15 C 3.69 4.92 3.07 5.53 2.31 5.53 C 1.54 5.53 0.92 4.92 0.92 4.15 C 0.92 3.39 1.54 2.77 2.31 2.77 C 3.07 2.77 3.69 3.39 3.69 4.15 Z M 2.31 4.15}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.61 8.3 M 6 8.3 C 6 9.07 5.38 9.69 4.61 9.69 C 3.85 9.69 3.23 9.07 3.23 8.3 C 3.23 7.54 3.85 6.92 4.61 6.92 C 5.38 6.92 6 7.54 6 8.3 Z M 4.61 8.3}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of width
w=O(1/\varepsilon^{18})
.
By
Theorem

11.1
(
i
) we have that each class of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
has size at most
r=2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}
.
Finally, by
Theorem

11.1
(
iv
)(
a
),(
b
) there exists a solution
S^{\prime}
to the instance
(G^{\prime},\omega,T^{\prime})
of weight
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mbox{{\sc opt}}
that is
(\kappa,\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
-bounded where
\kappa=O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})
.
Thus by
Lemma

11.13
we can find a solution
S^{\prime}
of weight at most
(1+O(\varepsilon))\mbox{{\sc opt}}
in
(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time, where the first term can be bounded as:

(rw)^{O(\kappa w)}=\Big(2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})}\cdot O(1/\varepsilon^{17})\Big)^{O(1/\varepsilon^{7.5})\cdot O(1/\varepsilon^{18})}=2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{33})}.

Using
Theorem

11.1
(
iii
) we now construct a solution of
(G,T)
based on
S^{\prime}
whose weight can be bounded as

|S|=\omega(S^{\prime})+O(\varepsilon)\cdot\mbox{{\sc opt}}=(1+O(\varepsilon))\mbox{{\sc opt}}

where we used that there are at most
O(1/\varepsilon)
terminals in each clique.
The dominant term in the running time is the algorithm of
Lemma

11.13
, which takes
2^{O(1/\varepsilon^{33})}\mathrm{poly}(n)
time.
∎

12
Lower bounds and connection to planar problems

12.1
APX-hardness results

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following theorem.

See
1.7

A
split graph
is a graph whose vertex set can be partitioned into
A\dot{\cup}B
such that
A
induces a clique and
B
induces an independent set.
Our proof of
Theorem

1.7
will rely on the fact that two of the above graph classes are able to represent subdivisions of bounded degree graphs, while the other two are able to represent arbitrary split graphs.

Lemma 12.1
.

Steiner Tree
and
Subset TSP
are APX-hard in split graphs.

Proof.

The traveling salesman problem (TSP) is APX-hard in discrete metric spaces where all distances are
2
or
3

[
80
]
. One can make an equivalent instance of
Subset TSP
in a split graph: let
M=(X,\mathrm{dist})
be a metric space on
n
points where distances of distinct points are either
2
or
3
. Then let
G
be a split graph with a clique
C
of size
\binom{n}{2}+n
indexed by edges of the complete graph on
X
plus a dummy vertex
d_{x}
for each
x\in X
. The independent set
I
of
G
is of size
n
and indexed by
X
. We have an edge from vertex
v_{x}\in I
to
w_{yz}\in C
if and only if
x\in{y,z}
and
\mathrm{dist}(y,z)=2
, and we connect each
v_{x}\in I
to
d_{x}\in C
. Since the dummy vertices are neighbors and together with
I
they form a perfect matching, we have that any pair of vertices in
I
has distance at most
3
. On the other hand,
v_{x},v_{y}\in I
has a common neighbor in
G
if and only if
\mathrm{dist}(x,y)=2
, and as a result,
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v_{x},v_{y})=2
holds. Thus when considering the terminal set
T=I
in
G
, the metric of
G
for the terminals is isomorphic to
M
. As a result, a subset TSP tour of
(G,T)
is in a direct one-to-one correspondence with a TSP tour of
M
and they have the same length. Since the reduction is polynomial, this shows that
Subset TSP
is APX-hard in split graphs.

For Steiner Tree our reduction is based on the fact that
Vertex Cover
is APX-hard on connected graphs of maximum degree
3

[
4
]
. In an instance of vertex cover, we are given some graph
G
and the goal is to find a minimum subset of vertices
S
such that each edge of
G
is incident to some vertex in
S
.
Notice that in a connected graph of maximum degree
3
each vertex can cover at most
3
edges, and there are at least
n-1
edges to cover, thus the minimum vertex cover has size at least
(n-1)/3
.

Based on
G
, we can build a split graph
S=(I,C)
where the independent set
I
has a vertex
u_{xy}
for each edge
xy
of
G
, and the clique
C
has a vertex
v_{z}
for each vertex
z
of
G
. We add an edge from
v_{z}\in I
to
w_{xy}\in C
if and only if
z
is incident to the edge
xy\in E(G)
, that is,
z=x
or
z=y
. Let
T=I
be the set of terminals, and consider the
Steiner Tree
instance
(S,T)
. It is easy to see that a vertex cover of size
k
in
G
corresponds to a Steiner tree of size
|I|+k
in
X
: namely, we take the terminals
T=I
and the vertices in
C
that correspond to vertices of the vertex cover. On the other hand, any Steiner tree must contain all terminals (
I
) and each vertex
w_{xy}
must be connected to the rest of the tree either by having
v_{x}
or
v_{y}
in the tree, thus the vertices of
G
corresponding to tree vertices of
C
form a vertex cover. This establishes a bijection between vertex covers of
G
and Steiner trees of
G
with an additive size difference of
|I|
. Notice moreover that
n-1<|I|<3n/2
and
(n-1)\leqslant k\leqslant n
implies that this is a valid L-reduction. This concludes the proof that
Steiner Tree
is APX-hard in split graphs.
∎

Our next goal is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 12.2
.

Steiner Tree
and
Subset TSP
are APX-hard in graphs of maximum degree
3
.

We note that the claim about
Subset TSP
follows from the APX-hardness of
Graphic TSP
on the same graph class
[
65
]
; we give an independent proof specifically for
Subset TSP
.

We will give a PTAS reduction from the same problems in arbitrary graphs. Notice that the optimum of both
Subset TSP
and
Steiner Tree
in an
n
-vertex graph is less than
2n
. In particular, a spanning tree of
G
is a feasible Steiner tree on at most
n
vertices, and the walk that travels this tree twice is a feasible subset TSP tour of length
2n-2
. Thus for any
\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{2n-2}
a solution is
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate if and only if it is optimal. Thus, for
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2n-2}
, we set
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{2n}
and for the rest of the reduction, we assume without loss of generality that
\varepsilon\geqslant\frac{1}{2n}
.

An
\ell
-subdivision
of a graph
G
is the graph
G_{t}
obtained by replacing its edges with paths of length
\ell
. Let
S^{\prime}_{uv}
be the length-
\ell
path of
G_{\ell}
that replaced the edge
uv
of
G
. Notice that there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between minimal Steiner trees of
G
and Steiner trees of
G_{\ell}
, where the terminals in
G
and
G_{\ell}
are the same. If
X
is a Steiner tree for
(G,T)
of size
k
, then consider any spanning tree
E_{X}
of
X
, and let
X^{\prime}
be the set of vertices occurring on paths corresponding to
E_{X}
in
G^{\prime}
. Then
X^{\prime}
spans a feasible Steiner tree of
(G_{\ell},T)
of size
|X^{\prime}|=\ell\cdot(k-1)+k
. Similarly, a minimal Steiner tree of
(G^{\prime},T)
consists of some collection of paths
S^{\prime}_{uv}
for some
uv\in E(G)
; if it contains
k-1
such paths, then its size is
\ell(k-1)+k
, and there is clearly a corresponding set
X\supset T
in
V(G)
that consists of
k
vertices.

We say that a subset TSP walk
W
of the instance
(G,T)
is minimal if it can be decomposed into
|T|
shortest paths of
G
that connect
T
in some cyclic permutation. (Note that one can change any feasible walk
W
into a minimal feasible walk that is not longer than
W
in polynomial time.) We claim that there is a simple bijection between minimal subset TSP walks of
(G,T)
and
(G^{\prime},T)
: one can consider the length-
\ell
paths corresponding to the edges of a minimal walk
W
of
(G,T)
of length
k
: this leads to a minimal walk
W^{\prime}
of
(G^{\prime},T)
of length
\ell k
. Similarly, any minimal walk of
(G^{\prime},T)
consists of shortest paths between terminal pairs, which in turn are concatenations of length-
\ell
paths.

We can now prove the following lemma.

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph class where
Subset TSP
and
Steiner Tree
are APX-hard. Then for any positive integer
\ell
they are also APX-hard in the graph class
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
consisting of the
\ell
-subdivisions of the graphs in
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

Given the above polynomial-time-computable bijection between minimal feasible Steiner trees and minimal feasible subset TSP paths of
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
G_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
, it remains to show that the approximation ratio is preserved. In case of subset TSP paths the approximation ratios are identical as the corresponding walk lengths differ by a multiplicative factor of
\ell
. For Steiner tree, the function
f(k)=\ell(k-1)+k
is monotone, thus an optimum tree of
(G,T)
corresponds to an optimum tree in
G_{\ell},T
. If the optimum tree of
(G_{\ell},T)
has
\kappa-1
paths, then a minimal
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate tree of
(G_{\ell},T)
has size at most
(1+\varepsilon)(\ell(\kappa-1)+\kappa)
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this approximate tree has
k-1
paths where
k>(1+\varepsilon)\kappa
paths. Then

(1+\varepsilon)(\ell(\kappa-1)+\kappa)\geqslant f(k)>f((1+\varepsilon)\kappa)=(1+\varepsilon)(\ell\kappa-1)+\kappa),

which simplifies to
-(1+\varepsilon)\ell>-(1+\varepsilon)
, which is a contradiction as
\ell\geqslant 1
. Thus the approximate tree has at most
(1+\varepsilon)\kappa-1
paths, and the corresponding tree has at most
(1+\varepsilon)\kappa
vertices, concluding the proof.
∎

In order to prove
Lemma

12.2
we will need to decrease the vertex degrees of graphs using the following method.

A
subcubic dilation
of a vertex set
S
in the graph
G
replaces each vertex
v\in S
with a path
H_{v}
on at least
\deg(v)
vertices, and connects the vertices of
N_{G}(v)
to distinct vertices of
H_{v}
. Consequently, if
G^{\prime}
is a subcubic dilation of
S
in
G
where
S
contains all vertices of
G
of degree at least
4
, then
G^{\prime}
is a graph of maximum degree
3
where all vertices of degree
3
are on the paths
H_{v}
, and contracting the paths
H_{v}
results in the graph
G
. We say that a subcubic dilation
G^{\prime}
of
S
in
G
is
f(n)
-bounded if all paths
H_{v}
have at most
f(n)
vertices.

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained as a subcubic dilation of
V(G)
in a subdivision
G_{\ell}
of
G
. Given
T\subset V(G)
, for each
v\in T
let
v^{\prime}
be an arbitrary vertex of
H^{\prime}_{v}
, and let
T^{\prime}
be the terminal set in
G^{\prime}
containing the points
v^{\prime}
for each
v\in T
. Assume moreover that
G^{\prime}
is
b=b(n)
-bounded where
b\leqslant\frac{\ell}{4n^{2}}
and
\ell=n^{O(1)}
. Then the following hold.

(i)

If
(G,T)
has Steiner tree of size
s
, then
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a Steiner tree of size at most
(s-1)\ell+\varepsilon\ell
. If
(G,T)
has a subset TSP walk of size
s
, then
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a subset TSP walk of size at most
s\ell+\varepsilon\ell
.

(ii)

If
X^{\prime}
is a Steiner Tree for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
where
|X^{\prime}|<s\ell
, then in polynomial time we can construct a Steiner tree
X
for
(G,T)
of size at most
s
.
If
W^{\prime}
is a subset TSP walk for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
of size
|W^{\prime}|<(s+1)\ell
, then in polynomial time we can construct a subset TSP walk
W
of
(G,T)
of size at most
s
.

Proof.

Let
G
be an arbitrary graph on
n
vertices, and subdivide each edge
uv
of
G
into a path
S^{\prime}_{uv}
of length
6n^{2}
. Replace each original vertex
v
of
G
by a path
H^{\prime}_{v}
with
\deg(v)
vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{\deg{v}}
, so that each edge incident to
v
is now incident to distinct edges of the new path. Let
G^{\prime}
denote the resulting graph. Notice that
G^{\prime}
is constructed in polynomial time and has maximum degree
3
. Let
T
be the set of terminals in
G
, and let
T^{\prime}
be the vertex set in
G^{\prime}
given by the vertices
v_{1}
for each
v\in T
.

Using the correspondence between vertices of
G
and the paths
H^{\prime}_{v}
as well as between edges of
G
and the length-
\ell
paths
S^{\prime}_{uv}
it is easy to construct the desired tree and walk. Let
X
be a Steiner tree of size
|X|=k
. For each vertex
v
in of
G
take all vertices of
H^{\prime}_{v}
into a set
X^{\prime}
, and for each edge
uv
in some fixed spanning tree of
G[X]
we take all vertices of the path
S^{\prime}_{uv}
into
X^{\prime}
. Then
X^{\prime}
induces a connected subgraph of
G^{\prime}
containing all terminals of
T^{\prime}
. It has
(s-1)\ell
vertices that are internal vertices of some
S^{\prime}_{uv}
and it has at most
sb
vertices from the sets
H^{\prime}_{u}
as each such set has at most
b
vertices. Since
s<n
,
b\leqslant\frac{\ell}{4n^{2}}
and
\varepsilon\geqslant 1/(2n)
, we have that
X^{\prime}
has at most

(s-1)\ell+sb\leqslant(s-1)\ell+\frac{\ell n}{4n^{2}}<(s-1)\ell+\varepsilon\ell

such vertices.

Similarly, for a walk
W
we take the paths
S^{\prime}_{uv}
corresponding to each edge of the walk into a walk
W^{\prime}
. For each vertex
v
between consecutive edges
uv
and
vw
of
W
we take the walk along
H^{\prime}_{v}
from the entry point of
S^{\prime}_{uv}
to the leftmost point of
H^{\prime}_{v}
, and then to the exit point of
S^{\prime}_{vw}
. This defines a closed walk
W^{\prime}_{0}
in
G^{\prime}
. Notice however that some edges of a path
H^{\prime}_{v}
may be traversed by the walk several times in the same direction. Every such double traversal can be eliminated while retaining the closed walk: suppose that
ab
is traversed twice from
a
to
b
by
W^{\prime}_{0}
. Then the directed walk
W^{\prime}_{0}
can be written as
x_{1},\dots,x_{a},a,b,y_{1},\dots,y_{b},a,b
. We can then change this to the walk
x_{1},\dots,x_{a},a,y_{b},y_{b-1},\dots,y_{1},b
that is shorter but visits the same set of vertices. Using such simplifications exhaustively, we get a walk
W^{\prime}
that traverses each edge of each
H^{\prime}_{v}
at most once in each direction, so altogether each edge of
H^{\prime}_{v}
is traversed at most twice. Now
W^{\prime}
has the desired size: in addition to the
s
edge paths of length
\ell
, we included at most
2nb
edges incident to the sets
H^{\prime}_{v}
. Thus
b\leqslant\frac{\ell}{4n^{2}}
and
\varepsilon\geqslant 1/(2n)
implies:

|W^{\prime}|\leqslant s\ell+2nb\leqslant s\ell+\frac{2n}{4n^{2}}\leqslant s\ell+\varepsilon\ell.

(
ii
) Suppose that
X^{\prime}
has vertices from
k
distinct sets
H^{\prime}_{u}
; let
X
contain all such vertices
u
, which is clearly feasible and of size
k
. Notice that
X^{\prime}
must contain enough paths
S^{\prime}_{uv}
to connect these vertices, i.e., at least
k-1
such paths. It follows that
X^{\prime}
has size at least
|X|\geqslant(k-1)\ell
. Consequently
|X^{\prime}|<s\ell
implies that
k-1<\ell
and thus the constructed Steiner tree satisfies
|X|=k\leqslant\ell
, as required.

Similarly, we may assume without loss of generality that
W^{\prime}
is a minimal feasible walk in the sense that it can be decomposed into
n
interior-disjoint paths connecting the terminals according to some cyclic permutation. Recall that as a result, if
W^{\prime}
contains some edge of
G^{\prime}
incident to some edge path
S^{\prime}_{uv}
then it must traverse the entire edge path. For each path
P^{\prime}
of the decomposition we can create an analogous path
P
in
G
by simply taking the edges
uv
of
G
such that the path
S^{\prime}_{uv}
representing the edge
uv
are traversed by
P^{\prime}
, and we set
W
to be the concatenation of these paths. If
W^{\prime}
traverses
k
edge paths, then it has length at least
k\ell
, thus
|W^{\prime}|<(s+1)\ell
implies
|W|=k\leqslant\ell
as required.
∎

We can now easily prove
Lemma

12.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

12.2
.

Let
G
be an aribtrary graph, and let
G^{\prime}
be a subcubic dilation of the subdivision
G_{4n^{3}}
, where each vertex
v
has been dilated into a path
H^{\prime}_{v}
of length at most
n-1
. Consequently,
G^{\prime}
is
b=(n-1)
-bounded, and satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

12.4
. Assuming that the optimum Steiner tree of
(G,T)
has size
s
, a minimum Steiner tree of
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has size at most
(s-1)\ell+\varepsilon\ell
. Thus a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution has size at most
(1+\varepsilon)((s-1)\ell+\varepsilon\ell)<(1+\varepsilon)s\ell
as
\varepsilon<1
without loss of generality. Now (
ii
) implies that in polynomial time we can create a Steiner tree for
(G,T)
of size at most
(1+\varepsilon)s
.

Similarly, if
(G,T)
has an optimum subset TSP walk of size
s
, then the optimum subset TSP walk of
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has length at most
s\ell+\varepsilon\ell
, thus a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution is of size at most
(1+\varepsilon)(s\ell+\varepsilon\ell)<(1+\varepsilon)(s+1)\ell
. Consequently, (
ii
) constructs a subset TSP walk of size at most
(1+\varepsilon)(s+1)-1<(1+2\varepsilon)s
. This concludes the PTAS reduction.
∎

We can also wrap up the proof of
Theorem

1.7
by realizing split graphs and (subdivisions of) graphs of degree at most
3
as intersection graphs.

Proof of
Theorem

1.7
.

We claim that in cases (a) and (d) one can realize some subdivision of any graph of degree at most
3
that has at least
10
vertices. If we can do this, then
Lemma

12.2
and
Lemma

12.3
implies that both
Steiner Tree
and
Subset TSP
are APX-hard on these graph classes.

Similarly, we claim that in cases (b) and (c) we can realize any split graph, so by
Lemma

12.1
we have that both problems are APX-hard on these intersection graphs.

Let us now define these realizations.

(a)
We show that a
28n
-subdivision of any graph of maximum degree
3
can be realized as the intersection graph of the following type of objects. Each object consists of two disks of diameter
1/2
, whose centers are at distance
1
from each other. Moreover, each disk center’s coordinates are integral or half-integral. These are clearly intersection graphs of similarly sized fat but disconnected objects.

Our strategy is to first construct a drawing of
G
in a grid (with crossings) where all edges are represented by paths of equal lengths. We will then replace these paths with the objects in such a way that crossings in the drawing will be “jumped” over by the corresponding objects. Let
G
be an arbitrary graph of maximum degree
3
. Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
denote its vertices, and let
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
denote its edges where
m\leqslant 3n/2
. To construct a grid drawing, we first assign each vertex
v_{i}
to the grid point
(6i,0)
. When
v_{i}
has degree
\deg(v_{i})\leqslant 3
, then
v_{i}
will be represented by a horizontal segment of length
2(k-1)
whose left endpoint is
(6i-2,0)
, and the grid points
(6i-2,0),(6i,0),(6i+2,0)
will be used as starting points of grid paths representing the edges.

For each
j=1,\dots,m
, and corresponding edge
e_{j}=v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}
we create a path from one of the grid points
(6j_{1}-2,0),(6j_{1},0),(6j_{1}+2,0)
that are all on the segment of
v_{j_{1}}
. Similarly, the path is assigned to one of the grid points
(6j_{2}-2,0),(6j_{2},0),(6j_{2}+2,0)
from the segment of
v_{j_{2}}
. Since the maximum degree is
3
, we can assign distinct grid points from the segment of each
v_{i}
to distinct edges incident to
v_{i}
. Let
x_{j_{1}}<x_{j_{2}}
denote the
x
-coordinates of these assigned grid points. Then the grid path
P_{j}
corresponding to
e_{j}
is defined as the union of the following axis-parallel grid path
P_{j}
through the following sequence of grid points:

(6j_{1},0),(x_{j_{1}},0),(x_{j_{1}},y_{j}),(x_{j_{1}}+1,y_{j}),(x_{j_{1}}+1,j),(x_{j_{2}},j),(x_{j_{2}},0),(6j_{2},0),

where
y_{j}\in[2n,14n]
is set so that the total length of the path is exactly
14n
. See
Figure

16
. We claim that such a value
y
exists. The points
(x_{j_{1}},0)
and
(x_{j_{2}},0)
are both even-coordinate points, and their horizontal distance is at least
2
and at most
6n
, and
j\leqslant m\leqslant\frac{3}{2}n
. Thus the path

(6j_{1},0),(x_{j_{1}},0)(x_{j_{1}},j),(x_{j_{2}},j),(x_{j_{2}},0),(6j_{2},0)

has even length at least
4
(when
j=1
and
x_{j_{2}}-x_{j_{1}}=2
) and at most
2j+6n+4\leqslant 9n+4<10n
. The length of
P_{j}
is
2(y_{j}-j)
longer than this path, therefore the desired
y_{j}\in[2n,14n]
exists such that the length of
P_{j}
is exactly
14n
.

Figure 16:
Realizing the subdivision of a
3
-regular graph (
K_{3,3}
) using disconnected objects. Each object is a pair of disks, depicted with a connected red line that is not part of the object. The different types of vertices are realized with appropriately placed object pairs.

Next, we scale the grid drawing by a factor of
2
, so that each edge of the original drawing is realized by two grid edges in the new drawing. Let
D
denote this new drawing. Consequently,
D
is a drawing of the
28n
-subdivision of the original graph where crossings can only happen at a point
(a,b)
when
a,b
are both even and
b\geqslant 2
.

To realize the subdivision
G_{28n}
with the disconnected objects (the disk pairs), notice first that the vertices of the
D
can be categorized as follows:

C1

vertices of degree
3
(with
2
horizontal edges and one vertical edge on the top)

C2

vertices of degree
2
where both incident edges are horizontal

C3

vertices of degree
2
where both incident edges are vertical

C4

vertices with a vertical incident edge on the top and a horizontal incident edge

C5

vertices with a vertical incident edge on the bottom and a horizontal incident edge

For the vertex
(a,b)
of type
C1
or
C4
we realize it with the object whose disk centers are
(a,b)
and
(a-1,b)
. If
(a,b)
is of type
C5
, then we realize it with the disk pair of centers
(a,b)
and
(a+1,b)
. If
(a,b)
has type
C2
, then it is realized with the disks
(a-\frac{1}{2},b),(a+\frac{1}{2},b)
, and finally, if it is of type
C3
, then it is realized with the disks
(a,b-\frac{1}{2}),(a,b+\frac{1}{2})
. It is routine to check that all adjacencies of
G_{28n}
are realized, while every crossing of
D
is “jumped over” by our disks, i.e., there is no intersection with the horizontal and vertical paths of
G_{28n}
at the crossings of
G
.

(d)
We can realize the subdivision
G_{14n}
with balls of diameter
1
. In fact, we will realize
G_{14n}
as an induced subgraph of the
3
-dimensional grid. Clearly placing unit diameter balls at the vertices of such a grid graph gives the desired ball graph. The idea is similar to that seen in case (a): we use the same assignment of vertices
v_{i}
to the point
(6i,0,0)
on the
x
-axis, and we also use the points
(6i-2,0),(6i,0),(6i+2,0)
as starting points of grid paths representing the edges of
G_{14n}
. Then the edge
e_{j}=v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}
will have its ends assigned to
(x_{j_{1}},0,0)
and
(x_{j_{2}},0,0)
. The path corresponding to
e_{j}
is then realized by the following axis-parallel grid path
P_{j}
:

(6j_{1},0,0),(x_{j_{1}},0,0),(x_{j_{1}},2j,0),(x_{j_{1}},2j,z_{j}),(x_{j_{2}},2j,z_{j}),(x_{j_{2}},2j,0),(x_{j_{2}},0,0),(6j_{2},0,0),

where
z_{j}\in[2,14n]
is chosen so that
P_{j}
has length exactly
14n
. We can again notice that the path

(6j_{1},0,0),(x_{j_{1}},0,0),(x_{j_{1}},2j,0),(x_{j_{2}},2j,0),(x_{j_{2}},0,0),(6j_{2},0,0)

has length at least
6
and at most
2\cdot 2j+6n+4\leqslant 12n+4<14n
. Thus there exists some
z_{j}
such that the extra length of
2z_{j}
compared to the above paths will ensure that
P_{j}
has length exactly
14n
.

It is routine to check that the described realization with the paths
P_{j}
is an induced grid graph.

(b) and (c)
: For a given split graph
G=(C\cup I,E)
where
C
is a clique of size
n_{C}
and
I
is an independent set of size
n_{I}
, we will assign each vertex
v_{j}
of
I
to the complex points
p_{j}:=e^{i\cdot\frac{j}{4n_{I}\pi}}
on the open upper half-circle of the unit circle. The vertex
w\in C
where
W:=N(w)\cap I
is the set of vertices in
I
neighboring
w
is assigned to the convex hull (in the planar sense) of the points
\{1,-1,-i\},\cup\{p_{j}\mid v_{j}\in W\}
. Thus each vertex
w\in C
has been assigned to a convex fat object of diameter
2
, as the objects are inside the unit disk, and they all contain the fat triangle
\{1,-1,-i\}
. It also follows that all objects assigned to the vertices of
C
are pairwise intersecting. One can verify that the triangle with vertices
\{1,-1,-i\}
is
0.18
-fat.
In (b), we can finish the realization by assigning to each
v_{j}
a segment of length
2
of radial orientation (that is, whose line passes through
0
) whose closest point to the origin is
p_{j}
. These segments are clearly connected and have diameter
2
. In (c), we can assign to each vertex
v_{j}
a small disk (say, of radius
\frac{1}{10n}
) that touches the unit disk from the outside at
p_{j}
. Clearly disks are connected and fat. It is routine to check that the resulting intersection graph in both cases is exactly
G
.
∎

Remark 12.5
.

We crucially leave open the case of intersection graphs of disks (of arbitrary size) and pseudodisks: neither our algorithm nor our lower bounds extend to these graph classes.

12.2
Reducing planar approximation problems to unit disk graphs

In this section we will prove that there is a PTAS-reduction from
Planar Subset TSP
to
Unit Disk Subset TSP
and from
Planar Steiner Tree
to
Unit Disk Steiner Tree
.

Although our reduction is from the unweighted variant, we note that one can easily construct a PTAS reduction form these problems in the case when the edges have non-negative weights. In this sense, our algorithms for
Subset TSP
and
Steiner Tree
in intersection graphs also implies (E)PTAS algorithms for the unweighted and weighted variants of these problems in planar graphs.

To prove a formal PTAS reduction, it suffices to prove the following
[
42
]
. Given
\varepsilon>0
, a planar graph
G
with terminals
T\subset V(G)
, we need to construct a unit disk graph
G^{\prime}
and terminal set
T^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{poly}(n)
time, such that given a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution
S
to
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
, we can generate a
(1+c\varepsilon)
-approximate solution to
(G,T)
in polynomial time, where
c
is a constant.

Construction of
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})

Given
\varepsilon,G,T
, we start by computing a so-called bar visibility layout of
G
. In such a layout the vertices of
G
are represented as horizontal segments whose endpoints have integer coordinates, and edges are represented as vertical segments whose endpoints have integer coordinates, and each edge starts and ends on the segments corresponding to its end vertices, and the segments is disjoint from all other vertex-segments of the drawing.

We will use the following algorithm to compute the desired layout:

Theorem 12.6

(Tamassia and Tollis
[
78
]
, Rosenstiehl and Tarjan
[
77
]
)
.

Given a planar graph
G
, a bar visibility layout of G inside the bounding box
[1,n]\times[1,2n-4]
can be computed in
O(n)
time.

Figure 17:
(i) Realizing a planar graph as a bar visibility graph. (ii) After scaling, the space between neighboring vertical paths allows us to create a grid path of length exactly
36n^{4}
.

We apply the transformation
f(x,y)=(3n\cdot x,36n^{3}\cdot y)
on the bar layout to get a representation in the bounding box. As a result, the vertex-representing segments have length between
3n
and
6n^{2}-12n
, and the edge-representing vertical segments have length between
36n^{3}
and
36n^{4}
, while their horizontal distance is at least
3n
, see
Figure

17
. Using the horizontal space of width of
3n
between consecutive vertical segments, we can change each vertical segment into an
induced
grid path of length exactly
36n^{4}
connecting the same pair of endpoints: indeed, a grid rectangle of height
36n^{3}
and width
3n-2
does contain such a path connecting two of its corners.
Since the result is an induced grid graph, it can be represented as a unit disk graph where the disks have diameter 1 and the disk centers are the grid vertices of the drawing. Finally, for each vertex
v\in T
we define
v^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
to be the unit disk centered leftmost vertex of the segment corresponding to
v
in the drawing. For
v\in V(G)
let
H^{\prime}_{v}
denote the set of vertices on the horizontal segment of
G^{\prime}
representing
v
. Similarly, for each edge
uv
of
G
let
S^{\prime}_{uv}
denote the
internal
vertices of the grid path representing the edge
uv
in
G^{\prime}
. We can now observe the following.

Observation 12.7
.

Given
(G,T)
, the instance
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
can be constructed in
O(n^{6})
time and the resulting graph has size
O(n^{6})
. Moreover,
G^{\prime}
is a subcubic dilation of a
36n^{4}
-subdivision of
G
that is
(6n^{2}-12n)
-bounded. In particular,
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

12.4
.

Using our construction and the above observation, we can wrap up the section with the following theorem.

See
1.8

Proof.

Let
k
be the number of vertices of the minimum Steiner tree in a planar graph
G
with terminals
T
, and let
X^{\prime}
be the vertex set of a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution for the instance
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
constructed above. Then in polynomial time we construct
X\subset V(G)
. Notice that by
Lemma

12.4
(
i
) we have that the minimum Steiner tree for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has at most
36(k-1)n^{4}+\varepsilon\cdot 36n^{4}
vertices, thus a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution has size at most
|X^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)(36(k-1)n^{4}+\varepsilon\cdot 36n^{4})<(1+\varepsilon)k\cdot 36n^{4}
. Thus by
Lemma

12.4
(
ii
) we can construct a Steiner tree of size at most
(1+\varepsilon)k
for
(G,T)
.

Now let
k
be the length of the minimum subset TSP tour of
G
for the terminal set
T
, and let
W^{\prime}
be a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate closed walk for the instance
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
constructed above. Then in polynomial time we construct the closed walk
W
using
Lemma

12.4
(
ii
). Notice that by
Lemma

12.4
(
i
) we have that the minimum subset TSP for
(G^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has at most
36kn^{4}+\varepsilon\cdot 36n^{4}
edges, thus a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solution has size at most
|W^{\prime}|\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)(36kn^{4}+\varepsilon\cdot 36n^{4})\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)(k+1)\cdot 36n^{4}
. Thus
Lemma

12.4
(
ii
) gives that
|W|<(1+\varepsilon)(k+1)-1<(1+2\varepsilon)k
, concluding the reduction.
∎

Appendix A
Converting into
\alpha
-standard intersection graphs

The following lemmas allow us to turn an intersection graph of similarly sized (e.g. unit) disks or arbitrary connected similarly sized fat polygons into an isomorphic intersection graph that is
\alpha
-standard.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of
\delta
-similarly-sized disks given via its representation. Then in polynomial time we can build an intersection graph
G^{\prime}
of
\delta
-similarly sized connected
(1/\sqrt{2})
-fat polygons with polynomial complexity.

Proof.

For each disk
d\in V(G)
let
S(d)
be the maximum axis-parallel square inscribed into
d
.
For each
d,d^{\prime}\in V(G)
where
d\cap d^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
let
p_{d,d^{\prime}}
be the midpoint of the arc
\partial d\cap d^{\prime}
. Let
p(d)
be the convex hull of the set
\sigma(d)\cup\bigcup_{d^{\prime}\in N(d)\setminus\{d\}}p_{d,d^{\prime}}
. Notice that the vertices of
p(d)
are on
\partial d
. Consider now the intersection graph
G^{\prime}
given by the convex polygons
\{p(d)\mid d\in V(G)\}
. We claim that
p(d),p(d^{\prime})
intersect in
G^{\prime}
if and only if
d,d^{\prime}
intersect in
G
. Since
p(d)\subset d
for each
d\in V(G)
, no new intersections are created. To check the maintenance of edges, let
c(x)
denotes the center of
x\in D_{2}
. For each
d^{\prime}\in N(d)
the intersection between
d,d^{\prime}
is maintained as the segment
c(d)p_{d,d^{\prime}}
intersects the segment
p_{d^{\prime},d}c(d^{\prime})
as both segments are on the line
c(d)c(d^{\prime})
, and the ordering of these four points on the line is
c(d),p_{d^{\prime},d},p_{d,d^{\prime}},c(d^{\prime})
, where
p_{d^{\prime},d}
and
p_{d,d^{\prime}}
might coincide.
It remains to show the fatness and similar size bound. Notice that
\sigma(d)\subset p(d)
and
\sigma(d)
contains an inscribed disk of radius
r_{d}/\sqrt{2}
where
r_{d}
is the radius of
d
, on the other hand
p(d)\subset d
, which proves the fatness bound.
Finding the points
p_{d,d^{\prime}}
takes
O(n^{2})
time. Using a standard convex hull algorithm
[
59
]
, the construction of the polygons
p(d)
can be executed in
O(n^{2}\log n)
time, as each
p(d)
is the convex hull of at most
4+n-1
vertices.
∎

We say that a pair of intersecting segments have a
true crossing
if they intersect in a single point, and neither segment contains any endpoint of the other segment.

Lemma A.2
.

If
p
and
q
are simple polygons where any edge
e
of
p
and any edge
f
of
q
are either disjoint or have a true crossing, then
\{p,q\}
satisfies the second fairness condition, that is, any component of their intersection is a simple polygon.

Proof.

Let
x
be some connected component of
p\cap q
. The lemma follows if
p\subset q
or
q\subset p
as then
x=p
or
x=q
, and
p
and
q
are simple polygon; assume that this is not the case, and let
A=\mathcal{A}(p,q)
be the arrangement given by
p
and
q
.

If
s
is a segment of
A
in
x
, then at least one of the two faces of
A
incident to
s
must also be in
x
, as otherwise
s
would be a segment of positive length in the intersection of some edge of
p
and some edge of
q
. Moreover, if
\dot{v}\in\partial x
is a vertex of
\partial x
, then either
\dot{v}\in V(p)\cap\inter q
,
v\in V(q)\cap\inter p
, or
\dot{v}
is a true crossing. Observe that in each of these cases
\dot{v}
has degree
2
in
\partial x
and has some incident face of
A
that is both in
p
and
q
. Finally, since
p
and
q
are closed,
x
is closed. We conclude that
x
is a non-self-intersecting polygon; it remains to show that it has exactly one boundary component.

Suppose that
h
is a hole of
x
, and let
f
be some edge of
h
. Then
f
cannot be on an edge of both
\partial p
and
\partial q
, so assume without loss of generality that
f\subset\partial p
. Since the other side of
\partial h
(namely,
x
) is in
p
, it follows that the face
F_{f}\subset\inter h
of
A
on the other side of
f
is not in
p
. On the other hand,
\partial h\subset x\subset p
, thus
F_{f}
is a bounded region outside
p
that is in the bounded region of the Jordan curve
\partial h\subset p
and thus in the bounded region of
\partial p
. Thus the bounded region of
\partial p
is not covered by
p
, which contradicts the fact that
p
is a simple polygon.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of
\delta
-similarly-sized
\beta
-fat non-self-intersecting polygons, possibly with holes, given via its representation of polynomial complexity. Then in polynomial time we can build an intersection graph
G^{\prime}
of
\delta
-similarly-sized
(\beta/4)
-fat simple polygons. Moreover, each polygon of
G^{\prime}
is a subpolygon of the corresponding polygon of
G
, and if
G
is
\alpha
-standard, then scaling the representation of
G^{\prime}
by
4
results in an
4\alpha
-standard graph.

Proof.

Let
W
be the collection of all vertices in the arrangement of the polygons of
V(G)
, and let
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
such that
x_{1}\leqslant x_{2}\dots\leqslant x_{N}
be the set of
x
-coordinates in
W
. Let
P=V(G)
be the initial collection of polygons, and fix in each polygon a large inscribed disk
d_{p}
of radius at least
\frac{\beta}{4}\mathrm{diam}(P)
we will modify
P
in several steps as follows.

Consider some polygon
p\in P
and let
h
be a hole of
p
. Take the vertical slab between the vertical lines
x=x_{p,h}
and
x=x^{\prime}_{p,h}
that intersects
h
such that
x_{p,h}
and
x^{\prime}_{p,h}
subdivide the distance between two consecutive numbers of
X
into three equal parts, i.e.,
x_{p,h}=\frac{2x_{i}+x_{i+1}}{3}
and
x^{\prime}_{p,h}=\frac{x_{i}+2x_{i+1}}{3}
for some
i\in[N-1]
. Note that since
h
is a simple polygon whose vertices project into some subset of
X
, there exists such a slab. Notice that the slab
s:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x_{p,h}\leqslant x\leqslant x^{\prime}_{p,h}\}
does not contain any polygon vertices. Consider the intersection
s
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus p
. Each region of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus p
belongs to either some hole
h^{p}_{i}
of
p
or the unbounded
\mathsf{U}_{p}
region of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus p
; see
Figure

18
.

Since there are no vertices of
p
in
s
, each edge of
p
cuts the slab with a segment, and thus there is a top-to-bottom ordering of the arrangement
\mathcal{A}(s\cap\partial p)
, where the regions of
p
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus p
alternate, and the top and bottom unbounded regions are in
\mathsf{U}_{p}
. Moreover, each region is a trapezoid and thus convex, so there is a unique region that intersects the disk
d_{p}
. Since
s
intersects
h
, consider the region of
p
before the first occurrence of a region of
h
as well as the region of
p
after the last occurrence of a region of
h
in this top-to-bottom order. At least one of these regions is disjoint from
d_{p}
; let
\mathsf{R}_{p,h}
be this region. Notice that and exactly one of the regions neighboring
\mathsf{R}_{p,h}
is in
h
, and the other is either in the unbounded face of
p
or in a different hole. Let
p^{\prime}
be the closure of
p\setminus\mathsf{R}_{p,h}
. As a result, either
h
is merged into the unbounded face of
p^{\prime}
or some other hole of
p^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{R}_{p,h}
.

If
q
is a polygon intersecting
p
, then it must have some region
\mathsf{A}
of the arrangement
\mathcal{A}(P)
that is inside
p\cap q
. Notice that
s
contains no vertices of
\mathsf{A}
, thus
\mathsf{A}\setminus s
is non-empty, and thus
p^{\prime}
intersects
q
. Moreover,
\mathrm{diam}(p)=\mathrm{diam}(p^{\prime})
(as the diameter of a polygon is realized between some pair of vertices, and no vertices of
p
have been removed), and
d_{p}\subset p^{\prime}
. Next, we replace the polygon
p
in
P
with
p^{\prime}
, and also update
X
to include the new points
x_{p,h},x^{\prime}_{p,h}
.

Figure 18:
Puncturing a polygon with holes to make it simple. (i) A polygon with holes and an inscribed disk. (ii) Finding a narrow slab of the arrangement without any vertices of the arrangement. The region after the last occurrence of the hole
h
cannot be removed as that would hurt fatness. (iii) Removing a part of
s\cap p
neighboring
h
decreases the number of holes. (iv) A simple fat polygon
p^{\prime}
with the same diameter and similar fatness.

We repeat this step exhaustively until we get a polygon collection
P
without holes. The step retains the connectedness of
p
: indeed, the top and bottom edge of the trapezoid
\mathsf{R}_{p,h}
remains connected in
p
via
\partial h
, as each hole
h
is a simple polygon (and thus has a single boundary component) that intersects
\partial\mathsf{R}_{p,h}
in a segment. Notice that each step reduces the total number of holes in the polygons of
P
by at least one. The polygon diameters remain unchanged, and we maintain a ball of radius
\frac{\beta}{4}\mathrm{diam}(p)
in each polygon
p
, thus the final polygon set
P
is a collection of
\delta
-similarly-sized
(\beta/4)
-fat simple polygons. Moreover, the intersection graph is maintained in each step as the polygon
p^{\prime}
replacing
p
is a subset of
p
(thus no new intersections can be created) but all intersections with any other
q\in P
are maintained by
p^{\prime}
. Thus the resulting intersection graph
G^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
G
.

If
G
was an
\alpha
-standard graph, then observe that no edges or vertices have been introduced to
G^{\prime}
that could create intersecting edges that do not make a true crossing, and no new concurrent edge triples are created as new edges avoid existing edges of the arrangement.
Lemma

A.2
implies that fairness is maintained. The newly introduced polygons are subpolygons of earlier polygons, and the maximum inscribed disks decreased by a factor of at most
4
. Thus, after scaling the new representation by
4
, the resulting representation is
4\alpha
-standard.

It remains to bound the running time of this construction. Notice that covering a polygon
p
with a disk requires radius at least
\mathrm{diam}(p)/2
, thus by the fatness bound each polygon contains a disk
d^{\prime}_{p}
of radius at least
\frac{\beta}{2}\mathrm{diam}(p)
. To find the desired disk
d_{p}
, we can consider a square grid of side length
\frac{\beta}{4\sqrt{2}}\mathrm{diam}(p)
. Among the grid points inside
p
there must be one whose center is within distance
\sqrt{2}\cdot\frac{\beta}{4\sqrt{2}}\mathrm{diam}(p)=\frac{\beta}{4}\mathrm{diam}(p)
to the center of
d^{\prime}_{p}
; the disk of radius
\frac{\beta}{4}\mathrm{diam}(p)
centered at this point is covered by
d^{\prime}_{p}
and thus covered by
p
, so it is a suitable choice for
d_{p}
. Clearly the grid has constantly many points inside the bounding box of
p
and we can check whether a disk is contained in
p
in linear time. Finally, notice that the initial arrangement
\mathcal{A}(P)
is polynomial and has
\mathrm{poly}(n)
holes; and each step adds at most
O(|\mathcal{A}(P)|)
new elements to the arrangement as there are only
2
new vertical segments introduced. Thus all configurations have polynomial complexity (and the same holds for their arrangements). We conclude that the construction of the representation of
G^{\prime}
can be executed in polynomial time.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
G
be an intersection graph of
\delta
-similarly-sized
\beta
-fat simple polygons given via its representation of polynomial complexity. Then in polynomial time we can build the representation of an
\alpha
-standard intersection graph
G^{\prime}
that is isomorphic to
G
where
\alpha=9/(\delta\beta)
.

Proof.

Let
P_{0}=V(G)
be the initial collection of simple polygons. We find a
1/4
-approximation
d_{\mathrm{in}}(p)
of the maximum enclosed disk of all polygons
p\in P_{0}
in polynomial time using the same method as seen in the proof of
Lemma

A.3
. We modify
P_{0}
in several steps. First, we scale
P_{0}
to ensure each polygon contains a disk of radius
1
(thus diameter
2
), i.e., let
P_{1}
be the set of polygons after
P_{0}
is scaled by
\frac{2}{\min_{p\in P_{0}}\mathrm{diam}(d_{\mathrm{in}}(p))}
. As a result, every polygon of
P_{1}
has diameter at most
8/(\beta\delta)
. Consider the arrangement
\mathcal{A}(P_{1})
, and let
E_{1}
and
V_{1}
denote its set of edges and vertices. We denote by
N
the total number of edges in the polygons of
P_{1}
, and let
\mu_{0}
the minimum distance among
V_{1}
and the minimum distance between non-incident vertices of
V_{1}
and
E_{1}
. Let
\mu_{1}:=\min\{\mu_{0}/(100N^{2}),1/(100N^{2})\}
.

We will now modify the polygons so that any pair of edges from distinct polygons intersect in a point, and no three polygons edges are concurrent. We say that an edge
f
of the polygon
p
is
bad
if either (a) there is some edge
f^{\prime}
of some polygon
p^{\prime}
where
p\neq p^{\prime}
and
f\cap f^{\prime}
contains some segment of positive length (i.e., they overlap) (b) there is some vertex
v\in V(p^{\prime})
on
f
from some polygon
p^{\prime}\neq p
, or (c) there is some vertex
v\in V_{1}
of degree at least
5
on
f
.

If
a,b
are the endpoints of
f
, then consider the outer angle bisector rays of
p
at
a
and
b
. For each
i=1,\dots,N
let
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
be points on the angle bisector from
a
and
b
at distance
i\mu_{1}
from
a
and
b
, respectively, where
i\leqslant 12N^{2}
. Let
p(i)
be the polygon where
a
and
b
are replaced with
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
. Notice that
p_{f}(i)
stays within distance
\mu_{0}/4
from
p
, thus if
p,q\in P_{1}
are disjoint, then
p_{f}(i),q_{f^{\prime}}(j)
are also disjoint for any
i,j\in[12N^{2}]
. Moreover,
p\subset p_{f}(i)
thus if
p
intersects
q
then
p_{f}(i)
intersects
q_{f^{\prime}}(j)
for all
i\neq j
. Finally, note that
p
and
p_{f}(i)
differ on three edges, and the curves
\partial p_{f}(i)\setminus\partial p
are pairwise disjoint for different
i
. Notice also that the polygon
p
remains simple after the modification.

We now exchange bad edges of
P_{1}
one by one so that whenever we replace some polygon
p
by
p(i)
, we ensure that all edges on the curve
\partial p_{f}(i)\setminus\partial p
are non-bad. We claim that choosing such an index
i\in[N^{2}]
is always possible. First, we observe that the total number
N
of edges in the polygon collection is unchanged after such a modification. Consequently, the number of vertices in the arrangement of the
N
total polygon edges is always at most
\binom{N}{2}
, thus any arrangement of this many edges produces a planar graph with at most
3\binom{N}{2}-6<2N^{2}
edges. We conclude that there is always a choice of
i\in[12N^{2}]
such that the
3
new edges given by the curve
\partial p_{f}(i)\setminus\partial p
are disjoint from all vertices of the current arrangement and they do not overlap with any edges of the current arrangement. Observe that a modification eliminates at least one bad edge, and the edges neighboring the modified edge cannot be bad either. In particular, if a vertex of
p
is moved, then the incident edges remain good, so no vertex of
p
is moved more than once. After at most
N
steps the resulting polygon collection
P_{2}
is free of bad edges.

By definition, each polygon of
P_{2}
includes a disk of radius
1
, and they have diameter less than
8/(\beta\delta)+1/2
. Moreover, the intersection graph is maintained throughout the modifications, so the resulting graph
G^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
G
.

By
Lemma

A.2
that
G^{\prime}
satisfies all properties of being
\alpha
-standard with
\alpha=8/(\beta\delta)+1/2<9/(\beta\delta)
.
∎

See
3.4

Proof.

In case of (a), use
Lemma

A.1
to obtain an isomorphic intersection graph of simple polygons of fatness
1/\sqrt{2}
that are
\delta
-similarly sized with representation complexity
O(n^{2})
. In case of (b), use
Lemma

A.3
to obtain an intersection graph of
\beta/4
-fat
\delta
-similarly-sized objects. On both of these graphs, we use
Lemma

A.4
to get an
\alpha
-standard graph of with
\alpha=9/(\delta\sqrt{2})
and
\alpha=36/(\beta\delta)
, respectively. Notice that each step increases the complexity of the representation polynomially, thus the final graph
G^{\prime}
also has
\mathrm{poly}(n)
complexity.
∎
